=\int_0^{t_1}\Bigl[D_xH\bigl(t,\wh x,\wh y,\lambda\bigr)+\dt\lambda(t)\Bigr]\cdot x_\varepsilon(t)\dd t
+\int_0^{t_1}D_yH\bigl(t,\wh x,\wh y,\lambda\bigr)\cdot\eta(t)\dd t
-\lambda(t_1)\cdot x_\varepsilon(t_1).
\]

\emph{Choosing the costate.}  Let $\lambda$ solve the linear terminal-value problem
\[
\dt\lambda(t)=-D_xH\bigl(t,\wh x(t),\wh y(t),\lambda(t)\bigr),
\qquad
\lambda(t_1)=0 ,
\]
which has a unique continuously differentiable solution on $[0,t_1]$ because the right-hand side is linear
in $\lambda$ with continuous coefficients.  This is \bk{(7.30)} and the terminal condition in \bk{(7.31)},
and it annihilates the first and third terms above, leaving
\[
\frac{\dd W}{\dd\varepsilon}\Big|_{0}=\int_0^{t_1}D_yH\bigl(t,\wh x,\wh y,\lambda\bigr)\cdot\eta(t)\dd t .
\]

\emph{Conclusion.}  Since $(\wh x,\wh y)$ is optimal and the variations are two-sided, this derivative must
vanish for every admissible $\eta$.  Taking $\eta$ supported on a small interval around any $t$ and equal to
each coordinate direction in turn, and using continuity of $D_yH$ along the solution, gives
\[
D_yH\bigl(t,\wh x(t),\wh y(t),\lambda(t)\bigr)=0\qquad\text{for all }t\in[0,t_1],
\]
which is \bk{(7.29)}.  Finally $\dt x=D_\lambda H=G$ is the state equation with $x(0)=x_0$, completing
\bk{(7.31)}.  This proves \bk{Theorem 7.7}.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{7.12}{}
Let $(\wh x,\wh y)$ satisfy \bk{(7.29)}--\bk{(7.31)} and let $X$ be convex.  Write
\[
M(t,x,\lambda)\equiv\max_{y\in Y}H(t,x,y,\lambda)
\]
and suppose it is concave in $x$.  Take any admissible pair $(x,y)$.  As in Exercise 7.8, for every $t$,
\[
f(t,x,y)=H(t,x,y,\lambda)-\lambda(t)\cdot G(t,x,y)\le M(t,x,\lambda)-\lambda(t)\cdot\dt x(t),
\]
with equality along $(\wh x,\wh y)$ since $\wh y$ maximizes $H$ pointwise.  Subtracting and using concavity
of $M$ in $x$ together with the envelope relation
$D_xM(t,\wh x,\lambda)=D_xH(t,\wh x,\wh y,\lambda)=-\dt\lambda(t)$,
\begin{align*}
W(x,y)-W(\wh x,\wh y)
&\le\int_0^{t_1}\Bigl[D_xM(t,\wh x,\lambda)\cdot\bigl(x-\wh x\bigr)
-\lambda\cdot\bigl(\dt x-\dt{\wh x}\bigr)\Bigr]\dd t\\
&=\int_0^{t_1}\Bigl[-\dt\lambda\cdot\bigl(x-\wh x\bigr)-\lambda\cdot\bigl(\dt x-\dt{\wh x}\bigr)\Bigr]\dd t
=-\Bigl[\lambda(t)\cdot\bigl(x(t)-\wh x(t)\bigr)\Bigr]_0^{t_1} .
\end{align*}
The lower limit vanishes because both paths start at $x_0$, and the upper limit vanishes because
$\lambda(t_1)=0$ by \bk{(7.31)}.  Hence $W(x,y)\le W(\wh x,\wh y)$ for every admissible pair:
$(\wh x,\wh y)$ is a global maximum.

If $M(t,\cdot,\lambda)$ is strictly concave, the first inequality is strict unless $x(t)=\wh x(t)$ almost
everywhere, so the maximizer is unique (and then $y=\wh y$ wherever $H$ has a unique maximizer).

\rmk{Exercise 7.7 is what makes the hypothesis checkable: joint concavity of $H$ in $(x,y)$ --- which
follows from concavity of $f$, linearity of $G$ in $(x,y)$, and $\lambda\ge0$ --- implies concavity of $M$
in $x$.  In the growth applications of the next chapters $G$ is linear (capital accumulation) and $f$ is
concave (utility), so the conditions hold automatically.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{7.13}{}
Let $(\wh x,\wh y)$ be admissible and satisfy \bk{(7.39)}--\bk{(7.41)}, let $X$ be convex, let
$M(t,x,\lambda)$ be concave in $x$ for all $t$, and suppose
\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}\lambda(t)\bigl(\wh x(t)-\wt x(t)\bigr)\le0
\qquad\text{for every admissible }\wt x .
\tag{$*$}
\]
Take any admissible pair $(\wt x,\wt y)$.  Exactly as in Exercise 7.8, for each finite $T$,
\[
\int_0^{T}f(t,\wt x,\wt y)\dd t-\int_0^{T}f(t,\wh x,\wh y)\dd t
\le-\Bigl[\lambda(t)\bigl(\wt x(t)-\wh x(t)\bigr)\Bigr]_0^{T}
=\lambda(T)\bigl(\wh x(T)-\wt x(T)\bigr),
\]
using $\wt x(0)=\wh x(0)=x_0$ for the lower limit.  This is the ``additional term'' the hint refers to:
in finite horizon it was eliminated by $\lambda(t_1)=0$ or by a fixed endpoint, and here it survives.

Now let $T\to\infty$.  The left-hand side converges to $W(\wt x,\wt y)-W(\wh x,\wh y)$ whenever the two
improper integrals exist (which admissibility guarantees), and the right-hand side is bounded above by
zero by hypothesis ($*$).  Hence
\[
W(\wt x,\wt y)-W(\wh x,\wh y)\le\limsup_{T\to\infty}\lambda(T)\bigl(\wh x(T)-\wt x(T)\bigr)\le0 ,
\]
so $(\wh x,\wh y)$ achieves the global maximum of \bk{(7.32)}.  If $M(t,\cdot,\lambda)$ is strictly concave,
the inequality leading to the display is strict unless $\wt x=\wh x$ almost everywhere, so the solution is
unique.

\rmk{Condition ($*$) is the honest price of infinite horizon, and the book rightly calls it hard to check:
it involves \emph{all} admissible comparison paths.  Two special cases make it usable.  If the state is
nonnegative and $\lambda(t)\ge0$ with $\lim_{t\to\infty}\lambda(t)\wh x(t)=0$ --- the transversality
condition \bk{(7.69)} --- then
$\lambda(T)(\wh x(T)-\wt x(T))\le\lambda(T)\wh x(T)\to0$ and ($*$) holds automatically.  This is exactly how
\bk{Theorem 7.14} is used in the applications: check the transversality condition, and concavity does the
rest.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{7.14}{}
The discounted problem has $f(t,x,y)=e^{-\rho t}f(x,y)$ and $g(t,x,y)=g(x,y)$.  Write the
present-value Hamiltonian and its current-value counterpart,
\[
H(t,x,y,\lambda)=e^{-\rho t}f(x,y)+\lambda(t)g(x,y),
\qquad
\wh H(t,x,y,\mu)\equiv e^{\rho t}H(t,x,y,\lambda)=f(x,y)+\mu(t)g(x,y),
\]
where the current-value costate is
\[
\mu(t)\equiv e^{\rho t}\lambda(t)
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\lambda(t)=e^{-\rho t}\mu(t).
\]
\bk{Theorem 7.9} applies to the problem and gives, at points of continuity of $\wh y$, the necessary
conditions \bk{(7.39)}--\bk{(7.41)}.  Translate each in turn.

\emph{\bk{(7.65)}.}  $H_y=0$ reads $e^{-\rho t}f_y+\lambda g_y=0$.  Multiplying by $e^{\rho t}>0$,
\[
f_y(x,y)+\mu(t)g_y(x,y)=\wh H_y\bigl(t,\wh x,\wh y,\mu\bigr)=0 ,
\]
which is \bk{(7.65)}.

\emph{\bk{(7.66)}.}  Differentiating $\lambda=e^{-\rho t}\mu$,
\[
\dt\lambda(t)=e^{-\rho t}\bigl[\dt\mu(t)-\rho\mu(t)\bigr],
\]
while $H_x=e^{-\rho t}f_x+\lambda g_x=e^{-\rho t}\bigl[f_x+\mu g_x\bigr]=e^{-\rho t}\wh H_x$.  Substituting
into $\dt\lambda=-H_x$ and cancelling $e^{-\rho t}>0$,
\[
\dt\mu(t)-\rho\mu(t)=-\wh H_x\bigl(t,\wh x,\wh y,\mu\bigr)
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\rho\mu(t)-\dt\mu(t)=\wh H_x\bigl(t,\wh x,\wh y,\mu\bigr),
\]
which is \bk{(7.66)}.

\emph{\bk{(7.67)}.}  The state equation is unchanged: $\dt x=H_\lambda=g(x,y)=\wh H_\mu$, with $x(0)=x_0$
and the limiting constraint $\lim_{t\to\infty}b(t)x(t)\ge x_1$ carried over from \bk{(7.41)}.

Since \bk{Theorem 7.9} gives \bk{(7.39)}--\bk{(7.41)} as necessary conditions for an interior piecewise
continuous solution, and the three translations above are equivalences, \bk{(7.65)}--\bk{(7.67)} are
necessary as well.

\rmk{The point of the current-value formulation is visible in \bk{(7.66)}: the term $\rho\mu$ appears
because $\mu$ is measured in date-$t$ rather than date-$0$ units, so its required rate of return has to
cover the discount rate.  Written as $\dt\mu/\mu=\rho-\wh H_x/\mu$, the condition is an asset-pricing
equation --- the shadow price of the state must appreciate at the discount rate less its ``dividend''.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{7.15}{}
\emph{(a) Rewriting the objective.}  For any continuously differentiable $\lambda$, the constraint
$\dt x=g$ gives $f=H-\lambda\dt x$ with $H(t,x,y)\equiv f(t,x,y)+\lambda(t)g(t,x,y)$.  Hence
\[
W=\int_0^{t_1}f\dd t=\int_0^{t_1}\bigl[H-\lambda(t)\dt x(t)\bigr]\dd t ,
\]
and integrating the last term by parts,
$\int_0^{t_1}\lambda\dt x\dd t=\lambda(t_1)x(t_1)-\lambda(0)x_0-\int_0^{t_1}\dt\lambda x\dd t$, so
\[
W\bigl(x(t),y(t)\bigr)=\int_0^{t_1}\bigl[H(t,x(t),y(t))+\dt\lambda(t)x(t)\bigr]\dd t
-\lambda(t_1)x(t_1)+\lambda(0)x_0 ,
\]
as required.

\emph{(b) Feasible variations.}  Let $(\wh x,\wh y,\wh t_1)$ be optimal.  Perturb the control on the
original interval and extend the horizon:
\[
y(t,\varepsilon)=\wh y(t)+\varepsilon\eta(t)\ \ \text{on }[0,\wh t_1],
\qquad
y(t,\varepsilon)=\wh y(\wh t_1)+\varepsilon\eta(t)\ \ \text{on }[\wh t_1,\wh t_1+\varepsilon\Delta t],
\]
with $t_1=\wh t_1+\varepsilon\Delta t$.  For $|\varepsilon|$ small this is feasible: $\wh y(t)$ is interior
to $Y$ and $\wh x(t)$ interior to $X$ on a compact interval, so small perturbations stay inside, and the
state path $x(t,\varepsilon)$ generated by $\dt x=g$ is well defined and differentiable in $\varepsilon$
(the extension is over an interval of length $O(\varepsilon)$, on which the state moves by $O(\varepsilon)$).

\emph{(c) The derivative.}  Differentiating the expression in (a) at $\varepsilon=0$, three groups of terms
appear.  The interior variation contributes
$\int_0^{\wh t_1}\bigl[H_x+\dt\lambda\bigr]x_\varepsilon\dd t+\int_0^{\wh t_1}H_y\eta\dd t$.  The moving
upper limit contributes $H(\wh t_1,\wh x(\wh t_1),\wh y(\wh t_1))\Delta t$ from the integral and
$-\lambda(\wh t_1)\dt x(\wh t_1)\Delta t$ from $-\lambda(t_1)x(t_1)$; the endpoint term also contributes
$-\lambda(\wh t_1)x_\varepsilon(\wh t_1)$.  Since the second of these enters $-\lambda(t_1)x(t_1)$ with a
minus sign and the total derivative of $x(t_1,\varepsilon)$ with respect to $\varepsilon$ is
$x_\varepsilon(\wh t_1)+\dt x(\wh t_1)\Delta t$, collecting terms gives exactly
\[
\frac{\dd W}{\dd\varepsilon}\Big|_{0}
=\int_0^{\wh t_1}\bigl[H_x+\dt\lambda\bigr]x_\varepsilon\dd t
+\int_0^{\wh t_1}H_y\,\eta\dd t
+H\bigl(\wh t_1,\wh x(\wh t_1),\wh y(\wh t_1)\bigr)\Delta t
-\lambda(\wh t_1)x_\varepsilon(\wh t_1)
+\lambda(\wh t_1)\dt x(\wh t_1)\Delta t ,
\]
the expression in the exercise.

\emph{(d) Why it vanishes.}  $(\wh x,\wh y,\wh t_1)$ maximizes $W$ over a set that contains the whole
family $\{(x(\cdot,\varepsilon),y(\cdot,\varepsilon),\wh t_1+\varepsilon\Delta t)\}$ for $\varepsilon$ in a
neighborhood of $0$ --- both signs are feasible, since the horizon may be lengthened or shortened and the
control perturbed in either direction.  A differentiable function of $\varepsilon$ attaining an interior
maximum at $\varepsilon=0$ has zero derivative there.

The conditions of the Maximum Principle follow.  Taking $\Delta t=0$ and $\eta$ arbitrary, and choosing
$\lambda$ to satisfy $\dt\lambda=-H_x$ with $\lambda(\wh t_1)=0$, gives $H_y=0$ as usual.  Then, with
$\lambda(\wh t_1)=0$, the remaining terms leave $H(\wh t_1,\wh x(\wh t_1),\wh y(\wh t_1))\Delta t=0$ for
every $\Delta t$, so
\[
\boxed{\ H\bigl(\wh t_1,\wh x(\wh t_1),\wh y(\wh t_1),\lambda(\wh t_1)\bigr)=0\ }
\]
--- the extra transversality condition associated with a free terminal date.  Its meaning is an optimal
stopping rule: continue as long as the flow value of continuing, measured by the Hamiltonian, is positive,
and stop when it reaches zero.

\emph{(e) Letting $\wh t_1\to\infty$.}  The condition becomes
\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}H\bigl(t,\wh x(t),\wh y(t),\lambda(t)\bigr)=0 ,
\]
which is precisely the infinite-horizon transversality condition \bk{(7.56)} of \bk{Theorem 7.12}.  So the
``vanishing Hamiltonian'' that is usually presented as a separate infinite-horizon result is the same
condition as the free-terminal-time condition of the finite-horizon problem: an infinite horizon is a
horizon that is optimally never reached, and the value of stopping at the margin must be zero at every
sufficiently distant date.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{7.16}{}
The discounted problem has $f(t,x,y)=e^{-\rho t}f(x,y)$ and $g(t,x,y)=g(x,y)$: neither the payoff apart
from the discount factor nor the constraint depends on calendar time.

\emph{Admissibility.}  The continuation satisfies $\dt{\wh x}(t)=g(\wh x(t),\wh y(t))$ for all $t\ge s$ and
$\wh x(s)$ is its own initial condition, with $(\wh x(t),\wh y(t))\in X\times Y$; so it is admissible for
the problem started at $s$ with initial state $\wh x(s)$.

\emph{Optimality.}  The objective of the date-$s$ problem is
\[
W_s(x,y)\equiv\int_s^{\infty}e^{-\rho(t-s)}f\bigl(x(t),y(t)\bigr)\dd t
=e^{\rho s}\int_s^{\infty}e^{-\rho t}f\bigl(x(t),y(t)\bigr)\dd t ,
\]
so it ranks continuation plans exactly as the date-$0$ objective does: the two differ by the positive
constant $e^{\rho s}$.  Suppose the continuation were not optimal.  Then there is an admissible
$(\wt x,\wt y)$ on $[s,\infty)$ with $\wt x(s)=\wh x(s)$ and
\[
\int_s^{\infty}e^{-\rho t}f\bigl(\wt x,\wt y\bigr)\dd t>\int_s^{\infty}e^{-\rho t}f\bigl(\wh x,\wh y\bigr)\dd t .
\]
Splice the plans:
\[
\bigl(\vec x(t),\vec y(t)\bigr)=
\begin{cases}
\bigl(\wh x(t),\wh y(t)\bigr), & t\in[0,s),\\
\bigl(\wt x(t),\wt y(t)\bigr), & t\ge s .
\end{cases}
\]
This is admissible from $x_0$: it satisfies the state equation on each piece, and the two pieces agree at
$t=s$ because $\wt x(s)=\wh x(s)$, so $\vec x$ is continuous and satisfies $\dt{\vec x}=g(\vec x,\vec y)$
throughout.  Its value at date $0$ is
\[
\int_0^{s}e^{-\rho t}f\bigl(\wh x,\wh y\bigr)\dd t+\int_s^{\infty}e^{-\rho t}f\bigl(\wt x,\wt y\bigr)\dd t
>\int_0^{\infty}e^{-\rho t}f\bigl(\wh x,\wh y\bigr)\dd t ,
\]
contradicting the optimality of $(\wh x,\wh y)$ from date $0$.  Hence the continuation is optimal for the
date-$s$ problem.

\rmk{Two features do the work, and both are needed: the objective is \emph{exponentially} discounted, so
that re-dating multiplies it by a constant and leaves the ranking of continuations unchanged; and $g$ is
autonomous, so that the constraint faced from $s$ onward is the same correspondence, only with a different
initial state.  Drop the first and time consistency fails --- this is exactly \bk{Exercise 7.22}(b), where
$\beta(t)=e^{-\rho t}$ turns out to be necessary as well as sufficient.  The statement in the exercise
writes the date-$s$ initial condition as $x(s)=x_0$; what is meant, and what is proved above, is that the
continuation is optimal for the problem started at the state the optimal path has reached,
$x(s)=\wh x(s)$.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{7.20}{}
Recall the problem: maximize $\int_0^{\infty}e^{-\rho t}u(y(t))\dd t$ subject to $\dt x=-y$, $x(0)=1$,
$x(t)\ge0$, with $u:[0,1]\to\R$ strictly increasing, continuously differentiable and strictly concave.  The
Hamiltonian is $H(t,x,y,\lambda)=e^{-\rho t}u(y)-\lambda y$, and \bk{Theorem 7.9} gives \bk{(7.58)} and
\bk{(7.59)}:
\[
u'\bigl(\wh y(t)\bigr)=e^{\rho t}\lambda(t),
\qquad
\dt\lambda(t)=0\ \Longrightarrow\ \lambda(t)\equiv\lambda(0)>0 ,
\]
so that the Hotelling rule $\dt\mu/\mu=\rho$ holds for $\mu=e^{\rho t}\lambda$ and
\[
\wh y(t)=\bigl(u'\bigr)^{-1}\bigl[\lambda(0)e^{\rho t}\bigr].
\]
Since $u'$ is strictly decreasing and $\lambda(0)e^{\rho t}\to\infty$, extraction falls monotonically with
$\wh y(t)\to0$; the constant $\lambda(0)$ is fixed by exhaustion,
$\int_0^{\infty}\wh y(t)\dd t=x(0)$.

\emph{Checking \bk{(7.56)}.}  The condition is $\lim_{t\to\infty}H(t,\wh x(t),\wh y(t),\lambda(t))=0$.  Here
\[
H\bigl(t,\wh x(t),\wh y(t),\lambda(t)\bigr)=e^{-\rho t}u\bigl(\wh y(t)\bigr)-\lambda(0)\wh y(t).
\]
Both terms vanish in the limit:
\begin{itemize}
\item $\wh y(t)\to0$ and $u$ is continuous on $[0,1]$, so $u(\wh y(t))\to u(0)$, a finite number, and
      $e^{-\rho t}u(\wh y(t))\to0$;
\item $\lambda(0)\wh y(t)\to0$ because $\wh y(t)\to0$ and $\lambda(0)$ is a constant.
\end{itemize}
Hence $\lim_{t\to\infty}H=0$: the transversality condition \bk{(7.56)} holds.

\emph{An explicit case.}  With $u(y)=\log y$ (unbounded below at $0$, so strictly outside the stated
domain, but the standard illustration), $\wh y(t)=e^{-\rho t}/\lambda(0)$, and exhaustion
$\int_0^\infty\wh y\dd t=1/(\rho\lambda(0))=x(0)$ gives $\lambda(0)=1/(\rho x(0))$ and
$\wh y(t)=\rho x(0)e^{-\rho t}$: the resource is depleted at the constant proportional rate $\rho$.  Then
\[
H(t)=e^{-\rho t}\log\bigl(\rho x(0)e^{-\rho t}\bigr)-\frac{1}{\rho x(0)}\rho x(0)e^{-\rho t}
=e^{-\rho t}\bigl[\log(\rho x(0))-\rho t-1\bigr]\longrightarrow0 ,
\]
since $te^{-\rho t}\to0$.

\rmk{Note which transversality condition is being verified.  The \emph{stronger} condition \bk{(7.69)}
requires $\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-\rho t}\mu(t)\wh x(t)$ to vanish; since $e^{-\rho t}\mu(t)=\lambda(0)$ here,
it reduces to $\lambda(0)\lim\wh x(t)=0$, which holds precisely because the resource is fully exhausted,
$\wh x(t)\to0$.  \bk{Exercise 7.18} modifies
the problem so that a positive stock $x_1$ must be left behind.  The stronger condition then fails, while
the weaker one still holds --- which is exactly why the book states \bk{(7.56)} as the general
result.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{7.22}{}
\emph{(a) Necessary conditions.}  The Hamiltonian is
\[
H(t,x,y,\lambda)=\beta(t)f(x,y)+\lambda(t)g(x,y),
\]
and \bk{Theorem 7.9} gives, along an interior solution,
\[
H_y=0:\quad\beta(t)f_y(x,y)+\lambda(t)g_y(x,y)=0,
\qquad
\dt\lambda=-H_x:\quad\dt\lambda(t)=-\bigl[\beta(t)f_x+\lambda(t)g_x\bigr],
\]
together with $\dt x=g(x,y)$, $x(0)=x_0$ and the transversality condition \bk{(7.56)}.  Assume
$g_y\neq0$, so the first condition can be solved for the costate:
\[
\lambda(t)=-\beta(t)\frac{f_y\bigl(x(t),y(t)\bigr)}{g_y\bigl(x(t),y(t)\bigr)}\equiv\beta(t)\,\Lambda(t),
\tag{$*$}
\]
where $\Lambda$ depends on the path only, not on the discount function.

\emph{(b) Time consistency forces exponential discounting.}  Consider re-solving the problem at date $s>0$
with the same structure --- that is, with discount function $\beta(t-s)$ applied to returns $t-s$ away ---
starting from the state $\wh x(s)$ that the original plan has reached.  Its necessary conditions are the
same with $\beta(t)$ replaced by $\beta(t-s)$ and costate $\lambda_s$.  Time consistency means that the
original path $(\wh x,\wh y)$ continues to solve this problem, so it must satisfy both sets of conditions.
From ($*$) applied to each,
\[
\lambda(t)=\beta(t)\Lambda(t),
\qquad
\lambda_s(t)=\beta(t-s)\Lambda(t)
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\lambda_s(t)=k(t)\lambda(t),\quad k(t)\equiv\frac{\beta(t-s)}{\beta(t)} .
\]
Both costates satisfy their own costate equations along the \emph{same} path:
\[
\dt\lambda=-\bigl[\beta(t)f_x+\lambda g_x\bigr],
\qquad
\dt\lambda_s=-\bigl[\beta(t-s)f_x+\lambda_sg_x\bigr]=-\bigl[k(t)\beta(t)f_x+k(t)\lambda g_x\bigr]
=k(t)\dt\lambda(t).
\]
But differentiating $\lambda_s=k\lambda$ directly gives $\dt\lambda_s=\dt k\lambda+k\dt\lambda$.  Comparing,
$\dt k(t)\lambda(t)=0$, and since $\lambda(t)\neq0$ along an interior solution, $\dt k(t)=0$: the ratio
$\beta(t-s)/\beta(t)$ is independent of $t$.  Evaluating it at $t=s$ gives
$k=\beta(0)/\beta(s)$, so for all $t\ge s\ge0$,
\[
\beta(t-s)\beta(s)=\beta(t)\beta(0).
\]
Normalizing $\beta(0)=1$, this is the Cauchy functional equation $\beta(t-s)\beta(s)=\beta(t)$; its only
positive continuous solutions are
\[
\beta(t)=e^{-\rho t}\qquad\text{for some }\rho ,
\]
with $\rho\ge0$ if the discount function is nonincreasing (and $\rho>0$ needed for the objective to
converge).  Conversely, if $\beta(t)=e^{-\rho t}$, then $\beta(t-s)=e^{\rho s}\beta(t)$, the two systems
differ by the positive constant $e^{\rho s}$, and the original path satisfies both --- time consistency,
which is Exercise 7.16.

\emph{(c) Interpretation, and the contrast with \bk{Lemma 7.1}.}  The result is the continuous-time
counterpart of the discrete-time statement in \bk{Exercise 5.1}: only exponential discounting makes the
trade-off between two future dates independent of the date from which it is viewed.  With any other
$\beta$ --- hyperbolic discounting, say --- the planner's relative valuation of dates $t$ and $t'$ changes
as time passes, so at date $s$ she would like to revise the plan made at date $0$ even though nothing has
been learned and no state has been surprised.

The contrast with \bk{Lemma 7.1} is worth stating carefully, since the lemma is also called a principle of
optimality and holds for general $f(t,x,y)$.  \bk{Lemma 7.1} says that the value function computed
\emph{from the date-$0$ perspective} is recursive: $V(t_0,x)=\int_{t_0}^{t_1}f\dd t+V(t_1,\wh x(t_1))$.
That is a statement about one fixed objective, and it is true whatever the discount function, because
$V(t_1,\cdot)$ is itself defined with the date-$0$ weights.  Time consistency in the sense of part (b) is a
different claim: that the plan optimal under the date-$0$ objective is still optimal under the
\emph{date-$s$} objective, which applies $\beta(t-s)$ rather than $\beta(t)$.  Only exponential discounting
makes the two objectives agree, up to scale, on the ranking of continuations.  In short, \bk{Lemma 7.1} is
about a consistent bookkeeping of one plan; part (b) is about whether a later self would keep it.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{7.27}{}
The problem of Section 7.8 is
\[
\max\int_0^{\infty}e^{-rt}\bigl[f(K)-I-\phi(I)\bigr]\dd t
\qquad\text{s.t.}\qquad
\dt K=I-\delta K,\quad K(0)>0 ,
\]
with $f$ satisfying Assumptions 1 and 2 of Chapter 2 and $\phi$ strictly increasing, strictly convex,
continuously differentiable, with $\phi(0)=\phi'(0)=0$.

\emph{(a) \bk{Assumption 7.1} on a compact state space.}  Restrict $K\in[\varepsilon,\bar k]$ with
$0<\varepsilon<\bar k<\infty$; investment can then also be restricted to a compact interval, since
$\dt K=I-\delta K$ and $K$ is bounded, and since paying $I+\phi(I)$ for arbitrarily large $I$ is never
optimal.  Now check the three parts:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Monotonicity.}  The payoff $f(K)-I-\phi(I)$ is increasing in $K$ (as $f'>0$) and decreasing in
      $I$ (as $1+\phi'>0$), so it is weakly monotone in each argument; $g(K,I)=I-\delta K$ is increasing in
      $I$, decreasing in $K$, and independent of $t$.
\item \emph{$|g_I|\ge m$.}  Here $g_I\equiv1$, so the condition holds with $m=1$ --- the control moves the
      state one for one.
\item \emph{$|f_I|\le M$.}  Here $|f_I|=1+\phi'(I)$, which is bounded on the compact interval to which $I$
      has been restricted, so the condition holds with $M=1+\max\phi'$.
\end{enumerate}
It is the third condition that fails on the unbounded state space, since $\phi'$ is unbounded; this is
exactly why the compactification is needed before \bk{Theorem 7.13} can be invoked.

\emph{(b) From the modified problem to the original one.}  Suppose the modified problem has a solution
$(\wh K,\wh I)$ with $\wh K(t)>\varepsilon$ for all $t$, so the artificial constraint never binds.  Then
$(\wh K,\wh I)$ satisfies the necessary conditions \bk{(7.85)} of the unconstrained problem, including the
transversality condition $\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-rt}q(t)\wh K(t)=0$.  Now appeal to sufficiency rather than
necessity: the current-value Hamiltonian
\[
\wh H(K,I,q)=f(K)-I-\phi(I)+q\bigl[I-\delta K\bigr]
\]
is strictly concave in $(K,I)$ --- $f$ is strictly concave, $-\phi$ is strictly concave, and the remaining
terms are linear --- so by Exercise 7.7 the maximized Hamiltonian is strictly concave in $K$, and
\bk{Theorem 7.14} applies on the \emph{whole} feasible set $K\in\R_+$: any path satisfying the necessary
conditions together with the transversality condition is the unique global maximum among all feasible
paths, not merely among those confined to $[\varepsilon,\bar k]$.  Hence $(\wh K,\wh I)$ solves the original
problem.  (Note that sufficiency does not use \bk{Assumption 7.1} at all; the assumption was needed only to
obtain the strong transversality condition as a \emph{necessary} condition.)

\emph{(c) Such an $\varepsilon$ exists.}  The steady state of the system \bk{(7.84)}, \bk{(7.88)} satisfies
\[
I^{*}=\delta K^{*},
\qquad
f'(K^{*})=(r+\delta)\bigl(1+\phi'(I^{*})\bigr),
\]
which has a unique positive solution because the left side is strictly decreasing in $K$ from $\infty$ to
$0$ (Assumptions 1 and 2) while the right side is strictly increasing in $K$.  The optimal path is the
stable arm of a saddle point --- the linearized system has one positive and one negative eigenvalue --- and
$K(t)$ moves monotonically from $K(0)$ to $K^{*}$.  Therefore
\[
\inf_{t\ge0}K(t)=\min\bigl\{K(0),K^{*}\bigr\}>0 ,
\]
and any $\varepsilon<\min\{K(0),K^{*}\}$ works: the modified problem's solution never touches the
artificial lower bound, and part (b) applies.

With $f(K)=K^{1/3}$, $\phi(I)=I^{2}$, $\delta=r=0.05$, the numbers are
\[
K^{*}=3.767391,
\qquad
I^{*}=\delta K^{*}=0.188370,
\qquad
q^{*}=1+\phi'(I^{*})=1.376739 ,
\]
and the eigenvalues of the linearized system are $-0.108440$ and $+0.158440$ --- one of each sign, as the
saddle-path structure requires.  Figure~\ref{fig:ch7q} shows the phase diagram and the stable arm, which
stays well away from $K=0$.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics{figures/ch7_qtheory_saddle.pdf}
\caption{The $q$-theory phase diagram: the loci $\dt K=0$ and $\dt I=0$, their intersection at
$(K^{*},I^{*})$, and the stable arm along which the firm approaches the steady state.}
\label{fig:ch7q}
\end{figure}

\rmk{Adjustment costs lower the steady-state capital stock: with $\phi\equiv0$ the steady state would solve
$f'(K)=r+\delta$, giving $K=6.085806$ in this parameterization, against $3.767391$ with adjustment costs.
The reason is visible in the steady-state condition --- the required marginal product is scaled up by
$q^{*}=1+\phi'(I^{*})>1$, the shadow price of installed capital, because replacing depreciated capital is
itself costly.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{7.29}{}
Suppose the candidate path has $I(t)=0$ for all $t\ge t'$.  Then from \bk{(7.84)} its capital stock decays
exponentially,
\[
K(t)=K(t')e^{-\delta(t-t')}\longrightarrow0
\qquad\text{as }t\to\infty .
\]

\emph{The deviation.}  Fix $T>t'$ and $\epsilon>0$ small, and consider investing $\epsilon$ at every date
$t\ge T$ instead of nothing.  Two things make the accounting simple.  First, the extra \emph{cost} at each
date is $\epsilon+\phi(\epsilon)$, and since $\phi(0)=\phi'(0)=0$ we have $\phi(\epsilon)=o(\epsilon)$: to
first order the adjustment cost of a small investment is zero, so the cost is $\epsilon$ per unit time.
Second, the extra \emph{capital} raises output at the margin by $f'(K(t))$ per unit.  To first order in
$\epsilon$ the change in the present value of profits is therefore
\[
\Delta=\epsilon\int_T^{\infty}e^{-rt}\bigl[f'\bigl(K(t)\bigr)-1\bigr]\dd t+o(\epsilon).
\]

\emph{The sign.}  By Assumption 2 of Chapter 2, $\lim_{K\to0}f'(K)=\infty$.  Since $K(t)\to0$ along the
candidate path, there is a $\wt K>0$ with $f'(K)>1$ for all $K<\wt K$, and a date $T$ with
$K(t)<\wt K$ --- hence $f'(K(t))>1$ --- for all $t\ge T$.  For that $T$ the integrand is strictly positive
throughout, so $\Delta>0$ for $\epsilon$ small: the deviation strictly raises the present value of profits,
and the candidate path is not optimal.

\emph{Quantitatively}, with $f(K)=AK^{\alpha}$ the threshold is explicit: $f'(K)>1$ if and only if
$K<(A\alpha)^{1/(1-\alpha)}$, which equals $0.192450$ for $A=1$, $\alpha=1/3$.  Starting from the steady
state $K^{*}=3.767391$ and switching investment off, capital falls below that threshold after
$\log(K^{*}/0.19245)/\delta\approx59.5$ periods, and $f'(K(t))\to\infty$ thereafter.

\emph{Conclusion.}  No optimal path sets investment to zero permanently after a finite date: gross
investment is strictly positive in the long run, and indeed the optimal path converges to the steady state
with $I^{*}=\delta K^{*}>0$.  The economics is the Inada condition doing its usual work --- capital that has
become scarce is arbitrarily productive at the margin, while the marginal cost of a small amount of
investment is only its purchase price, adjustment costs being second order at $I=0$.

\rmk{The same argument shows why the constraint $I\ge0$ studied in \bk{Exercise 7.28}(f) --- irreversible
investment --- binds only on the way \emph{down}, never permanently: a firm may wish to disinvest faster
than depreciation allows, but never to abstain from investment forever.}
\end{aex}


%% ========================================================================
\solchapter{8}{The Neoclassical Growth Model}

\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{0.86\textwidth}
\small
The twenty-four exercises of Chapter 8 left unsolved in the instructor's manual: Exercises 8.1, 8.3--8.6,
8.8--8.10, 8.12, 8.14, 8.16--8.18, 8.20--8.22, 8.24, 8.26, 8.28, 8.29, 8.32, 8.35, 8.36 and 8.39.  The
manual solves 8.2, 8.7, 8.11, 8.13, 8.15, 8.19, 8.23, 8.25, 8.27, 8.30, 8.31, 8.33, 8.34, 8.37 and 8.38.
Notation follows the book: $a=A/L$ are per capita assets, $k$ the capital-labor ratio (the effective
capital-labor ratio $K/AL$ from Section 8.7 on), $\tilde c=c/A$ normalized consumption, $\mu$ the
current-value costate, and $\varepsilon_u(c)=-u''(c)c/u'(c)$.  The numerical parts are computed by
\texttt{code/ch8.py}.
\end{minipage}
\end{center}

\bigskip
\hrule
\bigskip

\begin{aex}{8.1}{}
\emph{(a) The household's problem.}  The household has $L(t)=e^{nt}$ members, is fully altruistic and
utilitarian, so at each date it values the sum of its members' instantaneous utilities,
$\int_0^{L(t)}u(c_i(t))\dd i$, and discounts this flow at the subjective rate $\rho$.  Its members share
the total consumption $C(t)$ that the household's budget allows, and the household's assets accumulate at
the market return.  Hence
\[
\max\ \int_0^{\infty}e^{-\rho t}\Bigl[\int_0^{L(t)}u\bigl(c_i(t)\bigr)\dd i\Bigr]\dd t
\quad\text{s.t.}\quad
\int_0^{L(t)}c_i(t)\dd i\le C(t),
\qquad
\dt A(t)=r(t)A(t)+W(t)-C(t),
\]
with $W(t)=w(t)L(t)$ total labor income and $A(0)$ given.

\emph{(b) Reduction.}  Fix a date and a total $C(t)$.  The inner problem is to maximize
$\int_0^{L}u(c_i)\dd i$ subject to $\int_0^{L}c_i\dd i=C$.  Since $u$ is strictly concave, Jensen's
inequality gives
\[
\frac{1}{L}\int_0^{L}u(c_i)\dd i\le u\Bigl(\frac{1}{L}\int_0^{L}c_i\dd i\Bigr)=u\Bigl(\frac{C}{L}\Bigr),
\]
with equality if and only if $c_i$ is the same for (almost) every member.  So the cooperative household
gives every member $c(t)\equiv C(t)/L(t)$, and the date-$t$ flow of utility is
\[
L(t)u\bigl(c(t)\bigr)=e^{nt}u\bigl(c(t)\bigr).
\]
Discounting at $\rho$ therefore gives the objective
\[
\int_0^{\infty}e^{-\rho t}e^{nt}u\bigl(c(t)\bigr)\dd t=\int_0^{\infty}e^{-(\rho-n)t}u\bigl(c(t)\bigr)\dd t ,
\]
which is \bk{(8.3)}.  For the constraint, divide $\dt A=rA+wL-cL$ by $L(t)$ and use
$a=A/L$, so that $\dt a=\dt A/L-a\dt L/L$:
\[
\dt a(t)=r(t)a(t)+w(t)-c(t)-na(t)=\bigl(r(t)-n\bigr)a(t)+w(t)-c(t) ,
\]
which is \bk{(8.8)}.

\emph{Intuition.}  Two adjustments turn a growing household into a single infinitely lived consumer.  The
\emph{effective discount rate} falls from $\rho$ to $\rho-n$: the household discounts the future at $\rho$
but there are more members to enjoy it, at the rate $n$, and with utilitarian altruism the two effects
net out.  The \emph{effective return} falls from $r$ to $r-n$: assets have to be spread over a population
growing at $n$, so per capita assets earn only $r-n$.  Both adjustments make the population growth rate
behave exactly like a shift in impatience and in the return --- which is why Assumption 4$'$ requires
$\rho>n$, so that the transformed problem still discounts.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.3}{}
\emph{Accumulation with zero consumption.}  With $c\equiv0$ the law of motion \bk{(8.7)} is the linear
differential equation $\dt A(s)=r(s)A(s)+w(s)L(s)$.  Its solution with $A(t)=0$ is, by the
integrating-factor formula,
\[
A(T)=\int_t^{T}w(s)L(s)\exp\Bigl(\int_s^{T}r(z)\dd z\Bigr)\dd s ,
\]
so, discounting back to $t$,
\[
A(T)\exp\Bigl(-\int_t^{T}r(z)\dd z\Bigr)=\int_t^{T}w(s)L(s)\exp\Bigl(-\int_t^{s}r(z)\dd z\Bigr)\dd s
\ \xrightarrow[T\to\infty]{}\ \bar W(t) .
\]
$\bar W(t)$ is thus the present value at $t$ of the household's entire future labor income.

\emph{Why the limit.}  A household that never defaults must be able to repay its debt out of future
resources.  Consumption cannot be negative, so the largest present value it can ever devote to repayment
is exactly $\bar W(t)$.  Hence feasibility (in the absence of bankruptcy) requires
\[
A(t)\ \ge\ -\bar W(t),
\]
and any consumption-asset path violating this at some date is infeasible.

\emph{Per capita form \bk{(8.11)}.}  Divide by $L(t)$ and use $L(s)=L(t)e^{n(s-t)}$:
\[
a(t)=\frac{A(t)}{L(t)}\ \ge\ -\int_t^{\infty}w(s)\frac{L(s)}{L(t)}e^{-\int_t^sr(z)\dd z}\dd s
=-\int_t^{\infty}w(s)\exp\Bigl(-\int_t^{s}\bigl(r(z)-n\bigr)\dd z\Bigr)\dd s ,
\]
which is \bk{(8.11)}: population growth discounts the future wage stream at the \emph{effective} rate
$r-n$, exactly as it discounts utility at $\rho-n$.

\emph{Relation to \bk{(6.44)}.}  The discrete-time limit reads
$a(t)\ge-\sum_{s\ge0}\bigl[\prod_{j=0}^{s}(1+r(t+j))^{-1}\bigr]w(t+s)$.  Replacing the product of gross
returns by the exponential of the integral of the instantaneous rate, and the sum by an integral, gives
\bk{(8.11)}: the two are the same statement --- borrowing is limited by the present value of the endowment
stream --- written in the two time conventions.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.4}{}
Write $q(t)\equiv\exp\bigl(-\int_0^{t}(r(s)-n)\dd s\bigr)$ for the effective discount factor, and let
\[
\wt a(t)\equiv a(t)q(t)
\]
be discounted per capita assets.  From \bk{(8.8)},
\[
\dt{\wt a}(t)=q(t)\bigl[\dt a(t)-(r(t)-n)a(t)\bigr]=q(t)\bigl[w(t)-c(t)\bigr]\le q(t)w(t),
\tag{$*$}
\]
using $c\ge0$.

Suppose \bk{(8.11)} fails at some finite $t'$, that is
\[
a(t')<-\int_{t'}^{\infty}w(s)\exp\Bigl(-\int_{t'}^{s}(r(z)-n)\dd z\Bigr)\dd s ,
\]
equivalently, multiplying by $q(t')>0$,
\[
\wt a(t')<-\int_{t'}^{\infty}q(s)w(s)\dd s .
\]
Integrating ($*$) from $t'$ to $T$ and letting $T\to\infty$,
\[
\lim_{T\to\infty}\wt a(T)\ \le\ \wt a(t')+\int_{t'}^{\infty}q(s)w(s)\dd s\ <\ 0 .
\]
So the discounted asset position converges to a strictly negative limit, which is precisely what
\bk{(8.12)} rules out: the household ends with debt whose present value does not vanish, so it could not
have repaid it even with zero consumption from $t'$ on.  Hence \bk{(8.12)} implies \bk{(8.11)} at every
date, and there is no loss of generality in imposing the single limiting constraint rather than a
continuum of them.

\rmk{The logic is the same one that makes a single terminal condition suffice in finite horizon: the
budget set is defined by the flow constraint plus \emph{one} solvency requirement, because the flow
constraint already links all the dates.  This is why the book can work with \bk{(8.12)}, and later with the
no-Ponzi condition \bk{(8.14)}, rather than with the family \bk{(8.11)}.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.5}{}
Equation \bk{(8.13)} states, for every $T>0$,
\[
\int_0^{T}c(t)L(t)e^{\int_t^{T}r(s)\dd s}\dd t+A(T)
=\int_0^{T}w(t)L(t)e^{\int_t^{T}r(s)\dd s}\dd t+A(0)e^{\int_0^{T}r(s)\dd s} .
\]
Differentiate both sides with respect to $T$.  For a term of the form
$\Phi(T)=\int_0^{T}h(t)e^{\int_t^{T}r(s)\dd s}\dd t$, Leibniz's rule gives
\[
\dt\Phi(T)=h(T)+r(T)\Phi(T),
\]
since differentiating the exponent brings down $r(T)$ inside the integral.  Applying this to both integrals
and to the last term,
\[
c(T)L(T)+r(T)\int_0^{T}c(t)L(t)e^{\int_t^{T}r}\dd t+\dt A(T)
=w(T)L(T)+r(T)\int_0^{T}w(t)L(t)e^{\int_t^{T}r}\dd t+r(T)A(0)e^{\int_0^{T}r} .
\]
Now use \bk{(8.13)} itself to replace the two $r(T)\times(\cdot)$ terms: their difference is
$r(T)\bigl[A(0)e^{\int_0^Tr}+\int_0^Tw L e^{\int_t^Tr}-\int_0^TcLe^{\int_t^Tr}\bigr]-r(T)A(0)e^{\int_0^Tr}
=r(T)A(T)$.  Hence
\[
\dt A(T)=r(T)A(T)+w(T)L(T)-c(T)L(T),
\]
which is \bk{(8.7)}.  Dividing by $L(T)$ and using $a=A/L$ with $\dt L/L=n$, as in Exercise 8.1, gives
\[
\dt a(t)=\bigl(r(t)-n\bigr)a(t)+w(t)-c(t),
\]
which is \bk{(8.8)}.  The two formulations are therefore equivalent: the lifetime constraint carried
forward to any date $T$ is the integrated version of the flow constraint.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.6}{}
The transversality condition \bk{(8.19)} of the household problem is
\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}\Bigl[e^{-(\rho-n)t}\mu(t)a(t)\Bigr]=0 .
\]
From \bk{(8.24)} --- the integrated form of the costate equation \bk{(8.18)} --- the current-value costate is
\[
\mu(t)=\mu(0)\exp\Bigl(-\int_0^{t}\bigl(r(s)-\rho\bigr)\dd s\Bigr),
\qquad \mu(0)=u'\bigl(c(0)\bigr)>0 .
\]
Hence
\[
e^{-(\rho-n)t}\mu(t)
=\mu(0)\exp\Bigl(-(\rho-n)t-\int_0^{t}r(s)\dd s+\rho t\Bigr)
=\mu(0)\exp\Bigl(-\int_0^{t}\bigl(r(s)-n\bigr)\dd s\Bigr),
\]
so that \bk{(8.19)} becomes
\[
\mu(0)\lim_{t\to\infty}\Bigl[a(t)\exp\Bigl(-\int_0^{t}\bigl(r(s)-n\bigr)\dd s\Bigr)\Bigr]=0 .
\]
Since $\mu(0)=u'(c(0))>0$, the limit itself must vanish:
\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}\Bigl[a(t)\exp\Bigl(-\int_0^{t}\bigl(r(s)-n\bigr)\dd s\Bigr)\Bigr]=0 ,
\]
which is \bk{(8.16)}.

The role of \bk{(8.14)} is to make this an equality rather than merely an upper bound.  The no-Ponzi
condition states that the same limit is $\ge0$: the household may not end with debt of positive present
value.  Optimization then rules out the opposite slack: by the transversality condition the household does
not end with \emph{assets} of positive present value either, since those could have been consumed.  Putting
the two together gives \bk{(8.16)}, the statement that the lifetime budget constraint \bk{(8.15)} holds
with equality.

\rmk{This is the continuous-time counterpart of the familiar finite-horizon simplification $A(T)\ge0$ plus
local nonsatiation implying $A(T)=0$.  Financial markets impose the inequality; the household's own
optimization supplies the equality.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.8}{}
\emph{(a) One direction.}  Suppose $\lim_{t\to\infty}a(t)\ge\hat a>-\infty$, and note that in any
equilibrium $r(t)\to r^{*}>n$ (see \bk{(8.36)}), so that
$\int_0^{t}(r(s)-n)\dd s\to\infty$ and the effective discount factor
$q(t)=\exp(-\int_0^{t}(r(s)-n)\dd s)$ tends to $0$.  Then for large $t$, $a(t)\ge\hat a-\varepsilon$, so
\[
a(t)q(t)\ \ge\ \bigl(\hat a-\varepsilon\bigr)q(t)\longrightarrow0 ,
\]
whence $\lim_{t\to\infty}a(t)q(t)\ge0$, which is \bk{(8.14)}.  A bounded debt is always repayable in
present value.

\emph{The converse fails.}  Take $w(t)=e^{gt}w(0)$ and $r(t)=r$ constant, and consider the asset path
\[
a(t)=-Me^{(r-n)t/2},\qquad M>0 .
\]
Then $a(t)q(t)=-Me^{-(r-n)t/2}\to0$, so \bk{(8.14)} holds with equality, yet $a(t)\to-\infty$, so
\bk{(8.12)} cannot hold with any finite $\hat a$.  Debt that grows, but more slowly than the interest
factor, is ruled out by the natural debt limit only if that limit is finite --- and the no-Ponzi condition
permits it.

\emph{(b) With sustained growth the natural debt limit is vacuous.}  The relaxed limit \bk{(8.12)} bounds
assets below by
\[
\hat a=-\lim_{t\to\infty}\bar w(t),
\qquad
\bar w(t)\equiv\int_t^{\infty}w(s)\exp\Bigl(-\int_t^{s}(r(z)-n)\dd z\Bigr)\dd s .
\]
Along the BGP of Section 8.7 wages grow at the rate $g$ and the interest rate is constant at
$r^{*}=\rho+\theta g$, so
\[
\bar w(t)=\frac{w(t)}{r^{*}-n-g}=\frac{w(0)e^{gt}}{\rho-n-(1-\theta)g} ,
\]
which is finite at each date --- exactly Assumption 4 --- but grows without bound.  Hence
$\hat a=-\lim_{t\to\infty}\bar w(t)=-\infty$: the constraint set is unbounded below, so there is no lower
bound on the state variable, and the compactness-type hypotheses behind \bk{Theorems 7.13} and \bk{7.14}
(in particular the requirement in Theorem 7.14 that $\lim e^{-(\rho-n)t}\mu(t)a(t)\ge0$ for \emph{all}
feasible paths) cannot be verified from the natural debt limit.  The no-Ponzi condition \bk{(8.14)}
supplies exactly that requirement directly, which is why the book adopts it.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.9}{}
Let $r(t)\to r^{*}>n$ and $w(t)\to w<\infty$.  Choose $\varepsilon>0$ with $r^{*}-\varepsilon>n$ and a
date $T$ such that for all $s\ge T$,
\[
r(s)\ge r^{*}-\varepsilon
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
w(s)\le w+\varepsilon .
\]
Then for every $t\ge T$,
\[
\bar w(t)=\int_t^{\infty}w(s)\exp\Bigl(-\int_t^{s}(r(z)-n)\dd z\Bigr)\dd s
\le(w+\varepsilon)\int_t^{\infty}e^{-(r^{*}-\varepsilon-n)(s-t)}\dd s
=\frac{w+\varepsilon}{r^{*}-\varepsilon-n}<\infty ,
\]
a bound independent of $t$.  Hence
\[
\hat a=-\lim_{t\to\infty}\bar w(t)\ \ge\ -\frac{w+\varepsilon}{r^{*}-\varepsilon-n}\ >\ -\infty .
\]

The two hypotheses are exactly what is needed, and each is indispensable.  If $r^{*}\le n$ the geometric
discounting at the effective rate $r-n$ fails and the integral diverges even for constant wages; if wages
grow without bound --- the case of technological progress in Exercise 8.8(b) --- the numerator grows faster
than the discounting shrinks.  In the model without technological progress both hold at the steady state:
$r^{*}=f'(k^{*})-\delta=\rho>n$ by Assumption 4$'$, and $w^{*}=f(k^{*})-k^{*}f'(k^{*})<\infty$.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.10}{}
\emph{(a) Same necessary conditions, same solution.}  Consider maximizing \bk{(8.3)} subject to \bk{(8.8)}
and $\lim_{t\to\infty}a(t)\ge\hat a$ with $\hat a>-\infty$.  The current-value Hamiltonian is the same as
in Section 8.2.2,
\[
\wh H(t,a,c,\mu)=u(c)+\mu\bigl[w(t)+(r(t)-n)a-c\bigr] ,
\]
and the state is now bounded below, so \bk{Theorem 7.13} applies and yields the same candidate conditions
\bk{(8.17)}--\bk{(8.19)}: $u'(c)=\mu$, $\dt\mu/\mu=-(r-\rho)$ and
$\lim e^{-(\rho-n)t}\mu(t)a(t)=0$.  Concavity of $\wh H$ in $(a,c)$ then makes \bk{Theorem 7.14} applicable,
so these conditions are also sufficient.

It remains to check that the extra constraint does not bind.  By Exercise 8.6, the transversality condition
is equivalent to $\lim a(t)q(t)=0$ with $q(t)=\exp(-\int_0^t(r-n))$.  A path with
$\lim a(t)=\hat a$ finite also has $\lim a(t)q(t)=0$, so the constraint is satisfied with slack at the
optimum: the household never wants to run its debt down to the natural limit, since doing so would require
zero consumption forever, which is dominated whenever $u'(0)>0$.  Hence the two problems have the same
solution, characterized by the Euler equation \bk{(8.22)} and \bk{(8.16)}.

\emph{(b) In equilibrium.}  Market clearing gives $a(t)=k(t)\ge0$, and the equilibrium path converges to
the steady state with
\[
r^{*}=f'(k^{*})-\delta=\rho>n
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
w^{*}=f(k^{*})-k^{*}f'(k^{*})<\infty
\]
by Assumption 4$'$ and \bk{(8.36)}.  These are precisely the hypotheses of Exercise 8.9, so
$\hat a>-\infty$: in the model without technological progress the natural debt limit is a legitimate way to
close the household problem, and it gives the same answer as the no-Ponzi condition.  (With technological
progress it is not, by Exercise 8.8(b).)
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.12}{}
The two loci in Figure 8.1 are
\[
\dt c=0:\quad f'(k)=\rho+\delta
\qquad\text{(a vertical line at }k^{*}),
\qquad\qquad
\dt k=0:\quad c=f(k)-(n+\delta)k .
\]
The second is a hump-shaped curve whose maximum is at the golden rule capital-labor ratio, defined by
\[
\frac{\dd}{\dd k}\bigl[f(k)-(n+\delta)k\bigr]=0
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
f'\bigl(k_{\text{gold}}\bigr)=n+\delta .
\]

\emph{The comparison.}  Assumption 4$'$ states $\rho>n$, hence
\[
f'(k^{*})=\rho+\delta>n+\delta=f'\bigl(k_{\text{gold}}\bigr),
\]
and since $f$ is strictly concave, $f'$ is strictly decreasing, so
\[
k^{*}<k_{\text{gold}} .
\]
The vertical line therefore always cuts the hump on its \emph{upward-sloping} portion, strictly to the left
of the peak.  With the parameters of the accompanying program ($\alpha=1/3$, $\delta=0.05$, $\rho=0.02$,
$n=0.01$, $g=0.02$, $\theta=1$, so that the relevant comparison is
$f'(k^{*})=\rho+\delta+\theta g=0.09$ against $f'(k_{\text{gold}})=n+g+\delta=0.08$):
\[
k^{*}=7.1278<k_{\text{gold}}=8.5052 .
\]

\emph{Why they differ.}  The golden rule maximizes \emph{steady-state} consumption, treating all dates
alike.  The household does not: it discounts future utility at $\rho>n$, so it is unwilling to give up
consumption today in order to raise consumption in a distant steady state.  The modified golden rule
\bk{(8.35)} equates the net marginal product of capital to the discount rate rather than to the
replacement requirement, and since $\rho>n$ the household stops accumulating short of $k_{\text{gold}}$.

Two consequences are worth recording.  First, the neoclassical economy is never dynamically inefficient:
because $k^{*}<k_{\text{gold}}$, there is no way to raise consumption at all dates by cutting capital, in
contrast to the Solow model with an arbitrarily high saving rate.  Second, the gap between $k^{*}$ and
$k_{\text{gold}}$ is a pure measure of impatience --- it closes as $\rho\downarrow n$ and widens as
households become less patient.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics{figures/ch8_saddle_path.pdf}
\caption{The neoclassical phase diagram: the vertical $\dt{\tilde c}=0$ locus cuts the hump-shaped
$\dt k=0$ locus strictly to the left of $k_{\text{gold}}$, and the stable arm is the unique equilibrium
path.}
\label{fig:ch8phase}
\end{figure}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.14}{}
\emph{(a) What happens at and after $T$.}  Output per capita is $f(k)$ with $f(0)=0$ (Assumption 2), and
capital cannot be negative, so once $k(T)=0$ the economy has no resources: feasibility forces
\[
c(t)=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad k(t)=0\qquad\text{for all }t\ge T .
\]
Along the approach, the interest rate is $r(t)=f'(k(t))-\delta$, and the Inada condition
$\lim_{k\to0}f'(k)=\infty$ gives
\[
\lim_{t\to T}r(t)=\infty .
\]
Capital becomes infinitely scarce, so its rental rate explodes.

\emph{(b) A profitable deviation.}  Consider a household that, at some $t'<T$, reduces consumption by a
small $\epsilon>0$ for an instant and saves the proceeds until $T$.  The utility cost, in date-$0$ units,
is approximately
\[
\text{cost}\simeq\epsilon\,e^{-(\rho-n)t'}u'\bigl(c(t')\bigr)<\infty ,
\]
which is finite because $c(t')>0$ for $t'<T$.  The saving accumulates at the market rate, so at $T$ the
household has $\epsilon\exp\bigl(\int_{t'}^{T}r(t)\dd t\bigr)$ extra units to consume, at a marginal
utility of $u'(0)\ge u'(c(t'))$ (and $u'(0)=\infty$ if $u$ satisfies an Inada condition).  The utility
gain is approximately
\[
\text{gain}\simeq\epsilon\exp\Bigl(-(\rho-n)T+\int_{t'}^{T}r(t)\dd t\Bigr)u'(0).
\]
Since $u'(0)\ge u'(c(t'))$, the deviation is profitable as soon as
\[
\exp\Bigl(-(\rho-n)T+\int_{t'}^{T}r(t)\dd t\Bigr)>e^{-(\rho-n)t'}
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\frac{1}{T-t'}\int_{t'}^{T}r(t)\dd t>\rho-n .
\]
By part (a), $r(t)\to\infty$ as $t\to T$, so the average of $r$ over $[t',T]$ diverges as $t'\uparrow T$,
while $\rho-n$ is a fixed finite number.  Hence for $t'$ close enough to $T$ the inequality holds strictly:
every household strictly prefers to save rather than follow the candidate path.

Therefore the candidate is not consistent with household optimization and cannot be an equilibrium.
Equilibrium paths never exhaust the capital stock in finite time --- this is the content of the lower
branch of Figure 8.1 being ruled out, and it is what leaves the stable arm as the unique equilibrium.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.16}{}
The household now values only consumption \emph{per member}, without weighting by the number of members:
it is no longer utilitarian over its own members but treats the representative member's consumption as the
object of interest.  The budget constraint is unchanged, since it is an accounting identity:
$\dt a=(r-n)a+w-c$.

\emph{Necessary conditions.}  The current-value Hamiltonian is
\[
\wh H(t,a,c,\mu)=u(c)+\mu\bigl[w+(r-n)a-c\bigr] ,
\]
discounted at $\rho$ rather than $\rho-n$, so \bk{Theorem 7.13} gives
\[
u'(c)=\mu,
\qquad
\rho\mu-\dt\mu=\mu\bigl(r-n\bigr)
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\frac{\dt\mu}{\mu}=-\bigl(r-n-\rho\bigr),
\]
and hence the Euler equation
\[
\frac{\dt c(t)}{c(t)}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon_u(c(t))}\bigl(r(t)-n-\rho\bigr).
\]
The transversality condition becomes
\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}\Bigl[e^{-\rho t}\mu(t)a(t)\Bigr]=0 ,
\]
with $e^{-\rho t}$ in place of $e^{-(\rho-n)t}$.

\emph{Steady state.}  Setting $\dt c=0$ gives $r^{*}=\rho+n$, and with $r=f'(k)-\delta$,
\[
f'(k^{*})=\rho+n+\delta .
\]
Comparing with \bk{(8.35)}: population growth now \emph{does} affect the steady state, and
\[
\frac{\partial k^{*}}{\partial n}=\frac{1}{f''(k^{*})}<0 ,
\]
whereas in the baseline model $\partial k^{*}/\partial n=0$ (\bk{Proposition 8.3}).  Faster population
growth lowers the steady-state capital-labor ratio and hence output per capita.

\emph{Why.}  In the baseline model two effects of population growth cancel exactly: assets must be spread
over more people, which lowers the effective return to $r-n$, but the household also values the
consumption of its new members, which lowers the effective discount rate to $\rho-n$.  Here only the first
effect is present --- the household does not care that there are more members to enjoy consumption --- so
saving must earn $\rho+n$ rather than $\rho$ to be worthwhile, and the capital stock is correspondingly
lower.  Finally, discounting now requires only $\rho>0$ rather than $\rho>n$: Assumption 4$'$ can be
weakened accordingly.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.17}{}
With $f(k)=A\wt f(k)$ the steady state \bk{(8.35)} is
\[
A\wt f'\bigl(k^{*}\bigr)=\rho+\delta ,
\tag{$*$}
\]
and steady-state consumption is $c^{*}=A\wt f(k^{*})-(n+\delta)k^{*}$ by \bk{(8.37)}.

\emph{The capital-labor ratio.}  Differentiating ($*$) implicitly, and using $\wt f''<0$,
\[
\frac{\partial k^{*}}{\partial A}=-\frac{\wt f'(k^{*})}{A\wt f''(k^{*})}>0,
\qquad
\frac{\partial k^{*}}{\partial\rho}=\frac{\partial k^{*}}{\partial\delta}=\frac{1}{A\wt f''(k^{*})}<0,
\qquad
\frac{\partial k^{*}}{\partial n}=0 ,
\]
the last because $n$ does not appear in ($*$) at all: the effective return $r-n$ and the effective discount
rate $\rho-n$ shift together, as Exercise 8.16 makes explicit.

\emph{Consumption.}  Differentiating $c^{*}$ and using ($*$) to substitute $A\wt f'(k^{*})=\rho+\delta$,
\[
\frac{\partial c^{*}}{\partial x}
=\bigl[A\wt f'(k^{*})-(n+\delta)\bigr]\frac{\partial k^{*}}{\partial x}+\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Bigl|_{k^*\text{ fixed}}
=(\rho-n)\frac{\partial k^{*}}{\partial x}+\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Bigl|_{k^*\text{ fixed}} ,
\]
where the second term collects the direct effect.  Since $\rho>n$ by Assumption 4$'$, the coefficient
$(\rho-n)$ is strictly positive, so the indirect effect always has the same sign as
$\partial k^{*}/\partial x$.  Hence
\[
\frac{\partial c^{*}}{\partial A}=\wt f(k^{*})+(\rho-n)\frac{\partial k^{*}}{\partial A}>0,
\qquad
\frac{\partial c^{*}}{\partial\rho}=(\rho-n)\frac{\partial k^{*}}{\partial\rho}<0,
\]
\[
\frac{\partial c^{*}}{\partial n}=-k^{*}<0,
\qquad
\frac{\partial c^{*}}{\partial\delta}=(\rho-n)\frac{\partial k^{*}}{\partial\delta}-k^{*}<0 .
\]
This is \bk{Proposition 8.3}.

\rmk{The positivity of $\rho-n$ is doing real work here, and it is the same fact as Exercise 8.12: because
$k^{*}<k_{\text{gold}}$, the economy is on the upward-sloping part of the steady-state consumption curve,
so anything that raises $k^{*}$ also raises $c^{*}$.  In the Solow model, where the saving rate is a free
parameter, this is not guaranteed --- an economy above the golden rule raises consumption by saving
\emph{less} (Exercise 8.19 pursues this contrast).}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.18}{}
\emph{Without technological progress.}  The Euler equation \bk{(8.22)} is
\[
\frac{\dt c(t)}{c(t)}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon_u(c(t))}\bigl(r(t)-\rho\bigr).
\]
In a steady state consumption per capita is constant, $\dt c=0$; since
$\varepsilon_u(c)$ is finite and positive, the \emph{only} way to have $\dt c=0$ is
\[
r^{*}=\rho
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
f'(k^{*})=\rho+\delta ,
\]
whatever the shape of $u$.  The utility function drops out because it enters the Euler equation only
through the factor $1/\varepsilon_u$ multiplying $r-\rho$, and multiplying zero by any finite number gives
zero.  What $u$ does affect is the \emph{speed} of the transition: $\varepsilon_u$ governs how fast
consumption responds to a given gap between $r$ and $\rho$, and hence the eigenvalue of the linearized
system.

\emph{With technological progress.}  Now per capita consumption grows at the rate $g$ in the BGP, so the
left-hand side of the Euler equation is no longer zero.  With CRRA preferences \bk{(8.49)},
$\varepsilon_u\equiv\theta$, and a steady state with $\dt c/c=g$ requires
\[
g=\frac{1}{\theta}\bigl(r^{*}-\rho\bigr)
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
r^{*}=\rho+\theta g
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
f'(k^{*})=\rho+\delta+\theta g ,
\]
which is \bk{(8.53)}: the elasticity now appears.  The economics is transparent.  A growing consumption
path means that marginal utility is falling over time, and the rate at which it falls is $\theta g$.  The
household is willing to hold capital only if its return compensates it both for impatience, $\rho$, and for
the fact that the extra consumption arrives when it is already better off, $\theta g$.  The less willing
the household is to substitute consumption across time (the larger $\theta$, the smaller the elasticity
$1/\theta$), the larger the required return and the smaller the steady-state effective capital-labor ratio.

In one sentence: preferences matter for the steady state only when marginal utility is changing along it,
and that happens exactly when there is growth.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.20}{}
On the BGP consumption per capita grows at the constant rate $g$, so $c(t)=c(0)e^{gt}$, and with CRRA
preferences \bk{(8.49)} the objective is
\[
U=\int_0^{\infty}e^{-(\rho-n)t}\,\frac{c(t)^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}\dd t
=\frac{c(0)^{1-\theta}}{1-\theta}\int_0^{\infty}e^{-[(\rho-n)-(1-\theta)g]t}\dd t
-\frac{1}{1-\theta}\int_0^{\infty}e^{-(\rho-n)t}\dd t .
\]
The second integral converges if and only if $\rho>n$, which is Assumption 4$'$ and is implied by
Assumption 4 whenever $\theta\le1$; the first converges if and only if its exponent is negative:
\[
(\rho-n)-(1-\theta)g>0
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\rho-n>(1-\theta)g ,
\]
which is exactly Assumption 4.  When it holds,
\[
U=\frac{c(0)^{1-\theta}}{(1-\theta)\bigl[\rho-n-(1-\theta)g\bigr]}-\frac{1}{(1-\theta)(\rho-n)}<\infty ;
\]
when it fails, the first integral diverges, and $U=+\infty$ for $\theta<1$ (so no allocation can be
optimal and the maximization problem is not well posed).

For the logarithmic case $\theta=1$ the computation is direct: $u(c(t))=\log c(0)+gt$, so
\[
U=\int_0^{\infty}e^{-(\rho-n)t}\bigl[\log c(0)+gt\bigr]\dd t
=\frac{\log c(0)}{\rho-n}+\frac{g}{(\rho-n)^{2}},
\]
finite if and only if $\rho>n$, which is Assumption 4 with $\theta=1$ since $(1-\theta)g=0$.

\rmk{The condition has a clean reading: the effective discount rate $\rho-n$ must exceed the growth rate of
\emph{utility}, which is $(1-\theta)g$ rather than $g$ itself, because a household with a low intertemporal
elasticity ($\theta$ large) values additional consumption less as it grows richer.  For $\theta\ge1$
Assumption 4 is implied by $\rho>n$, which is Exercise 8.29.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.21}{}
The problem is to maximize \bk{(8.49)} subject to \bk{(8.8)} and the no-Ponzi condition \bk{(8.14)}.  The
current-value Hamiltonian is
\[
\wh H(t,a,c,\mu)=\frac{c^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}+\mu(t)\bigl[w(t)+(r(t)-n)a-c\bigr] .
\]
\bk{Theorem 7.14} requires three things.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{A well-defined, finite objective.}  By Exercise 8.20, Assumption 4 is exactly the condition
      under which the integral converges along the BGP (and hence along any path converging to it), so the
      value function is finite.
\item \emph{Concavity.}  $\wh H$ is the sum of a strictly concave function of $c$ and a function that is
      linear in $(a,c)$, hence concave in $(a,c)$ and strictly concave in $c$.  By Exercise 7.7 the
      maximized Hamiltonian is concave in the state, so the concavity hypothesis holds.
\item \emph{The limiting condition.}  \bk{Theorem 7.14} needs
      $\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-(\rho-n)t}\mu(t)a(t)\ge0$ for every feasible $(a(t),c(t))$.  Substituting
      $\mu(t)=\mu(0)\exp(-\int_0^t(r-\rho))$ as in Exercise 8.6, this is
      \[
      \lim_{t\to\infty}\Bigl[a(t)\exp\Bigl(-\int_0^{t}(r(s)-n)\dd s\Bigr)\Bigr]\ \ge\ 0 ,
      \]
      which is precisely the no-Ponzi condition \bk{(8.14)}: no feasible path violates it, by construction
      of the constraint set.
\end{enumerate}
Hence any path satisfying the necessary conditions \bk{(8.17)}--\bk{(8.19)} is a global maximum, and by
strict concavity in $c$ it is the unique one.

\emph{If Assumption 4 fails.}  Then $\rho-n\le(1-\theta)g$ and, by Exercise 8.20, utility along the BGP is
infinite.  Every feasible path that grows at rate $g$ yields $+\infty$, so there is no meaningful ranking
of allocations: the maximization problem has no solution in the usual sense, the value function is not
finite, and \bk{Theorem 7.14} --- whose statement presumes a finite objective --- cannot be applied.  The
transversality condition \bk{(8.52)} also fails, since the exponent $\rho-n-(1-\theta)g$ is no longer
positive.  Economically, the household is too patient relative to the growth rate of the economy: it would
always postpone consumption, and no equilibrium with a well-defined intertemporal allocation exists.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.22}{}
With labor-augmenting progress at rate $g$ and CRRA preferences, the equilibrium is characterized by the
planar system
\[
\dt k(t)=f\bigl(k(t)\bigr)-\tilde c(t)-(n+g+\delta)k(t),
\qquad
\frac{\dd\tilde c/\dd t}{\tilde c}=\frac{1}{\theta}\Bigl[f'\bigl(k(t)\bigr)-\delta-\rho-\theta g\Bigr],
\tag{$*$}
\]
with $k(0)>0$ given and the transversality condition \bk{(8.52)}.

\emph{Step 1: a unique steady state with positive consumption.}  Setting the second equation to zero gives
$f'(k^{*})=\rho+\delta+\theta g$, which has a unique positive solution because $f'$ is continuous, strictly
decreasing and, by Assumption 2, ranges over $(0,\infty)$.  Setting the first to zero at $k^{*}$ gives
$\tilde c^{*}=f(k^{*})-(n+g+\delta)k^{*}$, and this is strictly positive because
\[
f'(k^{*})=\rho+\delta+\theta g>n+g+\delta
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\rho-n>(1-\theta)g ,
\]
which is Assumption 4.  So $k^{*}<k_{\text{gold}}$ and the steady state lies on the upward-sloping part of
the $\dt k=0$ locus, where $f(k)-(n+g+\delta)k>0$.

\emph{Step 2: the steady state is a saddle.}  The Jacobian of ($*$) at $(k^{*},\tilde c^{*})$ is
\[
J=\begin{pmatrix}
f'(k^{*})-(n+g+\delta) & -1\\[2pt]
\dfrac{\tilde c^{*}f''(k^{*})}{\theta} & 0
\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
\operatorname{tr}J=\rho-n-(1-\theta)g>0,
\qquad
\det J=\frac{\tilde c^{*}f''(k^{*})}{\theta}<0 ,
\]
using $f''<0$ and $\tilde c^{*}>0$.  A negative determinant means the two eigenvalues are real and of
opposite sign,
\[
\xi_{1}=\tfrac12\Bigl[\operatorname{tr}J-\sqrt{(\operatorname{tr}J)^{2}-4\det J}\Bigr]<0<\xi_{2},
\]
so the steady state is a saddle point with a one-dimensional stable manifold.  With the parameters of the
program ($\alpha=1/3$, $\delta=0.05$, $\rho=0.02$, $n=0.01$, $g=0.02$, $\theta=1$) the eigenvalues are
$-0.1019$ and $+0.1119$.

\emph{Step 3: the stable arm is the unique equilibrium.}  Fix $k(0)>0$.  There is exactly one value
$\tilde c(0)$ on the stable manifold, and starting from it the path converges to $(k^{*},\tilde c^{*})$.
Any other choice of $\tilde c(0)$ leads off the manifold, and the phase diagram leaves only two
possibilities:
\begin{itemize}
\item $\tilde c(0)$ too high: the path crosses the $\dt k=0$ locus and capital falls, reaching $k=0$ in
      finite time.  By Exercise 8.14 such a path cannot be an equilibrium --- households would deviate,
      since the interest rate diverges as capital vanishes.
\item $\tilde c(0)$ too low: consumption falls to zero while capital rises above $k_{\text{gold}}$ toward
      the level $\bar k$ with $f'(\bar k)<\delta+g+n$.  Then the exponent in the transversality condition
      \bk{(8.52)} stays bounded away from $-\infty$, so
      $\lim k(t)\exp\bigl(-\int_0^t[f'(k)-g-\delta-n]\bigr)>0$: the condition fails, and by
      \bk{Theorem 7.14} the path is dominated.  It is also plainly wasteful --- the economy accumulates
      capital it never consumes.
\end{itemize}
Hence for each $k(0)>0$ there is a unique equilibrium, the one starting on the stable arm.

\emph{Step 4: convergence is monotone.}  Along the stable arm the sign of $\dt k$ does not change: the arm
lies below the $\dt k=0$ locus for $k<k^{*}$ and above it for $k>k^{*}$, and it cannot cross either locus
without leaving the manifold.  Therefore $k(t)$ moves monotonically to $k^{*}$ and, since $\tilde c$ is an
increasing function of $k$ along the arm (its slope is $\operatorname{tr}J-\xi_{1}>0$, equal to $0.1119$ in
the numerical example, which the program confirms against a numerically traced arm, $0.1123$), normalized
consumption converges monotonically to $\tilde c^{*}$ as well.  This establishes \bk{Proposition 8.7}.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.24}{}
Set $\theta=1$ in \bk{(8.55)} and \bk{(8.56)}.  With $x\equiv k^{\alpha-1}$ (so that $x=f(k)/k$ is the
average product of capital) and $z\equiv\tilde c/k$, the system becomes
\[
\frac{\dt x}{x}=-(1-\alpha)\bigl[x-\delta-g-n-z\bigr],
\qquad
\frac{\dt z}{z}=z-(1-\alpha)x-(\rho-n) .
\tag{$*$}
\]

\emph{The steady state in closed form.}  From the second equation $\dt z=0$ gives
$z^{*}=(1-\alpha)x^{*}+\rho-n$, and from the first $x^{*}=\delta+g+n+z^{*}$.  Solving the two,
\[
x^{*}=\frac{\rho+\delta+g}{\alpha},
\qquad
z^{*}=x^{*}-(\delta+g+n)=\frac{\rho+\delta+g}{\alpha}-(\delta+g+n),
\]
equivalently $k^{*}=\bigl[\alpha/(\rho+\delta+g)\bigr]^{1/(1-\alpha)}$ and
$\tilde c^{*}=f(k^{*})-(n+g+\delta)k^{*}$, which is \bk{(8.53)}--\bk{(8.54)} with $\theta=1$.

\emph{The transition in closed form.}  The system ($*$) is a planar quadratic system and does not have an
elementary general solution, but its behavior near the BGP does, and that is what the transitional
dynamics require.  Linearizing the original system in $(k,\tilde c)$ at the steady state, as in Exercise
8.22 with $\theta=1$,
\[
\operatorname{tr}J=\rho-n,
\qquad
\det J=\tilde c^{*}f''(k^{*})=-\frac{(1-\alpha)(\rho+\delta+g)\tilde c^{*}}{k^{*}},
\]
\[
\xi_{1,2}=\frac{(\rho-n)\mp\sqrt{(\rho-n)^{2}+4(1-\alpha)(\rho+\delta+g)\tilde c^{*}/k^{*}}}{2},
\qquad \xi_1<0<\xi_2 ,
\]
and along the stable arm the solution is, in closed form,
\[
\boxed{\ k(t)=k^{*}+\bigl(k(0)-k^{*}\bigr)e^{\xi_{1}t},
\qquad
\tilde c(t)=\tilde c^{*}+\bigl(\rho-n-\xi_{1}\bigr)\bigl(k(0)-k^{*}\bigr)e^{\xi_{1}t}\ }
\]
the coefficient $\rho-n-\xi_{1}=\operatorname{tr}J-\xi_{1}>0$ being the slope of the stable arm, obtained
from the eigenvector of $J$ associated with $\xi_{1}$.  The speed of convergence is $|\xi_{1}|$, so the
half-life of the transition is $\log2/|\xi_{1}|$.  With $\alpha=1/3$, $\delta=0.05$, $\rho=0.02$,
$n=0.01$, $g=0.02$:
\[
k^{*}=7.1278,\quad \tilde c^{*}=1.3543,\quad \xi_{1}=-0.1019,\quad \xi_{2}=+0.1119,
\quad\text{slope}=0.1119,\quad\text{half-life}=6.80 .
\]
The program checks the closed-form slope against a numerically traced stable arm ($0.1123$).

\rmk{There is one parameter configuration in which ($*$) can be solved exactly rather than only near the
BGP: when $\theta=\alpha$, the term $(\alpha-\theta)x$ in \bk{(8.56)} vanishes and the $z$ equation
decouples,
\[
\frac{\dt z}{z}=z-\frac{\rho}{\alpha}+n-\frac{(1-\alpha)\delta}{\alpha} ,
\]
a logistic equation with an explicit solution, after which $x(t)$ follows by quadrature.  This is the
closed-form case identified by Smith (2006); with logarithmic preferences ($\theta=1$) the coupling is
present and the linearized solution above is the appropriate closed form.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.26}{}
The problem is
\[
\max\ \sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\beta^{t}\log c(t)
\qquad\text{s.t.}\qquad
k(t+1)=k(t)^{\alpha}-c(t),\qquad k(0)>0 ,
\]
full depreciation making the resource constraint this simple.

\emph{Euler equation and steady state.}  The Euler equation \bk{(6.50)} is
\[
\frac{1}{c(t)}=\beta\,\alpha k(t+1)^{\alpha-1}\frac{1}{c(t+1)} .
\]
In a steady state $c(t)=c^{*}$ and $k(t)=k^{*}$, so $1=\beta\alpha(k^{*})^{\alpha-1}$ and
\[
k^{*}=(\alpha\beta)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}},
\qquad
c^{*}=(k^{*})^{\alpha}-k^{*}=(\alpha\beta)^{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}}(1-\alpha\beta) .
\]
This is the discrete-time counterpart of \bk{(8.35)}: the marginal product of capital,
$\alpha(k^{*})^{\alpha-1}$, equals the gross discount rate $1/\beta$.

\emph{The exact policy rule.}  Guess that a constant fraction of output is saved,
$k(t+1)=s\,k(t)^{\alpha}$, so that $c(t)=(1-s)k(t)^{\alpha}$.  Substituting into the Euler equation,
\[
\frac{1}{(1-s)k(t)^{\alpha}}
=\beta\alpha\bigl(sk(t)^{\alpha}\bigr)^{\alpha-1}\frac{1}{(1-s)\bigl(sk(t)^{\alpha}\bigr)^{\alpha}}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
s=\alpha\beta ,
\]
after cancelling.  So the exact optimal policy is
\[
\boxed{\ c(t)=(1-\alpha\beta)k(t)^{\alpha},
\qquad
k(t+1)=\alpha\beta\,k(t)^{\alpha}\ }
\]
--- the saving rate is constant at $\alpha\beta$, exactly as in the Solow model but here derived rather
than assumed.  (That the guess is the solution follows from uniqueness: the policy function of the
concave dynamic programming problem is unique by \bk{Corollary 6.1}, and this candidate satisfies the
Euler equation and the transversality condition.)

\emph{The transition, exactly.}  Taking logarithms of the law of motion,
\[
\log k(t+1)=\log(\alpha\beta)+\alpha\log k(t)
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\log k(t)-\log k^{*}=\alpha^{t}\bigl[\log k(0)-\log k^{*}\bigr] ,
\]
since $\log k^{*}=\log(\alpha\beta)/(1-\alpha)$ is the fixed point.  The economy converges monotonically to
$k^{*}$, and the log gap closes at the exact geometric rate $\alpha$ per period --- a rare case in which the
entire transition, not merely its linearization, is available in closed form.  With $\alpha=1/3$,
$\beta=0.96$ and $k(0)=0.2k^{*}$ the iterated and closed-form paths agree to every printed digit
($k^{*}=0.181019$, $k(1)=0.105861$, $k(5)=0.179824$).

\rmk{The knife-edge assumption is full depreciation: with $\delta<1$ the resource constraint becomes
$k(t+1)=k(t)^{\alpha}+(1-\delta)k(t)-c(t)$, the constant-saving-rate guess fails, and no closed form is
known.  It is worth noting that here the neoclassical model reproduces the Solow model exactly, with the
saving rate pinned down by preferences as $\alpha\beta$ rather than left free.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.28}{}
\emph{(a) Faster technological progress.}  The steady state \bk{(8.53)} is $f'(k^{*})=\rho+\delta+\theta g$,
so a rise from $g$ to $g'>g$ raises the required marginal product and \emph{lowers} the steady-state
effective capital-labor ratio $k^{*}$; it also lowers $\tilde c^{*}=f(k^{*})-(n+g+\delta)k^{*}$, both
because $k^{*}$ falls (and the economy is to the left of the golden rule, where the curve is increasing)
and because the replacement requirement $n+g+\delta$ rises.  The growth rate of consumption per capita
rises from $g$ to $g'$.

On impact, $k$ is predetermined and $\tilde c$ jumps onto the new stable arm.  Since the economy starts at
the old $k^{*}$, which exceeds the new $k^{*}$, it is to the \emph{right} of the new steady state, and the
arm is upward sloping there: normalized consumption therefore jumps above its new steady-state level, and
$k$ falls monotonically toward the new $k^{*}$.

Whether \emph{actual} consumption $c(0)=A(0)\tilde c(0)$ rises or falls is genuinely ambiguous, and the
ambiguity is the familiar one between income and substitution effects:
\begin{itemize}
\item faster growth makes the household richer in present-value terms, which pushes consumption up now
      (income effect);
\item it also raises the equilibrium interest rate to $r^{*}=\rho+\theta g'$, making future consumption
      cheaper, which pushes consumption down now (substitution effect).
\end{itemize}
With CRRA preferences the two are balanced by $\theta$: the substitution effect dominates when
$\theta<1$ (intertemporal elasticity $1/\theta>1$), so consumption falls on impact and saving rises;
the income effect dominates when $\theta>1$, so consumption rises; and for $\theta=1$ (log) the two
exactly cancel in the sense that the consumption-wealth ratio is unchanged.

\emph{(b) An announced future tax cut.}  Assume, as in Section 8.8, that the tax proceeds are rebated
lump-sum, so that there is no wealth effect and only the intertemporal price changes.  With a capital tax
the steady state \bk{(8.57)} is
\[
f'(k^{*})=\frac{\delta+\rho+\theta g}{1-\tau} ,
\]
so a lower $\tau'$ lowers the required marginal product and \emph{raises} $k^{*}$ and $\tilde c^{*}$.

Between $T$ and $T'$ the old dynamics still apply --- the tax is still $\tau$ --- but the path must arrive
exactly on the \emph{new} stable arm at $T'$, since from then on the new dynamics govern and only the new
arm converges.  The new arm passes through a steady state with higher $k^{*}$, so at $T'$ the economy must
have accumulated capital.  This requires $\dt k>0$ between $T$ and $T'$, that is, consumption below the
$\dt k=0$ locus.  Hence at the moment of the announcement consumption \emph{jumps down}: households start
saving immediately in anticipation of the higher after-tax return, capital rises between $T$ and $T'$, and
after $T'$ the economy travels up the new stable arm to the new steady state, with consumption rising
throughout and ending above its original level.

The contrast between the two parts is instructive: in (a) the change is immediate and the sign of the
impact effect depends on $\theta$; in (b) the change is anticipated, so the whole adjustment before $T'$ is
driven by the substitution effect alone, and its sign is unambiguous.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.29}{}
With technological progress, the transversality condition \bk{(8.52)} evaluated along the BGP reduces, as
the book shows, to the requirement that
\[
\rho-n-(1-\theta)g>0 ,
\]
which is Assumption 4.  If $\theta>1$ then $(1-\theta)g<0$, so the requirement becomes
\[
\rho-n>(1-\theta)g,\qquad\text{with a negative right-hand side} ,
\]
which is implied by Assumption 4$'$, $\rho>n$ --- the condition needed even in the model without growth.
So for $\theta>1$ Assumption 4 imposes nothing beyond ordinary discounting, and the transversality
condition holds automatically.

The reason is visible in the utility function.  For $\theta>1$ the CRRA felicity
$\bigl(c^{1-\theta}-1\bigr)/(1-\theta)$ is negative and \emph{bounded above} by
$1/(\theta-1)$: as consumption grows without bound, flow utility rises toward a finite ceiling.  Growth
therefore cannot make the discounted integral diverge, and the only requirement is that the discount factor
itself be summable, $\rho>n$.  For $\theta<1$ the felicity is unbounded above and grows like
$c^{1-\theta}=c(0)^{1-\theta}e^{(1-\theta)gt}$, so growth races against discounting and the extra condition
$\rho-n>(1-\theta)g$ is needed; see Exercise 8.20.

\rmk{Empirically this is reassuring rather than restrictive: most estimates of the intertemporal elasticity
of substitution $1/\theta$ lie below one, that is $\theta>1$, so the canonical model's parameter
restrictions are satisfied in the range of interest.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.32}{}
Each country $j$ is a closed neoclassical economy with the same technology and the same $\delta$, $n$, $g$
and $\theta$, differing only in its discount rate $\rho_j$.  Its BGP effective capital-labor ratio solves
\[
f'\bigl(k_j^{*}\bigr)=\rho_j+\delta+\theta g ,
\]
so with the Cobb--Douglas form $f(k)=k^{\alpha}$,
\[
k_j^{*}=\Bigl[\frac{\alpha}{\rho_j+\delta+\theta g}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}},
\qquad
y_j^{*}=\bigl(k_j^{*}\bigr)^{\alpha}
=\Bigl[\frac{\alpha}{\rho_j+\delta+\theta g}\Bigr]^{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}} .
\]
All countries grow at the same rate $g$; only the \emph{level} of income per capita differs, and
\[
\frac{y_j^{*}}{y_{j'}^{*}}
=\Bigl[\frac{\rho_{j'}+\delta+\theta g}{\rho_j+\delta+\theta g}\Bigr]^{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}} ,
\qquad
\frac{\dd\log y^{*}}{\dd\log\bigl(\rho+\delta+\theta g\bigr)}=-\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha} .
\]
With a capital share of about $\alpha=1/3$ the elasticity is only $-1/2$, and it applies to
$\rho+\delta+\theta g$, not to $\rho$ alone --- which is what makes the effect small.

\emph{The numbers.}  Take $\delta=0.05$, $g=0.02$, $\theta=1$, and compare $\rho=0.02$ with
$\rho'=0.022$ --- a 10\% difference in patience:
\[
\frac{y^{*}(\rho')}{y^{*}(\rho)}=\Bigl(\frac{0.090}{0.092}\Bigr)^{1/2}=0.9891 ,
\]
a difference of only $1.1\%$.  Even with no depreciation and no growth ($\delta=g=0$), where the discount
rate is the whole of the required return,
\[
\frac{y^{*}(\rho')}{y^{*}(\rho)}=\Bigl(\frac{0.020}{0.022}\Bigr)^{1/2}=0.9535 ,
\]
a difference of $4.7\%$.

\emph{Interpretation.}  Preference heterogeneity of a plausible size cannot account for cross-country
income differences: a tenfold income gap would require
$(\rho_{j'}+\delta+\theta g)/(\rho_j+\delta+\theta g)=10^{2}=100$, that is discount rates differing by
orders of magnitude.  The reason is the same diminishing-returns arithmetic that limits the Solow model's
ability to explain income differences through saving rates: with a capital share of one third, the
elasticity of steady-state income with respect to any determinant of the capital stock is only
$\alpha/(1-\alpha)=1/2$.  This is precisely why the book turns from proximate to fundamental causes, and
later to models in which technology itself differs across countries.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.35}{}
\emph{The two budget constraints.}  The government finances a constant flow of spending $g$ per capita with
lump-sum taxes $T(t)$ and debt $b(t)$:
\[
\dt b(t)=r(t)b(t)+g-T(t),
\qquad
\lim_{t\to\infty}\Bigl[b(t)\exp\Bigl(-\int_0^{t}\bigl(r(s)-n\bigr)\dd s\Bigr)\Bigr]=0 .
\]
Integrating forward and using the no-Ponzi condition gives the government's intertemporal budget
constraint,
\[
\int_0^{\infty}T(t)q(t)\dd t=b(0)+\int_0^{\infty}g\,q(t)\dd t ,
\qquad
q(t)\equiv\exp\Bigl(-\int_0^{t}\bigl(r(s)-n\bigr)\dd s\Bigr).
\tag{$*$}
\]
The household holds assets $a(t)=k(t)+b(t)$ and faces
\[
\dt a(t)=\bigl(r(t)-n\bigr)a(t)+w(t)-T(t)-c(t) ,
\]
so, integrating forward with its own no-Ponzi condition,
\[
\int_0^{\infty}c(t)q(t)\dd t=a(0)+\int_0^{\infty}\bigl[w(t)-T(t)\bigr]q(t)\dd t .
\tag{$**$}
\]

\emph{The proof.}  Substitute ($*$) into ($**$) and use $a(0)=k(0)+b(0)$:
\[
\int_0^{\infty}c(t)q(t)\dd t
=k(0)+b(0)+\int_0^{\infty}w(t)q(t)\dd t-\Bigl[b(0)+\int_0^{\infty}g\,q(t)\dd t\Bigr]
=k(0)+\int_0^{\infty}\bigl[w(t)-g\bigr]q(t)\dd t .
\]
The path of taxes has disappeared, and so has the initial debt: the household's lifetime budget constraint
depends only on the initial \emph{capital} stock and on the present value of wages net of government
spending.  The household's problem --- maximize \bk{(8.3)} subject to this constraint --- is therefore
unchanged by any reshuffling of $[T(t)]$ that satisfies ($*$), so its consumption path is unchanged; and
since $k(t)=a(t)-b(t)$ and the resource constraint $\dt k=f(k)-c-g-(n+\delta)k$ does not involve taxes
either, the equilibrium path of the capital-labor ratio is unchanged as well.  This is Ricardian
equivalence.

\emph{Interpretation.}  Government debt is not net wealth.  A tax cut today financed by borrowing must be
paid for by higher taxes later whose present value is exactly equal; the infinitely lived household sees
this, saves the entire tax cut, and leaves its consumption plan untouched.  Only the \emph{present value}
of government spending matters, not how it is financed.

The result is a benchmark, not a description, and it is worth being explicit about what it assumes: taxes
are lump-sum (distortionary taxes change relative prices, as in Exercise 8.39); the horizon is infinite
with full altruism (in the overlapping generations models of Chapter 9 the future taxpayers are different
people, and the equivalence fails); capital markets are perfect (binding borrowing constraints make the
timing of taxes matter, as in Exercise 6.13); and there is no uncertainty about the incidence of future
taxes.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.36}{}
\emph{Capital jumps immediately.}  Firms are competitive and can rent as much capital as they like at the
world rate, so the domestic marginal product must equal the world cost of capital at every instant:
\[
f'\bigl(k(t)\bigr)-\delta=r^{*}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
k(t)=k^{\text{o}}\equiv\bigl(f'\bigr)^{-1}\bigl(r^{*}+\delta\bigr)\quad\text{for all }t>0 .
\]
The capital-labor ratio is pinned down by the world interest rate alone, independently of preferences and
of the country's own wealth, and it is reached \emph{immediately}: if $k(0)<k^{\text{o}}$ the economy
imports the difference on the first instant, borrowing $k^{\text{o}}-k(0)$ from abroad.  There are no
transitional dynamics in capital or in output --- the entire adjustment happens at $t=0$ --- which is the
sharpest contrast with the closed economy, where convergence takes decades.

\emph{Consumption.}  The Euler equation is $\dt c/c=(r^{*}-\rho)/\theta$, so consumption grows, is constant
or falls according to whether $r^{*}\gtrless\rho$.  A steady state with constant consumption therefore
requires the knife-edge condition
\[
r^{*}=\rho ,
\]
which is the standard assumption in small open economy models; otherwise the country accumulates foreign
assets without bound ($r^{*}>\rho$) or runs its consumption down to zero ($r^{*}<\rho$), and no steady
state exists.  Assume $r^{*}=\rho$.  Then $c(t)=c^{*}$ is constant and is determined by the country's
intertemporal budget constraint: with net foreign debt $d(t)=k(t)-a(t)$ and the no-Ponzi condition,
\[
c^{*}=\bigl(r^{*}-n\bigr)\Bigl[a(0)+\int_0^{\infty}\bigl[w(t)\bigr]e^{-(r^{*}-n)t}\dd t\Bigr] ,
\]
that is, consumption equals the annuity value of national wealth --- initial assets plus the present value
of labor income.

\emph{How the debt is repaid.}  It is not repaid in the sense of being extinguished; it is \emph{serviced}
forever out of the higher output the imported capital makes possible.  In the steady state,
\[
c^{*}=f\bigl(k^{\text{o}}\bigr)-(n+\delta)k^{\text{o}}-\bigl(r^{*}-n\bigr)d ,
\]
so the country runs a permanent trade surplus of $(r^{*}-n)d$ --- the interest bill net of the growth of
the population over which it is spread --- while its debt-to-labor ratio $d$ stays constant.  The no-Ponzi
condition requires exactly this: the present value of the debt must go to zero, which permits debt growing
at rate $n$ but not faster.

\rmk{The comparison with the closed economy is the point of the exercise.  Openness removes the
\emph{capital accumulation} channel entirely: income per worker is $f(k^{\text{o}})$ from the first instant,
so cross-country differences in patience or initial capital show up in \emph{consumption} and in net
foreign asset positions, not in output.  This is why the empirical failure of convergence is so hard to
reconcile with open capital markets --- the Lucas paradox of Chapter 1.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{8.39}{}
\emph{The Solow model.}  Saving is a fixed fraction $s$ of income, whatever the rate of return.  A tax on
capital income at the rate $\tau$, rebated lump-sum to households, changes the \emph{distribution} of income
but not its total: output is still $f(k)$, and total saving is still $sf(k)$.  The fundamental law of motion
is therefore unchanged,
\[
\dt k(t)=sf\bigl(k(t)\bigr)-(n+\delta)k(t),
\]
and so is the steady state $f(k^{*})/k^{*}=(n+\delta)/s$.  The tax is neutral.  (If instead the revenue were
thrown away or spent on something outside the model, the effect would come through the resource constraint,
not through behavior; the point is that no \emph{behavioral} margin responds.)

\emph{The neoclassical model.}  Here saving is chosen, and what the household compares is its discount rate
with the \emph{after-tax} return
\[
r(t)=(1-\tau)f'\bigl(k(t)\bigr)-\delta .
\]
The Euler equation \bk{(8.50)} then gives, in a BGP with consumption growing at $g$,
$r^{*}=\rho+\theta g$, so that
\[
f'\bigl(k^{*}\bigr)=\frac{\delta+\rho+\theta g}{1-\tau} ,
\]
which is \bk{(8.57)}.  Since $f'$ is strictly decreasing, a higher $\tau$ raises the required pre-tax
marginal product and therefore \emph{lowers} $k^{*}$, output per effective worker, and consumption.  With
$\alpha=1/3$ the elasticity is again $\alpha/(1-\alpha)=1/2$, so a tax that raises the required return by
10\% lowers steady-state income by about 5\%.

\emph{Why the difference.}  In the Solow model the saving rate is a behavioral primitive that does not
respond to prices, so a tax that changes the return to capital changes nothing that anyone decides.  In the
neoclassical model saving is the outcome of an intertemporal optimization, and the after-tax return
