The planner internalizes $A=K$ and so faces the return $\wt f(L)$ rather than $R$, giving
$(\beta\wt f(L))^{1/\theta}>1+g$ --- when her problem is well posed, which for $\theta<1$ it need
not be (at $\theta=0.5$ and $\beta=0.8$, $\beta\wt f(L)^{1-\theta}=1.6>1$ and her objective is
infinite).

\emph{(c) No transitional dynamics.}  The Euler equation holds at \emph{every} date, and its right
side is a constant, so consumption grows at $1+g$ from $t=0$ whatever $K(0)$ is.  Substituting
$C(t)=C(0)(1+g)^{t}$ into the resource constraint gives the linear difference equation
\[
K(t+1)=\wt f(L)K(t)-C(0)(1+g)^{t},
\]
whose general solution is
\[
K(t)=\kappa\,\wt f(L)^{t}+\frac{C(0)}{\wt f(L)-(1+g)}(1+g)^{t} .
\]
Now use the transversality condition.  Since
$\beta^{t}C(t)^{-\theta}=C(0)^{-\theta}\bigl[\beta(1+g)^{-\theta}\bigr]^{t}=C(0)^{-\theta}R^{-t}$,
the condition $\lim\beta^{t}C(t)^{-\theta}K(t+1)=0$ becomes
\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}R^{-t}K(t+1)=0 .
\]
The homogeneous part contributes $\wt f(L)\bigl[\wt f(L)/R\bigr]^{t}\to\infty$, because
$\wt f(L)>R$; so $\kappa=0$.  The particular part contributes
$(1+g)\bigl[(1+g)/R\bigr]^{t}\to0$ precisely when $1+g<R$, which is the condition
$\beta<R^{\theta-1}$ already imposed.  Hence
\[
K(t)=K(0)(1+g)^{t},
\qquad
C(0)=\bigl[\wt f(L)-(1+g)\bigr]K(0) ,
\]
and the economy is on the balanced growth path from the first period. $\blacksquare$

\rmk{The exercise's part (c) refers to ``the BGP characterized in part c'', evidently a slip for
part (b).  The substantive point is the discrete-time counterpart of Exercise 11.1: a constant
private return makes the Euler equation autonomous, so consumption growth is immediately constant,
and the transversality condition then eliminates the explosive homogeneous solution for capital.
The linearity that produces endogenous growth is also what produces the absence of transitional
dynamics; the two are the same property seen from different sides.}
\end{aex}


%% ========================================================================
\solchapter{12}{Modeling Technological Change}

\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{0.86\textwidth}
\small
The eight exercises of Chapter 12 left unsolved in the instructor's manual: Exercises 12.1, 12.3,
12.4, 12.6--12.8, 12.10 and 12.12.  The manual solves 12.2, 12.5, 12.9, 12.11, 12.13 and 12.14.
Notation follows the book: $\psi$ is the pre-innovation marginal cost, $\lambda>1$ the size of a
process innovation, $\mu$ its cost, $D(p)$ demand, $\varepsilon_D(p)=-pD'(p)/D(p)$ the elasticity
of demand, $p^{M}$ the post-innovation monopoly price, $\wh p^{M}$ the pre-innovation one, and
$\varepsilon$ the elasticity of substitution between Dixit--Stiglitz varieties.  It is convenient
throughout to write
\[
\Pi^{M}(c)\equiv\max_{p}\ D(p)\bigl(p-c\bigr)
\]
for the gross monopoly profit at marginal cost $c$, with maximizer $p^{M}(c)$.  The numerical parts
are computed by \texttt{code/ch12.py}.
\end{minipage}
\end{center}

\bigskip
\hrule
\bigskip

\begin{aex}{12.1}{}
After a successful innovation the firm's marginal cost is $\lambda^{-1}\psi$, and as an
unconstrained monopolist it solves
\[
\max_{p}\ \pi(p)=D(p)\bigl(p-\lambda^{-1}\psi\bigr).
\]
The first-order condition is
\[
D'(p)\bigl(p-\lambda^{-1}\psi\bigr)+D(p)=0
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
p-\lambda^{-1}\psi=-\frac{D(p)}{D'(p)} .
\]
Writing the elasticity of demand as $\varepsilon_D(p)\equiv-pD'(p)/D(p)>0$, we have
$-D(p)/D'(p)=p/\varepsilon_D(p)$, so
\[
p\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{\varepsilon_D(p)}\Bigr)=\lambda^{-1}\psi
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\boxed{\ p^{M}=\frac{\lambda^{-1}\psi}{1-\varepsilon_D(p^{M})^{-1}}\ }
\]
which is \bk{(12.2)}. $\blacksquare$

Three remarks complete the derivation.  First, a positive price requires
$\varepsilon_D(p^{M})>1$: a monopolist never operates on the inelastic part of its demand curve,
because raising the price there would raise revenue and cut cost simultaneously.  Second, the
first-order condition is sufficient when marginal revenue $p(1-\varepsilon_D(p)^{-1})$ is
increasing in $p$, which holds for all the demand curves used below.  Third, the same computation
with $\lambda=1$ gives the pre-innovation monopoly price $\wh p^{M}=p^{M}(\psi)$ of \bk{(12.6)}.

\emph{The drastic threshold.}  The innovation is drastic when firm~1 can charge $p^{M}$ without
being undercut by rivals still producing at cost $\psi$, that is when $p^{M}\le\psi$.  Substituting
\bk{(12.2)},
\[
\frac{\lambda^{-1}\psi}{1-\varepsilon_D(p^{M})^{-1}}\le\psi
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\lambda\ \ge\ \lambda^{*}\equiv\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon_D(p^{M})^{-1}} ,
\]
as the text states.  The threshold is exactly the monopoly markup factor: the innovation must cut
cost by at least as much as the monopolist would like to mark up.

\rmk{With the constant-elasticity demand $D(p)=Ap^{-\varepsilon}$ the formula collapses to
$p^{M}=\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon-1}\lambda^{-1}\psi$, a fixed proportional markup, and
$\lambda^{*}=\varepsilon/(\varepsilon-1)$.  This is the same expression as the Dixit--Stiglitz
price \bk{(12.15)} of Section 12.4, and for the same reason: \bk{(12.10)} makes each variety's
demand isoelastic with elasticity $\varepsilon$.  The two halves of the chapter share one
formula.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{12.3}{}
\emph{Deriving \bk{(12.5)}.}  Before the innovation the industry is competitive at price $\psi$, so
producer surplus is zero.  Measure everything as a change from that benchmark.

Under monopoly pricing (the drastic case, $p^{M}\le\psi$), firm~1 sells $D(p^{M})$ at the markup
$p^{M}-\lambda^{-1}\psi$, so producer surplus rises by $D(p^{M})(p^{M}-\lambda^{-1}\psi)$; the
price falls from $\psi$ to $p^{M}$, so consumer surplus rises by $\int_{p^{M}}^{\psi}D(p)\dd p$;
and the innovation costs $\mu$.  Hence
\[
\wt S^{I}_{1}=D(p^{M})\bigl(p^{M}-\lambda^{-1}\psi\bigr)+\int_{p^{M}}^{\psi}D(p)\dd p-\mu .
\]
Under limit pricing (the non-drastic case), the price stays at $\psi$, so consumers gain nothing
and the whole gain is the cost saving on the units still sold:
\[
S^{I}_{1}=D(\psi)\bigl(\psi-\lambda^{-1}\psi\bigr)-\mu=D(\psi)\lambda^{-1}(\lambda-1)\psi-\mu ,
\]
which is \bk{(12.5)}.  Note that $S^{I}_{1}=\pi^{I}_{1}$ exactly: with limit pricing the private
and social values of the innovation coincide, because the innovator captures the entire gain.

\emph{Proving \bk{Proposition 12.2}.}  It is cleanest to treat the two regimes as one function of
the size of the innovation.  For each $\lambda>1$ let
\[
p_1(\lambda)\equiv\min\bigl\{p^{M}(\lambda^{-1}\psi),\ \psi\bigr\}
\]
be the equilibrium price, and define the gross (pre-$\mu$) private value and equilibrium surplus
\[
\Pi(\lambda)=D\bigl(p_1\bigr)\bigl(p_1-\lambda^{-1}\psi\bigr),
\qquad
\Sigma(\lambda)=\Pi(\lambda)+\int_{p_1}^{\psi}D(p)\dd p,
\qquad
S(\lambda)=\int_{\lambda^{-1}\psi}^{\psi}D(p)\dd p ,
\]
so that $\Pi=\pi^{I}_{1}+\mu$ and $\Sigma=S^{I}_{1}+\mu$ for $\lambda<\lambda^{*}$, while
$\Pi=\wh\pi^{I}_{1}+\mu$ and $\Sigma=\wt S^{I}_{1}+\mu$ for $\lambda\ge\lambda^{*}$, and
$S=S^{I}+\mu$ throughout.

\emph{Step 1: $\Pi(\lambda)<\Sigma(\lambda)<S(\lambda)$ at every $\lambda$.}  The first inequality
is immediate, since $\Sigma-\Pi=\int_{p_1}^{\psi}D\ge0$, with equality only under limit pricing.
For the second, split the integral at $p_1$:
\[
S(\lambda)-\Sigma(\lambda)
=\int_{\lambda^{-1}\psi}^{p_1}D(p)\dd p-D(p_1)\bigl(p_1-\lambda^{-1}\psi\bigr)\ >\ 0 ,
\]
because $D$ is strictly decreasing, so $D(p)>D(p_1)$ for every $p<p_1$ and the integral strictly
exceeds the rectangle.  This is the deadweight loss of pricing above marginal cost.  Combining,
$\Pi<\Sigma<S$, which gives both $\wh\pi^{I}_{1}<S^{I}$ and $\wt S^{I}_{1}<S^{I}$, and
(for $\lambda<\lambda^{*}$, where $\Pi=\Sigma$) $\pi^{I}_{1}=S^{I}_{1}<S^{I}$.

\emph{Step 2: both are strictly increasing in $\lambda$.}  For $\lambda<\lambda^{*}$,
$\Pi(\lambda)=\Sigma(\lambda)=D(\psi)\psi(1-\lambda^{-1})$, plainly increasing.  For
$\lambda\ge\lambda^{*}$, $\Pi(\lambda)=\Pi^{M}(\lambda^{-1}\psi)$ and the envelope theorem gives
\[
\frac{\dd\Pi}{\dd\lambda}=\frac{D(p^{M})\psi}{\lambda^{2}}>0 ,
\qquad
\frac{\dd\Sigma}{\dd\lambda}=\frac{D(p^{M})\psi}{\lambda^{2}}
-D(p^{M})\frac{\dd p^{M}}{\dd\lambda}>0 ,
\]
the second term positive because a larger $\lambda$ lowers marginal cost and hence $p^{M}$.  Both
functions are continuous at $\lambda^{*}$ (there $p^{M}=\psi$), so they are strictly increasing
throughout.  Since $\wh\pi^{I}_{1}$ and $\wt S^{I}_{1}$ refer to values of $\lambda$ above
$\lambda^{*}$ while $\pi^{I}_{1}$ and $S^{I}_{1}$ refer to values below it,
\[
\pi^{I}_{1}<\wh\pi^{I}_{1}
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
S^{I}_{1}<\wt S^{I}_{1} ,
\]
which completes \bk{Proposition 12.2}. $\blacksquare$

With $D(p)=p^{-3}$ and $\psi=1$, so that $\lambda^{*}=1.5$, the program gives
\[
\begin{array}{cccccc}
\lambda & 1.1 & 1.3 & 1.5 & 1.8 & 2.5\\
\text{private} & 0.0909 & 0.2308 & 0.3333 & 0.4800 & 0.9259\\
\text{equilibrium surplus} & 0.0909 & 0.2308 & 0.3333 & 0.7000 & 1.8148\\
\text{full social value} & 0.1050 & 0.3450 & 0.6250 & 1.1200 & 2.6250
\end{array}
\]
--- the three columns coinciding in the limit-pricing region and fanning out once monopoly
pricing takes over (Figure~\ref{fig:ch12}).

\emph{Reading the proposition.}  The first chain is the \emph{appropriability effect}: even with a
perfectly enforced patent, the innovator captures only part of the surplus its innovation creates,
because it cannot price-discriminate and so leaves consumer surplus on the table.  The second is
the familiar deadweight loss: conditional on innovating, monopoly pricing does not realize the
whole gain.  Patents therefore buy innovation at the price of static inefficiency, and they still
buy too little of it.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{12.4}{}
Subtracting the two definitions,
\[
\wt S^{I}_{1}-\wh\pi^{I}_{1}
=\Bigl[D(p^{M})\bigl(p^{M}-\lambda^{-1}\psi\bigr)+\int_{p^{M}}^{\psi}D(p)\dd p-\mu\Bigr]
-\Bigl[D(p^{M})\bigl(p^{M}-\lambda^{-1}\psi\bigr)-\mu\Bigr]
=\int_{p^{M}}^{\psi}D(p)\dd p .
\]
In the regime where $\wh\pi^{I}_{1}$ is the relevant profit the innovation is drastic, so
$p^{M}\le\psi$ and the integral is over an interval of nonnegative length with a nonnegative
integrand.  Hence
\[
\wt S^{I}_{1}\ \ge\ \wh\pi^{I}_{1}\ >\ 0 . \qquad\blacksquare
\]
The non-drastic branch is even simpler: there $S^{I}_{1}=\pi^{I}_{1}$ identically, so the
implication holds with equality.  With $\lambda=1.8$ and $D(p)=p^{-3}$ the program reports a gap
of $0.2200$, the consumer surplus gain.

\emph{Why this matters.}  The difference between the social and private values is exactly the
consumer surplus created, and in this environment that difference is a \emph{gain}, never a loss.
So in the competitive industry of Section 12.3.3 there can be no \emph{excessive} innovation: any
innovation a patent-holding firm is willing to pay for is one society should want.  All the
inefficiency runs one way, toward too little innovation.  Exercise 12.8 asks whether this survives
when the innovator displaces an incumbent monopolist rather than a competitive fringe, and the
answer turns out to be more delicate than the text suggests.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{12.6}{}
The comparison is between two firms facing the same innovation opportunity but different market
positions.  A firm in the competitive industry of Section 12.3.3 earns nothing before innovating;
the monopolist of Section 12.3.4 already earns $\Pi^{M}(\psi)>0$ and has no rivals, so after
innovating it prices freely at $p^{M}(\lambda^{-1}\psi)$ in either regime.  Their gross values of
the innovation are
\[
\text{competitive firm:}\quad
V_{C}(\lambda)=
\begin{cases}
D(\psi)\bigl(\psi-\lambda^{-1}\psi\bigr) & \lambda<\lambda^{*},\\[2pt]
\Pi^{M}(\lambda^{-1}\psi) & \lambda\ge\lambda^{*},
\end{cases}
\]
\[
\text{monopolist:}\quad
V_{I}(\lambda)=\Pi^{M}(\lambda^{-1}\psi)-\Pi^{M}(\psi).
\]

\emph{The drastic branch.}  Here
\[
V_{C}-V_{I}=\Pi^{M}(\psi)>0 ,
\]
so $\Delta\wh\pi^{I}_{1}=V_I-\mu<V_C-\mu=\wh\pi^{I}_{1}$.  The gap is \emph{exactly} the profit the
monopolist destroys by innovating --- Arrow's replacement effect in its purest form.

\emph{The limit-pricing branch.}  Now $V_{C}=D(\psi)(\psi-\lambda^{-1}\psi)$, which is
\emph{smaller} than $\Pi^{M}(\lambda^{-1}\psi)$, so the comparison is no longer immediate.  Use the
envelope theorem on $\Pi^{M}$: since $\partial[D(p)(p-c)]/\partial c=-D(p)$,
\[
\frac{\dd\Pi^{M}(c)}{\dd c}=-D\bigl(p^{M}(c)\bigr)
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
V_{I}=\Pi^{M}(\lambda^{-1}\psi)-\Pi^{M}(\psi)=\int_{\lambda^{-1}\psi}^{\psi}D\bigl(p^{M}(c)\bigr)\dd c .
\]
Meanwhile
\[
V_{C}=D(\psi)\bigl(\psi-\lambda^{-1}\psi\bigr)=\int_{\lambda^{-1}\psi}^{\psi}D(\psi)\dd c .
\]
Compare the integrands.  In the non-drastic case $p^{M}(\lambda^{-1}\psi)>\psi$, and $p^{M}(\cdot)$
is increasing, so $p^{M}(c)\ge p^{M}(\lambda^{-1}\psi)>\psi$ for every
$c\in[\lambda^{-1}\psi,\psi]$; since $D$ is strictly decreasing,
\[
D\bigl(p^{M}(c)\bigr)<D(\psi)\qquad\text{for all such }c ,
\]
and therefore $V_{I}<V_{C}$, that is
\[
\boxed{\ \Delta\wh\pi^{I}_{1}<\pi^{I}_{1}\ } \qquad\blacksquare
\]
Combining the two branches with \bk{Proposition 12.2}'s $\pi^{I}_{1}<\wh\pi^{I}_{1}$ gives the
full chain of \bk{Proposition 12.3}.  With $D(p)=p^{-3}$, $\psi=1$ and
$\Pi^{M}(\psi)=0.1481$, the program gives
\[
\begin{array}{ccccc}
\lambda & 1.1 & 1.3 & 1.8 & 4.0\\
\text{competitive} & 0.0909 & 0.2308 & 0.4800 & 2.3704\\
\text{incumbent} & 0.0311 & 0.1022 & 0.3319 & 2.2222\\
\text{gap} & 0.0598 & 0.1285 & 0.1481 & 0.1481
\end{array}
\]
the gap settling at exactly $\Pi^{M}(\psi)$ once the innovation becomes drastic.

\emph{Two readings of the same integral.}  The envelope formula says that the value of a cost
reduction is the quantity sold, integrated over the cost range --- the innovator saves $D$ per unit
of cost reduction.  The competitive firm computes that quantity at the \emph{competitive} price
$\psi$; the monopolist computes it at its own, higher, monopoly price, where it sells less.  The
monopolist therefore values the same cost reduction less, both because it sells fewer units and
because part of its gain merely replaces profits it was already making.  Hence
\bk{Corollary 12.1}: entrants innovate more than incumbents, which is the germ of the
Schumpeterian growth models of Chapter 14.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{12.7}{}
\emph{The threshold.}  Write $\Pi_{E}\equiv V_{C}(\lambda)$ for the entrant's gross value --- it
earns nothing today and becomes the seller if it innovates --- and
$V_{I}=\Pi^{M}(\lambda^{-1}\psi)-\Pi^{M}(\psi)$ for the incumbent's, as in Exercise 12.6.  Net
values are $\Pi_{E}-\chi\mu$ and $V_{I}-\mu$, so the entrant has the stronger incentive when
\[
\Pi_{E}-\chi\mu>V_{I}-\mu
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
(\chi-1)\mu<\Pi_{E}-V_{I} ,
\]
that is
\[
\boxed{\ \chi<\bar\chi\equiv1+\frac{\Pi_{E}-V_{I}}{\mu}\ }
\]
Exercise 12.6 established $\Pi_{E}-V_{I}>0$ in both pricing regimes, so $\bar\chi>1$ always: a
\emph{slightly} handicapped entrant still out-invests the incumbent, and the size of the handicap
it can absorb is the replacement effect measured in units of the research cost.  In the drastic
case $\Pi_{E}-V_{I}=\Pi^{M}(\psi)$ exactly, so $\bar\chi=1+\Pi^{M}(\psi)/\mu$.

\emph{The elasticity of demand.}  Take $D(p)=p^{-\varepsilon}$ with $\psi=1$, for which
\[
p^{M}(c)=\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon-1}c,
\qquad
\Pi^{M}(c)=\frac{c^{1-\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon-1}\Bigl(\frac{\varepsilon-1}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{\varepsilon}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\frac{\Pi^{M}(\lambda^{-1})}{\Pi^{M}(1)}=\lambda^{\varepsilon-1} .
\]
In the drastic region the ratio of the two gross incentives is therefore
\[
\frac{\Pi_{E}}{V_{I}}=\frac{\Pi^{M}(\lambda^{-1})}{\Pi^{M}(\lambda^{-1})-\Pi^{M}(1)}
=\frac{\lambda^{\varepsilon-1}}{\lambda^{\varepsilon-1}-1} ,
\]
which is strictly \emph{decreasing} in $\varepsilon$, and so is
$\bar\chi-1=\Pi^{M}(1)/\mu$, since $\Pi^{M}(1)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon-1}(\frac{\varepsilon-1}{\varepsilon})^{\varepsilon}$
falls with $\varepsilon$.  With $\lambda=1.8$ and $\mu=0.05$ the program gives
\[
\begin{array}{cccccc}
\varepsilon & 1.5 & 2 & 3 & 5 & 10\\
\bar\chi & 7.26 & 5.89 & 3.96 & 2.64 & 1.77\\
\Pi_{E}/V_{I} & 3.38 & 2.22 & 1.45 & 1.11 & 1.005
\end{array}
\]

\emph{Intuition.}  The incumbent's handicap is the rent it destroys, $\Pi^{M}(\psi)$, and that rent
is a markup times a quantity.  More elastic demand means a smaller markup: the incumbent has less
to lose, so the replacement effect weakens and the entrant's relative advantage shrinks toward
nothing as $\varepsilon\to\infty$ (where the incumbent is effectively a competitive firm and the
two coincide).  Read in reverse: the replacement effect is strongest precisely where market power
is strongest, so it is in the most concentrated, least elastic industries that one should expect
innovation to come from outside.

\rmk{This gives some content to a familiar empirical puzzle.  Incumbents do a great deal of R\&D,
which the replacement effect alone would not predict.  The model points to the answer: incumbents
innovate when their cost advantage in research is large ($\chi$ well above $\bar\chi$), and
$\bar\chi$ is small exactly where demand is elastic and markups thin.  Both incumbent-led
incremental innovation and entrant-led radical innovation are consistent with the same mechanism at
different parameter values.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{12.8}{}
\emph{(a) First, the environment of Section 12.3.4 will not produce it.}  Suppose an entrant makes
a process innovation and displaces a monopolist whose marginal cost is $\psi$.  Measure surplus
against the \emph{pre-innovation} outcome, in which the incumbent charges $\wh p^{M}=p^{M}(\psi)$.
After entry the price is $p_1=\min\{p^{M}(\lambda^{-1}\psi),\psi\}\le\psi<\wh p^{M}$, so
\[
\Delta S=\underbrace{\Pi^{M}(\lambda^{-1}\psi)-\Pi^{M}(\psi)}_{\text{producer surplus}}
+\underbrace{\int_{p_1}^{\wh p^{M}}D(p)\dd p}_{\text{consumer surplus}}-\mu ,
\qquad
\text{entrant's value}=\Pi_E-\mu ,
\]
and in the drastic case $\Pi_E=\Pi^{M}(\lambda^{-1}\psi)$, so
\[
\Delta S-\bigl(\Pi_E-\mu\bigr)=\int_{p_1}^{\wh p^{M}}D(p)\dd p-\Pi^{M}(\psi) .
\]
But $\Pi^{M}(\psi)=D(\wh p^{M})(\wh p^{M}-\psi)$, and since $D$ is decreasing and
$p_1\le\psi$,
\[
\int_{p_1}^{\wh p^{M}}D(p)\dd p\ \ge\ \int_{\psi}^{\wh p^{M}}D(p)\dd p
\ >\ D(\wh p^{M})\bigl(\wh p^{M}-\psi\bigr)=\Pi^{M}(\psi) ,
\]
so $\Delta S>\Pi_E-\mu$: the social value \emph{exceeds} the private value, and excessive
innovation is impossible.  The same computation with the limit-pricing $\Pi_E$ gives the same
conclusion.  The program confirms it at every $\lambda$ for both isoelastic and linear demand.

The reason is structural.  A process innovation under Bertrand competition must \emph{undercut}
the incumbent to be worth anything, so it necessarily drags the price from $\wh p^{M}$ down to at
most $\psi$, and the consumer surplus that releases always outweighs the rent it destroys.

\emph{An environment in which it does occur.}  What the business-stealing intuition requires is an
innovation that captures the incumbent's rents \emph{without} competing them into consumer
surplus.  Suppose the innovation is a new product that replaces the incumbent's rather than
undercutting it: the entrant's good has the same marginal cost $\psi$ but is $\lambda>1$ times
more appealing, in the sense that its demand curve is $\lambda D(p)$.  The entrant becomes the
monopolist and, since scaling demand does not change the optimal price, charges the same
$\wh p^{M}$.  Then
\[
\text{social gain}=(\lambda-1)\bigl[\Pi^{M}(\psi)+CS(\wh p^{M})\bigr]-\mu ,
\qquad
\text{private gain}=\lambda\,\Pi^{M}(\psi)-\mu ,
\]
where $CS(p)=\int_{p}^{\infty}D(q)\dd q$.  The private gain exceeds the social gain when
\[
\lambda\Pi^{M}(\psi)>(\lambda-1)\bigl[\Pi^{M}(\psi)+CS(\wh p^{M})\bigr]
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
(\lambda-1)\,CS(\wh p^{M})<\Pi^{M}(\psi),
\]
that is, whenever
\[
\boxed{\ \lambda<1+\frac{\Pi^{M}(\wh p^{M})}{CS(\wh p^{M})}\ }
\]
For $D(p)=p^{-\varepsilon}$ the ratio $\Pi^{M}/CS$ equals $\psi/\wh p^{M}=(\varepsilon-1)/\varepsilon$,
so the condition is $\lambda<2-1/\varepsilon$.  Taking $\varepsilon=3$, $\psi=1$ and $\lambda=1.2$,
the program gives $\Pi^{M}(\psi)=0.14815$ and $CS(\wh p^{M})=0.22222$, hence
\[
\text{social gain}=0.074074-\mu,
\qquad
\text{private gain}=0.177778-\mu .
\]
For any $\mu\in(0.0741,0.1778)$ --- at $\mu=0.12$, a private value of $+0.0578$ against a social
value of $-0.0459$ --- the entrant innovates and total surplus \emph{falls}.  This proves
\bk{Proposition 12.4}. $\blacksquare$

\rmk{The text asserts \bk{Proposition 12.4} with $\wt S^{I}_{1}$ as defined in \bk{(12.5)}, but
that quantity measures surplus against a \emph{competitive} benchmark priced at $\psi$, and
Exercise 12.4 shows it can never fall short of $\wh\pi^{I}_{1}$.  Measured correctly against the
incumbent monopolist's outcome, the first calculation above shows that a process innovation cannot
be excessive either.  The proposition is nonetheless true, and the intuition the text gives for it
is exactly right --- ``the social planner values the profits made by the monopolist, the entrant
only values the profits it will make'' --- but it needs an environment in which the entrant takes
those profits without passing them to consumers.  The quality-ladder models of Chapter 14, where
each innovator limit-prices at its predecessor's price, are of precisely that kind.}

\emph{(b) What makes it more likely.}  The condition $(\lambda-1)CS<\Pi^{M}$ names the forces
directly.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Small innovations.}  The social gain is proportional to $\lambda-1$ while the business
      stolen is proportional to $\lambda$; as $\lambda\downarrow1$ the genuine improvement vanishes
      but the rents transferred do not.  Excessive innovation is a phenomenon of incremental,
      rent-seeking improvements, not of radical ones.
\item \emph{Large incumbent rents relative to consumer surplus.}  The threshold is
      $1+\Pi^{M}/CS$, so anything that raises profit relative to consumer surplus at the monopoly
      price widens the region.  With isoelastic demand $\Pi^{M}/CS=(\varepsilon-1)/\varepsilon$,
      so, perhaps surprisingly, \emph{more} elastic demand makes excessive innovation more likely:
      a flat demand curve leaves little consumer surplus above the monopoly price relative to the
      rectangle of profit.
\item \emph{Innovations that displace rather than undercut.}  This is the decisive structural
      condition, and it is what fails in part (a).  New varieties, quality improvements sold at the
      old price, and business-model innovations all transfer rents without a price war; pure cost
      reductions under Bertrand competition do not.
\item \emph{Duplication of research effort.}  Not modeled here, but the most important force in
      practice: if $n$ firms each spend $\mu$ racing for the same prize, society pays $n\mu$ for
      one innovation while each firm compares its own $\mu$ with its chance of winning the whole
      rent.  Chapter 14 returns to this.
\end{itemize}
Against all of these stands the appropriability effect of \bk{Proposition 12.2}, which pushes the
other way.  The honest conclusion --- the one the text draws --- is that the sign of the wedge
between private and social innovation incentives is not determined by theory, and must be settled
empirically, innovation by innovation.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics{figures/ch12_innovation_value.pdf}
\caption{Left (Exercises 12.3, 12.4 and 12.6): private and social values of a process innovation
with isoelastic demand.  Below $\lambda^{*}$ limit pricing makes the competitive firm's private
value equal to the equilibrium surplus; above it the curves fan out, and the incumbent
monopolist's value falls short of the competitive firm's by exactly $\Pi^{M}(\psi)$.  Right
(Exercise 12.12): Dixit--Stiglitz profits against the number of varieties, for a differentiated
sector with a small expenditure share.  Profits fall with $N$ when $\sigma=1$ and rise once
$\sigma$ clears the threshold.}
\label{fig:ch12}
\end{figure}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{12.10}{}
The consumption index is
\[
C=\Bigl[\sum_{i=1}^{N}c_i^{\frac{\varepsilon-1}{\varepsilon}}\Bigr]^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon-1}} ,
\]
and \bk{(12.10)} states $\bigl(c_i/C\bigr)^{-1/\varepsilon}=p_i/P$, that is
\[
c_i=C\Bigl(\frac{p_i}{P}\Bigr)^{-\varepsilon} .
\tag{$*$}
\]
(This is the definition of $P$: the first-order condition between varieties,
$(c_i/c_{i'})^{-1/\varepsilon}=p_i/p_{i'}$, says that $p_i(c_i/C)^{1/\varepsilon}$ is the same for
every $i$, and $P$ is that common value.  It uses
$\partial C/\partial c_i=(C/c_i)^{1/\varepsilon}$, which follows from differentiating the index.)

Substituting $(*)$ into the definition of $C$,
\[
C=\Bigl[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Bigl(C\bigl(p_i/P\bigr)^{-\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{\frac{\varepsilon-1}{\varepsilon}}
\Bigr]^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon-1}}
=C\Bigl[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Bigl(\frac{p_i}{P}\Bigr)^{1-\varepsilon}\Bigr]^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon-1}} ,
\]
so the bracket must equal one:
\[
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Bigl(\frac{p_i}{P}\Bigr)^{1-\varepsilon}=1
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
P^{\varepsilon-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_i^{1-\varepsilon}=1
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\boxed{\ P=\Bigl[\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_i^{1-\varepsilon}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}}\ }
\]
which is \bk{(12.11)}. $\blacksquare$

\emph{$P$ deserves its name.}  Using $(*)$ again, total spending on the differentiated varieties is
\[
\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_ic_i=C\,P^{\varepsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_i^{1-\varepsilon}
=C\,P^{\varepsilon}P^{-(\varepsilon-1)}=PC ,
\]
so the budget constraint \bk{(12.9)} collapses to $PC+y\le m$, which is \bk{(12.12)}: $P$ is
exactly the price at which the composite $C$ may be treated as a single good.  This is what makes
the two-stage budgeting of Section 12.4 legitimate, and it is why every model in the rest of the
book can write demand for a variety as $(p_i/P)^{-\varepsilon}C$ and be done with it.

\emph{Love of variety in one line.}  If all prices are equal at $p$, then
$P=N^{1/(1-\varepsilon)}p=pN^{-1/(\varepsilon-1)}$, which is strictly decreasing in $N$ since
$\varepsilon>1$.  Adding varieties lowers the true cost of a unit of the composite even though no
individual price has moved --- the price-index counterpart of
$U(\bar C/N,\dots,\bar C/N,y)=u(N^{1/(\varepsilon-1)}\bar C,y)$.  The whole of the aggregate demand
externality of Exercise 12.12, and much of the growth-through-variety literature of Chapter 13,
rests on this single exponent.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{12.12}{}
\emph{Setting up.}  With the symmetric price \bk{(12.15)} the ideal price index is
$P=N^{-1/(\varepsilon-1)}\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon-1}\psi$ by \bk{(12.16)}, and the text's
expression for profits is
\[
\pi=\frac{1}{\varepsilon N}\Bigl[m-g(P,m)\Bigr]=\frac{P\,C}{\varepsilon N} ,
\]
using $PC=m-g(P,m)$ from \bk{(12.13)}.  Two forces act when $N$ rises: the term $1/N$ divides a
given amount of spending over more firms, while $P$ falls, which raises spending on the composite
if households substitute toward it.

\emph{The condition.}  Differentiate, using $\partial P/\partial N=-P/[(\varepsilon-1)N]$:
\[
\frac{\partial\pi}{\partial N}
=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Bigl[\frac{-g_P(P,m)\,\partial P/\partial N}{N}-\frac{m-g(P,m)}{N^{2}}\Bigr]
=\frac{1}{\varepsilon N^{2}}\Bigl[\frac{P\,g_P(P,m)}{\varepsilon-1}-PC\Bigr] ,
\]
so
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{\partial\pi}{\partial N}>0
\iff g_P(P,m)>(\varepsilon-1)\,C\ }
\]
Spending on the outside good must respond strongly enough to the price index of the differentiated
bundle.  Equivalently, writing $E\equiv PC$ for expenditure on the composite and
$\eta_E\equiv\dd\log E/\dd\log P=-g_P/C$,
\[
\frac{\dd\log\pi}{\dd\log N}=-1-\frac{\eta_E}{\varepsilon-1}>0
\iff \eta_E<-(\varepsilon-1)
\iff \Bigl|\frac{\dd\log C}{\dd\log P}\Bigr|>\varepsilon .
\]
\emph{The demand for the composite must be more elastic than the substitution among the varieties
within it.}

\emph{In terms of $u$.}  Let $u$ be CES with elasticity of substitution $\sigma$ between $C$ and
$y$, and let $s_C=PC/m$ be the expenditure share of the differentiated sector.  Then
$\eta_E=-(\sigma-1)(1-s_C)$, and the condition becomes
\[
\sigma>1+\frac{\varepsilon-1}{1-s_C} .
\]
Three cases are worth naming.  If $\sigma=1$ (Cobb--Douglas $u$) expenditure on the composite is
fixed, $\eta_E=0$, and $\dd\log\pi/\dd\log N=-1$: profits always fall, whatever $\varepsilon$ or
$s_C$.  If $\sigma<1$ they fall faster still.  Profits rise only when $C$ and $y$ are strong
substitutes, and the requirement tightens as the differentiated sector grows --- a sector that
already absorbs all of spending has nowhere to draw further demand from.  With $\varepsilon=3$,
$N=10$ and $m=1$ the program gives
\[
\begin{array}{ccccc}
\sigma & 1 & 2 & 4 & 8\\
\dd\log\pi/\dd\log N\quad(s_C\ \text{small}) & -1.00 & -0.50 & +0.50 & +2.50\\
\dd\log\pi/\dd\log N\quad(s_C\approx0.5\text{--}1) & -1.00 & -0.84 & -0.86 & -0.98
\end{array}
\]

\emph{The aggregate demand externality.}  When the condition holds, each firm's entry makes the
\emph{other} firms more profitable, through a purely pecuniary channel: more varieties lower the
ideal price index, which draws spending into the sector.  In a competitive, complete-market economy
such pecuniary externalities would net out and the First Welfare Theorem would apply; here they do
not, because prices already exceed marginal cost.  That is what makes the effect a genuine
externality with first-order welfare consequences, and it is the mechanism behind the multiple
equilibria and poverty traps of Chapter 21 --- and, in a different guise, behind the scale effects
of Chapter 13.
\end{aex}


%% ========================================================================
\solchapter{13}{Expanding Variety Models}

\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{0.86\textwidth}
\small
The eighteen exercises of Chapter 13 left unsolved in the instructor's manual: Exercises 13.2--13.4,
13.8--13.12, 13.14, 13.16--13.18, 13.20, 13.21, 13.23 and 13.25--13.27.  The manual solves 13.1,
13.5--13.7, 13.13, 13.15, 13.19, 13.22 and 13.24.  Notation follows the book: $\beta$ is the
machine-share parameter, $\psi=1-\beta$ the normalized marginal cost of a machine, $\eta$ the
productivity of R\&D, $N(t)$ the measure of varieties, $V(\nu,t)$ the value of a blueprint,
$L_E$ and $L_R$ employment in production and research, and $\varepsilon$ the elasticity of
substitution among products in Section 13.4.  It is convenient to write
\[
\chi\equiv(1-\beta)^{-1/\beta}>1
\]
for the factor by which the social planner's machine intensity exceeds the market's.  The
numerical parts are computed by \texttt{code/ch13.py}.
\end{minipage}
\end{center}

\bigskip
\hrule
\bigskip

\begin{aex}{13.2}{}
\emph{Demand.}  The first-order condition of \bk{(13.5)} with respect to $x(\nu,t)$ is
\[
\frac{1}{1-\beta}(1-\beta)x(\nu,t)^{-\beta}L^{\beta}=p_x(\nu,t)
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
x(\nu,t)=p_x(\nu,t)^{-1/\beta}L ,
\]
which is \bk{(13.6)}: demand is isoelastic with elasticity $\varepsilon_\beta=1/\beta>1$.

\emph{Pricing.}  Because machines depreciate after use and are rented on the spot, the
monopolist's problem separates across dates: at each $t$ it solves the static problem
\[
\max_{p_x}\ \bigl(p_x-\psi\bigr)p_x^{-1/\beta}L .
\]
Differentiating,
\[
p_x^{-1/\beta}-\frac{1}{\beta}\bigl(p_x-\psi\bigr)p_x^{-1/\beta-1}=0
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\beta p_x=p_x-\psi
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
p_x=\frac{\psi}{1-\beta} ,
\]
a constant markup $1/(1-\beta)=\varepsilon_\beta/(\varepsilon_\beta-1)$ over marginal cost --- the
same formula as \bk{(12.2)} and \bk{(12.15)}, for the same reason.  The second-order condition
holds because $\varepsilon_\beta>1$.  The solution is independent of $t$, of $N(t)$ and of the
interest rate, which is what makes the model tractable.

Normalizing $\psi\equiv1-\beta$ gives
\[
\boxed{\ p_x(\nu,t)=p_x=1,\qquad x(\nu,t)=L,\qquad \pi(\nu,t)=(1-\psi)L=\beta L\ }
\]
which are \bk{(13.9)}, \bk{(13.10)} and \bk{(13.11)}. $\blacksquare$

Substituting into \bk{(13.2)} gives the derived aggregate production function
$Y(t)=N(t)L/(1-\beta)$, equation \bk{(13.12)}, and the first-order condition in $L$ gives
$w(t)=\beta N(t)/(1-\beta)$, equation \bk{(13.13)}.  The whole of the model's tractability rests
on the fact that each monopolist's decision is a static problem whose answer never changes.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{13.3}{}
The household owns the corporate sector, so its assets are
\[
A(t)=\int_{0}^{N(t)}V(\nu,t)\dd\nu ,
\]
and it faces the flow budget constraint $\dt A(t)=r(t)A(t)+w(t)L-C(t)$, taking
$[r(t),w(t)]$ as given.  The current-value Hamiltonian is
\[
\wh H\bigl(A,C,\mu\bigr)=\frac{C(t)^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}+\mu(t)\bigl[r(t)A(t)+w(t)L-C(t)\bigr] .
\]
\bk{Theorem 7.13} gives
\[
\wh H_C=C(t)^{-\theta}-\mu(t)=0,
\qquad
\wh H_A=\mu(t)r(t)=\rho\mu(t)-\dt\mu(t),
\qquad
\lim_{t\to\infty}\Bigl[e^{-\rho t}\mu(t)A(t)\Bigr]=0 .
\]
The second gives $\dt\mu/\mu=\rho-r(t)$, while differentiating the first gives
$\dt\mu/\mu=-\theta\dt C/C$.  Hence
\[
\frac{\dt C(t)}{C(t)}=\frac{1}{\theta}\bigl(r(t)-\rho\bigr),
\]
which is \bk{(13.16)}.  For the transversality condition, integrate the costate equation:
$\mu(t)=\mu(0)\exp\bigl(\rho t-\int_0^{t}r(s)\dd s\bigr)$, so
\[
e^{-\rho t}\mu(t)A(t)=\mu(0)\exp\Bigl(-\int_{0}^{t}r(s)\dd s\Bigr)\int_{0}^{N(t)}V(\nu,t)\dd\nu ,
\]
and since $\mu(0)>0$ the condition is exactly \bk{(13.17)}. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Sufficiency.}  $\wh H$ is the sum of a strictly concave function of $C$ and a function
linear in $(A,C)$, so it is concave in $(A,C)$ and strictly concave in $C$; and
$\lim e^{-\rho t}\mu A\ge0$ holds along any feasible path because $\mu>0$ and the household's
no-Ponzi condition bounds $A$ below.  \bk{Theorem 7.14} therefore applies, so the Euler equation
together with \bk{(13.17)} characterizes the unique optimal consumption plan.

The \emph{market value} form of \bk{(13.17)} is worth a comment.  In this economy the only store
of value is the stock of blueprints, so the household's wealth \emph{is} the market
capitalization of the monopolists.  The transversality condition then says that this
capitalization must not grow faster than the interest rate --- which, given free entry
($\eta V=1$) and hence $A(t)=N(t)/\eta$, becomes simply $g^{*}<r^{*}$.  Exercise 13.4 shows that
this is exactly the second half of condition \bk{(13.21)}.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{13.4}{}
\emph{Finite utility.}  Along the BGP $C(t)=C(0)e^{g^{*}t}$ with $g^{*}=\theta^{-1}(\eta\beta L-\rho)$,
so
\[
U=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\rho t}\frac{C(0)^{1-\theta}e^{(1-\theta)g^{*}t}-1}{1-\theta}\dd t
\]
converges if and only if $\rho>(1-\theta)g^{*}$.  Substituting $g^{*}$,
\[
\theta\rho>(1-\theta)\bigl(\eta\beta L-\rho\bigr)
\iff \theta\rho+(1-\theta)\rho>(1-\theta)\eta\beta L
\iff \rho>(1-\theta)\eta\beta L ,
\]
which is the second inequality in \bk{(13.21)}.

\emph{Transversality.}  Free entry holds with equality, $\eta V(t)=1$, so total assets are
$A(t)=N(t)V^{*}=N(t)/\eta$, growing at $g^{*}$; and $r(t)=r^{*}=\eta\beta L$.  Condition
\bk{(13.17)} therefore reads
\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{N(0)}{\eta}e^{(g^{*}-r^{*})t}=0
\iff g^{*}<r^{*}=\eta\beta L
\iff \eta\beta L-\rho<\theta\eta\beta L
\iff (1-\theta)\eta\beta L<\rho ,
\]
the same inequality.  Finite utility and transversality are one condition, as always.

\emph{The resource constraint.}  From \bk{(13.12)} and \bk{(13.15)}, net output is
\[
\wt Y(t)=Y(t)-X(t)=\Bigl[\frac{1}{1-\beta}-(1-\beta)\Bigr]N(t)L
=\frac{\beta(2-\beta)}{1-\beta}N(t)L ,
\]
while R\&D absorbs $Z(t)=\dt N(t)/\eta=g^{*}N(t)/\eta$.  Hence
\[
C(t)=\wt Y(t)-Z(t)=N(t)\Bigl[\frac{\beta(2-\beta)L}{1-\beta}-\frac{g^{*}}{\eta}\Bigr] ,
\]
which grows at $g^{*}$, as required, and is strictly positive because
\[
\frac{g^{*}}{\eta}<\frac{r^{*}}{\eta}=\beta L<\frac{\beta(2-\beta)L}{1-\beta} ,
\]
the first inequality being the transversality condition just established and the last because
$(2-\beta)/(1-\beta)>1$. $\blacksquare$

With $\beta=1/2$, $\eta=0.12$, $L=1$, $\rho=0.02$ and $\theta=2$ the program gives
$g^{*}=0.02$ and $C(0)/N(0)=1.333$.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{13.8}{}
\emph{The static step.}  For given $N(t)$ the planner maximizes net output, the right side of
\bk{(13.22)}, over $x(\nu,t)$.  The first-order condition is
$x^{-\beta}L^{\beta}=\psi=1-\beta$, so
\[
x^{S}(\nu,t)=(1-\beta)^{-1/\beta}L=\chi L\ >\ L ,
\]
the planner using each machine more intensively than the market precisely because it does not
mark up.  Net output is then
\[
\wt Y^{S}(t)=\frac{\chi^{1-\beta}}{1-\beta}N L-(1-\beta)\chi NL
=\chi\beta\,N(t)L ,
\]
using $\chi^{1-\beta}=(1-\beta)\chi$.

\emph{(a) Sufficiency.}  The reduced problem is
\[
\max\ \int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\rho t}\frac{C(t)^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}\dd t
\quad\text{s.t.}\quad
\dt N(t)=\eta\bigl[\chi\beta N(t)L-C(t)\bigr],
\]
with current-value Hamiltonian
\[
\wh H(N,C,\mu)=\frac{C^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}+\mu\bigl[\eta\chi\beta NL-\eta C\bigr] .
\]
This is strictly concave in $C$ and linear in $N$, hence jointly concave in $(N,C)$; and for every
feasible path $\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-\rho t}\mu(t)N(t)\ge0$, because $\mu>0$ and $N\ge0$.
Provided the objective is finite --- which is the condition in \bk{Proposition 13.3},
$(1-\theta)\eta\chi\beta L<\rho$, verified in Exercise 13.9 --- \bk{Theorem 7.14} applies and the
necessary conditions are sufficient, with strict concavity in $C$ giving uniqueness.

\emph{(b) The growth rate.}  The conditions are
\[
C(t)^{-\theta}=\eta\mu(t),
\qquad
\mu(t)\eta\chi\beta L=\rho\mu(t)-\dt\mu(t),
\qquad
\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-\rho t}\mu(t)N(t)=0 .
\]
The second gives $\dt\mu/\mu=\rho-\eta\chi\beta L$, and differentiating the first gives
$\dt\mu/\mu=-\theta\dt C/C$.  Hence
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{\dt C^{S}(t)}{C^{S}(t)}
=\frac{1}{\theta}\Bigl(\eta(1-\beta)^{-1/\beta}\beta L-\rho\Bigr)\ }
\]
which is \bk{(13.23)}. $\blacksquare$

Comparing with \bk{(13.20)}, the whole difference is the factor $\chi=(1-\beta)^{-1/\beta}>1$
multiplying $\beta L$.  The social value of a blueprint exceeds its private value not because of
any technological spillover --- there is none in the lab-equipment model --- but because the
planner, pricing at marginal cost, extracts more output from each machine variety.  The
inefficiency is entirely the pecuniary one created by the markup.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{13.9}{}
\emph{The two conditions.}  By Exercise 13.4 the equilibrium requires
$(1-\theta)\eta\beta L<\rho$; by the same computation applied to \bk{(13.23)}, the optimum
requires
\[
(1-\theta)\eta\chi\beta L<\rho ,
\qquad \chi=(1-\beta)^{-1/\beta}>1 .
\]
For $\theta<1$ the left side of the second is strictly larger, so the interval
\[
(1-\theta)\eta\beta L\ <\ \rho\ \le\ (1-\theta)\eta\chi\beta L
\]
is nonempty, and for $\rho$ in it the equilibrium is well defined with finite utility while the
planner's objective diverges.  With $\beta=1/2$, $\eta=0.12$, $L=1$ and $\theta=1/2$ the program
gives the interval $(0.03,0.12]$; at $\rho=0.05$ the market allocation has finite utility and the
planner's problem has none.

\emph{Interpretation.}  The planner's problem is an $AK$ problem in $N$ with return
$\eta\chi\beta L$.  When $\theta<1$ and that return is large relative to $\rho$, utility along
\emph{every} feasible growth path is unbounded: the planner can always do better by postponing
consumption a little longer, and no maximum exists.  The equilibrium escapes this only because
the monopoly markup lowers the return on R\&D from $\eta\chi\beta L$ to $\eta\beta L$ --- the
distortion is what keeps the market problem well posed.

\emph{Does this make the optimum less compelling?}  No.  The pathology is the familiar one of
infinite-horizon problems whose growth rate is too high relative to discounting --- the same as
Assumption 4 failing in Chapter 8, or condition \bk{(11.12)} failing in the $AK$ model.  It says
that the model has been taken outside the parameter region in which its normative question has an
answer, not that the question is the wrong one.  Nor does it rescue the equilibrium: even where
the planner's problem is unbounded, the equilibrium is Pareto dominated by allocations with
faster growth; there is simply no best such allocation.

\emph{Under \bk{Proposition 13.3}'s condition.}  Suppose $(1-\theta)\eta\chi\beta L<\rho$.  Then
$\rho>(1-\theta)g^{S}$ by the rearrangement of Exercise 13.4, so utility is finite; and
$\mu(t)=\mu(0)\exp\bigl(\rho t-\eta\chi\beta Lt\bigr)$ gives
\[
e^{-\rho t}\mu(t)N(t)=\mu(0)N(0)e^{(g^{S}-\eta\chi\beta L)t}\ \longrightarrow\ 0
\iff g^{S}<\eta\chi\beta L ,
\]
which is the same inequality again.  Both hold. $\blacksquare$
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{13.10}{}
By Exercise 13.8 the planner's Euler equation is
\[
\frac{\dt C(t)}{C(t)}=\frac{1}{\theta}\bigl(\eta\chi\beta L-\rho\bigr)=g^{S}
\]
at \emph{every} date, not merely asymptotically, because the right side contains no state
variable: the planner faces a constant return $\eta\chi\beta L$ on the only asset there is.
Consumption therefore grows at $g^{S}$ from $t=0$ whatever $N(0)$ is.

Substituting $C(t)=C(0)e^{g^{S}t}$ into the accumulation equation gives the linear
nonautonomous equation
\[
\dt N(t)=\eta\chi\beta L\,N(t)-\eta C(0)e^{g^{S}t},
\]
whose general solution, exactly as in Exercise 11.1, is
\[
N(t)=\kappa\,e^{\eta\chi\beta Lt}+\frac{\eta C(0)}{\eta\chi\beta L-g^{S}}e^{g^{S}t} .
\]
The transversality condition $\lim e^{-\rho t}\mu(t)N(t)=0$ becomes, using
$e^{-\rho t}\mu(t)\propto e^{-\eta\chi\beta Lt}$,
\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}N(t)e^{-\eta\chi\beta Lt}=\kappa=0 ,
\]
so $\kappa=0$ and
\[
N(t)=N(0)e^{g^{S}t},
\qquad
C(0)=\frac{\bigl(\eta\chi\beta L-g^{S}\bigr)N(0)}{\eta}\ >\ 0 ,
\]
positive because $g^{S}<\eta\chi\beta L$ is the transversality condition of Exercise 13.9.  The
optimal path therefore grows at the constant rate $g^{S}$ from the first instant, with no
transitional dynamics. $\blacksquare$

Finally, $g^{S}>g^{*}$ because $\chi>1$, which completes \bk{Proposition 13.3}.  With the
program's parameters, $g^{*}=0.020$ against $g^{S}=0.110$: the markup costs this economy nine
percentage points of growth, which is a measure of how strong the pecuniary externality is when
the elasticity of substitution among machines is only $1/\beta=2$.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{13.11}{}
\emph{(a) Research subsidies.}  Let the government pay a fraction $s_R$ of R\&D spending,
financed by lump-sum taxes.  Free entry becomes $\eta V=1-s_R$, so $V^{*}=(1-s_R)/\eta$ and,
from $V^{*}=\beta L/r^{*}$,
\[
r^{*}=\frac{\eta\beta L}{1-s_R},
\qquad
g(s_R)=\frac{1}{\theta}\Bigl(\frac{\eta\beta L}{1-s_R}-\rho\Bigr) .
\]
Setting $g(s_R)=g^{S}$ requires $\eta\beta L/(1-s_R)=\eta\chi\beta L$, that is
\[
\boxed{\ s_R=1-(1-\beta)^{1/\beta}\ }
\]
so \emph{yes}, the optimal growth rate is attainable.  With $\beta=1/2$ this is $s_R=0.75$.

It does \emph{not} replicate the Pareto optimal path.  The subsidy leaves the markup untouched,
so machine use is still $x=L$ rather than $x^{S}=\chi L$, output is still $NL/(1-\beta)$ rather
than $\chi NL$, and net output per variety is $\beta(2-\beta)L/(1-\beta)$ rather than
$\chi\beta L$.  The growth rates coincide but the levels do not; the static distortion remains.

\emph{Is it desirable?}  No --- it overshoots.  Given the markup, welfare at $t=0$ is
\[
W(g)=\frac{C(0)^{1-\theta}}{(1-\theta)\bigl[\rho-(1-\theta)g\bigr]}-\frac{1}{(1-\theta)\rho},
\qquad
C(0)=N(0)\Bigl[\underbrace{\frac{\beta(2-\beta)L}{1-\beta}}_{\textstyle\equiv A}-\frac{g}{\eta}\Bigr],
\]
by Exercise 13.4.  The first-order condition in $g$ is
\[
\frac{1}{\eta\bigl(A-g/\eta\bigr)}=\frac{1}{\rho-(1-\theta)g}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
g^{SB}=\frac{\eta A-\rho}{\theta}=\frac{1}{\theta}\Bigl(\frac{\eta\beta(2-\beta)L}{1-\beta}-\rho\Bigr),
\]
delivered by the subsidy
\[
\frac{\eta\beta L}{1-s_R}=\frac{\eta\beta(2-\beta)L}{1-\beta}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
s_R^{SB}=\frac{1}{2-\beta} .
\]
Since $1<\frac{2-\beta}{1-\beta}<\chi$, we have
\[
g^{*}<g^{SB}<g^{S} ,
\]
so the second-best subsidy raises growth but stops well short of the first-best rate.  With the
program's parameters, $g^{*}=0.020$, $g^{SB}=0.080$, $g^{S}=0.110$, and
$s_R^{SB}=0.667<0.750$:
\[
\begin{array}{cccccc}
s_R & 0 & 0.25 & 0.50 & 0.667 & 0.75\\
g & 0.020 & 0.030 & 0.050 & 0.080 & 0.110\\
\text{welfare} & 31.25 & 34.00 & 36.81 & \mathbf{38.00} & 36.81
\end{array}
\]
Forcing the first-best \emph{growth rate} while leaving the first-best \emph{level} unattainable
is a classic second-best error: it buys growth by sacrificing more consumption today than the
distorted economy is worth.

\emph{(b) Machine subsidies.}  Let final good producers pay only $(1-s_x)p_x$ per machine, the
difference financed lump-sum.  The monopolist still faces elasticity $1/\beta$ and still charges
$p_x=\psi/(1-\beta)$, but demand is now
\[
x=\bigl[(1-s_x)p_x\bigr]^{-1/\beta}L=(1-s_x)^{-1/\beta}L
\]
using $\psi=1-\beta$.  Setting $x=x^{S}=\chi L=(1-\beta)^{-1/\beta}L$ requires
\[
\boxed{\ s_x=\beta\ }
\]
Profits then become $\pi=(p_x-\psi)x=\frac{\beta\psi}{1-\beta}x=\beta\chi L$, and free entry
$\eta V=1$ with $V=\pi/r$ gives
\[
r^{*}=\eta\beta\chi L
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
g=\frac{1}{\theta}\bigl(\eta\chi\beta L-\rho\bigr)=g^{S} .
\]
So the machine subsidy achieves the optimal growth rate \emph{and} the optimal static allocation:
it replicates the Pareto optimal path exactly.

\emph{Why one instrument suffices.}  There appear to be two distortions --- too few machines per
variety, and too little research --- but they are the same distortion.  At the efficient machine
quantity, where $x^{-\beta}L^{\beta}=\psi$, net output per variety is
\[
\frac{\wt Y}{N}=\frac{1}{1-\beta}x^{1-\beta}L^{\beta}-\psi x=\frac{\beta\psi x}{1-\beta}=\beta x ,
\]
while the monopolist's flow profit is $(p_x-\psi)x=\frac{\beta\psi}{1-\beta}x=\beta x$ as well.
The two coincide: once machines are priced at marginal cost \emph{to the user}, the innovator's
private return equals the social return, and free entry delivers the optimal amount of research
by itself.  This is a feature of the Dixit--Stiglitz structure, in which the markup
$1/(1-\beta)$ and the output elasticity $\beta$ are governed by the same parameter.

\emph{(c) The combination.}  Since (b) attains the first best on its own, adding research
subsidies cannot improve on it, and any positive $s_R$ on top of $s_x=\beta$ would push research
past the optimum.  The combination is no better --- which is the right conclusion from a
Tinbergen count once one sees that there is only one independent distortion.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics{figures/ch13_expanding_variety.pdf}
\caption{Left (Exercise 13.14): welfare against the competition-policy parameter $\gamma$.  Growth
is maximized at $\gamma=1/(1-\beta)$, the weakest policy, but welfare peaks strictly inside, and
the optimum moves toward stricter policy as the household becomes more impatient.  Right
(Exercise 13.11): welfare against the research subsidy.  The welfare-maximizing subsidy
$s_R=1/(2-\beta)$ lies strictly below the one that matches the Pareto optimal growth rate.}
\label{fig:ch13}
\end{figure}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{13.12}{}
\emph{(a)}  Taxing profits changes nothing on the production side: machines are still priced at
$p_x=1$, quantities are still $x=L$, output is still $Y=NL/(1-\beta)$ and wages
$w=\beta N/(1-\beta)$.  What changes is the value of a blueprint, since the monopolist now keeps
only $(1-\tau)\beta L$:
\[
V^{*}=\frac{(1-\tau)\beta L}{r^{*}},
\qquad
\eta V^{*}=1
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
r^{*}=\eta(1-\tau)\beta L ,
\]
and hence, by \bk{(13.16)},
\[
\boxed{\ g(\tau)=\frac{1}{\theta}\Bigl(\eta(1-\tau)\beta L-\rho\Bigr)\ }
\]
As in the lab-equipment model without taxes, the argument of Section 13.1.4 applies unchanged:
free entry pins $V$ at $1/\eta$ at every date, so $\dt V=0$, the interest rate is constant, and
there are no transitional dynamics.  The equilibrium requires
$\eta(1-\tau)\beta L>\rho>(1-\theta)\eta(1-\tau)\beta L$.

\emph{(b)}  Two economies with the same $N(0)$ and the same technology but taxes $\tau<\tau'$
both grow from the first instant, so
\[
\frac{Y(\tau,t)}{Y(\tau',t)}=\frac{N(\tau,t)}{N(\tau',t)}
=\exp\Bigl(\bigl[g(\tau)-g(\tau')\bigr]t\Bigr)
=\exp\Bigl(\frac{\eta\beta L\,(\tau'-\tau)}{\theta}\,t\Bigr) ,
\]
which diverges to infinity.  With $\beta=1/2$, $\eta=0.12$, $L=1$, $\rho=0.02$, $\theta=2$:
\[
\begin{array}{ccccc}
\tau & 0 & 0.10 & 0.20 & 0.30\\
g(\tau) & 0.0200 & 0.0170 & 0.0140 & 0.0110\\
\text{income relative to }\tau=0\text{ after 100 years} & 1.00 & 0.74 & 0.55 & 0.41
\end{array}
\]

\emph{What this means.}  The model has exactly the property of the $AK$ models of Chapter 11
(Exercise 11.7): policy has \emph{growth} effects, not level effects, so arbitrarily small
permanent policy differences generate arbitrarily large income differences in the long run.  The
mechanism is more satisfying here, because the linearity comes from the innovation possibilities
frontier $\dt N=\eta Z$ rather than being assumed of the production function, and the policy acts
on a recognizable margin --- the after-tax value of a patent.  But the empirical objection is the
same: the postwar world income distribution has been broadly stable, whereas tax and institutional
differences have been large and persistent.  The models without scale effects of Section 13.3,
where the growth rate is pinned by demography and does \emph{not} respond to policy, are one
response; the Schumpeterian models of Chapter 14 are another.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{13.14}{}
\emph{(a) Equilibrium.}  With a competitive fringe able to produce at $\gamma\psi$, and
$\gamma\in(1,1/(1-\beta))$, the monopolist limit-prices at $p_x=\gamma\psi=\gamma(1-\beta)$.
Then
\[
x=p_x^{-1/\beta}L=\bigl[\gamma(1-\beta)\bigr]^{-1/\beta}L,
\qquad
\pi(\gamma)=(p_x-\psi)x=\gamma^{-1/\beta}(\gamma-1)(1-\beta)^{-\frac{1-\beta}{\beta}}L ,
\]
and free entry $\eta V=1$ with $V=\pi/r$ gives $r=\eta\pi(\gamma)$, hence
\[
\wh g(\gamma)=\frac{1}{\theta}\Bigl(\eta\gamma^{-1/\beta}(\gamma-1)(1-\beta)^{-\frac{1-\beta}{\beta}}L-\rho\Bigr),
\]
the expression in the text.  Output and net output per variety are
\[
\frac{Y}{N}=\frac{\bigl[\gamma(1-\beta)\bigr]^{-\frac{1-\beta}{\beta}}L}{1-\beta},
\qquad
\Omega(\gamma)\equiv\frac{\wt Y}{NL}=\bigl[\gamma(1-\beta)\bigr]^{-1/\beta}\bigl(\gamma-1+\beta\bigr) .
\]
At $\gamma=1/(1-\beta)$ this reduces to $\beta(2-\beta)/(1-\beta)$, the unconstrained-monopoly
value of Exercise 13.4; at $\gamma=1$ it reduces to $\chi\beta$, the planner's value.  There are
no transitional dynamics, by the argument of Section 13.1.4.

\emph{Two monotonicities.}  Differentiating logarithms,
\[
\frac{\dd\log\pi}{\dd\gamma}=\frac{1}{\gamma-1}-\frac{1}{\beta\gamma},
\qquad
\frac{\dd\log\Omega}{\dd\gamma}=\frac{1}{\gamma-1+\beta}-\frac{1}{\beta\gamma} .
\]
The first vanishes exactly at $\gamma=1/(1-\beta)$ and is positive below it: \emph{profits, and
hence growth, are increasing in $\gamma$ over the whole relevant range and are maximized at the
unconstrained monopoly price}.  The second vanishes at $\gamma=1$ and is negative above it:
\emph{net output per variety is strictly decreasing in $\gamma$}.  Weaker competition buys growth
at the cost of the level.

\emph{(b) Welfare.}  Since $C(t)=\bigl[\Omega(\gamma)L-\wh g(\gamma)/\eta\bigr]N(t)$ with
$N(t)=N(0)e^{\wh gt}$,
\[
\boxed{\ W(\gamma)=\frac{C(0)^{1-\theta}}{(1-\theta)\bigl[\rho-(1-\theta)\wh g(\gamma)\bigr]}
-\frac{1}{(1-\theta)\rho},
\qquad
C(0)=N(0)\Bigl[\Omega(\gamma)L-\frac{\wh g(\gamma)}{\eta}\Bigr] .\ }
\]

\emph{(c) The optimum.}  Maximizing numerically with $\beta=1/2$, $\eta=0.12$, $L=1$,
$\rho=0.02$, $\theta=2$:
\[
\begin{array}{cccccc}
\gamma & 1.2 & 1.4 & 1.6 & 1.8 & 2.0\\
\wh g & 0.0067 & 0.0145 & 0.0181 & 0.0196 & 0.0200\\
\Omega L & 1.944 & 1.837 & 1.719 & 1.605 & 1.500\\
W & 30.15 & 33.10 & \mathbf{33.27} & 32.49 & 31.25
\end{array}
\]
with the maximum at $\gamma^{\mathrm{opt}}=1.517$, strictly inside $(1,2)$.

\emph{(d) Why not $\gamma\ge1/(1-\beta)$?}  Because growth is not welfare.  Setting
$\gamma\ge1/(1-\beta)$ frees the monopolist to charge its ideal markup, which maximizes $\wh g$;
but it also minimizes $\Omega$, and the level of consumption at every $N$ falls.  A household
that discounts the future trades the two off: the level effect is felt immediately and in full,
while the growth effect accumulates only gradually and is discounted.  The interior optimum is
exactly the point at which the marginal gain in $\wh g$, valued at
$\partial W/\partial\wh g$, equals the marginal loss in $\Omega$.  This is the same second-best
logic as in Exercise 13.11(a): with the markup distortion unavoidable, the growth-maximizing
policy is not the welfare-maximizing one.

\emph{(e) The role of $\rho$.}  A more impatient household weights the immediate level effect more
and the distant growth effect less, so it prefers stricter competition policy.  The program
confirms this:
\[
\begin{array}{ccccc}
\rho & 0.01 & 0.02 & 0.04 & 0.08\\
\gamma^{\mathrm{opt}} & 1.545 & 1.517 & 1.470 & 1.400
\end{array}
\]
and $\gamma^{\mathrm{opt}}\to1/(1-\beta)$ as $\rho\to0$: a perfectly patient household would
tolerate the full monopoly markup for the sake of growth.  The left panel of
Figure~\ref{fig:ch13} shows the whole curve.

\rmk{The prediction that stricter antitrust reduces growth is a genuine feature of this class of
models, and it is extreme: growth falls monotonically as competition tightens, all the way to
zero at $\gamma=1$.  The reason is that the only role of competition here is to reduce the rents
that finance R\&D.  Real competition also disciplines incumbents and forces them to innovate to
survive --- the ``escape competition'' effect --- which requires the strategic interaction the
Schumpeterian models of Chapter 14 supply, and which can reverse the sign.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{13.16}{}
\emph{The value of a blueprint is constant.}  Free entry \bk{(13.28)} is $\eta N(t)V(t)=w(t)$,
and the wage is still $w(t)=\beta N(t)/(1-\beta)$ from \bk{(13.13)}.  Hence
\[
V(t)=\frac{\beta}{\eta(1-\beta)}
\qquad\text{for all }t ,
\]
a constant, so $\dt V(t)=0$ identically.  The HJB equation \bk{(13.8)} with $\pi=\beta L_E(t)$
then gives the interest rate directly:
\[
r(t)=\frac{\pi(t)}{V(t)}=(1-\beta)\eta L_E(t) .
\]

\emph{The labor allocation cannot move.}  Consumption is $C(t)=Y(t)-X(t)=\frac{\beta(2-\beta)}{1-\beta}N(t)L_E(t)$,
so
\[
\frac{\dt C}{C}=\frac{\dt N}{N}+\frac{\dt L_E}{L_E}=\eta\bigl(L-L_E\bigr)+\frac{\dt L_E}{L_E} ,
\]
using the innovation possibilities frontier \bk{(13.25)}.  Equating this to the Euler equation
$\dt C/C=\theta^{-1}(r-\rho)$ gives a scalar differential equation in $L_E$ alone:
\[
\frac{\dt L_E}{L_E}=\frac{(1-\beta)\eta L_E-\rho}{\theta}-\eta\bigl(L-L_E\bigr) .
\]
Its unique rest point is \bk{(13.31)},
\[
L_E^{*}=\frac{\theta\eta L+\rho}{(1-\beta)\eta+\theta\eta} ,
\]
and the right side is strictly \emph{increasing} in $L_E$, with derivative
$(1-\beta)\eta/\theta+\eta>0$.  The rest point is therefore unstable.  Since $L_E$ is a jump
variable --- nothing is predetermined about the split of a given labor force --- any
$L_E(0)\ne L_E^{*}$ sends $L_E$ monotonically to $L$ or to $0$ in finite time, violating either
labor market clearing or the free-entry equality.  Hence $L_E(t)=L_E^{*}$ for all $t$: there are
no transitional dynamics. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Finiteness and transversality.}  With $L_E$ constant, $r(t)=r^{*}=(1-\beta)\eta L_E^{*}$ and
\[
g^{*}=\frac{1}{\theta}\bigl((1-\beta)\eta L_E^{*}-\rho\bigr) .
\]
Utility is finite iff $\rho>(1-\theta)g^{*}$, which rearranges as in Exercise 13.4 to
$\rho>(1-\theta)(1-\beta)\eta L_E^{*}$; and total assets $A(t)=N(t)V$ grow at $g^{*}$, so
\bk{(13.17)} holds iff $g^{*}<r^{*}$, the same inequality.  Positive growth requires
$\rho<(1-\beta)\eta L_E^{*}$.  Together these are exactly \bk{(13.32)}.

With $\beta=1/2$, $\eta=0.12$, $L=1$, $\rho=0.02$, $\theta=2$ the program gives
$L_E^{*}=0.8667$, $L_R^{*}=0.1333$, $r^{*}=0.052$ and $g^{*}=0.016$.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{13.17}{}
\emph{The planner's problem.}  Pricing machines at marginal cost, net output with $L_E$ workers in
production is $\chi\beta N L_E$ (Exercise 13.8 with $L\to L_E$), and all of it is consumed, since
research uses labor rather than final output.  The planner therefore chooses $L_E(t)\in[0,L]$ to
solve
\[
\max\ \int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\rho t}\frac{C(t)^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}\dd t
\quad\text{s.t.}\quad
C(t)=\chi\beta N(t)L_E(t),
\qquad
\dt N(t)=\eta N(t)\bigl(L-L_E(t)\bigr) .
\]
The current-value Hamiltonian is
\[
\wh H=\frac{\bigl(\chi\beta NL_E\bigr)^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}+\mu\,\eta N\bigl(L-L_E\bigr) .
\]

\emph{The conditions.}  Differentiating in the control,
\[
\wh H_{L_E}=\chi\beta N\,C^{-\theta}-\mu\eta N=0
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\chi\beta\,C^{-\theta}=\mu\eta ,
\]
and in the state,
\[
\wh H_{N}=L_E\chi\beta C^{-\theta}+\mu\eta\bigl(L-L_E\bigr)=\rho\mu-\dt\mu .
\]
Substituting the first into the second, the term $L_E\chi\beta C^{-\theta}$ becomes $\mu\eta L_E$,
so the left side collapses to $\mu\eta L$ and
\[
\frac{\dt\mu}{\mu}=\rho-\eta L .
\]
Since $\mu\propto C^{-\theta}$, this gives
\[
\boxed{\ g^{S}=\frac{\dt C}{C}=\frac{1}{\theta}\bigl(\eta L-\rho\bigr)\ }
\]
a constant, with the research allocation $L_R^{S}=g^{S}/\eta$ and $L_E^{S}=L-L_R^{S}$, both
interior provided $\eta L>\rho>(1-\theta)\eta L$.  Since $C=\chi\beta NL_E$ with $L_E$ constant,
$N$ grows at $g^{S}$ from $t=0$: no transitional dynamics, by the same instability argument as in
Exercise 13.16.

\emph{The comparison.}  The equilibrium growth rate is
$g^{*}=\theta^{-1}\bigl((1-\beta)\eta L_E^{*}-\rho\bigr)$, so the two differ through
\[
\underbrace{\eta L}_{\text{social return}}
\qquad\text{versus}\qquad
\underbrace{(1-\beta)\eta L_E^{*}}_{\text{private return}} ,
\]
and $\eta L>(1-\beta)\eta L_E^{*}$ strictly, for \emph{two} separate reasons.  The factor
$(1-\beta)<1$ is the markup wedge already present in the lab-equipment model: the innovator
captures only part of the surplus its variety creates.  The replacement of $L$ by $L_E^{*}<L$ is
new: it is the \emph{knowledge spillover}.  A research firm values a blueprint only for the
profits it yields, whereas the planner also values it for raising the productivity of all future
research through the term $N(t)$ in \bk{(13.25)} --- and that benefit accrues to the whole labor
force, not merely to the part employed in production.

With the program's parameters, $\eta L=0.120$ against $(1-\beta)\eta L_E^{*}=0.052$, giving
$g^{S}=0.050$ against $g^{*}=0.016$.  This model therefore has both sources of inefficiency,
where the lab-equipment model of Section 13.1 had only the first.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{13.18}{}
The value of a blueprint is, by \bk{(13.7)},
\[
V(t)=\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-r^{*}(s-t)}\pi(s)\dd s,
\qquad
\pi(s)=\beta L_E(s) .
\]
Along the BGP a constant fraction of the labor force works in production, so $L_E$ grows at the
rate of population growth, $L_E(s)=L_E(t)e^{n(s-t)}$.  Hence
\[
V(t)=\beta L_E(t)\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-(r^{*}-n)(s-t)}\dd s=\frac{\beta L_E(t)}{r^{*}-n} ,
\]
the integral converging precisely because $r^{*}>n$.  The free-entry condition
$\eta N(t)^{\phi}V(t)=w(t)$ therefore reads
\[
\boxed{\ \eta N(t)^{\phi}\,\frac{\beta L_E(t)}{r^{*}-n}=w(t)\ }
\]
which is \bk{(13.36)}. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Why $r^{*}-n$.}  In the baseline model of Section 13.1 the profit flow $\beta L$ is constant
and is discounted at $r^{*}$, giving $V^{*}=\beta L/r^{*}$ as in \bk{(13.18)}.  Here the flow is
not constant: each monopolist sells one machine to each production worker, and the number of
production workers grows at $n$, so its profits grow at $n$ too.  A stream growing at $n$ and
discounted at $r^{*}$ has present value $\pi(t)/(r^{*}-n)$: the \emph{effective} discount rate is
the excess of the interest rate over the growth rate of the flow.  This is the same arithmetic as
the Gordon growth formula, and the same reason Exercise 11.3's human wealth was $B/(r-n)$.

Note also what the condition $r^{*}>n$ is doing.  In the BGP of Section 13.3,
$r^{*}=\theta n/(1-\phi)+\rho>n$ whenever $\rho>(1-\phi-\theta)n/(1-\phi)$ --- exactly the
condition of \bk{Proposition 13.5}.  If it failed, a single patent would have infinite value and
no equilibrium with positive research could exist: the market size effect would be unbounded.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{13.20}{}
Both countries have the same BGP growth rate $g_N^{*}=n/(1-\phi)$ by \bk{(13.37)}, which depends
on neither $L(0)$ nor anything else that differs between them, and the same interest rate
$r^{*}=\theta n/(1-\phi)+\rho$.  So growth rates cannot separate them.  Levels can.

\emph{The production share is the same.}  Combining the free-entry condition
$\eta N^{\phi-1}(1-\beta)L_E=r^{*}-n$ with $\dt N/N=\eta N^{\phi-1}L_R=g_N^{*}$ and dividing,
\[
\frac{L_R(t)}{L_E(t)}=\frac{(1-\beta)\,g_N^{*}}{r^{*}-n}=\frac{n(1-\beta)}{(1-\phi)(r^{*}-n)} ,
\]
a number built only from $\beta,\phi,n,\rho,\theta$.  Hence $L_E(t)/L(t)$ is the same constant in
both countries.

\emph{Technology scales with population.}  Solving the free-entry condition for $N$,
\[
N(t)=\Bigl[\frac{\eta(1-\beta)L_E(t)}{r^{*}-n}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{1-\phi}} ,
\]
so, since $L_E$ is proportional to $L$ with the same factor in both countries,
\[
\frac{N_2(t)}{N_1(t)}=\Bigl[\frac{L_2(t)}{L_1(t)}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{1-\phi}}
=\Bigl[\frac{L_2(0)}{L_1(0)}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{1-\phi}}\ >\ 1 .
\]
Finally, output per capita is $y(t)=Y(t)/L(t)=N(t)L_E(t)/[(1-\beta)L(t)]$, and the last factor is
common, so
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{y_2(t)}{y_1(t)}=\frac{N_2(t)}{N_1(t)}
=\Bigl[\frac{L_2(0)}{L_1(0)}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{1-\phi}}>1
\qquad\text{at every }t .\ }
\]
$\blacksquare$

The elasticity $1/(1-\phi)>1$ is large: with $\phi=1/2$ the program gives
\[
\begin{array}{cccc}
L_2/L_1 & 1.5 & 2 & 5\\
y_2/y_1 & 2.25 & 4.00 & 25.0
\end{array}
\]
a twofold population difference producing a fourfold, permanent, income difference.

\rmk{This is the caveat the text attaches to the label ``growth without scale effects''.  Removing
the scale effect from the \emph{growth rate} does not remove it from the \emph{level}; it relocates
it.  Indeed the level effect here is stronger than anything in the neoclassical model, and it is
no more visible in the data --- large countries are not systematically richer.  Together with the
model's other prediction that faster population growth raises the growth rate, for which the
postwar evidence is at best absent, this suggests that the semi-endogenous formulation trades one
awkward implication for two others.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{13.21}{}
\emph{Two state-like variables.}  Write $n_E(t)\equiv L_E(t)/L(t)$ for the production share and
\[
z(t)\equiv N(t)^{\phi-1}L(t)
\]
for a transformed state; $z$ is predetermined (it moves only with $N$ and $L$), while $n_E$ is a
jump variable.

\emph{The interest rate out of the BGP.}  Free entry $\eta N^{\phi}V=w=\beta N/(1-\beta)$ gives
\[
V(t)=\frac{\beta\,N(t)^{1-\phi}}{\eta(1-\beta)}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\frac{\dt V}{V}=(1-\phi)\frac{\dt N}{N}=(1-\phi)\eta z(t)\bigl(1-n_E(t)\bigr) ,
\]
using $\dt N/N=\eta N^{\phi-1}L_R=\eta z(1-n_E)$.  The HJB equation $rV-\dt V=\pi=\beta L_E$ then
gives
\[
r(t)=\frac{\pi}{V}+\frac{\dt V}{V}=\eta z(t)\Bigl[(1-\beta)n_E(t)+(1-\phi)\bigl(1-n_E(t)\bigr)\Bigr]
=\eta z\bigl[(1-\phi)+(\phi-\beta)n_E\bigr] .
\]

\emph{The planar system.}  Differentiating $z$ and using the Euler equation for
$c=C/L$ together with $C\propto NL_E$,
\[
\boxed{\
\dt z=z\Bigl[n-(1-\phi)\eta z\bigl(1-n_E\bigr)\Bigr],
\qquad
\dt n_E=n_E\Bigl[\frac{\eta z\bigl[(1-\phi)+(\phi-\beta)n_E\bigr]-\rho}{\theta}
-\eta z\bigl(1-n_E\bigr)\Bigr] .\ }
\]

\emph{The steady state.}  Setting $\dt z=0$ gives $\eta z^{*}(1-n_E^{*})=n/(1-\phi)=g_N^{*}$,
recovering \bk{(13.37)}; setting $\dt n_E=0$ gives
$\eta z^{*}[(1-\phi)+(\phi-\beta)n_E^{*}]=\rho+\theta n/(1-\phi)=r^{*}$.  Solving the pair,
\[
n_E^{*}=\frac{(1-\phi)\bigl(r^{*}-n\bigr)}{r^{*}(1-\phi)+n(\phi-\beta)},
\qquad
\eta z^{*}=\frac{n}{(1-\phi)\bigl(1-n_E^{*}\bigr)} ,
\]
which one checks is equivalent to the BGP free-entry condition
$\eta z^{*}n_E^{*}(1-\beta)=r^{*}-n$.

\emph{Saddle-path stability.}  With $\beta=1/2$, $\phi=1/2$, $n=0.01$, $\rho=0.02$, $\theta=2$
and $\eta=0.12$ the program gives $n_E^{*}=0.8333$, $z^{*}=1.000$, and Jacobian eigenvalues
\[
\xi_1=-0.01437,\qquad \xi_2=+0.10437,
\qquad \det J=-0.00150<0 ,
\]
one of each sign.  Since $z$ is predetermined by $N(0)$ and $L(0)$ while $n_E$ jumps, there is
exactly one initial $n_E(0)$ on the stable manifold, and the equilibrium path is unique.

\emph{The economics of the transition.}  Suppose $N(0)$ is below its BGP level relative to $L(0)$,
so $z(0)>z^{*}$.  Research is then unusually productive relative to the stock of knowledge, so the
economy devotes an unusually large share of labor to R\&D: $n_E(0)<n_E^{*}$.  Technology grows
faster than $n/(1-\phi)$, $z$ falls toward $z^{*}$, and the research share declines to its BGP
value.  Convergence is slow --- the half-life is $\log2/|\xi_1|\approx48$ periods --- which is a
point in the model's favor: unlike the lab-equipment model, where there are no transitional
dynamics at all, this one predicts protracted convergence of the kind Chapter 3 documents.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{13.23}{}
\emph{(b) first: rentals, not sales.}  The hint matters.  If the monopolist \emph{sold} durable
machines it would face the Coase problem: having sold to the high-valuation users today it would
want to cut the price tomorrow, buyers would anticipate this, and with perfectly durable goods the
monopolist's power would unravel.  Renting avoids the issue entirely, because the monopolist
retains ownership and re-optimizes each instant against the same demand curve.  So let the
monopolist build machines at cost $\psi$ each and rent them at $p_x(\nu,t)$ per unit of time.

A machine built at date $t$ yields the rental stream $p_x$ while its physical mass decays at
$\delta$, so its value to the monopolist is $\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-(r+\delta)s}p_x\dd s=p_x/(r+\delta)$
along a path with constant $r$ and $p_x$.  The \emph{user cost} of a unit of machine services is
therefore
\[
(r(t)+\delta)\psi ,
\]
interest forgone plus depreciation, and the monopolist's instantaneous problem is
\[
\max_{p_x}\ \bigl[p_x-(r+\delta)\psi\bigr]p_x^{-1/\beta}L .
\]

\emph{(a) and (c).}  This is the problem of Exercise 13.2 with $\psi$ replaced by
$(r+\delta)\psi$.  Its solution is
\[
p_x^{*}=\frac{(r^{*}+\delta)\psi}{1-\beta},
\qquad
x^{*}=\bigl(p_x^{*}\bigr)^{-1/\beta}L,
\qquad
\pi^{*}=\bigl[p_x^{*}-(r^{*}+\delta)\psi\bigr]x^{*}=\beta\,p_x^{*}x^{*} ,
\]
all constant along a BGP.  Free entry $\eta V=1$ with $V=\pi^{*}/r^{*}$ gives
\[
r^{*}=\eta\beta\,p_x^{*}x^{*}=\eta\beta L\Bigl[\frac{(r^{*}+\delta)\psi}{1-\beta}\Bigr]^{-\frac{1-\beta}{\beta}} ,
\]
an implicit equation whose left side is increasing in $r^{*}$ and whose right side is decreasing,
so there is a unique root; and then $g^{*}=\theta^{-1}(r^{*}-\rho)$, with no transitional dynamics
by the argument of Section 13.1.4 ($\eta V=1$ at all dates forces $\dt V=0$ and hence constant
$r$).

Every structural feature of Section 13.1 survives: a constant markup $1/(1-\beta)$ over the user
cost, a constant machine quantity per variety, constant profits, an interest rate pinned by free
entry, and constant growth from the first instant.  And under the natural normalization
\[
(r^{*}+\delta)\psi\equiv1-\beta ,
\]
which replaces the text's $\psi\equiv1-\beta$, one gets $p_x=1$, $x=L$, $\pi=\beta L$,
$r^{*}=\eta\beta L$ and
\[
g^{*}=\frac{1}{\theta}\bigl(\eta\beta L-\rho\bigr) ,
\]
literally \bk{(13.20)}. $\blacksquare$

\rmk{The exercise is a check that the model's conclusions do not depend on the odd assumption that
machines evaporate after a single use.  They do not: all that assumption does is set the user cost
equal to $\psi$ rather than $(r+\delta)\psi$.  What \emph{would} change the analysis is selling
durable machines rather than renting them, which is why the hint points to rentals --- a reminder
that ``monopoly power'' in a durable-goods market is a subtler object than a markup formula.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{13.25}{}
The household allocates a given total expenditure across varieties to maximize the Dixit--Stiglitz
index \bk{(13.40)}.  The first-order condition between two products $\nu$ and $\nu'$ is
\[
\Bigl(\frac{c(\nu,t)}{c(\nu',t)}\Bigr)^{-1/\varepsilon}=\frac{p_c(\nu,t)}{p_c(\nu',t)}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
c(\nu,t)=c(\nu',t)\Bigl(\frac{p_c(\nu,t)}{p_c(\nu',t)}\Bigr)^{-\varepsilon} .
\]
Substituting this into the expenditure identity
$\int_{0}^{N(t)}p_c(\nu,t)c(\nu,t)\dd\nu=E(t)$ and solving for $c(\nu',t)$,
\[
c(\nu',t)\,p_c(\nu',t)^{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{N(t)}p_c(\nu,t)^{1-\varepsilon}\dd\nu=E(t)
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
c(\nu',t)=\frac{p_c(\nu',t)^{-\varepsilon}E(t)}{\int_{0}^{N(t)}p_c(\nu,t)^{1-\varepsilon}\dd\nu} .
\]
Finally, by Exercise 12.10 the ideal price index is
$P(t)=\bigl[\int_{0}^{N(t)}p_c(\nu,t)^{1-\varepsilon}\dd\nu\bigr]^{1/(1-\varepsilon)}$ and
$E(t)=P(t)C(t)$, so
\[
c(\nu,t)=p_c(\nu,t)^{-\varepsilon}
\Bigl[\int_{0}^{N(t)}p_c(\nu,t)^{1-\varepsilon}\dd\nu\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}-1}C(t)
=\frac{p_c(\nu,t)^{-\varepsilon}}
{\Bigl[\int_{0}^{N(t)}p_c(\nu,t)^{1-\varepsilon}\dd\nu\Bigr]^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}}}
\,C(t) ,
\]
which is \bk{(13.44)}. $\blacksquare$

The denominator is the ideal price index raised to the power $-\varepsilon$, as the text notes.
The demand facing each producer is therefore isoelastic with elasticity $\varepsilon$, which is
why the monopoly price is the constant markup $p_c=\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon-1}w$ --- the
same structure as the machine demand \bk{(13.6)} with $\varepsilon$ in place of $1/\beta$.  The
expanding product and expanding input models are the same model with different labels on the
elasticity.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{13.26}{}
\emph{The budget constraint.}  By Exercise 13.25 the household's expenditure on the differentiated
goods is $\int_{0}^{N(t)}p_c(\nu,t)c(\nu,t)\dd\nu=P(t)C(t)$.  With the normalization \bk{(13.45)},
$P(t)\equiv1$, so expenditure is simply $C(t)$ and $C$ is real consumption.  The household holds
the corporate sector, $A(t)=\int_{0}^{N(t)}V(\nu,t)\dd\nu$, and supplies $L$ units of labor, so
\[
\dt A(t)=r(t)A(t)+w(t)L-C(t) .
\]

\emph{The Hamiltonian.}  With the logarithmic preferences \bk{(13.39)},
\[
\wh H(A,C,\mu)=\log C(t)+\mu(t)\bigl[r(t)A(t)+w(t)L-C(t)\bigr] ,
\]
so
\[
\wh H_C=\frac{1}{C(t)}-\mu(t)=0,
\qquad
\wh H_A=\mu(t)r(t)=\rho\mu(t)-\dt\mu(t),
\qquad
\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-\rho t}\mu(t)A(t)=0 .
\]
The costate equation gives $\dt\mu/\mu=\rho-r(t)$, while $\mu=1/C$ gives
$\dt\mu/\mu=-\dt C/C$.  Hence
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{\dt C(t)}{C(t)}=r(t)-\rho\ }
\]
which is \bk{(13.46)}: the CRRA Euler equation with $\theta=1$. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Why the numeraire matters.}  Had the wage or a single variety been chosen as numeraire, the
interest rate in this equation would be a nominal one and the Euler equation would carry an extra
$\dt P/P$ term, exactly as in Exercise 11.19's second policy.  Setting the ideal price index to
one means measuring income in constant units of the composite at every date, so $r(t)$ is the real
interest rate and $C(t)$ is real consumption.  This is also what makes it legitimate to say, as
the text does, that ``real incomes grow at the same rate as $C(t)$'' even though no individual
product's technology ever improves: the growth is entirely in the price index, which falls as
$N^{1/(1-\varepsilon)}$ because of love of variety.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{13.27}{}
\emph{(a) Finiteness and transversality.}  With logarithmic preferences and
$C(t)=C(0)e^{g^{*}t}$,
\[
U=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\rho t}\bigl[\log C(0)+g^{*}t\bigr]\dd t
=\frac{\log C(0)}{\rho}+\frac{g^{*}}{\rho^{2}}<\infty
\]
for any $\rho>0$ --- no additional parameter restriction is needed, which is the advantage of the
log specification.  For transversality, the Euler equation \bk{(13.46)} gives
$r^{*}=g^{*}+\rho>g^{*}$.  Total assets are $A(t)=N(t)V(t)$, and by the free-entry condition
\bk{(13.50)}, $N V=w/\eta$, so $A$ grows at the growth rate of the wage, which is $g^{*}$.  Hence
\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-r^{*}t}A(t)=A(0)\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{(g^{*}-r^{*})t}=A(0)\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-\rho t}=0 . \qquad\checkmark
\]

\emph{(b) No transitional dynamics.}  Write $\ell(t)\equiv L_E(t)$.  With the numeraire
\bk{(13.45)} and the common price $p_c=\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon-1}w$, the normalization
$N^{1/(1-\varepsilon)}p_c=1$ gives
\[
p_c(t)=N(t)^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon-1}},
\qquad
w(t)=\frac{\varepsilon-1}{\varepsilon}N(t)^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon-1}},
\qquad
V(t)=\frac{w(t)}{\eta N(t)}=\frac{\varepsilon-1}{\varepsilon\eta}N(t)^{-\frac{\varepsilon-2}{\varepsilon-1}} ,
\]
the last from free entry.  Profits \bk{(13.48)} are $\pi=\frac{1}{\varepsilon-1}\frac{\ell}{N}w
=\frac{\ell}{\varepsilon}N^{-\frac{\varepsilon-2}{\varepsilon-1}}$, so $\pi/V=\eta\ell/(\varepsilon-1)$,
and the HJB equation gives
\[
r(t)=\frac{\pi}{V}+\frac{\dt V}{V}
=\frac{\eta\ell}{\varepsilon-1}-\frac{\varepsilon-2}{\varepsilon-1}\,\eta\bigl(L-\ell\bigr) .
\]
Since $C=\ell\,N^{1/(\varepsilon-1)}$ and $\dt N/N=\eta(L-\ell)$, the Euler equation
$\dt C/C=r-\rho$ becomes, after cancelling,
\[
\frac{\dt\ell}{\ell}=\frac{\eta\ell}{\varepsilon-1}-\eta\bigl(L-\ell\bigr)-\rho ,
\]
whose right side has derivative $\eta/(\varepsilon-1)+\eta>0$ in $\ell$: the rest point is
unstable, so the jump variable $\ell$ must sit at it for all $t$.  Solving,
\[
\ell^{*}=\frac{(\varepsilon-1)\bigl(\eta L+\rho\bigr)}{\varepsilon\eta},
\qquad
L_R^{*}=L-\ell^{*}=\frac{\eta L-(\varepsilon-1)\rho}{\varepsilon\eta} ,
\]
which is \bk{(13.53)}, and $g^{*}=\eta L_R^{*}/(\varepsilon-1)$ gives \bk{(13.54)}.  There are no
transitional dynamics. $\blacksquare$

\emph{(c) The Pareto optimum.}  Allocating $\ell$ workers symmetrically across the $N$ products
gives $c(\nu)=\ell/N$ and hence $C=N^{\varepsilon/(\varepsilon-1)}(\ell/N)=N^{1/(\varepsilon-1)}\ell$,
the same expression as in equilibrium (there is no markup distortion \emph{across} varieties here,
since all prices are equal).  The planner therefore solves
\[
\max\ \int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\rho t}\log\bigl(N^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon-1}}\ell\bigr)\dd t
\quad\text{s.t.}\quad
\dt N=\eta N\bigl(L-\ell\bigr) ,
\]
with $\wh H=\log\bigl(N^{1/(\varepsilon-1)}\ell\bigr)+\mu\eta N(L-\ell)$.  The conditions are
\[
\wh H_{\ell}=\frac{1}{\ell}-\mu\eta N=0,
\qquad
\wh H_{N}=\frac{1}{(\varepsilon-1)N}+\mu\eta\bigl(L-\ell\bigr)=\rho\mu-\dt\mu .
\]
In a steady state $\dt\mu/\mu=-\dt N/N=-\eta(L-\ell)$; substituting $\mu=1/(\eta N\ell)$ and
multiplying through by $N\ell$, the terms in $L-\ell$ cancel and
\[
\frac{\ell}{\varepsilon-1}=\frac{\rho}{\eta}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\ell^{S}=\frac{(\varepsilon-1)\rho}{\eta},
\qquad
L_R^{S}=L-\frac{(\varepsilon-1)\rho}{\eta} ,
\]
and therefore
\[
\boxed{\ g^{S}=\frac{\eta L_R^{S}}{\varepsilon-1}=\frac{\eta L-(\varepsilon-1)\rho}{\varepsilon-1}
=\varepsilon\,g^{*}\ }
\]
The optimal growth rate is \emph{exactly $\varepsilon$ times} the equilibrium rate, and it is
positive under the same condition $\eta L>(\varepsilon-1)\rho$.  With $\varepsilon=3$,
$\eta=0.12$, $L=1$ and $\rho=0.02$ the program gives $g^{*}=0.0133$ against $g^{S}=0.0400$.

\emph{Why the factor is $\varepsilon$.}  The monopolist charges $\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon-1}w$
and so keeps a fraction $1/\varepsilon$ of its revenue as profit.  The planner values a new
product at its full contribution to the consumption index --- the love-of-variety term
$N^{1/(\varepsilon-1)}$ --- which corresponds to the whole revenue.  The appropriability wedge is
therefore exactly $1/\varepsilon$, and as $\varepsilon\to\infty$ (products becoming perfect
substitutes) the equilibrium growth rate collapses to zero relative to the optimum: with no market
power there is nothing to finance innovation, which is \bk{Proposition 12.1} seen in general
equilibrium.
\end{aex}


%% ========================================================================
\solchapter{14}{Models of Schumpeterian Growth}

\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{0.86\textwidth}
\small
The twenty exercises of Chapter 14 left unsolved in the instructor's manual: Exercises 14.1,
14.3--14.5, 14.8--14.11, 14.16, 14.17, 14.23--14.25, 14.28--14.34.  The manual solves 14.2, 14.6,
14.7, 14.12--14.15, 14.18--14.22, 14.26, 14.27 and 14.35.  Notation follows the book: $q(\nu,t)$
is the quality of machine line $\nu$, $\lambda>1$ the size of a quality step, $\eta$ the
productivity of R\&D, $z$ the flow rate of innovation, $Q=\int_0^1q\dd\nu$ average quality,
$\phi$ the incumbent's incremental R\&D productivity and $\kappa$ the entrant's step size in
Section 14.3, and $n(\nu,t)$ the technology gap in Section 14.4.  It is convenient to write
\[
\chi\equiv(1-\beta)^{-1/\beta}\ \ge\ e
\]
for the planner's machine-intensity factor.  The numerical parts are computed by
\texttt{code/ch14.py}.
\end{minipage}
\end{center}

\bigskip
\hrule
\bigskip

\begin{aex}{14.1}{}
Take any machine line $\nu$ with positive R\&D.  Then the free-entry condition \bk{(14.14)} holds
as an equality,
\[
\eta V(\nu,t\mid q)=\lambda^{-1}q(\nu,t) ,
\]
and in a BGP the right side is constant between innovations, so $\dt V(\nu,t\mid q)=0$ and
\bk{(14.13)} gives
\[
V(\nu,t\mid q)=\frac{\pi(\nu,t\mid q)}{r^{*}+z(\nu,t\mid q)}=\frac{\beta q(\nu,t)L}{r^{*}+z(\nu,t\mid q)} ,
\]
using $\pi=\beta qL$ from \bk{(14.11)}.  Combining the two,
\[
\frac{\eta\beta q L}{r^{*}+z(\nu,t\mid q)}=\frac{q}{\lambda}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
r^{*}+z(\nu,t\mid q)=\lambda\eta\beta L .
\]
The quality $q$ has cancelled: both the profit from an innovation and the cost of achieving it
are proportional to quality, which is exactly what the specification of the innovation
possibilities frontier was designed to deliver.  Since $r^{*}$ is common to all lines,
\[
\boxed{\ z(\nu,t\mid q)=z^{*}\equiv\lambda\eta\beta L-r^{*}\qquad\text{for every }\nu .\ }
\]

It remains to rule out lines with \emph{no} R\&D.  If line $\nu$ had $z(\nu,t)=0$, free entry
would hold as a weak inequality, $\eta\beta qL/r^{*}\le\lambda^{-1}q$, that is
$r^{*}\ge\lambda\eta\beta L$.  But $r^{*}$ is common across lines, so this would then hold for
\emph{every} line, forcing $z^{*}\le0$ everywhere and hence, by \bk{(14.22)}, zero growth --- which
contradicts the parameter restriction $\lambda\eta\beta L>\rho$ of \bk{Proposition 14.1}.
$\blacksquare$

\rmk{This is the property that makes the model tractable and, arguably, realistic: research is
spread evenly across the whole spectrum of machines rather than concentrated on the most or least
advanced.  It depends on the linearity of \bk{(14.3)} in $q$ together with the assumption that
R\&D on a machine of quality $q$ is proportionately harder.  Exercise 14.14 shows that changing
the production function to $q(\nu,t)x^{1-\beta}L_E^{\beta}$ with a common $L_E$ concentrates all
R\&D on the single highest-quality line.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{14.3}{}
Suppose a firm pays $\epsilon$ and acquires the innovator's technology for machine line $\nu$ of
quality $q$.  The two firms then have \emph{identical} constant marginal costs $\psi q$ and
produce a homogeneous good --- machines of the same variety and quality are perfect substitutes.
Bertrand competition between two firms with the same marginal cost has a unique equilibrium in
which both price at marginal cost and both earn zero operating profits.

The copier therefore earns
\[
0-\epsilon=-\epsilon<0 ,
\]
a strict loss, whatever $q$, whatever the date, and however small $\epsilon$ is.  No firm copies.
$\blacksquare$

Since no copying occurs on the equilibrium path, the innovator faces only the previous vintage
$\lambda^{-1}q$, exactly as in the model with patents.  Every equilibrium object is therefore
