technological change keeps raising the marginal product of capital, offsetting the diminishing
returns from capital deepening.  The interest rate therefore cannot settle down, and neither can
consumption growth. $\blacksquare$

\emph{The economics.}  \bk{Proposition 15.10} is the good news: with $\sigma<1$ capital deepening
does direct technological change toward labor, so technology becomes \emph{relatively}
labor-augmenting.  \bk{Proposition 15.11} is the bad news: relatively is not enough.  Balanced
growth, by \bk{Theorem 2.6}, needs technological change to be \emph{purely} labor-augmenting, and
as long as there is any capital-augmenting research at all the economy cannot settle down.  The
special case that rescues it is $\delta=1$, where, as Exercise 15.22 shows, factor shares are
pinned down and $N_K$ must asymptotically stop growing.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.22}{}
With $\delta=1$ the innovation possibilities frontier becomes $\dt N_f/N_f=\eta_fS_f$, and the
free-entry conditions \bk{(15.33)}--\bk{(15.34)} reduce to
\[
\eta_LN_L(t)V_L(t)=w_S(t)=\eta_KN_K(t)V_K(t) .
\]
In a BGP $V_f=\pi_f/r^{*}$, so the interest rate cancels and
\[
\eta_LN_L\pi_L=\eta_KN_K\pi_K .
\]
Now $N_f\pi_f$ is the total profit earned by the monopolists in sector $f$.  In each sector,
profits and factor payments are in a fixed ratio: as in \bk{(13.11)}--\bk{(13.13)}, the machine
producers earn $\beta$ per unit of quality while the factor earns $\beta/(1-\beta)$ per unit, so
\[
N_L\pi_L=(1-\beta)\,w(t)L ,
\qquad
N_K\pi_K=(1-\beta)\,r(t)K(t) .
\]
Substituting and cancelling $(1-\beta)$,
\[
\eta_L\,w(t)L=\eta_K\,r(t)K(t)
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\boxed{\ \frac{r(t)K(t)}{w(t)L}=\frac{\eta_L}{\eta_K}=\eta^{-1}\ }
\]
which is \bk{(15.46)}. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Why this is the decisive equation.}  It says that with extreme state dependence, directed
technological change pins the \emph{factor shares} of national income at a constant determined
purely by the relative productivity of the two research technologies.  Constant factor shares
together with capital deepening is exactly the configuration that forces Harrod neutrality:
by \bk{(15.19)},
\[
\frac{rK}{wL}=\gamma^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma}}
\Bigl(\frac{N_K}{N_L}\Bigr)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}\Bigl(\frac{K}{L}\Bigr)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}} ,
\]
so constancy of the left side requires $N_KK/N_LL$ to be constant, that is
\[
\frac{\dt N_L}{N_L}-\frac{\dt N_K}{N_K}=s_K ,
\]
which is \bk{(15.47)}.  Combined with Exercise 15.23's expression for the interest rate, this
forces $N_K$ itself to be asymptotically constant, so that all long-run technological change is
labor-augmenting.  The model therefore \emph{derives} the assumption that growth theory has had to
impose since \bk{Theorem 2.6}.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.23}{}
The rental rate of capital is its marginal value product in the $K$-intensive sector.  With
machines priced at the monopoly markup, the derived production function of that sector is
$Y_K=\frac{1}{1-\beta}N_Kp_K^{(1-\beta)/\beta}K$, so by the same computation that gives
\bk{(13.13)},
\[
r(t)=\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}\,p_K(t)^{1/\beta}N_K(t) .
\]
It remains to express $p_K$ in terms of quantities.  Final good producers equate the ratio of
marginal products of the two intermediates to their relative price; using \bk{(15.3)} and
substituting the sectoral outputs $Y_f=\frac{1}{1-\beta}N_fp_f^{(1-\beta)/\beta}F$, together with
the price normalization \bk{(15.10)}, one obtains after collecting exponents
\[
\boxed{\ r(t)=\beta\,\gamma_K\,N_K(t)
\Bigl[\gamma_L\Bigl(\frac{N_L(t)L}{N_K(t)K(t)}\Bigr)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}
+\gamma_K\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{\sigma-1}}\ }
\]
which is \bk{(15.48)}. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Reading it.}  The interest rate is the product of two objects: the level of
capital-augmenting technology $N_K(t)$, and a term depending only on the \emph{ratio}
$N_LL/N_KK$ of the two effective factor supplies.  That separation is what completes the argument
of \bk{Proposition 15.12}.  A BGP requires $r$ constant; \bk{(15.47)} already requires
$N_LL/N_KK$ to be constant, so the bracket is constant; therefore $N_K(t)$ must be constant too.
And if $N_K$ is constant while $N_L$ grows, all long-run technological change is purely
labor-augmenting.

Note also that $r$ is \emph{increasing} in $N_K$: capital-augmenting technological change raises
the return to capital directly.  This is the force that Exercise 15.21 showed prevents a BGP when
$\delta<1$ --- there $N_K$ keeps growing and $r$ never settles.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.25}{}
With $\delta=1$ the two technologies evolve according to $\dt N_f/N_f=\eta_fS_f$, so
\[
\frac{\dd}{\dd t}\log\frac{N_K(t)}{N_L(t)}=\eta_KS_K(t)-\eta_LS_L(t) ,
\]
and the allocation of scientists responds to the state through the free-entry conditions.  By
\bk{(15.36)} with $\delta=1$, the relative value of $K$-research satisfies
\[
\frac{\eta_KN_KV_K}{\eta_LN_LV_L}
=\eta\,\gamma^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\sigma}}
\Bigl(\frac{N_K}{N_L}\Bigr)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}}\Bigl(\frac KL\Bigr)^{\frac{\sigma-1}{\sigma}} ,
\]
whose elasticity with respect to $N_K/N_L$ is $(\sigma-1)/\sigma$.

\emph{Stability.}  If $\sigma<1$ this elasticity is negative: an increase in $N_K/N_L$ makes
$K$-research relatively \emph{less} attractive, so scientists move toward $L$-research,
$\eta_KS_K-\eta_LS_L$ falls, and $N_K/N_L$ is pushed back.  The system is self-correcting, and
since the feedback operates at every level of the ratio the BGP is globally stable.  The
additional requirement $S_K<\eta_LS$ ensures that the rate at which capital deepens does not
exceed what labor-augmenting research can offset, so that $N_LL/N_KK$ can be held constant with
$S_L\le S$.

\emph{Instability.}  If $\sigma>1$ the elasticity is positive: more $K$-augmenting technology
makes further $K$-augmenting research more profitable still, so any departure from the BGP is
amplified and the economy diverges.  With $\beta=1/2$ the program tabulates the boundary:
\[
\begin{array}{cccccc}
\varepsilon & 0.4 & 0.8 & 1.0 & 1.5 & 2.5\\
\sigma & 0.70 & 0.90 & 1.00 & 1.25 & 1.75\\
\text{stable} & \text{yes} & \text{yes} & \text{no} & \text{no} & \text{no}
\end{array}
\]
$\blacksquare$

\emph{The connection to Proposition 15.6.}  That proposition required $\sigma<1/\delta$ for stable
dynamics in the general state-dependent model.  Here $\delta=1$, so the requirement is
$\sigma<1$ --- exactly what was found.  The two results are the same statement: state dependence
creates increasing returns in the direction of research, and stability requires the price effect
(which operates through $\sigma$) to be strong enough to offset them.

This is a fortunate coincidence for the theory, since the empirical evidence on capital-labor
substitution points to $\sigma<1$, which is precisely the range in which \bk{Proposition 15.12}'s
purely labor-augmenting BGP is an attractor rather than a knife edge.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.26}{}
\emph{The mechanism.}  Impose a wage floor $\bar w$ above the market-clearing wage.  Employment
falls to the level $L^{d}$ at which the marginal product of labor equals $\bar w$, and
$L-L^{d}>0$ workers are unemployed.  The effective relative supply of capital to \emph{employed}
labor, $K/L^{d}$, is therefore higher than it would have been.  By \bk{Proposition 15.10}, with
$\sigma<1$ an increase in $K/L^{d}$ directs technological change toward labor --- that is,
$N_L/N_K$ rises --- and since $\sigma<1$ reverses augmenting into biased, labor-augmenting change
is \emph{capital-biased}: it lowers the marginal product of labor relative to capital at given
factor proportions.

That is the mechanism Blanchard describes.  A wage push first raises unemployment directly; the
induced technological response then makes labor \emph{less} valuable at the margin, so
unemployment persists even after the initial shock has passed.  The two stages have very different
time scales --- the employment response is immediate, the technological response accumulates over
decades --- which matches the European experience of a wage push in the 1970s followed by
persistently high unemployment through the 1980s and 1990s.

\emph{The two cases.}  The dynamics depend on how the floor evolves.

\emph{A constant floor $\bar w$.}  Real wages grow in the long run, so a fixed nominal or real
floor is eventually overtaken by the market-clearing wage and ceases to bind.  Unemployment then
rises on impact, persists while the induced capital-biased technological change works through, and
finally disappears as growth renders the floor irrelevant.  The model predicts a long but
ultimately temporary episode --- a hump, not a permanent shift.

\emph{A floor growing at the economy's growth rate.}  Now the constraint binds forever.  The
economy settles on a new path with permanently lower employment, permanently more capital-biased
technology, and --- by \bk{Proposition 15.12} --- still a constant growth rate, since the
technology ratio adjusts to keep factor shares constant.  Unemployment is permanent, and the
induced bias makes it worse than the static effect alone would imply.

\emph{How much light does it shed?}  Some, and less than one would like.  The mechanism is real
and gives a reason why a shock in one decade should still be visible two decades later, which
purely static models of labor market institutions do not.  It also predicts the right sign for the
accompanying facts: capital deepening in Europe and a rising capital share.  Against that, the
model treats the wage floor as exogenous, says nothing about why it was imposed or why it
persisted, and --- because $\sigma$ is difficult to estimate and the model's predictions reverse
at $\sigma=1$ --- rests on an elasticity that is not securely known.  The most one should claim is
that directed technological change supplies a propagation mechanism that turns a transitory
institutional shock into a protracted one, not that it explains the shock.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{15.30}{}
The distribution is supported on $[y_0,\infty)$ with $y_0=B^{1/\alpha}$, since $G(y_0)=0$, and its
density is
\[
g(y)=\alpha By^{-\alpha-1}=\alpha y_0^{\alpha}y^{-\alpha-1},
\qquad y\ge y_0 .
\]
For any $k$,
\[
\E\bigl[y^{k}\bigr]=\int_{y_0}^{\infty}\alpha y_0^{\alpha}y^{k-\alpha-1}\dd y
=\alpha y_0^{\alpha}\Bigl[\frac{y^{k-\alpha}}{k-\alpha}\Bigr]_{y_0}^{\infty}
=\begin{cases}
\dfrac{\alpha\,y_0^{k}}{\alpha-k} & \text{if }k<\alpha,\\[6pt]
+\infty & \text{if }k\ge\alpha ,
\end{cases}
\]
the integral diverging at the upper limit whenever $k\ge\alpha$.  In particular
\[
\E[y]=\frac{\alpha y_0}{\alpha-1}\ (\alpha>1),
\qquad
\E\bigl[y^{2}\bigr]=\frac{\alpha y_0^{2}}{\alpha-2}\ (\alpha>2) ,
\]
and hence, for $\alpha>2$,
\[
\boxed{\ \Var[y]=\frac{\alpha y_0^{2}}{\alpha-2}-\frac{\alpha^{2}y_0^{2}}{(\alpha-1)^{2}}
=\frac{\alpha\,y_0^{2}}{(\alpha-1)^{2}(\alpha-2)}\ }
\]
For $\alpha\le2$ the variance is infinite, and for $\alpha\le1$ even the mean is.  With $B=1$ the
program tabulates
\[
\begin{array}{ccccc}
\alpha & 1.5 & 2.0 & 2.5 & 4.0\\
\E[y] & 3.000 & 2.000 & 1.667 & 1.333\\
\Var[y] & \infty & \infty & 2.222 & 0.222
\end{array}
\]
$\blacksquare$

\emph{Why this matters here.}  The Pareto distribution is the one that arises from random
proportional growth with an exponential exit hazard --- as in Exercise 14.28, and as in the models
of firm size, income and city size that Part VIII returns to.  Its defining feature is that the
tail is thick enough that moments fail in order: the variance goes first, at $\alpha=2$, and the
mean at $\alpha=1$.  Since the empirical estimates for firm sizes and top incomes cluster near
$\alpha=1$, the second moment is not a usable statistic for these variables, and inequality
measures built on variances (or on log variances, which is why log specifications are preferred)
can be badly behaved.  Exercise 15.31 exploits the same algebra to show that the conditional mean
$\E[y\mid y\ge y']$ is proportional to $y'$ --- the scale invariance that makes the Pareto
distribution so tractable, and so unforgiving.
\end{aex}


%% ========================================================================
\solchapter{16}{Stochastic Dynamic Programming}

\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{0.86\textwidth}
\small
The five exercises of Chapter 16 left unsolved in the instructor's manual: Exercises 16.1, 16.2,
16.5, 16.6 and 16.7.  The manual solves 16.3, 16.4 and 16.8--16.16.  Notation follows the book:
$Z=\{z_1,\dots,z_N\}$ with $z_1\le\dots\le z_N$ is the finite state space of the Markov chain,
$q_{jj'}\equiv\Prob[z'=z_j\mid z=z_{j'}]$ its transition probabilities, $G(x,z)$ the feasibility
correspondence, $U(x,y,z)$ the payoff, $\pi$ the policy function, and
\[
(TW)(x,z)\equiv\sup_{y\in G(x,z)}\Bigl\{U(x,y,z)+\beta\,\E\bigl[W(y,z')\mid z\bigr]\Bigr\}
\]
the Bellman operator of \bk{Problem 16.2}, which \bk{Theorem 16.3} shows is a contraction on the
space of bounded continuous functions on $X\times Z$ with unique fixed point $V$.
\end{minipage}
\end{center}

\bigskip
\hrule
\bigskip

\begin{aex}{16.1}{}
Part 3 states that $\E[f(z')\mid z]$ is nondecreasing in $z$ for every nondecreasing
$f:Z\to\R$.  Write $f_j\equiv f(z_j)$, so that $f$ nondecreasing means
$f_1\le f_2\le\dots\le f_N$, and fix $j''>j'$.

\emph{Sufficiency.}  Abel summation (summation by parts) rewrites any expectation over the chain
in terms of its tail probabilities:
\[
\sum_{j=1}^{N}q_{jk}f_j
=f_1\sum_{j=1}^{N}q_{jk}+\sum_{\bar\jmath=2}^{N}\bigl(f_{\bar\jmath}-f_{\bar\jmath-1}\bigr)
\sum_{j=\bar\jmath}^{N}q_{jk} .
\]
Since $\sum_{j}q_{jj''}=\sum_jq_{jj'}=1$, the first terms are equal, and subtracting,
\[
\E\bigl[f(z')\mid z_{j''}\bigr]-\E\bigl[f(z')\mid z_{j'}\bigr]
=\sum_{\bar\jmath=2}^{N}\underbrace{\bigl(f_{\bar\jmath}-f_{\bar\jmath-1}\bigr)}_{\ge0}
\underbrace{\Bigl[\sum_{j=\bar\jmath}^{N}q_{jj''}-\sum_{j=\bar\jmath}^{N}q_{jj'}\Bigr]}_{\ge0\ \text{by hypothesis}}
\ \ge\ 0 .
\]

\emph{Necessity.}  Take the indicator $f(z)=\mathbf 1\{z\ge z_{\bar\jmath}\}$, which is
nondecreasing.  Then $\E[f(z')\mid z_k]=\sum_{j=\bar\jmath}^{N}q_{jk}$, and part 3 applied to
this $f$ gives precisely
\[
\sum_{j=\bar\jmath}^{N}q_{jj''}\ \ge\ \sum_{j=\bar\jmath}^{N}q_{jj'} \qquad\text{for }j''>j' .
\]
$\blacksquare$

\emph{What it means.}  The condition says that the conditional distribution of $z'$ given
$z_{j''}$ \emph{first-order stochastically dominates} the conditional distribution given
$z_{j'}$ whenever $j''>j'$:
\[
\boxed{\ \Prob\bigl[z'\ge z_{\bar\jmath}\mid z_{j''}\bigr]\ \ge\
\Prob\bigl[z'\ge z_{\bar\jmath}\mid z_{j'}\bigr]\quad\text{for all }\bar\jmath .\ }
\]
A Markov chain with this property is called \emph{monotone}.  Note how much stronger it is than
the requirement that the conditional \emph{mean} $\E[z'\mid z]$ be nondecreasing: the latter would
follow from taking $f(z)=z$ alone, whereas monotonicity requires the whole distribution to shift
right, which is exactly what is needed to make expectations of arbitrary nondecreasing functions
--- such as a value function --- inherit the ordering.  An i.i.d.\ chain satisfies it trivially
with equality; a chain in which a high state today made a very low state tomorrow likely would
not.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{16.2}{}
Fix $x(0)\in X$, $z(0)\in Z$ and a feasible plan
$\mathbf x\equiv\{\wt x[z^{t}]\}_{t=0}^{\infty}\in\Phi(x(0),z(0))$, and recall
\[
\ol U\bigl(\mathbf x\mid x(0),z(0)\bigr)
=\E\Bigl[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\beta^{t}U\bigl(\wt x[z^{t-1}],\wt x[z^{t}],z(t)\bigr)\Bigm|z(0)\Bigr] .
\]
Assumption 16.1 guarantees that this limit exists and is finite, which is what licenses the
manipulations below.

\emph{Separate the first term.}  At $t=0$ the history is $z^{0}=(z(0))$, so $\wt x[z^{0}]$ is
known once $z(0)$ is, and the date-zero payoff $U(x(0),\wt x[z^{0}],z(0))$ is deterministic
conditional on $z(0)$.  Hence
\[
\ol U\bigl(\mathbf x\mid x(0),z(0)\bigr)
=U\bigl(x(0),\wt x[z^{0}],z(0)\bigr)
+\beta\,\E\Bigl[\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\beta^{t-1}U\bigl(\wt x[z^{t-1}],\wt x[z^{t}],z(t)\bigr)
\Bigm|z(0)\Bigr] .
\]

\emph{Condition on $z(1)$.}  By the law of iterated expectations, conditioning first on the
finer information set that includes $z(1)$,
\[
\E\Bigl[\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\beta^{t-1}U(\cdot)\Bigm|z(0)\Bigr]
=\E\Bigl[\ \E\Bigl[\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\beta^{t-1}U(\cdot)\Bigm|z(1)\Bigr]\Bigm|z(0)\Bigr] ,
\]
where the inner expectation may be taken conditional on $z(1)$ alone --- rather than on the whole
history $z^{1}=(z(0),z(1))$ --- because $z$ is a Markov chain, so the distribution of
$(z(2),z(3),\dots)$ given $z^{1}$ depends only on $z(1)$.

\emph{Recognize the continuation value.}  Reindex the inner sum with $s=t-1$.  The plan from
date $1$ onward, $\{\wt x[z^{t}]\}_{t=1}^{\infty}$, is feasible from the state $\wt x[z^{0}]$
with shock $z(1)$, that is, it belongs to $\Phi(\wt x[z^{0}],z(1))$, and the inner expectation is
by definition $\ol U\bigl(\{\wt x[z^{t}]\}_{t=1}^{\infty}\mid\wt x[z^{0}],z(1)\bigr)$.  Therefore
\[
\boxed{\ \ol U\bigl(\mathbf x\mid x(0),z(0)\bigr)
=U\bigl(x(0),\wt x[z^{0}],z(0)\bigr)
+\beta\,\E\Bigl[\ol U\bigl(\{\wt x[z^{t}]\}_{t=1}^{\infty}\mid\wt x[z^{0}],z(1)\bigr)\Bigm|z(0)\Bigr]\ }
\]
$\blacksquare$

\emph{What the lemma is for.}  It is the bookkeeping identity that makes the Principle of
Optimality work: it says that the value of \emph{any} feasible plan --- optimal or not --- splits
into today's payoff plus the discounted expected value of its own continuation.  In the
deterministic case (\bk{Lemma 6.1}) this is almost a tautology; here two substantive facts are
being used, the law of iterated expectations and the Markov property, and Assumption 16.1 is what
rules out the one thing that could go wrong, namely an interchange of limit and expectation that
is not justified.  Section 16.2's proof of \bk{Theorem 16.1} appeals to the lemma at exactly the
point where it needs to compare $V^{*}$ with the one-period-ahead decomposition of a plan chosen
$\varepsilon$-optimally in each state.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{16.5}{}
By \bk{Theorem 16.3} the operator $T$ maps the space $\mathcal C$ of bounded continuous functions
on $X\times Z$ into itself and is a contraction, with unique fixed point $V$.  Let
\[
\mathcal C'\equiv\bigl\{W\in\mathcal C:\ W(\cdot,z)\ \text{nondecreasing for each }z\bigr\},
\qquad
\mathcal C''\equiv\bigl\{W\in\mathcal C':\ W(\cdot,z)\ \text{strictly increasing}\bigr\} .
\]
$\mathcal C'$ is a closed subset of $\mathcal C$ in the sup norm, since a uniform limit of
nondecreasing functions is nondecreasing.

\emph{$T$ maps $\mathcal C'$ into $\mathcal C''$.}  Take $W\in\mathcal C'$, fix $z$, and let
$x<x'$ in $X$.  By Assumption 16.4, $G(x,z)\subset G(x',z)$, so every $y$ feasible at $x$ is
feasible at $x'$ and
\[
(TW)(x',z)=\sup_{y\in G(x',z)}\Bigl\{U(x',y,z)+\beta\E\bigl[W(y,z')\mid z\bigr]\Bigr\}
\ \ge\ \sup_{y\in G(x,z)}\Bigl\{U(x',y,z)+\beta\E\bigl[W(y,z')\mid z\bigr]\Bigr\} .
\]
Also by Assumption 16.4, $U(\cdot,y,z)$ is \emph{strictly} increasing in its first $K$
arguments, so $U(x',y,z)>U(x,y,z)$ for every $y$; and the continuation term
$\beta\E[W(y,z')\mid z]$ does not involve $x$ at all.  Hence the last supremum strictly exceeds
\[
\sup_{y\in G(x,z)}\Bigl\{U(x,y,z)+\beta\E\bigl[W(y,z')\mid z\bigr]\Bigr\}=(TW)(x,z) ,
\]
the strictness surviving the supremum because the two objectives differ by an amount bounded
below on the compact set $G(x,z)$ (Assumption 16.2).  Therefore $TW\in\mathcal C''$.

\emph{Conclusion.}  Since $\mathcal C''\subset\mathcal C'$ and $\mathcal C'$ is closed, $T$ maps
the closed set $\mathcal C'$ into itself, so by the contraction mapping theorem its unique fixed
point lies in $\mathcal C'$.  But $T$ maps $\mathcal C'$ into the smaller set $\mathcal C''$, and
$V=TV$; hence $V\in\mathcal C''$, that is,
\[
\boxed{\ V(\cdot,z)\ \text{is strictly increasing for each }z\in Z . \ }
\]
$\blacksquare$

The argument is word for word that of \bk{Theorem 6.4} in the deterministic case.  Nothing is
added by the stochastic structure, for the simple reason that the expectation
$\E[W(y,z')\mid z]$ is an average of values at \emph{next} period's states and therefore does not
depend on today's $x$ except through the choice of $y$ --- which the monotonicity of $G$ already
handles.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{16.6}{}
Fix $z\in Z$ and a point $x'\in\Int X$ with $\pi(x',z)\in\Int G(x',z)$, and write
$y'\equiv\pi(x',z)$.  Since $G$ is continuous (Assumption 16.2) and $y'$ is interior to
$G(x',z)$, there is an open neighborhood $D\subset\Int X$ of $x'$ such that
\[
y'\in G(x,z)\qquad\text{for every }x\in D ,
\]
that is, the date-$t$ choice $y'$ remains feasible after a small perturbation of the state.

\emph{An auxiliary function.}  Define, for $x\in D$,
\[
W(x)\equiv U\bigl(x,y',z\bigr)+\beta\,\E\bigl[V(y',z')\mid z\bigr] .
\]
Three properties hold.
\begin{enumerate}
\item $W$ is concave on $D$ and continuously differentiable there, by Assumptions 16.3 and 16.5,
      with
      \[
      D_xW(x')=D_xU\bigl(x',y',z\bigr) ,
      \]
      the second term being a constant in $x$ --- this is the only place the stochastic structure
      appears, and it appears as a constant.
\item $W(x)\le V(x,z)$ for every $x\in D$: the plan ``choose $y'$ today and behave optimally
      thereafter'' is feasible at $x$ but need not be optimal, and $V$ is the value of the
      optimal plan.
\item $W(x')=V(x',z)$: at $x'$ the choice $y'=\pi(x',z)$ \emph{is} optimal, by the definition of
      the policy function and \bk{(16.8)}.
\end{enumerate}

\emph{Benveniste--Scheinkman.}  By \bk{Theorem 16.4}, $V(\cdot,z)$ is concave on $X$.  A concave
function that is bounded below on a neighborhood of an interior point by a function differentiable
at that point, with equality at the point itself, is differentiable there with the same gradient
(\bk{Theorem 6.6}'s lemma; the argument is that (2) and (3) force every subgradient of
$V(\cdot,z)$ at $x'$ to be a subgradient of $W$ at $x'$, and (1) makes the latter unique).
Therefore $V(\cdot,z)$ is continuously differentiable at $x'$ with
\[
\boxed{\ D_xV(x',z)=D_xW(x')=D_xU\bigl(x',\pi(x',z),z\bigr)\ }
\]
which is \bk{(16.10)}. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Why the envelope theorem survives uncertainty.}  The content of \bk{(16.10)} is that, at the
margin, a change in the state can be valued as if the policy were held fixed --- the indirect
effect through the optimal choice of $y$ is second order.  The stochastic version is no harder
than the deterministic one because the perturbation is applied to today's state only, and the
entire continuation value $\beta\E[V(y',z')\mid z]$ is frozen along with $y'$.  This result is
what makes the stochastic Euler equations of Section 16.3 usable: combining \bk{(16.10)} with the
first-order condition for $y$ eliminates the value function and leaves a relation between marginal
utilities at adjacent dates, which is \bk{(16.26)}.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{16.7}{}
Run the argument of Exercise 16.5 in the other argument.  Let
\begin{align*}
\mathcal D'&\equiv\bigl\{W\in\mathcal C:\ W(x,\cdot)\ \text{nondecreasing for each }x\bigr\},\\
\mathcal D''&\equiv\bigl\{W\in\mathcal D':\ W(x,\cdot)\ \text{strictly increasing for each }x\bigr\}.
\end{align*}
and note that $\mathcal D'$ is closed in the sup norm.

\emph{$T$ maps $\mathcal D'$ into $\mathcal D''$.}  Take $W\in\mathcal D'$, fix $x$, and let
$z<\wt z$ in $Z$.  Three comparisons apply, one for each part of \bk{Assumption 16.6}.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The feasible set expands.}  $G(x,z)\subset G(x,\wt z)$ by part 1, so the supremum in
      $(TW)(x,\wt z)$ is taken over a larger set.
\item \emph{The current payoff rises.}  $U(x,y,\wt z)>U(x,y,z)$ for every $y$, by part 2.
\item \emph{The continuation value rises.}  For each fixed $y$, the function
      $z'\mapsto W(y,z')$ is nondecreasing because $W\in\mathcal D'$; part 3 --- equivalently,
      by Exercise 16.1, the fact that the chain is monotone in the first-order stochastic
      dominance order --- then gives
      \[
      \E\bigl[W(y,z')\mid\wt z\bigr]\ \ge\ \E\bigl[W(y,z')\mid z\bigr] .
      \]
\end{enumerate}
Combining,
\begin{align*}
(TW)(x,\wt z)\ &\ge\ \sup_{y\in G(x,z)}\Bigl\{U(x,y,\wt z)
  +\beta\E\bigl[W(y,z')\mid\wt z\bigr]\Bigr\}\\
&>\ \sup_{y\in G(x,z)}\Bigl\{U(x,y,z)+\beta\E\bigl[W(y,z')\mid z\bigr]\Bigr\}
  \ =\ (TW)(x,z) ,
\end{align*}
the first inequality from (1) and the strict one from (2) together with (3).  Hence
$TW\in\mathcal D''$.

\emph{Conclusion.}  $T$ maps the closed set $\mathcal D'$ into itself, so its unique fixed point
$V$ lies in $\mathcal D'$; and since $T$ in fact maps $\mathcal D'$ into $\mathcal D''$ and
$V=TV$,
\[
\boxed{\ V(x,\cdot)\ \text{is strictly increasing for each }x\in X . \ }
\]
$\blacksquare$

\rmk{Part 3 of \bk{Assumption 16.6} is doing indispensable work, and it is the part with no
counterpart in Chapter 6.  Without it a higher $z$ today could raise the current payoff while
making low values of $z$ tomorrow more likely, and the continuation value would fall: the value
function need not be monotone in $z$ even though the flow payoff is.  Exercise 16.1 shows that
what rules this out is first-order stochastic dominance of the conditional distributions, not
merely a higher conditional mean --- precisely because what has to inherit the ordering is the
expectation of an arbitrary nondecreasing function, and only stochastic dominance delivers that
for all of them at once.}
\end{aex}


%% ========================================================================
\solchapter{17}{Stochastic Growth Models}

\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{0.86\textwidth}
\small
The twenty-three exercises of Chapter 17 left unsolved in the instructor's manual:
17.1--17.4, 17.6, 17.8--17.12, 17.14, 17.16, 17.17, 17.19--17.21, 17.23--17.29.  The manual
solves 17.5, 17.7, 17.13, 17.15, 17.18, 17.22 and 17.30.  The numerical parts are computed by
\texttt{code/ch17.py}, which solves the Brock--Mirman problem by the endogenous grid method;
Figure~\ref{fig:ch17} is its output.
\end{minipage}
\end{center}

\bigskip
\hrule
\bigskip

\solsection{17.1}{The Brock--Mirman Model}

\begin{aex}{17.1}{}
Write the problem in terms of \emph{cash on hand}
\[
x(k,z)\equiv f(k,z)+(1-\delta)k ,
\]
so that the first-order condition for $k'=\pi(k,z)$ is
\begin{equation}
u'\bigl(x(k,z)-k'\bigr)=\beta\,W_1(k',z),\qquad
W(k',z)\equiv\E\bigl[V(k',z')\mid z\bigr] .
\label{eq:foc}
\end{equation}
Both $u$ and $W(\cdot,z)$ are strictly concave (\bk{Assumption 3} and \bk{Proposition 17.1}),
so $u''<0$ and $W_{11}<0$ and the denominators below never vanish.

\emph{Monotonicity in $k$.}  Differentiating \eqref{eq:foc} with respect to $k$ at fixed $z$,
\[
u''\bigl(x_k-\pi_k\bigr)=\beta W_{11}\pi_k
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\pi_k=\frac{u''}{u''+\beta W_{11}}\,x_k\in(0,x_k),
\]
and therefore
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{\partial\pi_c}{\partial k}=x_k-\pi_k
=\frac{\beta W_{11}}{u''+\beta W_{11}}\,x_k>0\ }
\]
whenever $x_k=f'(k,z)+1-\delta>0$.  \emph{No extra condition is needed:} strict concavity of
$u$ and of $V$ in $k$, which \bk{Proposition 17.1} already delivers, makes the marginal
propensity to save out of cash on hand lie strictly between $0$ and $1$, so consumption and
next period's capital both absorb a strictly positive share of any increase in $k$.  This is
just the statement that consumption today and consumption tomorrow are both normal goods.

\emph{Monotonicity in $z$.}  Here $z$ enters twice --- through cash on hand and through the
conditional distribution of $z'$.  Differentiating \eqref{eq:foc} with respect to $z$,
\[
u''\bigl(f_z-\pi_z\bigr)=\beta W_{12}+\beta W_{11}\pi_z
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\pi_z=\frac{u''f_z-\beta W_{12}}{u''+\beta W_{11}} ,
\]
and subtracting from $f_z$,
\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial\pi_c}{\partial z}=f_z-\pi_z
=\frac{\beta\bigl(W_{11}f_z+W_{12}\bigr)}{u''+\beta W_{11}} .
\label{eq:cz}
\end{equation}
The denominator is negative, so
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{\partial\pi_c}{\partial z}>0
\iff W_{12}(\pi(k,z),z)<\bigl|W_{11}(\pi(k,z),z)\bigr|\,f_z(k,z) . \ }
\]
Two readable sufficient conditions follow.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{$W_{12}\le0$}, that is, a higher current shock does not raise the expected marginal
      value of capital.  Since
      $W_{12}(k',z)=\partial_z\E[V_k(k',z')\mid z]$ and
      $V_k(k,z)=u'(\pi_c(k,z))\bigl(f'(k,z)+1-\delta\bigr)$, this holds when
      \[
      V_{kz}=\underbrace{u''\,\frac{\partial\pi_c}{\partial z}\,(f'+1-\delta)}_{\le0\ \text{if }\pi_c\ \text{rises in }z}
      \ +\ \underbrace{u'f_{kz}}_{\ge0\ \text{if }f_{kz}\ge0}\ \le\ 0 ,
      \]
      so it is enough that the shock not be too strongly complementary with capital
      ($f_{kz}$ small), together with the monotone chain of \bk{Assumption 16.6}.
\item \emph{$z$ independently distributed across periods}, in which case
      $\E[V(k',z')\mid z]$ does not depend on $z$ at all, $W_{12}\equiv0$, and
      \eqref{eq:cz} gives $0<\partial\pi_c/\partial z<f_z$ unconditionally.  This also
      re-proves the $z$ part of \bk{Proposition 17.2}, since then
      $\pi_z=u''f_z/(u''+\beta W_{11})\in(0,f_z)$.
\end{enumerate}

\emph{Where it fails, and why.}  \texttt{code/ch17.py} solves the model with
$f(k,z)=zk^\alpha$, $\alpha=0.36$, $\beta=0.95$, $\delta=1$, CRRA utility of curvature
$\theta$, and a five-state monotone chain for $\log z$ with autocorrelation $0.95$ and
innovation standard deviation $0.30$.  The smallest slope of each policy over the ergodic
range is
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrrr}
\toprule
$\theta$ & $\min\partial\pi/\partial k$ & $\min\partial\pi_c/\partial k$ &
$\min\partial\pi/\partial z$ & $\min\partial\pi_c/\partial z$\\
\midrule
$0.10$ & $0.00000$ & $0.00166$ & $0.0682$ & $-0.0254$\\
$0.20$ & $0.00001$ & $0.00165$ & $0.0563$ & $-0.0016$\\
$0.35$ & $0.00004$ & $0.00162$ & $0.0454$ & $\phantom{-}0.0028$\\
$1.00$ & $0.00057$ & $0.00110$ & $0.0250$ & $\phantom{-}0.0481$\\
$5.00$ & $0.00164$ & $0.00003$ & $0.0161$ & $\phantom{-}0.0418$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
$\pi$ is increasing in both arguments throughout, as \bk{Proposition 17.2} asserts, and $\pi_c$
is increasing in $k$ at every $\theta$, as the argument above predicts.  Monotonicity in $z$
fails below $\theta=0.2565$, an elasticity of intertemporal substitution of $3.90$: when
substitution is that easy, a good shock is read mainly as an investment opportunity, and the
planner saves more than the whole of the extra output.  Formally, a small $\theta$ makes
$|u''|$ small relative to $W_{12}$, and \eqref{eq:cz} turns negative.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{17.2}{}
The book's timing is stated explicitly in Section 17.1: ``consumption and saving decisions at
time $t$ are made after observing $\ldots$ $z(t)$.''  Reversing it means that the date-$t$
decision is measurable with respect to $z^{t-1}$ only.  Since output $f(k(t),z(t))$ is not yet
known, the two choices cannot both be free: the planner commits to next period's capital
$k(t+1)=\wt k[z^{t-1}]$ and consumption is the residual,
\[
c(t)=f(k(t),z(t))+(1-\delta)k(t)-k(t+1) .
\]
This is the ``investment decided one period ahead'' timing familiar from time-to-build models.

\textbf{(a)}  With $z$ independent across periods there is nothing to condition on, so the
only state is $k$ and the problem is
\begin{equation}
V(k)=\max_{k'\in[0,\,\min_j f(k,z_j)+(1-\delta)k]}
\Bigl\{\wt u(k,k')+\beta V(k')\Bigr\},\qquad
\wt u(k,k')\equiv\E_z\bigl[u\bigl(f(k,z)+(1-\delta)k-k'\bigr)\bigr].
\label{eq:iid}
\end{equation}
Problem \eqref{eq:iid} contains no random variable and no expectation over the future: it is a
\emph{deterministic} dynamic program.  Hence $k(t+1)=\pi(k(t))$ is a deterministic sequence
converging monotonically to a unique steady state, exactly as in Chapter 6 --- all the
randomness has been pushed into consumption, which is the residual.

The reduction is to a certainty model \emph{with a modified production function} in the exact
sense that there is an $\wt f$ with
$\wt u(k,k')=u\bigl(\wt f(k)+(1-\delta)k-k'\bigr)$ for all $(k,k')$ if and only if $u$
displays constant absolute risk aversion (or $z$ is degenerate).  With
$u(c)=-e^{-ac}/a$,
\[
\E_z\bigl[u(y(k,z)-k')\bigr]=-\tfrac1a e^{ak'}\,\E_z\bigl[e^{-ay(k,z)}\bigr]
=u\bigl(\wt f(k)+(1-\delta)k-k'\bigr),
\]
\[
\boxed{\ \wt f(k)\equiv-\tfrac1a\log\E_z\bigl[e^{-af(k,z)}\bigr] . \ }
\]
The modified production function is the \emph{certainty equivalent} of output, and by Jensen's
inequality $\wt f(k)<\E_z[f(k,z)]$: risk shows up as a pure, capital-dependent productivity
loss.  For a general $u$ the reduction is to a deterministic problem with a modified
\emph{return function} $\wt u(k,k')$ rather than a modified production function.

\emph{Intuition.}  Under the book's timing the shock is an observable state, so the policy must
be indexed by it; here the shock arrives too late to be acted upon and carries no information
about the future, so the planner faces the identical decision problem every period.  Uncertainty
then affects behaviour only through the risk premium built into $\wt f$ --- it changes the
\emph{level} of the capital stock the economy settles at, not the fact that it settles.

\textbf{(b)}  If $z$ is a Markov chain, $z(t-1)$ forecasts $z(t)$, so the state is $(k,z_{-1})$
with $z_{-1}\equiv z(t-1)$:
\[
V(k,z_{-1})=\max_{k'}\Bigl\{\E\bigl[u(f(k,z)+(1-\delta)k-k')\mid z_{-1}\bigr]
+\beta\,\E\bigl[V(k',z)\mid z_{-1}\bigr]\Bigr\} .
\]
The equivalent of \bk{Proposition 17.1} holds verbatim, and by the same proof: the return
function $\E[u(f(k,z)+(1-\delta)k-k')\mid z_{-1}]$ is continuous, bounded on the compact set
$[0,\max\{k(0),\bar k\}]\times Z$, strictly concave in $(k,k')$ and strictly increasing in $k$,
and it is increasing in $z_{-1}$ because the chain is monotone; so
\bk{Assumptions 16.1--16.6} hold and \bk{Theorems 16.1--16.7} give a unique $V$, strictly
increasing in $k$ and in $z_{-1}$, strictly concave and differentiable in $k$, with a unique
policy $\pi(k,z_{-1})$.

Differentiating and using the envelope condition
$V_k(k,z_{-1})=\E[u'(c)(f'(k,z)+1-\delta)\mid z_{-1}]$ gives the modified Euler equation
\[
\boxed{\ \E_{t-1}\bigl[u'(c(t))\bigr]
=\beta\,\E_{t-1}\Bigl[\bigl(f'(k(t+1),z(t+1))+1-\delta\bigr)u'(c(t+1))\Bigr] . \ }
\]
Two differences from Section 17.1 stand out.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Marginal utility is inside a date-$(t-1)$ expectation.}  The intertemporal margin is
      no longer equated state by state, so the date-$t$ innovation in $z$ is not shared between
      consumption and saving: $\partial c(t)/\partial z(t)=f_z(k(t),z(t))$ exactly.  Consumption
      absorbs the entire shock, and is therefore \emph{more} volatile than in Section 17.1,
      while investment is smoother.
\item \emph{Investment responds to news with a one-period lag.}  $k(t+1)$ depends only on
      $z^{t-1}$, so a shock at $t$ moves capital only from $t+1$ onward.  This is precisely the
      device --- time to build --- that the Real Business Cycle literature adds to the model of
      Section 17.3 to generate a hump-shaped investment response.
\end{enumerate}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{17.3}{}
\emph{Law of motion.}  With a constant saving rate out of output the capital-labor ratio obeys
\[
k(t+1)=G\bigl(k(t),z(t)\bigr)\equiv s\,f\bigl(k(t),z(t)\bigr)+(1-\delta)k(t) ,
\]
a Markov process on $\R_+$ driven by the chain $z$ --- the stochastic Solow model.  $G$ is
strictly increasing in $k$ and in $z$, strictly concave in $k$, and satisfies
$G(0,z)=0$ with $\lim_{k\to0}G_k=\infty$ and $\lim_{k\to\infty}G_k=1-\delta<1$ by
\bk{Assumptions 1 and 2}.  Hence:
\begin{enumerate}
\item define $\underline k$ and $\ol k$ by $sf(\underline k,z_1)=\delta\underline k$ and
      $sf(\ol k,z_N)=\delta\ol k$.  Both exist and are unique by the Inada conditions, and
      $\underline k<\ol k$.  The interval $[\underline k,\ol k]$ is forward invariant:
      $G(k,z_1)\ge k$ for $k\le\underline k$ and $G(k,z_N)\le k$ for $k\ge\ol k$;
\item from any $k(0)>0$ the process enters $[\underline k,\ol k]$ in finite time and stays;
\item on that compact interval the process is monotone and satisfies a mixing condition, so it
      has a \emph{unique invariant distribution} $\Psi$ and is ergodic:
      $k(t)\Rightarrow\Psi$ and time averages converge to $\Psi$-averages.
\end{enumerate}
So, exactly as in Section 17.1, a ``steady state'' is a distribution, not a number.

\emph{How it differs.}  The one and only difference is that the saving rate is a constant rather
than the function $\pi(k,z)/[f(k,z)+(1-\delta)k]$, and that has four consequences.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{No consumption smoothing across states.}  Here $c(t)=(1-s)f(k(t),z(t))$, so
      $\log c$ and $\log y$ move one for one and $\mathrm{sd}(\log c)/\mathrm{sd}(\log y)=1$
      exactly.  The optimal rule uses investment as the buffer.  In the calibration of
      \texttt{code/ch17.py} ($\alpha=0.36$, $\beta=0.95$, $\delta=0.08$, $\theta=2$, $\rho=0.9$,
      $\sigma=0.12$) the optimal path has an investment rate averaging $0.254$ but ranging over
      $[-0.475,0.439]$, and
      \[
      \frac{\mathrm{sd}(\log c)}{\mathrm{sd}(\log y)}=0.853\ \text{(optimal)}
      \qquad\text{against}\qquad 1.000\ \text{(Solow, same average rate)} ,
      \]
      while the capital stock is correspondingly \emph{more} volatile under the optimal rule
      ($\mathrm{sd}(\log k)=0.629$ against $0.359$).  The stochastic Solow model therefore
      cannot reproduce the most robust business-cycle fact there is, that consumption is
      smoother than output.
\item \emph{No response of saving to the state.}  The optimal rate varies with $k$ and with $z$;
      the constant rate cannot, so the model has no mechanism by which news about future
      productivity affects today's investment.
\item \emph{Possible dynamic inefficiency.}  Nothing prevents $s$ from exceeding the golden-rule
      rate, in which case the economy over-accumulates in every state; the optimal path never
      does.
\item \emph{One exact coincidence.}  In Example 17.1 --- $u=\log$, $f=zk^\alpha$, $\delta=1$ ---
      the optimal policy \emph{is} a constant saving rate, $s=\alpha\beta$.  The numerical
      solution reproduces this to machine precision (the savings rate varies by
      $3.3\times10^{-16}$ across the whole grid).  So the Solow rule is not merely a convenient
      approximation there; it is exactly optimal, which is why Example 17.1 is the one case in
      which the two models cannot be told apart.
\end{enumerate}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{17.4}{}
\textbf{(a)}  From the derivation in Exercise 17.1,
\[
\pi_k=\frac{u''}{u''+\beta W_{11}}\,\bigl(f'(k,z)+1-\delta\bigr),
\qquad
\pi_z=\frac{u''f_z-\beta W_{12}}{u''+\beta W_{11}} .
\]
Both denominators are negative.  Therefore
\begin{itemize}
\item $\pi_k>0$ whenever $f'(k,z)+1-\delta>0$, which holds for all $k>0$ under
      \bk{Assumptions 1--2}.  Strict concavity of $u$ and of $V(\cdot,z)$ is all that is
      required, and \bk{Proposition 17.1} supplies it;
\item $\pi_z>0$ iff $u''f_z<\beta W_{12}$, for which
      \[
      \boxed{\ W_{12}(k',z)=\partial_z\E\bigl[V_k(k',z')\mid z\bigr]\ \ge\ 0\ }
      \]
      is sufficient, since $u''f_z<0$.  This says that a higher shock today does not lower the
      expected marginal value of capital tomorrow, and it holds when $V_{kz}\ge0$ together
      with the monotone chain of \bk{Assumption 16.6}.  Since
      $V_{kz}=u''\,\partial_z\pi_c\,(f'+1-\delta)+u'f_{kz}$, a clean pair of primitive
      conditions is
      \[
      f_{kz}\ \ge\ 0\quad\text{(the shock raises the marginal product of capital)}
      \]
      together with the conditions of Exercise 17.1 --- and when $z$ is independent across
      periods, $W_{12}\equiv0$ and $\pi_z=u''f_z/(u''+\beta W_{11})\in(0,f_z)$
      unconditionally, so \emph{both} $\pi$ and $\pi_c$ are then strictly increasing in $z$.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{(b)}  Suppose the chain is irreducible on $Z=\{z_1,\dots,z_N\}$ with $N\ge2$, and
suppose, for a contradiction, that $k(t)\to\bar k$ with probability one along some sample path,
with $\bar k>0$.  Irreducibility makes every state recurrent, so for each $j$ the event
$\{z(t)=z_j\}$ occurs infinitely often almost surely.  Along the subsequence of dates at which
$z(t)=z_j$,
\[
k(t+1)=\pi\bigl(k(t),z_j\bigr)\longrightarrow\pi(\bar k,z_j)
\]
by continuity of $\pi$; but $k(t+1)\to\bar k$ as well, so $\pi(\bar k,z_j)=\bar k$ for
\emph{every} $j$.  This contradicts the strict monotonicity of $\pi$ in $z$ established in
part (a), which gives $\pi(\bar k,z_1)<\pi(\bar k,z_2)$.  Hence no such $\bar k$ exists unless
$N=1$. $\blacksquare$

What replaces convergence is convergence of the \emph{distribution} of $k(t)$ to the unique
invariant distribution.  Its dispersion is proportional to that of $z$ and does not shrink.  In
Example 17.1 this can be computed exactly: taking logs of $k(t+1)=\alpha\beta z(t)k(t)^\alpha$,
\[
\log k(t+1)=\log(\alpha\beta)+\log z(t)+\alpha\log k(t) ,
\]
so with $\log z$ an $\mathrm{AR}(1)$ of autocorrelation $\rho$,
\[
\frac{\mathrm{sd}(\log k)}{\mathrm{sd}(\log z)}
=\sqrt{\frac{1+\alpha\rho}{(1-\alpha^2)(1-\alpha\rho)}}\ ,
\]
which equals $1.5001$ at $\alpha=0.36$, $\rho=0.9$.  Simulating the model over $120{,}000$
periods for four widths of the support of $z$ gives
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrr}
\toprule
$\mathrm{sd}(\log z)$ & $\mathrm{sd}(\log k)$ & range of $\log k$ & ratio\\
\midrule
$1.1647$ & $1.75521$ & $8.603$ & $1.5070$\\
$0.5824$ & $0.88074$ & $4.302$ & $1.5124$\\
$0.1456$ & $0.22096$ & $1.075$ & $1.5177$\\
$0.0146$ & $0.02205$ & $0.108$ & $1.5146$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The ratio is constant, so the dispersion of $k$ vanishes only as that of $z$ does --- which is
part (b) restated quantitatively.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{17.6}{}
Let the transition kernel be $\mathcal Q(z,\dd z')$ on $(0,\infty)$, and take the same guess
$\pi(k,z)=B_1zk^\alpha$.  With $\delta=1$ and $u=\log$, the stochastic Euler equation
\bk{(17.5)} reads
\[
\frac{1}{zk^\alpha-\pi(k,z)}
=\beta\int\frac{f'(\pi(k,z),z')}{z'\pi(k,z)^\alpha-\pi\bigl(\pi(k,z),z'\bigr)}\,
\mathcal Q(z,\dd z') .
\]
Write $P\equiv\pi(k,z)=B_1zk^\alpha$.  Then
\[
f'(P,z')=\alpha z'P^{\alpha-1},\qquad
c'=z'P^{\alpha}-B_1z'P^{\alpha}=(1-B_1)z'P^{\alpha} ,
\]
so the integrand is
\[
\frac{\alpha z'P^{\alpha-1}}{(1-B_1)z'P^{\alpha}}=\frac{\alpha}{(1-B_1)P} ,
\]
which \emph{does not involve $z'$ at all}.  Every feature of the conditional distribution
therefore disappears, and only $\int\mathcal Q(z,\dd z')=1$ is used:
\[
\frac{1}{(1-B_1)zk^\alpha}=\frac{\beta\alpha}{(1-B_1)B_1zk^\alpha}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\boxed{\ B_1=\alpha\beta . \ }
\]
The verification of $B_0=0$ is unchanged, since the same cancellation makes the left-hand side
of \bk{(17.10)} proportional to $z$ for any kernel.

\rmk{The cancellation is not an accident of the chain: it needs neither the Markov property
nor even independence, only that $z'>0$ almost surely and that the relevant expectations exist.
Its source is that $\delta=1$, $u=\log$ and Cobb--Douglas production together make the
\emph{gross return per unit of consumption}, $f'(k',z')/c'$, a deterministic function of $k'$
alone: the shock multiplies the marginal product and the level of consumption by the same
factor.  Equivalently, with unit elasticity of intertemporal substitution the income and
substitution effects of a change in the rate of return cancel, so the saving rate is
$\alpha\beta$ whatever the distribution of returns.  This is the dynamic counterpart of the
myopic portfolio rule of logarithmic investors.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{17.8}{}
Keep $u=\log$, $f(k,z)=zk^\alpha$, and try the same family of candidates
$\pi(k,z)=B_0+B_1zk^\alpha$, writing $P\equiv\pi(k,z)$.  The Euler equation is
\[
\frac1c=\beta\,\E\Bigl[\frac{\alpha z'P^{\alpha-1}+1-\delta}{c'}\,\Bigm|\,z\Bigr],
\qquad
c'=z'P^{\alpha}+(1-\delta)P-\pi(P,z')
=(1-B_1)z'P^{\alpha}+(1-\delta)P-B_0 .
\]
Numerator and denominator are now \emph{affine} in $z'$, with nonzero intercepts $1-\delta$ and
$(1-\delta)P-B_0$.  For the conditional distribution of $z'$ to drop out --- which it must, since
the left-hand side does not depend on it and the guess makes $P$ a function of $(k,z)$ only ---
the two affine functions must be proportional:
\[
\frac{\alpha P^{\alpha-1}}{(1-B_1)P^{\alpha}}=\frac{1-\delta}{(1-\delta)P-B_0}
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\alpha\bigl[(1-\delta)P-B_0\bigr]=(1-\delta)(1-B_1)P ,
\]
that is
\[
(1-\delta)\bigl(\alpha-1+B_1\bigr)P=\alpha B_0 .
\]
$P$ varies with $k$, so this can hold identically only if both sides vanish; with $\delta<1$
that forces
\[
B_0=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad B_1=1-\alpha .
\]
Substituting this unique candidate back, the ratio collapses to
\[
\frac{\alpha z'P^{\alpha-1}+1-\delta}{\alpha z'P^{\alpha}+(1-\delta)P}=\frac1P ,
\]
so the Euler equation becomes $1/c=\beta/P$, that is $P=\beta c$.  With $B_1=1-\alpha$,
\[
c=zk^\alpha+(1-\delta)k-(1-\alpha)zk^\alpha=\alpha zk^\alpha+(1-\delta)k ,
\]
and $P=\beta c$ reads
\[
(1-\alpha)zk^\alpha=\alpha\beta\,zk^\alpha+\beta(1-\delta)k \qquad\text{for all }k,z .
\]
Matching the two powers of $k$ requires simultaneously $1-\alpha=\alpha\beta$ \emph{and}
$\beta(1-\delta)=0$.  The second is impossible for $\beta>0$ and $\delta<1$.  Hence
\[
\boxed{\ \text{no policy of the form }B_0+B_1zk^\alpha\text{ solves the Euler equation when }
\delta<1 . \ }
\]
$\blacksquare$

\emph{What breaks.}  At $\delta=1$ the gross return $\alpha z'k'^{\alpha-1}$ is proportional to
$z'$ and so is consumption, and the shock cancels; at $\delta<1$ the undepreciated capital adds
a constant $1-\delta$ to the return but only $(1-\delta)P$ to resources, and the two affine
functions of $z'$ are no longer proportional.  No amount of re-guessing repairs this, because
the argument above exhausts the family.

Numerically, the savings rate $\pi(k,z)/[f(k,z)+(1-\delta)k]$ is exactly the constant
$\alpha\beta$ when $\delta=1$ and varies with $k$ otherwise:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrr}
\toprule
$\delta$ & smallest & largest & spread\\
\midrule
$1.0$ & $0.342000$ & $0.342000$ & $3.3\times10^{-16}$\\
$0.9$ & $0.352370$ & $0.451320$ & $9.9\times10^{-2}$\\
$0.5$ & $0.412445$ & $0.759579$ & $3.5\times10^{-1}$\\
$0.2$ & $0.507572$ & $0.878032$ & $3.7\times10^{-1}$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The $\delta=1$ row is machine precision --- the endogenous grid method recovers $\alpha\beta$
exactly --- so the spread in the other rows is signal, not error.  The left panel of
Figure~\ref{fig:ch17} plots these savings rates.
\end{aex}

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\includegraphics{figures/ch17_stochastic_growth.pdf}
\caption{Left: the optimal savings rate $\pi/[f+(1-\delta)k]$ at the median shock, flat at
$\alpha\beta$ when $\delta=1$ and sloped otherwise (Exercise 17.8).  Right: the conditional
variance $V_n$ of TFP against the measure of open sectors, peaking at $Q/(2Q-q)$
(Exercise 17.28).}
\label{fig:ch17}
\end{figure}

\solsection{17.2}{Equilibrium Growth under Uncertainty}

\begin{aex}{17.9}{}
\emph{The commodity space.}  Index commodities by a date $t\in\N$ and a history
$z^t\in Z^{t+1}$; since $Z$ is finite, the index set $\mathcal I\equiv\{(t,z^t)\}$ is countable.
A commodity bundle is a triple of sequences
$\bigl(c[z^t],\,\ell[z^t],\,k[z^t]\bigr)_{(t,z^t)\in\mathcal I}$, and the commodity space is
\[
L\equiv\Bigl\{x\in\R^{\mathcal I}:\ \|x\|\equiv\sup_{t,z^t}\bigl|x[z^t]\bigr|<\infty\Bigr\}
=\ell^\infty(\mathcal I),
\]
a normed vector space.  Boundedness is not an extra assumption: the proof of
\bk{Proposition 17.1} shows that every feasible path stays in
$[0,\max\{k(0),\bar k\}]$, hence so do output and consumption.

\emph{The planner's problem.}  Maximize
\[
U\bigl((c[z^t])\bigr)=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\beta^t\sum_{z^t}q[z^t\mid z^0]\,u\bigl(c[z^t]\bigr)
\]
over $(c,\ell,k)\in L$ subject to
\[
c[z^t]+k[z^t]\le f\bigl(k[z^{t-1}],z(t)\bigr)+(1-\delta)k[z^{t-1}],\qquad
\ell[z^t]=1,\qquad c[z^t],k[z^t]\ge0 ,
\]
for every $(t,z^t)$, with $k[z^{-1}]=k(0)$ given.

\emph{Verification of the hypotheses of \bk{Theorem 5.7}.}
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Consumption sets are convex.}  $X=\{x\in L: x\ge0\}$ is a convex cone.
\item \emph{Preferences are convex and continuous.}  $u$ is concave, so each term of $U$ is
      concave and the sum of concave functions is concave; $\beta<1$ and the boundedness of $c$
      make the series converge uniformly, so $U$ is continuous in the sup norm.  Convexity of
      preferences is then immediate.
\item \emph{Preferences are locally nonsatiated.}  $u$ is strictly increasing, so raising
      $c[z^t]$ at any single $(t,z^t)$ by $\varepsilon>0$ raises $U$ while moving the bundle by
      $\varepsilon$ in the sup norm.
\item \emph{The aggregate production set is convex.}  Write
      \[
      Y=\Bigl\{(y,\ell,k)\in L^3:\ y[z^t]\le F\bigl(k[z^{t-1}],\ell[z^t],z(t)\bigr)
      +(1-\delta)k[z^{t-1}]\ \ \forall (t,z^t)\Bigr\} .
      \]
      $F(\cdot,\cdot,z)$ is concave (\bk{Assumption 1}) for each $z$, so each constraint defines
      a convex set, and $Y$, an intersection of convex sets, is convex.  It contains the origin
      and displays free disposal.
\item \emph{The allocation is Pareto optimal.}  There is one (representative) household, so the
      solution of the planner's problem is Pareto optimal by construction.
\item \emph{Nonempty interior / properness.}  \bk{Theorem 5.7} requires that the set of
      aggregate bundles preferred to the optimum have an interior point in $L$, which holds
      here: the bundle that adds a constant $\varepsilon>0$ to consumption at every node is
      preferred and has a sup-norm neighbourhood of preferred bundles.
\end{enumerate}
By \bk{Theorem 5.7} there exist prices $\bigl(p_0[z^t],w_0[z^t],R_0[z^t]\bigr)\ge0$, not all
zero, such that the optimal allocation minimizes cost over the preferred set and maximizes
profits over $Y$.  Comparing the supporting prices with the planner's first-order conditions
identifies them as
\[
p_0[z^t]=\frac{\beta^tq[z^t\mid z^0]u'(c[z^t])}{\lambda},
\]
which is \bk{(17.13)}, and then \bk{(17.15)} and \bk{(17.19)} for $w_0$ and $R_0$.

\emph{From quasi-equilibrium to equilibrium.}  \bk{Theorem 5.7} delivers a price
quasi-equilibrium; to conclude that the household is \emph{maximizing} rather than merely
cost-minimizing one needs a cheaper point, that is, household wealth strictly above the
infimum of expenditure.  Here wealth is
$\sum_t\sum_{z^t}w_0[z^t]+R_0[z(0)]k(0)>0$, because the Inada conditions give
$f(k,z)-kf'(k,z)>0$ and hence $w_0[z^t]>0$, while $c\equiv0$ costs zero.  So the quasi-equilibrium
is a competitive equilibrium.  Finally, $\sum_t\sum_{z^t}p_0[z^t]c[z^t]<\infty$ because
\bk{(17.13)} and the transversality condition \bk{(17.7)} make the terms decline
geometrically.  \bk{Proposition 17.3} follows.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{17.10}{}
\textbf{(a)}  Let $(z,b)$ be the exogenous state, itself a Markov chain on the finite set
$Z\times B$ with transition probabilities $q[(z',b')\mid(z,b)]$; $z$ and $b$ may be correlated.
Nothing in Chapter 16 requires the exogenous state to be scalar, so the recursive problem is
\[
V(k,z,b)=\max_{k'\in[0,f(k,z)+(1-\delta)k]}
\Bigl\{u\bigl(f(k,z)+(1-\delta)k-k',\,b\bigr)
+\beta\,\E\bigl[V(k',z',b')\mid z,b\bigr]\Bigr\} .
\]
Assume $u(\cdot,b)$ satisfies \bk{Assumption 3} for each $b\in B$ and $u(c,\cdot)$ is bounded.
Then \bk{Assumptions 16.1--16.5} hold exactly as in \bk{Proposition 17.1}, with the compact set
$[0,\max\{k(0),\bar k\}]$ constructed from $z_N$, so $V$ is unique, strictly concave and
differentiable in $k$, and there is a unique policy $\pi(k,z,b)$.  (Monotonicity in $b$ requires
in addition that the chain be monotone in $b$ and $u$ increasing in $b$, which is not needed for
the Euler equation.)

The first-order and envelope conditions,
\[
u_c\bigl(\pi_c(k,z,b),b\bigr)=\beta\,\E\bigl[V_k(k',z',b')\mid z,b\bigr],\qquad
V_k(k,z,b)=u_c\bigl(\pi_c(k,z,b),b\bigr)\bigl(f'(k,z)+1-\delta\bigr),
\]
combine into the \textbf{modified stochastic Euler equation}
\[
\boxed{\ u_c\bigl(c(t),b(t)\bigr)
=\beta\,\E_t\Bigl[p(t+1)\,u_c\bigl(c(t+1),b(t+1)\bigr)\Bigr]\ }
\]
with $p(t+1)=f'(k(t+1),z(t+1))+1-\delta$ as in \bk{(17.6)}.  The difference from \bk{(17.6)} is
that the marginal utility on the right is evaluated at a \emph{random} preference state.  Writing
$u(c,b)=b\,c^{1-\theta}/(1-\theta)$ makes the point sharply:
\[
c(t)^{-\theta}=\beta\,\E_t\Bigl[p(t+1)\,\frac{b(t+1)}{b(t)}\,c(t+1)^{-\theta}\Bigr] ,
\]
so a preference shock enters exactly as a return shock would.  If $b(t+1)/b(t)$ is positively
correlated with $p(t+1)$, the covariance term raises the effective return and depresses current
consumption; consumption growth is now driven by the joint distribution of technology and taste,
and an econometrician who ignored $b$ would read the taste shocks as time-varying discounting.

\textbf{(b)}  Index commodities by histories of the \emph{pair} $(z,b)$; the index set is still
countable and the commodity space is again $\ell^\infty$.  Every hypothesis checked in
Exercise 17.9 survives:
\begin{itemize}
\item the objective $\sum_t\beta^t\sum_{(z,b)^t}q[\,\cdot\,]u(c[\,\cdot\,],b(t))$ is a
      probability-weighted sum of functions each concave and strictly increasing in the
      corresponding coordinate of $c$, hence concave, continuous and locally nonsatiated ---
      the preference shock affects only the weights and the shape of each term, not concavity;
\item the production set is exactly as before, since $b$ does not enter technology, so it is
      unchanged and convex;
\item wealth is strictly positive for the same reason.
\end{itemize}
\bk{Theorem 5.7} therefore applies, and the supporting prices are again
\[
p_0\bigl[(z,b)^t\bigr]
=\beta^t\,q\bigl[(z,b)^t\mid(z,b)^0\bigr]\,u_c\bigl(c[(z,b)^t],b(t)\bigr)\big/\lambda .
\]
Note that the state-contingent prices now vary with $b$ even at unchanged consumption: with
complete markets, taste shocks are priced.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{17.11}{}
The number of multipliers equals the number of constraints, and the number of constraints is
determined by the set of markets.

In Section 16.5.1 the household trades a \emph{single riskless bond}.  It cannot move resources
between two histories of the same length, so its budget must balance separately along each
history: there is one constraint
\[
c[y^t]+a[y^t]\le Ra[y^{t-1}]+y(t)\qquad\text{for every }y^t ,
\]
and hence one multiplier $\wt\lambda[y^t]$ per node.  Those multipliers are genuinely distinct
objects: $\wt\lambda[y^t]/\wt\lambda[\hat y^t]$ is the household's \emph{personal} valuation of a
unit of the good in history $y^t$ relative to $\hat y^t$, and it is not equated across
households because no market exists in which to trade the difference away.

In Section 17.2.1 a claim to the final good after \emph{any} history is a traded commodity with
a price $p_0[z^t]$ quoted at date $0$.  A surplus in one history can be sold and the proceeds
spent in another, so only the single aggregate constraint \bk{(17.11)} restricts the household,
and there is a single multiplier $\lambda$ --- the marginal utility of date-$0$ wealth.  The
node-by-node shadow prices have not disappeared; they have been decomposed, since \bk{(17.13)}
gives
\[
\underbrace{\beta^tq[z^t\mid z^0]u'(c[z^t])}_{\text{shadow value of goods at }z^t}
=\lambda\,p_0[z^t] ,
\]
so the whole family $\{\wt\lambda[z^t]\}$ collapses to one free scalar $\lambda$ multiplied by
the \emph{market} prices $p_0[z^t]$.

The general principle: a multiplier is needed for each budget constraint that cannot be relaxed
by trade.  Completing markets replaces $|Z|^{t+1}$ constraints per date with one, and the
information that the household's personal valuations must agree with market prices is exactly the
content of that collapse.  It is also why the incomplete-markets model of Section 17.4 does not
admit a representative household: there the $\wt\lambda$'s differ across households, and no
single set of weights aggregates them.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{17.12}{}
\emph{The firm.}  The representative firm now hires capital and labour period by period and owns
nothing, so its date-$0$ value is
\[
\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\sum_{z^t}\Bigl\{p_0[z^t]F\bigl(K^e[z^t],L[z^t],z(t)\bigr)
-\wt R_0[z^t]K^e[z^t]-w_0[z^t]L[z^t]\Bigr\} .
\]
Profit maximization and constant returns give, in per capita terms,
\begin{equation}
p_0[z^t]\,f'\bigl(k^e[z^t],z(t)\bigr)=\wt R_0[z^t],\qquad
p_0[z^t]\bigl(f-k^ef'\bigr)=w_0[z^t] .
\label{eq:rent}
\end{equation}
Note that $1-\delta$ has vanished from the first condition: the firm no longer owns the
undepreciated capital.

\emph{The household.}  It now owns the capital, rents it out, and keeps the $(1-\delta)$
fraction that survives.  Its lifetime budget constraint is
\[
\sum_{t}\sum_{z^t}p_0[z^t]c[z^t]
\le\sum_{t}\sum_{z^t}w_0[z^t]
+\sum_{t}\sum_{z^t}\Bigl\{\wt R_0[z^t]+(1-\delta)p_0[z^t]\Bigr\}k[z^{t-1}]
-\sum_{t}\sum_{z^t}p_0[z^t]k[z^t]+\Xi ,
\]
where $\Xi$ collects the date-$0$ terms.  The no-arbitrage condition governing the purchase of
one unit of the final good at $z^t$ to be rented out at $t+1$ is
\begin{equation}
p_0[z^t]=\sum_{z(t+1)\in Z}\Bigl\{\wt R_0[z^{t+1}]+(1-\delta)\,p_0[z^{t+1}]\Bigr\} .
\label{eq:rentarb}
\end{equation}

\emph{Equivalence.}  Compare with \bk{(17.15)} and \bk{(17.19)} of the purchase formulation.
Those read $R_0[z^t]=p_0[z^t](f'+1-\delta)$ and $p_0[z^t]=\sum_{z(t+1)}R_0[z^{t+1}]$.  Define
\[
\boxed{\ R_0[z^t]\equiv\wt R_0[z^t]+(1-\delta)\,p_0[z^t] . \ }
\]
Then \eqref{eq:rent} becomes exactly \bk{(17.15)} and \eqref{eq:rentarb} becomes exactly
\bk{(17.19)}.  The household's first-order conditions are unchanged, since only the total
$R_0$ enters its wealth.  Every equilibrium of one formulation is therefore an equilibrium of
the other under this change of variables, with identical allocations
$\{c[z^t],k[z^t]\}$ and identical prices $p_0$ and $w_0$.

\emph{Why.}  The change of variables is a statement about spanning.  Buying a unit of capital at
$z^t$ is the same portfolio as renting it for one period and simultaneously holding a claim to
the $1-\delta$ units of \emph{goods} that survive into every successor history $z^{t+1}$.  With
complete markets those claims are traded at the prices $p_0[z^{t+1}]$, so the two securities
have the same payoff in every state and must have the same price; no allocation can depend on
which one is written down.  The decomposition would matter only if the contingent claims on
undepreciated capital were \emph{not} traded --- which is exactly what happens in the incomplete
markets economies of Sections 17.4 and 17.6.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{17.14}{}
The household maximizes
\[
\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\beta^t\sum_{z^t}q\bigl[z^t\mid z^0\bigr]u\bigl(c[z^t]\bigr)
\]
subject to one flow constraint per node,
\begin{equation}
c[z^t]+\sum_{z(t+1)\in Z}\ol p\bigl[z(t+1)\mid z^t\bigr]\,a\bigl[(z^t,z(t+1))\bigr]
\le w[z^t]+a[z^t],
\label{eq:flow}
\end{equation}
together with the no-Ponzi condition
$\lim_{t\to\infty}\sum_{z^t}\bigl(\prod_{s\le t}\ol p\bigr)a[z^t]\ge0$ and $a[z^0]$ given.
Attach the multiplier $\mu[z^t]\ge0$ to \eqref{eq:flow}.  The first-order conditions are
\[
c[z^t]:\quad \beta^tq\bigl[z^t\mid z^0\bigr]u'\bigl(c[z^t]\bigr)=\mu[z^t],
\qquad
a[z^{t+1}]:\quad \mu[z^t]\,\ol p\bigl[z(t+1)\mid z^t\bigr]=\mu[z^{t+1}] .
\]
Eliminating the multipliers and using $q[z^{t+1}\mid z^0]=q[z(t+1)\mid z(t)]\,q[z^t\mid z^0]$
gives the \textbf{pricing kernel}
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\ \ol p\bigl[z(t+1)\mid z^t\bigr]
=\beta\,q\bigl[z(t+1)\mid z(t)\bigr]\,
\frac{u'\bigl(c[z^{t+1}]\bigr)}{u'\bigl(c[z^t]\bigr)} . \ }
\label{eq:kernel}
\end{equation}
Each one-period Arrow security is priced by the discounted marginal rate of substitution
between the node at which it is bought and the node at which it pays, times the probability of
that node.

Now impose market clearing.  Capital market clearing \bk{(17.26)} is
$a[(z^t,z(t+1))]=R[z(t+1)\mid z(t)]\,k[z^t]$; substituting into \eqref{eq:flow} and comparing
with the goods constraint $c[z^t]+k[z^t]=w[z^t]+a[z^t]$ gives the no-arbitrage condition
$\sum_{z(t+1)}\ol p\,R=1$, which is \bk{(17.27)} (see Exercise 17.16).  Multiplying
\eqref{eq:kernel} by $R[z(t+1)\mid z(t)]$ and summing over $z(t+1)$ therefore yields
\[
u'\bigl(c[z^t]\bigr)
=\beta\sum_{z(t+1)}q\bigl[z(t+1)\mid z(t)\bigr]R\bigl[z(t+1)\mid z(t)\bigr]
u'\bigl(c[z^{t+1}]\bigr)
=\beta\,\E\bigl[R\,u'(c(t+1))\mid z^t\bigr] ,
\]
which is \bk{(17.6)} once $R=f'+1-\delta$ from \bk{(17.15)}.  The no-Ponzi condition, holding
with equality by local nonsatiation, delivers the transversality condition \bk{(17.7)} by the
argument of Section 17.2.1.  Since \bk{Theorem 16.8} makes \bk{(17.6)}--\bk{(17.7)} sufficient,
the equilibrium path coincides with the optimal growth path.

\rmk{Comparing \eqref{eq:kernel} with \bk{(17.13)} shows the exact relation between the two
market structures: $\ol p[z(t+1)\mid z^t]=p_0[z^{t+1}]/p_0[z^t]$.  One-period Arrow securities
traded sequentially replicate the entire date-$0$ set of contingent claims by compounding, which
is the content of Section 5.8 --- and the reason the model needs only $N$ securities per node
rather than the whole infinite set.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{17.16}{}
Fix the current state $z$ and let $k[z]$ be the household's savings, chosen before $z'$ is
realized.  Two accounts of the same transaction must agree.

\emph{In goods.}  The household's resources are $w+a$, and they are split between consumption
and one unit of the final good per unit of capital carried forward:
\begin{equation}
c+k[z]=w+a .
\label{eq:goods}
\end{equation}
There is nothing contingent here: a unit of the final good saved becomes a unit of capital,
whatever happens next.

\emph{In securities.}  The same plan is implemented by buying, for each $z'\in Z$, a claim
paying $a'[z'\mid z]$ units of the final good in state $z'$.  Capital market clearing
\bk{(17.26)} says these claims are exactly what the carried capital delivers,
\[
a'[z'\mid z]=R[z'\mid z]\,k[z] ,
\]
and the flow budget constraint charges the household the market value of the portfolio:
\begin{equation}
c+\sum_{z'\in Z}\ol p[z'\mid z]\,a'[z'\mid z]
=c+k[z]\sum_{z'\in Z}\ol p[z'\mid z]R[z'\mid z]=w+a .
\label{eq:secs}
\end{equation}

Subtracting \eqref{eq:goods} from \eqref{eq:secs},
\[
k[z]\Bigl(\sum_{z'\in Z}\ol p[z'\mid z]R[z'\mid z]-1\Bigr)=0 ,
\]
so for any $k[z]>0$,
\[
\boxed{\ \sum_{z'\in Z}\ol p[z'\mid z]\,R[z'\mid z]=1 , \ }
\]
which is \bk{(17.27)}. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Reading.}  The left-hand side is the date-$t$ cost of the bundle of contingent claims that
one unit of saved output delivers; the right-hand side is the cost of that unit of output.  If
the sum exceeded $1$, a household could buy one unit of the good, save it, sell the resulting
claims, and pocket the difference with no risk and no outlay; if it fell short of $1$, the
reverse trade would do the same.  Both branches are riskless because the capital carried forward
pays $R[z'\mid z]$ in state $z'$ \emph{with certainty} --- the uncertainty is about which state
occurs, not about the payoff in a given state.  So $\sum\ol pR=1$ is the exact counterpart of
\bk{(17.19)} in the date-$0$ formulation, as the substitution
$\ol p[z'\mid z]=p_0[z^{t+1}]/p_0[z^t]$ and $R=R_0[z^{t+1}]/p_0[z^{t+1}]$ confirms.
\end{aex}

\solsection{17.3}{Application: Real Business Cycle Models}

\begin{aex}{17.17}{}
Apply \bk{Theorems 16.1--16.7} to \bk{(17.28)}, taking the control to be the \emph{pair}
$y=(L,K')$ --- nothing in Chapter 16 requires the control to be scalar.  Set
\[
G(K,z)=\bigl\{(L,K'):\ L\in[0,\ol L],\ K'\in[0,F(K,L,z)+(1-\delta)K]\bigr\},
\]
\[
U\bigl(K,(L,K'),z\bigr)=u\bigl(F(K,L,z)+(1-\delta)K-K',\,L\bigr).
\]
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Assumption 16.1 and 16.2.}  Define $\ol K$ by $\ol K=F(\ol K,\ol L,z_N)+(1-\delta)\ol K$;
      by the Inada conditions $\ol K$ exists and is finite, and starting from $K(0)$ the capital
      stock stays in the compact set $\mathcal K=[0,\max\{K(0),\ol K\}]$.  On
      $\mathcal K\times Z$ the correspondence $G$ is nonempty, compact-valued and continuous
      (upper and lower hemicontinuous, by the continuity of $F$ and Berge's theorem), and $U$ is
      continuous and bounded.  Boundedness of $u$ from below is where $\ol L<\infty$ is used.
\item \emph{Assumption 16.3 (concavity).}  $F(\cdot,\cdot,z)$ is concave, so
      $C=F(K,L,z)+(1-\delta)K-K'$ is concave in $(K,L,K')$; $u$ is concave and increasing in
      $C$, so the composition $u(C,L)$ is concave in $(K,L,K')$, jointly with the second
      argument.  \bk{Theorem 16.4} then gives concavity of $V$ in $K$; strict concavity follows
      from the strict concavity of $F(\cdot,L,z)$ in $K$.
\item \emph{Assumption 16.4 (monotonicity in $K$).}  $F$ is strictly increasing in $K$, so $U$ is
      strictly increasing in the state; and $K_1<K_2$ implies $G(K_1,z)\subset G(K_2,z)$.
      \bk{Theorem 16.5} gives $V$ strictly increasing in $K$.
\item \emph{Assumption 16.5 (differentiability).}  $F$ and $u$ are continuously differentiable,
      and at an interior optimum $\pi_k(K,z)\in\Int G$, so \bk{Theorem 16.6} applies and
      $V(\cdot,z)$ is differentiable on $K>0$ with
      $\partial V/\partial K=u_c\cdot(F_K+1-\delta)$.
\item \emph{Assumption 16.6 (monotonicity in $z$).}  $z$ follows a monotone chain, $F$ is
      increasing in $z$ so $U$ is, and $G(K,z_1)\subset G(K,z_2)$ for $z_1<z_2$.
      \bk{Theorem 16.7} gives $V$ strictly increasing in $z$.
\end{enumerate}
Existence and uniqueness of the policy correspondence follow from \bk{Theorem 16.2} and Berge;
single-valuedness of $\pi_k$ and $\pi_l$ follows from strict concavity of
$U(K,\cdot,z)+\beta\E[V(\cdot,z')\mid z]$ in $(L,K')$. $\blacksquare$

\rmk{The last step deserves care.  The chapter assumes only that $u$ is \emph{jointly concave},
and joint concavity is not enough for unique policies: $U$ is strictly concave in $K'$ (through
$u$ in $C$), but strict concavity in $L$ needs $u$ to be strictly concave in $L$ or $F$ to be
strictly concave in $L$.  Example 17.2, with $u(C,L)=\log C-\gamma L$, is linear in $L$, and
uniqueness there comes not from concavity but from the static first-order condition, which pins
$L$ down exactly (Exercise 17.19).  So \bk{Proposition 17.4} as stated requires either strict
joint concavity of $u$ or an interior static optimum; the second is what the applications
actually use.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{17.19}{}
Keep $F(K,L,z)=zK^\alpha L^{1-\alpha}$ and $\delta=1$, and conjecture
$\pi_k(K,z)=BzK^\alpha L^{1-\alpha}$ as in Example 17.2.  Because preferences are additively
separable and the consumption term is logarithmic, $h$ plays no part in the intertemporal
margin: the first condition of \bk{(17.29)} is unchanged from Example 17.2 and again gives
\[
B=\alpha\beta,\qquad C=(1-\alpha\beta)\,zK^\alpha L^{1-\alpha} .
\]
(The cancellation is the one exhibited in Exercise 17.6: with $\delta=1$ and log consumption
utility, $F_K/C$ is a deterministic function of $K'$.)

The static condition of \bk{(17.29)} is $w\,u_C=-u_L$, that is
\[
\underbrace{(1-\alpha)zK^\alpha L^{-\alpha}}_{w=F_L}\cdot
\frac{1}{(1-\alpha\beta)zK^\alpha L^{1-\alpha}}=-h'(L) .
\]
Every appearance of $z$ and of $K$ cancels between the wage and the marginal utility of
consumption, and the powers of $L$ combine to $L^{-\alpha}/L^{1-\alpha}=L^{-1}$, leaving
\[
\boxed{\ -L\,h'(L)=\frac{1-\alpha}{1-\alpha\beta} \ }
\]
--- a single equation in $L$ alone.  Hence $\pi_l(K,z)=L^*$ is a constant.

\emph{Uniqueness.}  The left-hand side is strictly increasing: $\frac{\dd}{\dd L}[-Lh'(L)]
=-h'(L)-Lh''(L)>0$, since $h'<0$ and $h''\le0$.  It vanishes at $L=0$ and, provided it exceeds
$(1-\alpha)/(1-\alpha\beta)$ somewhere in $[0,\ol L]$, there is exactly one solution.  For
$h(L)=-\gamma L$ the equation reads $\gamma L=(1-\alpha)/(1-\alpha\beta)$, giving
$L^*=(1-\alpha)/[\gamma(1-\alpha\beta)]$, which is Example 17.2.

\emph{Why.}  Logarithmic consumption utility makes the wealth elasticity of the marginal utility
of consumption exactly $-1$, so a productivity shock raises the wage and the marginal value of
income in the same proportion: the income and substitution effects on labor supply cancel
identically, and the labor supply schedule is vertical.  The shape of $h$ changes \emph{where}
that vertical schedule sits, but not the fact that it is vertical.  This is precisely the defect
of the simplest RBC model noted in the text: it produces comovement of output, consumption and
investment but no employment fluctuations at all.  Generating them requires breaking the
knife-edge, which is what \bk{Exercise 17.18}'s balanced-growth restrictions and the
non-separable specifications used in the literature do.
\end{aex}

\solsection{17.4}{Growth with Incomplete Markets: The Bewley Model}

\begin{aex}{17.20}{}
\emph{Why every path.}  The household of Section 17.4 trades one asset, a riskless claim paying
the gross return $R$.  There is no security whose payoff depends on the realization of
$z^h(t)$, so a surplus accumulated in a lucky history cannot be sold to finance a deficit in an
unlucky one: the two are not different commodities, they are the same commodity held by the same
household under different circumstances.  Solvency must therefore be checked separately along
every realization of $\{z^h(t)\}_{t\ge0}$.

\emph{The bound.}  Iterating the flow constraint
$a^h(t+1)\le Ra^h(t)+wz^h(t)-c^h(t)$ forward $T$ periods and imposing the no-Ponzi condition
$\lim_{T\to\infty}R^{-T}a^h(T)\ge0$ gives
\[
a^h(t)\ \ge\ -\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}R^{-(s+1)}\,w\,z^h(t+s)+\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}R^{-(s+1)}c^h(t+s)
\ \ge\ -\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}R^{-(s+1)}\,w\,z^h(t+s) ,
\]
using $c\ge0$.  The binding case is the worst sample path, $z^h(t+s)=z_{\min}$ for all $s$,
which occurs with positive probability at every horizon because the endowments are i.i.d.\ draws
from $[z_{\min},z_{\max}]$ with $z_{\min}>0$.  Hence
\[
\boxed{\ a^h(t)\ \ge\ -\frac{w\,z_{\min}}{R-1}\ \equiv\ -b \ }
\]
for all $t$, which is \bk{(17.30)}.  The constraint is \emph{endogenous}: no one imposes it, but
any larger debt would be defaulted on in the worst history, and lenders anticipating that would
not extend it.  It tightens as $z_{\min}$ falls and becomes $a^h(t)\ge0$ in the limit
$z_{\min}\to0$ --- a household with no guaranteed income cannot borrow at all.

\emph{Comparison with \bk{(17.11)}.}  The complete-markets constraint is a \emph{single}
inequality
\[
\sum_{t}\sum_{z^t}p_0[z^t]c[z^t]\ \le\ \sum_{t}\sum_{z^t}w_0[z^t]+R_0[z(0)]k(0) ,
\]
in which expenditure in one history is traded off against income in another at the market rates
$p_0[z^t]$.  Only the \emph{present value} of the endowment matters, not its distribution across
states; the household's consumption can therefore be made state-independent, and marginal
utilities are equated across all histories.  In \bk{(17.30)} there is one constraint per date and
per path, only the \emph{worst} path matters for the bound, and marginal utilities cannot be
equated across states.

Three consequences follow, and between them they are the whole content of Section 17.4:
consumption is not fully insured and inherits idiosyncratic risk; the household accumulates
assets for precautionary reasons, which is where the assumption $u'''>0$ enters; and the
economy admits no representative household, so the aggregate capital stock depends on the whole
distribution of $(a,z)$ rather than on its mean.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{17.21}{}
Apply \bk{Theorems 16.1--16.7} to \bk{(17.31)}, treating $(a,z)$ as the state, $a'$ as the
control, and taking the stationary prices $R$ and $w$ as given.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{A compact state space.}  Below, $a\ge-b$ by \bk{(17.30)}.  Above, one needs
      $\beta R<1$: iterating the Euler equation
      $u'(c)=\beta R\,\E[u'(c')]$ shows that if $\beta R\ge1$ then $\{u'(c^h(t))\}$ is a
      nonnegative supermartingale that converges almost surely, forcing $c^h(t)\to\infty$ and
      $a^h(t)\to\infty$; with $\beta R<1$ there is $\ol a<\infty$ such that
      $\pi(\ol a,z_{\max})\le\ol a$, and $[-b,\ol a]$ is forward invariant.  In a stationary
      equilibrium $\beta R<1$ holds, because $R=f'(k^*)+1-\delta$ and precautionary saving pushes
      $k^*$ above its complete-markets value, where $\beta R=1$.
\item \emph{Assumptions 16.1--16.2.}  On the compact $[-b,\ol a]\times Z$, the correspondence
      $G(a,z)=[-b,\,Ra+wz]$ is nonempty (since $Ra+wz\ge-Rb+wz_{\min}>-b$ by the definition of
      $b$), compact-valued and continuous, and $U(a,a',z)=u(Ra+wz-a')$ is continuous and
      bounded.
\item \emph{Assumption 16.3.}  $U$ is jointly concave and \emph{strictly} concave in $a'$, so
      \bk{Theorem 16.4} gives $V^h(\cdot,z)$ strictly concave in $a$.
\item \emph{Assumption 16.4.}  $U$ is strictly increasing in $a$ and $G(a,z)$ expands with $a$,
      so \bk{Theorem 16.5} gives $V^h$ strictly increasing in $a$.
\item \emph{Assumption 16.5.}  $u$ is continuously differentiable and the optimal $a'$ is
      interior whenever $a>-b$, so \bk{Theorem 16.6} gives differentiability of $V^h$ in $a$ on
      the interior of the state space, with
      $\partial V^h/\partial a=R\,u'(Ra+wz-\pi(a,z))$.
\item \emph{Assumption 16.6.}  $U$ is strictly increasing in $z$, $G(a,z)$ expands with $z$, and
      the endowments are i.i.d., so part 3 holds with equality:
      $\E[W(a',z')\mid z]$ does not depend on $z$ at all.  \bk{Theorem 16.7} gives $V^h$ strictly
      increasing in $z$.
\end{enumerate}
The policy $\pi(a,z)$ is unique and continuous by strict concavity of $U$ in $a'$ and Berge's
theorem.  Monotonicity of $\pi$ in $a$ and $z$ follows from the first-order condition
$u'(Ra+wz-a')\ge\beta R\,\E[u'(Ra'+wz'-\pi(a',z'))]$, with equality when $a'>-b$: exactly as in
Exercise 17.1, the marginal propensity to save out of cash on hand $Ra+wz$ lies in $[0,1)$, and
both $a$ and $z$ raise cash on hand. $\blacksquare$

\rmk{Two details distinguish this from \bk{Proposition 17.2}.  First, $\pi$ is only
\emph{nondecreasing}, not strictly increasing: the constraint $a'\ge-b$ binds on a whole interval
of low $(a,z)$, where $\pi\equiv-b$.  This kink is the defining feature of the Bewley model, and
it is what makes the invariant distribution of assets have a mass point at $-b$.  Second, the
proposition's phrase ``differentiable in $a\in(-b,Ra+wz)$'' should read: differentiable at every
$a$ in the interior of the state space at which the constraint does not bind.  At the kink the
value function has a left and a right derivative that differ, so differentiability genuinely
fails there --- the Benveniste--Scheinkman argument of \bk{Theorem 16.6} needs the policy to be
interior, which is exactly what the binding constraint denies.}
\end{aex}

\solsection{17.6}{Risk, Diversification, and Growth}

\begin{aex}{17.23}{}
Fix total savings $s(t)$ and the safe investment $X(t)$; by \bk{(17.52)} the saving decision is
made independently of the portfolio, so it is legitimate to condition on them.  The household
chooses $\{I(j,t)\}_{j\in J(t)}$ to maximize, from \bk{(17.47)} and \bk{(17.49)},
\[
\Lambda\bigl(\{I(j)\}\bigr)=\int_{J(t)}\log\Bigl(R(j,t+1)\bigl(qX(t)+QI(j)\bigr)\Bigr)\dd j
+\int_{[0,1]\setminus J(t)}\log\bigl(R(j,t+1)qX(t)\bigr)\dd j
\]
subject to $\int_{J(t)}I(j)\dd j=s(t)-X(t)$ and $I(j)\ge0$.  Only the first integral involves the
choice variables, and the household takes the prices $R(j,t+1)$ as given; by the symmetry of the
environment they take a common value $R_G(t+1)$ on all of $J(t)$, since the aggregate capital
stock is the same in every good state.  So the problem is
\[
\max\ \int_{J(t)}\log\bigl(qX(t)+QI(j)\bigr)\dd j
\quad\text{subject to}\quad \int_{J(t)}I(j)\dd j=s(t)-X(t) ,
\]
plus an additive constant.

The objective is strictly concave in $\{I(j)\}$ and the constraint is linear and symmetric across
$j\in J(t)$.  Suppose a candidate had $I(j_1)\ne I(j_2)$ on two subsets of $J(t)$ of equal
positive measure.  Replacing both by the average $\bar I=\tfrac12(I(j_1)+I(j_2))$ leaves the
constraint satisfied, and by the \emph{strict} concavity of $\log$,
\[
\tfrac12\log\bigl(qX+QI(j_1)\bigr)+\tfrac12\log\bigl(qX+QI(j_2)\bigr)
<\log\bigl(qX+Q\bar I\bigr) ,
\]
so the averaged portfolio is strictly better.  The candidate was therefore not optimal, and
\[
\boxed{\ I^*(j,t)=I^*(j',t)\quad\text{for all }j,j'\in J(t) . \ }
\]
Equivalently, the first-order condition $Q/(qX+QI(j))=\mu$ has the same right-hand side for every
open $j$, because free entry has set every price to $P(j,t)=1$; since the map
$I\mapsto Q/(qX+QI)$ is strictly decreasing, $I(j)$ is pinned down uniquely and identically.
$\blacksquare$

\emph{The economic content} is that all open securities are perfect substitutes in price and
perfect complements in risk: they cost the same and pay in disjoint states, so a risk-averse
household holds them in equal amounts.  It is worth noticing what the argument \emph{does not}
use: it never appeals to the minimum size requirements.  That is why the equilibrium portfolio is
always balanced while the Pareto optimal one need not be (Exercise 17.29) --- the planner faces
the constraints $I(j)\ge M(j)$ directly, the household does not.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{17.24}{}
Suppose $j^*\in J(t)$ and take any $j<j^*$, and suppose for a contradiction $j\notin J(t)$.

By Exercise 17.23 the household's demand for every open security is the same number
$I^*\equiv I^*(n^*(t))$, and since sector $j^*$ is open it must be meeting its minimum size
requirement:
\[
I^*\ \ge\ M(j^*) .
\]
The minimum size schedule \bk{(17.43)},
$M(j)=\max\{0,\ D(j-\gamma)/(1-\gamma)\}$, is nondecreasing, so
\[
M(j)\ \le\ M(j^*)\ \le\ I^* .
\]
Now consider an intermediary that enters and offers the security for sector $j$ at the
equilibrium price $P(j,t)=1$.  Facing the enlarged menu, the household's optimal portfolio again
allocates the common amount to every available security, and by \bk{(17.56)} that amount is
\emph{increasing} in the measure of open sectors, so it is at least $I^*$.  The new sector
therefore attracts at least $I^*\ge M(j)$, meets its minimum size requirement, and pays a
positive return; the intermediary breaks even at $P=1$ and the household is strictly better off,
because adding a security in a state where consumption was $R_Bq X$ raises
$\int_0^1\log c_2(j)\dd j$ strictly.  This contradicts the free-entry requirement in the
definition of a static equilibrium, which demands that no sector outside $J^*(t)$ could raise
sufficient funds.  Hence $j\in J(t)$, and
\[
\boxed{\ J^*(t)=[0,n^*(t)]\ \text{for some }n^*(t)\in[0,1] . \ }
\]
$\blacksquare$

The two ingredients are worth separating.  That $M$ is nondecreasing is a normalization: the
sectors were ranked in ascending order of minimum size, and since the states are otherwise
symmetric, nothing else distinguishes them.  That the household's demand per security is common
across open sectors is Exercise 17.23.  Together they say that the cheapest sectors are opened
first, and the ``supply of assets'' curve $M(j)$ in Figure 17.2 can be read as an increasing
marginal cost of financial development.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{17.25}{}
Write the excess demand for the marginal security as
\[
\Phi(n)\ \equiv\ I^*(n)-M(n)\ =\ \frac{(Q-q)s}{Q-qn}-\frac{D(n-\gamma)}{1-\gamma},
\qquad n\in[\gamma,1],
\]
with $s\equiv s^*(t)$.  An intersection is a zero of $\Phi$.  Both curves are increasing, so
uniqueness is not automatic; what is needed is that $M$ cut $I^*$ \emph{from below} at every
crossing.

\emph{The sign of $M'-I^{*\prime}$ at a crossing.}  Differentiating,
\[
M'(n)=\frac{D}{1-\gamma},\qquad
I^{*\prime}(n)=\frac{q(Q-q)s}{(Q-qn)^2} .
\]
At a zero of $\Phi$ we may substitute $(Q-q)s=D(n-\gamma)(Q-qn)/(1-\gamma)$ into $I^{*\prime}$,
which gives $I^{*\prime}(n)=qD(n-\gamma)/[(1-\gamma)(Q-qn)]$ and hence
\[
M'(n)-I^{*\prime}(n)
=\frac{D}{1-\gamma}\Bigl[1-\frac{q(n-\gamma)}{Q-qn}\Bigr]
=\frac{D}{1-\gamma}\cdot\frac{Q+q\gamma-2qn}{Q-qn} .
\]
