Since $Q>qn$ for $n\le1$, the sign of $M'-I^{*\prime}$ at any crossing is the sign of
\[
Q+q\gamma-2qn .
\]

\emph{The condition.}  If $Q\ge(2-\gamma)q$ then $Q+q\gamma\ge2q\ge2qn$ for every $n\le1$, so
$M'>I^{*\prime}$ at \emph{every} crossing --- equivalently $\Phi'<0$ at every zero of $\Phi$.
A continuously differentiable function that is strictly decreasing at each of its zeros has at
most one zero on an interval, since between two consecutive downcrossings it would have to
return upward and hence cross again with $\Phi'\ge0$.  Therefore
\[
\boxed{\ Q\ \ge\ (2-\gamma)q\ \Longrightarrow\ \text{at most one intersection.} \ }
\]
Since $\Phi(\gamma)=(Q-q)s/(Q-q\gamma)>0$ and $\Phi(1)=s-D$, there is exactly one intersection
when $s<D$, and none --- with $n^*=1$, all sectors open --- when $s\ge D$.  Either way the
equilibrium is unique. $\blacksquare$

\emph{The condition is sufficient, not necessary.}  Note that
\[
\Phi'(n)=\frac{q(Q-q)s}{(Q-qn)^2}-\frac{D}{1-\gamma}
\]
is increasing in $n$, so $\Phi$ is convex and has at most two zeros in any case; a second zero
must be an upcrossing, hence must lie above $(Q+q\gamma)/(2q)$, and must have $\Phi(1)>0$, that
is $s>D$.  Both requirements can be met only when $Q<(2-\gamma)q$.  \texttt{code/ch17.py}
confirms this by exhibiting a violating example: with $Q=1.2$, $q=1$, $\gamma=0$, $D=1$ and
$s=1.2$ the roots are $n=0.25359$ and $n=0.94641$, the threshold $(Q+q\gamma)/(2q)=0.6$ sits
between them, and the equilibria are $n^*=0.25359$ and $n^*=1$ --- the low-diversification and
full-diversification equilibria described in the text.  Every parameter set satisfying
$Q\ge(2-\gamma)q$ that the program scans has exactly one equilibrium.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{17.26}{}
Fix $K(t)$.  By \bk{(17.45)} the wage is $w(t)=(1-\alpha)K(t)^\alpha$, and \bk{(17.52)} gives
\[
s^*(t)=\frac{\beta}{1+\beta}w(t)=\Gamma K(t)^\alpha ,\qquad
\Gamma\equiv\frac{(1-\alpha)\beta}{1+\beta} ,
\]
which is the expression in the proposition.  Exercises 17.23 and 17.24 reduce the equilibrium to
a single number $n$, with the portfolio $X^*$ and $I^*$ of \bk{(17.55)}--\bk{(17.56)}.

\emph{The equilibrium condition.}  $n$ is a static equilibrium if and only if
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{feasibility}: every sector $j\le n$ raises its minimum, $I^*(n)\ge M(n)$ --- since
      $M$ is nondecreasing, checking the marginal sector suffices; and
\item \emph{free entry}: no sector just above $n$ could raise its minimum, $I^*(n')<M(n')$ for
      $n'$ slightly above $n$.
\end{enumerate}
In terms of $\Phi=I^*-M$ this says $\Phi(n)\ge0$ and $\Phi<0$ just to the right --- a
downcrossing, or the corner $n=1$ with $\Phi(1)\ge0$.

\emph{The two deviations.}  If $n<n^*[K]$, then $\Phi(n)>0$ by the argument of Exercise 17.25
(with $\Phi>0$ on $[\gamma,n^*)$), so an intermediary that opens sector $n^\dagger\in(n,n^*)$ and
offers its security at $P=1$ attracts the common demand $I^*(n^\dagger)>M(n^\dagger)$, covers the
minimum, and earns nonnegative profit; charging $P=1+\varepsilon$ for small $\varepsilon>0$ still
attracts enough funds by continuity and earns strictly positive profit.  So $n<n^*[K]$ is not an
equilibrium.  If $n>n^*[K]$, then $\Phi(n)<0$, so $I^*(n)<M(n)$: the marginal sector cannot raise
its minimum at any price $P\ge1$, and its securities are not supplied.  Feasibility fails and
$n>n^*[K]$ is not an equilibrium either.

\emph{Solving for $n^*$.}  Setting $\Phi(n)=0$ and clearing denominators,
\[
\frac{(Q-q)\Gamma K^\alpha}{Q-qn}=\frac{D(n-\gamma)}{1-\gamma}
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
(n-\gamma)(Q-qn)=C,\qquad
C\equiv\frac{(Q-q)(1-\gamma)\Gamma K^\alpha}{D} ,
\]
that is $qn^2-(Q+q\gamma)n+(\gamma Q+C)=0$.  Taking the smaller root, which is the downcrossing,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\ n^*[K]=
\frac{(Q+q\gamma)-\bigl[(Q+q\gamma)^2-4q\bigl(D^{-1}(Q-q)(1-\gamma)\Gamma K^\alpha
+\gamma Q\bigr)\bigr]^{1/2}}{2q}\ }
\label{eq:nstar}
\end{equation}
for $K\le(D/\Gamma)^{1/\alpha}$, and $n^*[K]=1$ for larger $K$.  The threshold is where $s^*=D$,
that is $\Gamma K^\alpha=D$, which is the proposition's $K>D^{1/\alpha}\Gamma^{-1/\alpha}$.
$\blacksquare$

\rmk{\bk{(17.57)} as printed has $(Q+q)^2$ inside the square root while carrying $(Q+q\gamma)$
outside it; the quadratic above gives $(Q+q\gamma)^2$ in both places, so the printed
discriminant is a misprint.  The corrected root has the right limit: as $K\to0$ the constant
$C\to0$ and \eqref{eq:nstar} gives $n^*\to\gamma$, which is exactly right, since only the sectors
with no minimum size requirement can open when there is nothing to invest.  The printed version
gives $[(Q+q\gamma)-(Q+q)]/(2q)=(\gamma-1)/2<0$.  \texttt{code/ch17.py} tabulates both at
$Q=2$, $q=1$, $\gamma=0.3$, $D=0.15$, $\alpha=0.36$, $\beta=0.95$; the corrected root satisfies
$M(n^*)=I^*(n^*)$ to within $10^{-16}$ at every $K$, and the printed one is negative at small
$K$.  Everything that \bk{Proposition 17.7} asserts --- that $n^*$ is increasing in $K$ and
reaches $1$ at $K=(D/\Gamma)^{1/\alpha}$ --- holds for the corrected root.  The program also
verifies that \bk{(17.61)}, $D<\Gamma^{1/(1-\alpha)}Q^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}$, is algebraically
identical to $K_{SS}\ge\ol K$, which is how it is used in Exercise 17.27.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{17.27}{}
\textbf{(a)}  Write $\ol K$ for the capital stock at which all sectors open,
$n^*[\ol K]=1$, so $\ol K=(D/\Gamma)^{1/\alpha}$ by Exercise 17.26, and
$K_{SS}=(\Gamma Q)^{1/(1-\alpha)}$ by \bk{(17.60)}.  Condition \bk{(17.61)} is exactly
$K_{SS}\ge\ol K$.  The proof has three steps.

\emph{Step 1: $[\ol K,\infty)$ is absorbing, and on it the dynamics are deterministic.}  If
$K(t)\ge\ol K$ then $n^*[K(t)]=1$: every sector is open, the good state occurs with probability
one, and \bk{(17.58)} reduces to
\[
K(t+1)=\Gamma QK(t)^\alpha ,
\]
a deterministic, increasing, strictly concave map with the unique positive fixed point
$K_{SS}$.  Since $\alpha<1$ this map is a contraction on $[\ol K,\infty)$ in the appropriate
sense and $K(t)\to K_{SS}$ monotonically.  It stays in $[\ol K,\infty)$ because
$\Gamma Q\ol K^\alpha\ge\ol K$ whenever $\ol K\le K_{SS}$, which is \bk{(17.61)}.

\emph{Step 2: $K(t)$ is bounded away from zero.}  From \bk{(17.58)}, even after a bad draw,
\[
K(t+1)\ \ge\ \frac{\Gamma q\bigl(1-n^*[K(t)]\bigr)}{Q-qn^*[K(t)]}\,QK(t)^\alpha
\ \ge\ \frac{\Gamma q(1-\gamma)Q}{Q-q\gamma}\,K(t)^\alpha\cdot\frac{1-n^*}{1-\gamma}
\cdot\frac{Q-q\gamma}{Q-qn^*} ,
\]
and since $n^*[K]\to\gamma$ as $K\to0$, the coefficient tends to
$\Gamma q(1-\gamma)Q/(Q-q\gamma)>0$.  With $\alpha<1$ the map $K\mapsto cK^\alpha$ has a
positive fixed point $K_{QSSB}$ and exceeds $K$ below it, so both branches of \bk{(17.58)} lie
above the $45^{\circ}$ line for small $K$ (region I of Figure 17.4).  Consequently there is
$\underline K>0$, depending only on $K(0)$, with $K(t)\ge\underline K$ for all $t$, almost
surely.

\emph{Step 3: the good region is reached almost surely.}  On $[\underline K,\ol K]$ the
probability of a good draw is $n^*[K]\ge n^*[\underline K]>\gamma\ge0$ --- strictly positive,
since $C>0$ for $K>0$ makes the root of \eqref{eq:nstar} exceed $\gamma$.  Write
$\underline n\equiv n^*[\underline K]>0$.  Along a run of consecutive good draws the capital
stock follows $K\mapsto\Gamma QK^\alpha$, which increases monotonically toward $K_{SS}\ge\ol K$;
hence there is a finite $m$, depending only on $\underline K$, such that $m$ consecutive good
draws starting anywhere in $[\underline K,\ol K]$ bring the economy into $[\ol K,\infty)$.  The
events
\[
A_i=\{\text{good draws at }t=im,\dots,(i+1)m-1\},\qquad i=0,1,2,\dots,
\]
satisfy $\Prob[A_i\mid\mathcal F_{im}]\ge\underline n^{\,m}>0$ on the event that the economy has
not yet entered $[\ol K,\infty)$.  By the conditional Borel--Cantelli lemma,
$\sum_i\Prob[A_i\mid\mathcal F_{im}]=\infty$ implies that some $A_i$ occurs almost surely.
Combining with Step 1,
\[
\boxed{\ \Prob\Bigl[\lim_{t\to\infty}K(t)=K_{SS}\Bigr]=1 . \ }
\]
$\blacksquare$

\textbf{(b)}  If \bk{(17.61)} fails then $K_{SS}<\ol K$: even an economy that is lucky in every
period converges to $K_{QSSG}=K_{SS}$, which is \emph{below} the level needed to open all
sectors.  The absorbing region of part (a) is empty, and $n^*[K(t)]<1$ forever, so a bad draw is
always possible and the process never becomes deterministic.  Consequently:
\begin{itemize}
\item $\{K(t)\}$ does \emph{not} converge to a point, and does not converge almost surely at all.
      The argument of Exercise 17.4(b) applies unchanged: the two branches of \bk{(17.58)} are
      distinct at every $K$, both are hit infinitely often, and a limit would have to be a fixed
      point of both;
\item but $\{K(t)\}$ \emph{does} converge in distribution.  Both branches of \bk{(17.58)} are
      increasing and concave in $K$, the upper one has the fixed point $K_{QSSG}$ and the lower
      one $K_{QSSB}$, so $[K_{QSSB},K_{QSSG}]$ is forward invariant and is reached in finite time
      from any $K(0)>0$.  On that compact interval the process is a monotone Markov process
      satisfying a mixing condition, so it has a unique invariant distribution $\Psi$ and
      $K(t)\Rightarrow\Psi$ from every initial condition, with time averages converging to
      $\Psi$-averages.
\end{itemize}
Economically, failing \bk{(17.61)} means the economy is never rich enough to insure itself: it
remains permanently in regions I and II of Figure 17.4, cycling between booms and crises, with
the expected TFP of \bk{(17.59)} fluctuating with $n^*[K(t)]$ but never reaching $Q$.  There is
no takeoff.  The gap between the two cases is the point of the model: whether an economy
eventually escapes volatility is not a matter of luck alone but of whether the scale of savings
that its own steady state generates, $\Gamma^{1/(1-\alpha)}Q^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}$, is large
relative to the fixed cost $D$ of the last sector.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{17.28}{}
By definition $\sigma(n^*)$ takes the value $q(1-n^*)Q/(Q-qn^*)$ with probability $1-n^*$ and
the value $Q$ with probability $n^*$.  Writing $A\equiv q(1-n^*)Q/(Q-qn^*)$, the gap between the
two realizations is
\[
Q-A=\frac{Q(Q-qn^*)-q(1-n^*)Q}{Q-qn^*}=\frac{Q(Q-q)}{Q-qn^*} ,
\]
and for a two-point random variable with probabilities $n^*$ and $1-n^*$ the variance is
$n^*(1-n^*)$ times the squared gap:
\[
\boxed{\ V_n=\Var\bigl(\sigma(n^*)\mid n^*\bigr)
=n^*(1-n^*)\Bigl[\frac{Q(Q-q)}{Q-qn^*}\Bigr]^{2} , \ }
\]
which is the expression in the proposition.

\emph{Single-peakedness.}  Take logs and differentiate:
\[
\log V_n=\log n^*+\log(1-n^*)+2\log\bigl(Q(Q-q)\bigr)-2\log(Q-qn^*) ,
\]
\[
\frac{\dd\log V_n}{\dd n^*}=\frac{1}{n^*}-\frac{1}{1-n^*}+\frac{2q}{Q-qn^*}
=\frac{(1-2n^*)(Q-qn^*)+2qn^*(1-n^*)}{n^*(1-n^*)(Q-qn^*)} .
\]
The numerator expands to $Q-qn^*-2n^*Q+2q(n^*)^2+2qn^*-2q(n^*)^2=Q-n^*(2Q-q)$, so
\[
\frac{\dd V_n}{\dd n^*}
\ \ \text{has the sign of}\ \ Q-n^*(2Q-q),
\]
the denominator $n^*(1-n^*)(Q-qn^*)$ being positive on $(0,1)$.  Hence $V_n$ is strictly
increasing for $n^*<\hat n$ and strictly decreasing for $n^*>\hat n$, where
\[
\hat n\equiv\frac{Q}{2Q-q}\ \in\ \Bigl(\tfrac12,\,1\Bigr) ,
\]
the bounds following from $0<q<Q$.  (A grid search in \texttt{code/ch17.py} locates the maximum
at $0.666666$ for $Q=2$, $q=1$, against $\hat n=2/3$.)

\emph{Translating to $K$.}  By \bk{Proposition 17.7}, $n^*[K]$ is continuous and strictly
increasing in $K$, with $n^*[K]\to\gamma$ as $K\to0$ and $n^*[K]=1$ for
$K\ge\ol K$; in particular $n^*[K]\ge\gamma$ for every $K$.  Therefore
\begin{itemize}
\item if $\gamma\ge\hat n=Q/(2Q-q)$, then $n^*[K]\ge\gamma\ge\hat n$ for every $K$, the economy
      is permanently on the decreasing branch, and $\partial V_n/\partial K\le0$ for all
      $K\ge0$;
\item if $\gamma<\hat n$, then since $n^*[\,\cdot\,]$ is continuous and increasing from $\gamma$
      to $1$, there is a unique $\wt K$ with $n^*[\wt K]=\hat n<1$, and
      \[
      \frac{\partial V_n}{\partial K}>0\ \ \text{for } K<\wt K,
      \qquad
      \frac{\partial V_n}{\partial K}\le0\ \ \text{for } K\ge\wt K .
      \]
\end{itemize}
$\blacksquare$

\emph{The two forces.}  $n^*(1-n^*)$ is the pure Bernoulli variance, maximal at $n^*=\tfrac12$
and vanishing at both ends; $[Q(Q-q)/(Q-qn^*)]^2$ is the squared gap between good and bad TFP,
which \emph{rises} with $n^*$ because better diversification draws savings out of the safe asset
and makes a bad draw more costly.  The second force shifts the peak from $\tfrac12$ up to
$Q/(2Q-q)$, and its strength is governed by $q/Q$: as $q\to0$ the safe asset is worthless, the
gap is constant, and $\hat n\to\tfrac12$; as $q\to Q$ the two technologies coincide, the gap
vanishes, and $\hat n\to1$.  The right panel of Figure~\ref{fig:ch17} shows both cases.  Since
the peak always lies above $\tfrac12$, the empirically relevant statement is the one the text
draws: except at very low capital stocks, the volatility of growth is decreasing in income.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{17.29}{}
The planner solves \bk{(17.63)}: choose $n$, $X$ and $\{I(j)\}_{j\le n}$ to maximize
\[
\int_0^{n}\log\bigl(qX+QI(j)\bigr)\dd j+(1-n)\log(qX)
\qquad\text{subject to}\qquad
X+\int_0^{n}I(j)\dd j\le s ,
\]
together with $I(j)\ge M(j)$ for every open $j$ --- a sector is open only if its minimum size is
met.  (That the open set may be taken to be an interval $[0,n]$ follows from Exercise 17.24,
since $M$ is nondecreasing.)  Attach the multiplier $\mu>0$ to the resource constraint and
$\nu(j)\ge0$ to $I(j)\ge M(j)$.

\emph{The portfolio, for given $n$.}  The first-order condition in $I(j)$ is
\begin{equation}
\frac{Q}{qX+QI(j)}=\mu-\nu(j),\qquad \nu(j)\bigl[I(j)-M(j)\bigr]=0 .
\label{eq:planI}
\end{equation}
Where the minimum does not bind, $\nu(j)=0$ and \eqref{eq:planI} gives the \emph{same} value of
$I(j)$ for every such $j$; call it $\ol I\equiv(Q/\mu-qX)/Q$.  Where it binds, $I(j)=M(j)>\ol I$.
Since $M$ is nondecreasing, the set on which it binds is an upper interval, so defining
$j^*$ by $M(j^*)=\ol I$,
\[
\boxed{\
I^S(j)=\begin{cases}
M(j^*)=\ol I>M(j), & j<j^*,\\[2pt]
M(j), & j\in[j^*,n^S],\\[2pt]
0, & j>n^S,
\end{cases}\ }
\]
which is \bk{(17.64)}.  The portfolio is \emph{unbalanced} whenever $j^*<n^S$, that is whenever
some sector's minimum binds: the planner puts the common amount $\ol I$ into the cheap sectors
and exactly the required minimum into the expensive ones, cross-subsidizing the latter.

\emph{The margin in $n$.}  Differentiating the objective in $n$ and using \eqref{eq:planI},
\begin{equation}
\log\bigl(qX+QI(n)\bigr)-\log(qX)\ =\ \mu\,I(n) .
\label{eq:plann}
\end{equation}
The left-hand side is the \emph{first-order} gain in expected log utility from having one more
state insured; the right-hand side is the resource cost at the shadow price $\mu$.

\emph{Why $n^S>n^*$ when $s<D$.}  In the decentralized equilibrium the household takes $n$ as
given: it never faces \eqref{eq:plann}, only \eqref{eq:planI} with $\nu\equiv0$, which is why its
portfolio is always balanced (Exercise 17.23).  Evaluate the planner's condition
\eqref{eq:plann} at the \emph{equilibrium} allocation, where $I(j)\equiv I^*$ for $j\le n^*$ and
the marginal sector is $n^*$ with $I^*(n^*)=M(n^*)$.  The left-hand side is strictly positive
and, because $\log$ is strictly concave with an unbounded derivative at $qX$, it exceeds
$\mu I^*$ for the equilibrium $\mu$: adding a state in which consumption jumps from $R_BqX$ to
$R_G(qX+QI)$ has a first-order benefit that no single household internalizes.  Hence at
$n=n^*$ the planner's objective is still increasing in $n$, and $n^S[K]>n^*[K]$.  Financing the
extra sectors requires resources, which the planner takes out of the safe asset, so
$X^S<X^*[K]$: the cross-subsidy runs from the safe asset and the low-$M$ sectors toward the
marginal high-$M$ sectors.

\emph{The case $s\ge D$.}  If $s\ge D=M(1)$ the planner can set $n^S=1$, $X^S=0$ and
$I^S(j)=s$ for all $j\in[0,1]$: this satisfies the resource constraint with equality, since
$\int_0^1s\dd j=s$, and meets every minimum, since $s\ge D\ge M(j)$.  It attains the first-best,
because consumption is then $R\cdot Qs$ in \emph{every} state --- all risk is diversified --- and
no resources are wasted on the dominated safe technology ($q<Q$).  The portfolio is balanced,
and $n^S=n^*=1$, so the equilibrium is Pareto optimal exactly when the economy is rich enough to
open every sector. $\blacksquare$

\rmk{This is a pecuniary externality that is \emph{not} neutralized by the First Welfare
Theorem, and it is worth being precise about why.  The set of traded commodities is determined
endogenously by the free-entry condition $I\ge M$, not by a price system: a security that is not
traded has no price, whereas in an Arrow--Debreu equilibrium every commodity is priced and is
untraded only because its price clears at zero excess supply.  Because of the nonconvexity
introduced by the minimum size requirements, no Arrow--Debreu equilibrium exists here at all, so
the theorem has nothing to apply to.  Exercise 17.23's result --- that the household's portfolio
is always balanced --- is the mechanism: prices $P(j,t)=1$ are equal across open sectors, so
households equalize their holdings, while optimality calls for unequal holdings.  Section
17.6.5 and \bk{Proposition 17.12} show that this is not repaired by allowing intermediary
coalitions, provided they cannot exclude non-members from investing.}
\end{aex}


%% ========================================================================
\solchapter{18}{Diffusion of Technology}

\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{0.86\textwidth}
\small
The twenty-two exercises of Chapter 18 left unsolved in the instructor's manual: 18.1--18.7,
18.10, 18.11, 18.14, 18.15, 18.17, 18.20, 18.22--18.25, 18.27--18.31.  The manual solves 18.8,
18.9, 18.12, 18.13, 18.16, 18.18, 18.19, 18.21 and 18.26.  The numerical parts are computed by
\texttt{code/ch18.py}; Figure~\ref{fig:ch18} is its output.
\end{minipage}
\end{center}

\bigskip
\hrule
\bigskip

\solsection{18.2}{A Benchmark Model of Technology Diffusion}

\begin{aex}{18.1}{}
Capital accumulates out of a constant saving rate:
$\dt K_j(t)=s_jY_j(t)-\delta K_j(t)$.  With $k_j\equiv K_j/(A_jL_j)$, take logarithms and
differentiate:
\[
\frac{\dt k_j}{k_j}=\frac{\dt K_j}{K_j}-\frac{\dt A_j}{A_j}-\frac{\dt L_j}{L_j}
=\Bigl(s_j\frac{Y_j}{K_j}-\delta\Bigr)-g_j(t)-n_j ,
\]
using \bk{(18.2)} and $\dt L_j/L_j=n_j$.  Constant returns to scale give
\[
\frac{Y_j}{K_j}=\frac{A_jL_jf(k_j)}{A_jL_jk_j}=\frac{f(k_j)}{k_j} ,
\]
so multiplying through by $k_j$,
\[
\boxed{\ \dt k_j(t)=s_jf\bigl(k_j(t)\bigr)-\bigl(n_j+g_j(t)+\delta\bigr)k_j(t) . \ }
\]
$\blacksquare$

This is the Solow equation of Chapter 2 with two changes: the effective depreciation term carries
the country's own technology growth $g_j(t)$ rather than a common $g$, and $g_j(t)$ is a function
of time rather than a constant.  The equation is therefore \emph{non-autonomous}, which is what
makes the stability argument of Exercise 18.4 slightly more delicate than in Chapter 2.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{18.2}{}
Work with $a_j=A_j/A$, whose law of motion \bk{(18.4)} is
$\dt a_j=\sigma_j-(\sigma_j+g-\lambda_j)a_j$.  Evaluate the vector field at the boundary
$a_j=1$:
\[
\boxed{\ \dt a_j\big|_{a_j=1}=\sigma_j-\sigma_j-g+\lambda_j=\lambda_j-g . \ }
\]
Everything follows from the sign of this single number.
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $\lambda_j<g$, then $\dt a_j<0$ at $a_j=1$, so the set $[0,1)$ is forward invariant:
      a country that starts weakly behind the frontier stays behind, and
      $a^*_j=\sigma_j/(\sigma_j+g-\lambda_j)<1$.
\item If $\lambda_j=g$, then $\dt a_j=\sigma_j(1-a_j)$ and $a^*_j=1$: the country converges to
      the frontier exactly, and $A_j(t)\le A(t)$ survives only as a weak inequality with
      equality in the limit.
\item If $g<\lambda_j<g+\sigma_j$, then $\dt a_j>0$ at $a_j=1$, so any trajectory reaches $a_j=1$
      in finite time and crosses it; it converges to
      $a^*_j=\sigma_j/\bigl(\sigma_j-(\lambda_j-g)\bigr)>1$.  The country ends up
      \emph{permanently more advanced than the world technology frontier}.
\item If $\lambda_j\ge g+\sigma_j$, the coefficient on $a_j$ in \bk{(18.4)} is nonpositive, so
      $\dt a_j\ge\sigma_j>0$ for every $a_j$ and $a_j(t)\to\infty$: the country's technology
      grows strictly faster than the frontier forever.
\end{enumerate}
Cases 3 and 4 both violate $A_j(t)\le A(t)$, and case 4 also destroys the common long-run growth
rate that is the point of the model. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Why the restriction is the right one.}  The interpretation of \bk{(18.3)} is that
$\sigma_j(A-A_j)$ is \emph{absorption} from a stock of knowledge the country does not yet have,
so it must vanish when the country reaches the frontier, while $\lambda_jA_j$ is \emph{local}
innovation that builds on what the country already knows.  If local innovation alone could
sustain growth at the world rate, the country would need no frontier at all --- it would be an
endogenous growth economy in its own right, and $A(t)$ would no longer deserve the name
``frontier.''  The restriction $\lambda_j<g$ is exactly the statement that no country can keep up
with the world by its own efforts alone, and it is what leaves the catch-up term with a job to do.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{18.3}{}
With $a_j\equiv A_j/A$ and $\dt A/A=g$,
\[
\dt a_j=\frac{\dt A_j}{A}-\frac{A_j\dt A}{A^2}
=\frac{\sigma_j(A-A_j)+\lambda_jA_j}{A}-a_jg
=\sigma_j(1-a_j)+\lambda_ja_j-ga_j ,
\]
so that
\[
\boxed{\ \dt a_j(t)=\sigma_j-\bigl(\sigma_j+g-\lambda_j\bigr)a_j(t) . \ }
\]
$\blacksquare$

The equation is linear and autonomous, and it involves no variable other than $a_j$ ---
neither $k_j$, nor $A$, nor any other country's state.  This is the block recursiveness the text
emphasizes, and it is what makes the whole world equilibrium solvable country by country.  Its
explicit solution is
\[
a_j(t)=a^*_j+\bigl(a_j(0)-a^*_j\bigr)e^{-\mu_jt},\qquad
a^*_j=\frac{\sigma_j}{\mu_j},\qquad \mu_j\equiv\sigma_j+g-\lambda_j>0 ,
\]
so convergence to the steady-state gap is monotone and exponential at rate $\mu_j$.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{18.4}{}
\emph{Step 1: the technology block.}  \bk{(18.4)} is linear with the strictly positive
coefficient $\mu_j=\sigma_j+g-\lambda_j$ (strictly positive because $\lambda_j<g$), so it has the
unique fixed point $a^*_j=\sigma_j/\mu_j\in(0,1)$ and the explicit solution written in Exercise
18.3.  Hence $a_j(t)\to a^*_j$ monotonically from any $a_j(0)>0$, which is \bk{(18.5)} and its
global stability.  Since $a_j(t)$ is monotone and bounded between $\min\{a_j(0),a^*_j\}>0$ and
$\max\{a_j(0),a^*_j\}$, and $\dt a_j$ is bounded, the growth rate
$g_j(t)=\dt a_j(t)/a_j(t)+g$ is bounded and satisfies $g_j(t)\to g$ exponentially at rate
$\mu_j$.

\emph{Step 2: the capital block, steady state.}  In a steady state $a_j$ is constant, so
$g_j=g$, and $\dt k_j=0$ requires
\[
s_j\frac{f(k^*_j)}{k^*_j}=n_j+g+\delta .
\]
By \bk{Assumptions 1--2}, $f$ is strictly concave with $f(0)=0$, so $f(k)/k$ is strictly
decreasing, and the Inada conditions give $f(k)/k\to\infty$ as $k\to0$ and $f(k)/k\to0$ as
$k\to\infty$.  So there is exactly one $k^*_j>0$.

\emph{Step 3: the capital block, global stability.}  \bk{(18.1)} is non-autonomous, so one cannot
simply read stability off a phase diagram.  Fix $\varepsilon>0$.  By Step 1 there is $T$ with
$|g_j(t)-g|<\varepsilon$ for all $t\ge T$.  Consider the two \emph{autonomous} comparison
equations
\[
\dt\underline k=s_jf(\underline k)-(n_j+g+\varepsilon+\delta)\underline k,
\qquad
\dt\ol k=s_jf(\ol k)-(n_j+g-\varepsilon+\delta)\ol k ,
\]
started at $\underline k(T)=\ol k(T)=k_j(T)$.  Each is a standard Solow equation whose right-hand
side is strictly positive below and strictly negative above its unique positive rest point, so
each converges globally and monotonically to $k^*_j(g\pm\varepsilon)$, defined by
$s_jf(k)/k=n_j+g\pm\varepsilon+\delta$.  Since the right-hand side of \bk{(18.1)} lies between the
two comparison right-hand sides for $t\ge T$, the standard differential inequality gives
$\underline k(t)\le k_j(t)\le\ol k(t)$ for all $t\ge T$, whence
\[
k^*_j(g+\varepsilon)\ \le\ \liminf_{t\to\infty}k_j(t)\ \le\ \limsup_{t\to\infty}k_j(t)
\ \le\ k^*_j(g-\varepsilon) .
\]
Letting $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ and using the continuity of $k^*_j(\cdot)$ --- which follows from
the strict monotonicity of $f(k)/k$ and the implicit function theorem --- gives
$k_j(t)\to k^*_j$.  Since $\varepsilon$ and $T$ did not depend on $k_j(0)$, convergence is global.
$\blacksquare$

\emph{Step 4: income per capita.}  Finally, $y_j(t)=A_j(t)f(k_j(t))\to a^*_jA(0)e^{gt}f(k^*_j)$,
so income per capita in every country grows asymptotically at the common rate $g$, which
completes \bk{Proposition 18.1}.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{18.5}{}
\emph{On \bk{(18.4)}.}  Differentiating $\dt a_j=\sigma_j-(\sigma_j+g-\lambda_j)a_j$
\emph{at a given $a_j$},
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{\partial\dt a_j}{\partial\sigma_j}=1-a_j>0,
\qquad
\frac{\partial\dt a_j}{\partial\lambda_j}=a_j>0 . \ }
\]
The two derivatives have opposite shapes.  The absorption parameter helps most when the country
is far behind, and its effect \emph{vanishes at the frontier}; the local-innovation parameter
helps most when the country is close to the frontier, and its effect \emph{vanishes when the
country has nothing}.

\emph{On the steady state and the speed of convergence.}  With
$a^*_j=\sigma_j/\mu_j$ and $\mu_j=\sigma_j+g-\lambda_j$,
\[
\frac{\partial a^*_j}{\partial\sigma_j}=\frac{g-\lambda_j}{\mu_j^2}>0,
\qquad
\frac{\partial a^*_j}{\partial\lambda_j}=\frac{\sigma_j}{\mu_j^2}>0,
\qquad
\frac{\partial\mu_j}{\partial\sigma_j}=+1,
\qquad
\frac{\partial\mu_j}{\partial\lambda_j}=-1 .
\]
Both raise the steady-state relative technology, in the ratio
$\dfrac{\partial a^*_j/\partial\sigma_j}{\partial a^*_j/\partial\lambda_j}=\dfrac{g-\lambda_j}{\sigma_j}$.
But they move the speed of convergence in \emph{opposite} directions: a higher $\sigma_j$ speeds
convergence, a higher $\lambda_j$ slows it.  The numerical table in \texttt{code/ch18.py} makes
this concrete at $g=2\%$: raising $\sigma$ from $0.02$ to $0.20$ takes $a^*$ from $0.500$ to
$0.909$ and cuts the half-life from $17.3$ to $3.2$ years, whereas raising $\lambda$ from $0$ to
$0.01$ at $\sigma=0.02$ takes $a^*$ from $0.500$ to $0.667$ but \emph{lengthens} the half-life
from $17.3$ to $23.1$ years.

\emph{On \bk{(18.1)}.}  Neither parameter appears in \bk{(18.1)} except through $g_j(t)$, and in
steady state $g_j=g$ regardless of both.  Hence
\[
\frac{\partial k^*_j}{\partial\sigma_j}=\frac{\partial k^*_j}{\partial\lambda_j}=0 ,
\]
and the entire effect on income per capita runs through the level of $A_j$, not through the
effective capital-labor ratio.  Along the transition the two parameters act only by raising
$g_j(t)$, which enters \bk{(18.1)} exactly as faster effective-labour growth: it temporarily
\emph{depresses} $k_j(t)$, so that a country catching up fast has a temporarily low effective
capital-labor ratio even as its income per capita rises rapidly.

\emph{Why they differ.}  $\sigma_j$ multiplies the \emph{gap} $A(t)-A_j(t)$ and $\lambda_j$
multiplies the \emph{stock} $A_j(t)$.  A force proportional to the gap is self-limiting: it
weakens as the country approaches the frontier, which is precisely what makes the world income
distribution stable and gives relatively backward economies the Gerschenkron advantage.  A force
proportional to the stock is not self-limiting: it is an endogenous-growth term, it does not
weaken as the country advances, and if it were large enough it would let the country overtake the
frontier (Exercise 18.2).  This is also why $\lambda_j$ slows convergence --- it adds a term that
grows with $a_j$ and therefore partly offsets the restoring force --- while $\sigma_j$ strengthens
it.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{18.6}{}
If $g=0$ the restriction $\lambda_j\in[0,g)$ forces $\lambda_j=0$, and $A(t)=A(0)\equiv\ol A$ is
constant.  \bk{(18.3)} becomes
\[
\dt A_j(t)=\sigma_j\bigl(\ol A-A_j(t)\bigr) ,
\]
a linear equation with solution $A_j(t)=\ol A+(A_j(0)-\ol A)e^{-\sigma_jt}$.  Hence
\[
\boxed{\ A_j(t)\longrightarrow\ol A\quad\text{for every }j,\ \text{whatever }\sigma_j . \ }
\]
Equivalently, \bk{(18.5)} gives $a^*_j=\sigma_j/(\sigma_j+0-0)=1$ for every $j$.  The absorption
rate $\sigma_j$ determines only how fast a country gets there --- the half-life is
$\log2/\sigma_j$ --- not where it ends up. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Why $g>0$ is different.}  In a steady state with a moving frontier every country must grow
at $g$, and \bk{(18.3)} says the country's own growth rate is
\[
\frac{\dt A_j}{A_j}=\sigma_j\Bigl(\frac{1}{a_j}-1\Bigr)+\lambda_j .
\]
Setting this equal to $g$ and solving gives $a^*_j=\sigma_j/(\sigma_j+g-\lambda_j)<1$.  The gap
is not a friction left over from history; it is the \emph{equilibrium price of keeping up}.
Absorption is proportional to the gap, so a country that wants to grow at the frontier rate with
a low $\sigma_j$ must run a correspondingly large gap to generate enough absorption flow.  A
country with a small $\sigma_j$, or a small $\lambda_j$, or facing a fast-moving frontier (large
$g$), needs a larger gap; if it ever closed the gap, absorption would stop and it would fall
behind again.

With $g=0$ the target stops moving, and the required absorption flow in the long run is zero.  A
zero flow is consistent with a zero gap, so the gap closes completely.  This is exactly the same
logic as in the Solow model, where the saving rate affects the steady-state capital-labor ratio
only because capital depreciates and the effective labour force grows: parameters governing the
speed of adjustment stop mattering for the destination once there is nothing to adjust to.  The
policy reading is stark: in a world of zero technological progress, barriers to technology
adoption would delay development but not make any country permanently poor.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{18.7}{}
From \bk{Proposition 18.1}, in the steady state
\[
y^*_j(t)=A_j(t)f\bigl(k^*_j\bigr)=a^*_j\,A(0)e^{gt}f\bigl(k^*_j\bigr)
\equiv e^{gt}\,y^*_j,\qquad
y^*_j=a^*_j\,A(0)\,f(k^*_j) ,
\]
which is the stated form.  The two factors depend on disjoint sets of parameters, so they may be
signed separately.

\emph{The technology factor.}  $a^*_j=\sigma_j/(\sigma_j+g-\lambda_j)$ and, as in Exercise 18.5,
\[
\frac{\partial a^*_j}{\partial\sigma_j}=\frac{g-\lambda_j}{(\sigma_j+g-\lambda_j)^2}>0,
\qquad
\frac{\partial a^*_j}{\partial\lambda_j}=\frac{\sigma_j}{(\sigma_j+g-\lambda_j)^2}>0 ,
\]
the first strictly positive precisely because $\lambda_j<g$.

\emph{The capital factor.}  $k^*_j$ solves $\phi(k)\equiv f(k)/k=(n_j+g+\delta)/s_j$, and
$\phi$ is strictly decreasing under \bk{Assumptions 1--2}.  Hence $k^*_j$ is strictly increasing
in $s_j$ and strictly decreasing in $n_j$ and $\delta$, and since $f$ is strictly increasing so
is $f(k^*_j)$.  Explicitly,
$\partial k^*_j/\partial s_j=-\phi(k^*_j)/(s_j\phi'(k^*_j))>0$.

\emph{Independence of other countries.}  \bk{(18.4)} determines $a_j$ from $(\sigma_j,\lambda_j,g)$
alone, and \bk{(18.1)} determines $k_j$ from $(s_j,n_j,\delta)$ and the path of $g_j$, which by
\bk{(18.4)} depends on country $j$'s own parameters only.  The frontier $A(t)$ is exogenous.  So
no parameter of any country $j'\ne j$ enters, and
$\partial y^*_j/\partial\sigma_{j'}=\partial y^*_j/\partial\lambda_{j'}
=\partial y^*_j/\partial s_{j'}=\partial y^*_j/\partial n_{j'}=0$. $\blacksquare$

\rmk{The independence result is a property of this reduced form, not of technology diffusion in
general, and it is the price paid for the tractability of \bk{(18.3)}.  Every country draws on a
frontier that no country's behaviour affects, so there is diffusion but no interaction.  Section
18.3.2 removes it by making $N(t)$ the average of the $N_j(t)$, at which point the block
recursiveness --- and with it \bk{Proposition 18.2} --- fails: Exercise 18.15 shows that each
country's parameters then move the world growth rate, and hence every other country's income.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{18.10}{}
\textbf{(a)}  Let the firm rent capital at $R=r+\delta$.  With one worker, constant returns give
an operating surplus
\[
\Pi(A_j)=\max_K\bigl\{F(K,A_j)-RK\bigr\}=A_j\,\pi_0,
\qquad \pi_0\equiv f(k^*)-k^*f'(k^*),\quad f'(k^*)=R ,
\]
so $\pi_0$ is the marginal product of a unit of effective labour and is common to all firms.  The
firm's technology is a match-specific state evolving at a rate that depends on \emph{this}
worker's human capital,
\[
\dt A_j=\sigma(h_j)\bigl(A(t)-A_j\bigr)+\lambda A_j .
\]
Write the firm's current-value Hamiltonian, with $q$ the shadow value of a unit of $A_j$:
\[
\mathcal H=A_j\pi_0-w+q\bigl[\sigma(h_j)(A-A_j)+\lambda A_j\bigr] .
\]
Free entry into firms drives the value of a match to zero, so the wage absorbs the entire flow
surplus,
\[
\boxed{\ w(h_j,t)=\underbrace{A_j(t)\,\pi_0}_{\text{value of marginal product}}
\ +\ \underbrace{q(t)\,\sigma(h_j)\bigl[A(t)-A_j(t)\bigr]}_{\text{value of the technology the worker absorbs}} \ }
\]
--- the decomposition the hint asks for; the purely local term $q\lambda A_j$ is not attributable
to the worker and is netted out of $\pi_0$ by the same free-entry condition.  The costate obeys
$\dt q=\bigl(r+\sigma(h_j)-\lambda\bigr)q-\pi_0$, so in a steady state with $\pi_0$ constant,
\[
q=\frac{\pi_0}{r+\sigma(h_j)-\lambda} ,
\]
and, using $A_j=a^*(h_j)A$ with $a^*(h)=\sigma(h)/(\sigma(h)+g-\lambda)$,
\[
w(h,t)=\pi_0A(t)\Bigl[a^*(h)+\frac{\sigma(h)\bigl(1-a^*(h)\bigr)}{r+\sigma(h)-\lambda}\Bigr] .
\]

\textbf{(b)}  Hold the firm's own technology $A_j$ fixed and let the frontier run faster.  Since
$a^*(h)$ is decreasing in $g$, a larger $g$ means a larger steady-state gap $A-A_j$ at a given
$A_j$, and from the boxed expression
\[
\frac{\partial w}{\partial(A-A_j)}=q\,\sigma(h)>0 ,
\]
so wages rise.  The gain is proportional to $\sigma(h)$: faster frontier growth is worth more to
workers who can absorb faster.

\emph{Returns to human capital.}  Differentiating the boxed expression,
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{\partial w}{\partial h}
=\pi_0\bigl(A-A_j\bigr)\frac{\sigma'(h)\,(r-\lambda)}{\bigl(r+\sigma(h)-\lambda\bigr)^2}>0,
\qquad
\frac{\partial^2w}{\partial h\,\partial(A-A_j)}>0 . \ }
\]
The return to human capital is \emph{proportional to the size of the unexploited technology
gap}, so anything that widens the gap --- a faster-moving frontier, a country further from it,
a sector undergoing rapid technical change --- raises it.  This is the Nelson--Phelps proposition
in its sharpest form: human capital is valuable because technology is changing, and its return
would vanish in a stationary world ($g=\lambda$, whence $A=A_j$ by Exercise 18.6).

\emph{Contrast with Chapter 15.}  There the skill premium rises because innovation is
\emph{directed}: an increase in the relative supply of skills raises the market size for
skill-complementary machines, $N_H/N_L$ rises, and with an elasticity of substitution above two
the induced price effect can even make the relative demand curve slope upward.  The mechanism is
the endogenous \emph{composition} of technology, it operates through machine prices, and it
disappears if technology is exogenous.  Here the premium responds to the \emph{rate} rather than
the direction of technical change; nothing is directed, the technology is entirely exogenous to
the worker, and the skill is rewarded for \emph{adoption} rather than for production.  The two
channels are empirically distinguishable: the Chapter 15 channel predicts that the premium tracks
relative skill supplies and the bias of new technologies, while the Nelson--Phelps channel
predicts that it is highest in fast-changing sectors and in countries far from the frontier, and
that the returns should show up in managerial and entrepreneurial positions and in the speed of
diffusion rather than in routine production.
\end{aex}

\solsection{18.3}{Technology Diffusion and Endogenous Growth}

\begin{aex}{18.11}{}
\emph{Step 1: profits and values.}  From the analysis of Section 13.1, the flow profit of a
technology monopolist in country $j$ is $\pi_j=\beta L_j$, \emph{independent of $N_j$ and of
time}.  The value of a variety therefore satisfies the Hamilton--Jacobi--Bellman equation
$rV_j=\beta L_j+\dt V_j$, whose only solution bounded on a path with a constant interest rate
$r=\rho+\theta g$ is
\[
V_j=\frac{\beta L_j}{r} \qquad\text{at every date, not only in steady state.}
\]

\emph{Step 2: the only common growth rate is $g$.}  Suppose country $j$'s technology grew
asymptotically at $g_j\ne g$.  If $g_j<g$ then $\mu_j=N_j/N\to0$, the research productivity
$\eta_j\mu_j^{-\phi}\to\infty$, and $\eta_j\mu_j^{-\phi}V_j>\zeta_j$ eventually --- research is
strictly profitable, so $Z_j$ is bounded away from zero and $\dt N_j/N_j$ is bounded below by a
number exceeding $g$, a contradiction.  If $g_j>g$ then $\mu_j\to\infty$,
$\eta_j\mu_j^{-\phi}V_j<\zeta_j$ eventually, free entry fails, $Z_j=0$ and $\dt N_j=0$, giving
$g_j=0<g$, again a contradiction.  Hence $g_j=g$ for every $j$.

\emph{Step 3: the steady state.}  With $\mu_j$ constant, free entry with positive research reads
$\eta_j(\mu^*_j)^{-\phi}V_j=\zeta_j$, and substituting $V_j=\beta L_j/r^*$ with
$r^*=\rho+\theta g$ gives \bk{(18.11)},
\[
\mu^*_j=\Bigl(\frac{\eta_j\beta L_j}{\zeta_jr^*}\Bigr)^{1/\phi} ,
\]
which is unique.  The transversality condition requires $\rho>(1-\theta)g$, which is assumed.

\emph{Step 4: convergence.}  Because $V_j=\beta L_j/r$ holds at every date, the free-entry
condition is an \emph{inequality in $\mu_j$ alone}:
\[
\eta_j\mu_j^{-\phi}\frac{\beta L_j}{r}\ \begin{cases}>\zeta_j & \text{if }\mu_j<\mu^*_j,\\
=\zeta_j & \text{if }\mu_j=\mu^*_j,\\ <\zeta_j & \text{if }\mu_j>\mu^*_j.\end{cases}
\]
So if $\mu_j(t)>\mu^*_j$, research is unprofitable, $Z_j=0$, $N_j$ is constant, and
$\mu_j(t)=\mu_j(0)e^{-gt}$ falls at rate $g$ until it reaches $\mu^*_j$ --- which takes exactly
$g^{-1}\log\bigl(\mu_j(0)/\mu^*_j\bigr)$ units of time --- after which it stays there.  If
$\mu_j(t)<\mu^*_j$, research is strictly profitable at any scale, so $Z_j$ is at its upper bound;
the only thing limiting it is the resource constraint \bk{(18.8)}, $\zeta_jZ_j\le Y_j$, so
$\mu_j$ rises at the fastest rate the economy's output permits until it reaches $\mu^*_j$.  In
both directions $\mu_j(t)\to\mu^*_j$ from any $\mu_j(0)>0$, which is the global stability claimed.
$\blacksquare$

\rmk{Two features of this argument deserve emphasis.  First, ``saddle-path stability'' here is
not the usual two-dimensional saddle: the value $V_j$ is pinned down at $\beta L_j/r$ by constant
profits, so the free-entry condition collapses to a condition on the single predetermined
variable $\mu_j$, and the dynamics are one-dimensional with a corner at $Z_j=0$.  Second, the
adjustment is \emph{asymmetric}.  A country above its target technology waits for the frontier to
grow past it, which takes time $g^{-1}\log(\mu_j(0)/\mu^*_j)$; a country below its target adjusts
as fast as its output allows.  So the model predicts fast catch-up and slow relative decline ---
a pattern quite different from the symmetric exponential convergence of the reduced-form
\bk{(18.4)}.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{18.14}{}
Define
\[
\Psi(g)\ \equiv\ \frac1J\sum_{j=1}^{J}
\Bigl(\frac{\eta_j\beta L_j}{\zeta_j(\rho+\theta g)}\Bigr)^{1/\phi} ,
\]
so that \bk{(18.13)}, the requirement that the $\mu^*_j$ average to one, is $\Psi(g)=1$.

\emph{Necessity and sufficiency.}  $\Psi$ is continuous and, since $\rho+\theta g$ is strictly
increasing in $g$ and $\phi>0$, strictly decreasing, with $\Psi(g)\to0$ as $g\to\infty$.  A
strictly decreasing continuous function attains the value $1$ at some $g>0$ if and only if it
exceeds $1$ at $g=0$, and then at exactly one point.  Hence
\[
\boxed{\ g^*>0\iff\Psi(0)=\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^{J}
\Bigl(\frac{\eta_j\beta L_j}{\zeta_j\rho}\Bigr)^{1/\phi}>1 , \ }
\]
which is \bk{(18.14)}. $\blacksquare$

\emph{When it fails.}  Suppose $\Psi(0)\le1$.  A steady state with $g>0$ is impossible, so
$\dt N(t)=0$: world technology is constant.  Since $\eta_j>0$, $Z_j>0$ would give $\dt N_j>0$ and
hence $\dt N>0$; so the asymptotic equilibrium has $Z_j=0$ and $N_j(t)=\ol N_j$ constant for every
$j$, with $r=\rho$.  Writing $\mu_j=\ol N_j/\ol N$ with $\ol N=J^{-1}\sum_j\ol N_j$, the
equilibrium is characterized by
\[
\frac1J\sum_{j=1}^{J}\mu_j=1
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\eta_j\mu_j^{-\phi}\frac{\beta L_j}{\rho}\le\zeta_j\ \text{ for every }j ,
\]
the second being free entry holding with \emph{weak} inequality --- no country finds research
worth undertaking.  Rearranged, the second condition is
$\mu_j\ge\ol\mu_j\equiv(\eta_j\beta L_j/(\zeta_j\rho))^{1/\phi}$, and such a configuration exists
precisely because $J^{-1}\sum_j\ol\mu_j=\Psi(0)\le1$.  Starting from arbitrary $N_j(0)$, the
countries with $\mu_j(0)<\ol\mu_j$ do research --- which raises $\ol N$ and so lowers every other
country's $\mu$ --- until every inequality holds; growth then stops permanently.  The world
technology level reached is not pinned down by parameters alone: it depends on the initial
$\{N_j(0)\}$, so history matters for the level but never for the (zero) long-run growth rate.

\emph{A calibration.}  In \texttt{code/ch18.py}, with $J=5$, $\beta=0.5$, $\phi=2$, $\rho=0.05$,
$\theta=2$ and the drawn $(\eta_j,L_j,\zeta_j)$, one finds $\Psi(0)=1.2712>1$, so \bk{(18.14)}
holds, and the unique root is $g^*=0.015399$, with $\mu^*=(0.892,\,1.006,\,1.112,\,1.433,\,0.558)$
averaging to one to twelve digits.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{18.15}{}
\emph{Existence and uniqueness.}  Suppose a steady state with common growth rate $g$ exists.
Then $N(t)$ grows at $g$ by \bk{(18.12)}, consumption grows at $g$ in every country, and the Euler
equation gives the common interest rate $r^*=\rho+\theta g$.  \bk{Proposition 18.4}'s free-entry
argument applies country by country --- profits $\beta L_j$ and values $\beta L_j/r^*$ are
unchanged --- and yields \bk{(18.11)}.  Dividing \bk{(18.12)} by $N(t)$ gives
$J^{-1}\sum_j\mu^*_j=1$, which is \bk{(18.13)}, that is $\Psi(g)=1$.  By Exercise 18.14, $\Psi$ is
continuous and strictly decreasing with $\Psi(0)>1$ under \bk{(18.14)}, so there is exactly one
root $g^*>0$; the assumption $\rho>(1-\theta)g^*$ makes the households' problems well posed.  All
remaining objects --- $\mu^*_j$, $r^*$, the allocation of each country's output between $C_j$,
$X_j$ and $\zeta_jZ_j$ --- are then determined uniquely.

\emph{Comparative statics.}  Differentiate $\Psi(g^*)=1$ implicitly.  Since
$\Psi(g)=J^{-1}\sum_j\mu_j(g)$ with
$\mu_j(g)=(\eta_j\beta L_j/(\zeta_j(\rho+\theta g)))^{1/\phi}$,
\[
\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial g}=-\frac{\theta}{\phi(\rho+\theta g)}\,\Psi(g),
\qquad
\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial\log\eta_j}=\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial\log L_j}
=-\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial\log\zeta_j}=\frac{\mu^*_j}{J\phi} .
\]
At $g^*$ we have $\Psi=1$, so $\partial\Psi/\partial g=-\theta/[\phi(\rho+\theta g^*)]$ and the
implicit function theorem gives
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{\partial g^*}{\partial\log\eta_j}
=\frac{\partial g^*}{\partial\log L_j}
=-\frac{\partial g^*}{\partial\log\zeta_j}
=\frac{\mu^*_j\,(\rho+\theta g^*)}{J\,\theta}\ >\ 0 . \ }
\]
So a higher $\eta_j$ or $L_j$, or a lower $\zeta_j$, in \emph{any} country raises the world
growth rate, which is \bk{Proposition 18.5}. $\blacksquare$

\emph{What the formula says.}  The elasticities are all proportional to $\mu^*_j$: a country's
influence on world growth is exactly its relative technology level.  Advanced, large and
unencumbered countries pull the world frontier; a small distorted economy barely moves it.  The
factor $1/J$ makes the total effect of a uniform proportional improvement in all countries equal
to $(\rho+\theta g^*)/\theta$, independent of $J$ --- so the result is about the composition of
world research, not its raw number of participants.  \texttt{code/ch18.py} confirms the formula
against a numerical derivative of $g^*$ to eight decimal places for each of the five countries.

\rmk{This is where \bk{Proposition 18.2}'s independence result dies.  A change in $\zeta_j$ now
changes $g^*$, hence $r^*$, hence \emph{every} $\mu^*_{j'}$ through \bk{(18.11)} --- with the
opposite sign, since a higher $g^*$ raises $r^*$ and so lowers each $\mu^*_{j'}$.  A policy that
improves one country's innovation environment therefore raises world growth and simultaneously
lowers every other country's relative technology level.  The two effects work against each other
for foreigners, and the net effect on their income \emph{levels} is a level--growth trade-off:
they grow faster forever but start from a lower relative position.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{18.17}{}
There is no international asset market in this model, so each country's interest rate is
determined by its own Euler equation.  If every country's consumption grows at the common rate
$g$, then
\[
r^*_j=\rho_j+\theta g ,
\]
and the free-entry condition \bk{(18.11)}, which is country-specific in every other respect too,
becomes
\[
\mu^*_j=\Bigl(\frac{\eta_j\beta L_j}{\zeta_j(\rho_j+\theta g)}\Bigr)^{1/\phi} .
\]
Averaging as in \bk{(18.13)}, the steady-state world growth rate solves
\[
\Psi_\rho(g)\ \equiv\ \frac1J\sum_{j=1}^{J}
\Bigl(\frac{\eta_j\beta L_j}{\zeta_j(\rho_j+\theta g)}\Bigr)^{1/\phi}=1 .
\]
Every step of Exercise 18.14 goes through verbatim: $\Psi_\rho$ is continuous, strictly
decreasing in $g$, and tends to zero, so there is at most one root, and there is exactly one
positive root if and only if
\[
\boxed{\ \frac1J\sum_{j=1}^{J}
\Bigl(\frac{\eta_j\beta L_j}{\zeta_j\rho_j}\Bigr)^{1/\phi}>1 , \ }
\]
the obvious generalization of \bk{(18.14)}.  The transversality conditions now require
$\rho_j>(1-\theta)g^*$ for every $j$, which binds first for the most patient country.  Given
$g^*$, each $\mu^*_j$ is determined uniquely, and the comparative statics of Exercise 18.15
carry over with
$\partial g^*/\partial\log\eta_j=\mu^*_j(\rho_j+\theta g^*)/(J\theta)$. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Why growth rates stay equal.}  Impatience enters only through $r^*_j$, which raises the
required rate of return on research and hence lowers the level $\mu^*_j$ at which free entry
binds.  It does not enter the \emph{spillover} term $(N/N_j)^\phi$, which is what forces the
common growth rate: a country that fell behind would find research more productive and would
expand until it grew at $g^*$ again.  So the whole effect of a higher $\rho_j$ is a permanently
lower relative technology level and hence a permanently lower income level --- an impatient
country is poor, not slow-growing.  This is the same logic as in \bk{Exercise 18.9}, and it is why
a stable world income distribution does not require equal discount rates or asymptotically equal
saving rates.
\end{aex}

\solsection{18.4}{Appropriate and Inappropriate Technologies}

\begin{aex}{18.20}{}
\textbf{(a)}  Country $1$ saves more, so it is always the leader and $k'(t)=k_1(t)$ for all $t$.
Its own technology is therefore always appropriate: $A(k_1\mid k_1)=A$ and
\[
y_1=Ak_1^{1-\alpha},\qquad
\dt k_1=s_1Ak_1^{1-\alpha}-\delta k_1
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
k^*_1=\Bigl(\frac{s_1A}{\delta}\Bigr)^{1/\alpha} .
\]
Country $2$ uses the leader's technology at a lower capital intensity and by \bk{(18.15)}
\[
y_2=Ak_2^{1-\alpha+\gamma}(k^*_1)^{-\gamma},\qquad
\dt k_2=s_2Ak_2^{1-\alpha+\gamma}(k^*_1)^{-\gamma}-\delta k_2
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
k^*_2=\Bigl(\frac{s_2A(k^*_1)^{-\gamma}}{\delta}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{\alpha-\gamma}} .
\]
A positive steady state exists if and only if $\gamma<\alpha$, so that the exponent on $k_2$ is
below one.  Since $y^*_i=\delta k^*_i/s_i$ in both countries,
\[
\frac{y^*_1}{y^*_2}=\frac{s_2}{s_1}\cdot\frac{k^*_1}{k^*_2},
\qquad
\log\frac{k^*_1}{k^*_2}=\frac{1}{\alpha-\gamma}\log\frac{s_1}{s_2} ,
\]
the second identity following from substituting $k^*_1$ into $k^*_2$ and collecting terms.
Therefore
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{k^*_1}{k^*_2}=\Bigl(\frac{s_1}{s_2}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{\alpha-\gamma}},
\qquad
\frac{y^*_1}{y^*_2}=\Bigl(\frac{s_1}{s_2}\Bigr)^{\frac{1-\alpha+\gamma}{\alpha-\gamma}} . \ }
\]
At $\gamma=0$ this is the Solow benchmark $(s_1/s_2)^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}$.

\textbf{(b)}  Country $1$'s dynamics are the textbook Solow ones: monotone convergence to
$k^*_1$ at the local rate $\alpha\delta$.  Country $2$ faces
$\dt k_2/k_2=s_2Ak_2^{-(\alpha-\gamma)}k_1(t)^{-\gamma}-\delta$, a Solow equation whose
\emph{productivity term falls over time} as the leader accumulates.  Starting from a common
$k_0$, both countries grow, but country $1$ grows faster and, as it does, it drags country $2$'s
total factor productivity down.  The local convergence rate of country $2$ is $(\alpha-\gamma)\delta$,
so an increase in $\gamma$
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{slows} country $2$'s convergence, since $(\alpha-\gamma)\delta$ falls --- at
      $\alpha=2/3$ and $\delta=5\%$ the rate falls from $3.33\%$ at $\gamma=0$ to $0.83\%$ at
      $\gamma=0.5$;
\item deepens the drag from the leader, since the elasticity of $y_2$ with respect to $k'$ is
      $-\gamma$;
\item and lowers $k^*_2$ and $y^*_2$, leaving country $1$ untouched.
\end{itemize}
The follower's transition is therefore both slower and aimed at a lower target, and for
$\gamma$ close to $\alpha$ it becomes arbitrarily slow.

\textbf{(c)}  Writing $E(\gamma)\equiv\dfrac{1-\alpha+\gamma}{\alpha-\gamma}$,
\[
E'(\gamma)=\frac{(\alpha-\gamma)+(1-\alpha+\gamma)}{(\alpha-\gamma)^2}=\frac{1}{(\alpha-\gamma)^2}>0 ,
\]
so $y^*_1/y^*_2=(s_1/s_2)^{E(\gamma)}$ is strictly increasing in $\gamma$, and
$E(\gamma)\to\infty$ as $\gamma\uparrow\alpha$.  With $\alpha=2/3$ and $s_1/s_2=4$,
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrr}
\toprule
$\gamma$ & $E(\gamma)$ & $y^*_1/y^*_2$ & $k^*_1/k^*_2$\\
\midrule
$0.0$ & $0.50$ & $2.00$ & $8.0$\\
$0.2$ & $1.14$ & $4.88$ & $19.5$\\
$0.3$ & $1.73$ & $10.96$ & $43.9$\\
$0.5$ & $5.00$ & $1024$ & $4096$\\
$0.6$ & $14.00$ & $2.68\times10^{8}$ & $1.07\times10^{9}$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
(Simulating the two differential equations reproduces these ratios exactly.)  The interpretation
is a \emph{multiplier on the saving rate}.  Without inappropriateness, a low saving rate costs a
country output only through the direct diminishing-returns channel.  With it, a low $k_2$ also
makes the frontier technology work badly, which lowers output further, which lowers saving in
levels, which lowers $k_2$ again.  The exponent $1/(\alpha-\gamma)$ rather than $1/\alpha$ on the
capital ratio is exactly this feedback, and it becomes infinite when the inappropriateness
elasticity $\gamma$ reaches the capital share parameter $\alpha$ --- the point at which the
private return to capital stops diminishing.

\textbf{(d)}  The mechanism is real --- technologies are designed for particular factor
intensities, and using them at other intensities does cost efficiency --- but as an account of
large cross-country income differences it has four problems.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{It is knife-edge.}  Large effects require $\gamma$ close to $\alpha$, and at
      $\gamma=\alpha$ the model has no steady state at all.  The text's illustrative
      $\gamma=\alpha=2/3$ is legitimate for a level comparison at a \emph{given} capital gap
      (where an eightfold capital gap gives an eightfold rather than twofold output gap), but it
      cannot be used for the steady-state comparison of part (a).
\item \emph{It has the sign of the diffusion externality backwards.}  Here the leader's progress
      \emph{hurts} the follower, because it moves $k'$ further away.  The rest of this chapter,
      and the Gerschenkron evidence behind it, says the opposite: a larger gap is an opportunity.
      Postwar OECD convergence is hard to reconcile with a model in which the leader's
      accumulation is a negative externality on everyone else.
\item \emph{It assumes no adaptation.}  Firms in poor countries routinely modify imported
      technologies for local factor proportions, and much of what multinationals do when they
      enter low-wage economies is exactly this.  A model in which adaptation is possible at a
      cost would convert the productivity loss into an investment, with much smaller long-run
      effects.
\item \emph{The measured losses are too large.}  A $\gamma$ of $0.5$ implies that halving the
      capital-labor ratio relative to the design point costs $29\%$ of total factor productivity,
      which is far more than plant-level studies of technology transfer find.
\end{enumerate}
The skill-mismatch version of Section 18.4.3 is the more promising member of the family: skill
gaps between rich and poor countries are larger, more persistent and harder to arbitrage than
capital-intensity gaps, and there the direction of technology is endogenous rather than assumed.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{18.22}{}
Throughout, suppress $j$ and $t$, write $I$ for the threshold, and use \bk{Proposition 18.6}.

\emph{The relative price index, \bk{(18.20)}.}  The Cobb--Douglas aggregator \bk{(18.16)} has
$\partial Y/\partial y(i)=Y/y(i)=p(i)$, so \emph{revenue is the same in every sector},
$p(i)y(i)=Y$.  From \bk{(18.18)},
\[
p(i)y(i)=\tfrac{1}{1-\beta}p(i)^{1/\beta}\bigl[N_L(1-i)l(i)+N_Hi\omega h(i)\bigr]
=\begin{cases}
\tfrac{1}{1-\beta}P_L^{1/\beta}N_LL/I, & i<I,\\[2pt]
\tfrac{1}{1-\beta}P_H^{1/\beta}N_H\omega H/(1-I), & i>I,
\end{cases}
\]
where both powers of $(1-i)$ and of $i$ cancel.  Equating the two gives \bk{(18.19)}.  The
threshold sector is indifferent, $P_L(1-I)^{-\beta}=P_HI^{-\beta}$, that is
$P_H/P_L=[I/(1-I)]^{\beta}$.  Writing $R\equiv(N_H\omega H)/(N_LL)$ and $\tau\equiv I/(1-I)$,
\bk{(18.19)} reads $P_H/P_L=(R\tau)^{-\beta}$, so
\[
\tau^{\beta}=(R\tau)^{-\beta}\ \Longrightarrow\ \tau^2=R^{-1}\ \Longrightarrow\ \tau=R^{-1/2} ,
\]
which is \bk{(18.21)}, and substituting back,
$P_H/P_L=\tau^\beta=R^{-\beta/2}$, which is \bk{(18.20)}.

\emph{Output, \bk{(18.22)}.}  Since $p(i)=Y/y(i)$, the numeraire condition is
$\int_0^1\log p(i)\dd i=0$.  Using
$\int_0^{I}\log(1-i)\dd i=-I-(1-I)\log(1-I)$ and $\int_I^1\log i\dd i=-1+I-I\log I$, write
$S\equiv1+(1-I)\log(1-I)+I\log I$, so the numeraire condition is
\[
I\log P_L+(1-I)\log P_H=-\beta S .
\]
From \bk{(18.18)}, for $i<I$,
$\log y(i)=-\log(1-\beta)+\tfrac{1-\beta}{\beta}\log P_L+\beta\log(1-i)+\log(N_LL/I)$, and
symmetrically for $i>I$.  Integrating and substituting the numeraire condition, the two price
terms combine to $-(1-\beta)S-\beta S=-S$, leaving
\[
\log Y=-\log(1-\beta)-1-2(1-I)\log(1-I)-2I\log I+I\log(N_LL)+(1-I)\log(N_H\omega H) .
\]
Now set $u\equiv(N_LL)^{1/2}$ and $v\equiv(N_H\omega H)^{1/2}$, so that \bk{(18.21)} gives
$I=u/(u+v)$ and $1-I=v/(u+v)$.  Then
$I\log(N_LL)+(1-I)\log(N_H\omega H)=2I\log u+2(1-I)\log v$ and
$-2I\log I-2(1-I)\log(1-I)=-2I\log u-2(1-I)\log v+2\log(u+v)$, so everything cancels except
\[
\boxed{\ Y_j=\frac{1}{e\,(1-\beta)}\Bigl[(N_LL_j)^{1/2}+(N_H\omega H_j)^{1/2}\Bigr]^{2} . \ }
\]
This is \bk{(18.22)} up to the constant, which the text prints as $\exp(-\beta)$.  The check is
the degenerate case $H_j=0$: then $I=1$, the numeraire condition gives $P_L=e^{-\beta}$ exactly,
and direct integration gives $Y=N_LL/[e(1-\beta)]$, confirming the constant above.  Nothing
in the chapter depends on it, since it is common to all countries and all technologies.

\emph{The skill premium, \bk{(18.23)}.}  With the machine price normalized to $1$ and marginal
cost $\psi=1-\beta$, the demand for each machine is $x=p^{1/\beta}\,i\omega h$ and revenue net of
machine spending is
\[
p\,y-\!\!\int_0^{N_H}\!\!x(\nu)\dd\nu
=\Bigl(\tfrac{1}{1-\beta}-1\Bigr)p^{1/\beta}N_Hi\omega h
=\tfrac{\beta}{1-\beta}p^{1/\beta}N_Hi\omega h ,
\]
so $w_H=\tfrac{\beta}{1-\beta}p(i)^{1/\beta}N_H\omega\,i$.  With $p(i)=P_Hi^{-\beta}$ the factor
$i$ cancels and $w_H=\tfrac{\beta}{1-\beta}P_H^{1/\beta}N_H\omega$ --- necessarily independent of
$i$, as it must be if skilled workers are to be indifferent across sectors.  Symmetrically
$w_L=\tfrac{\beta}{1-\beta}P_L^{1/\beta}N_L$.  Hence, using \bk{(18.20)},
\[
\frac{w_{H,j}}{w_{L,j}}=\Bigl(\frac{P_{H,j}}{P_{L,j}}\Bigr)^{1/\beta}\frac{N_H\omega}{N_L}
=R^{-1/2}\frac{N_H\omega}{N_L}
=\boxed{\ \omega\Bigl(\frac{N_H}{N_L}\Bigr)^{1/2}\Bigl(\frac{\omega H_j}{L_j}\Bigr)^{-1/2} \ }
\]
which is \bk{(18.23)}. $\blacksquare$

\rmk{The square roots in \bk{(18.22)} are not an assumption but a consequence of the linear
comparative-advantage schedule $(1-i,\ i\omega)$ in \bk{(18.17)}: the multisector economy
aggregates \emph{exactly} into a CES production function in $N_LL$ and $N_H\omega H$ with an
elasticity of substitution of $2$.  Replacing $(1-i,i)$ by a different pair of schedules changes
that elasticity, which is what the text means by saying the phenomenon is more general.  The
aggregation is what makes the model of Section 18.4.3 a directed technological change model of
exactly the Chapter 15 kind, with $\sigma=2>1$ so that the market size effect dominates.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{18.23}{}
\emph{Machine profits.}  A monopolist owning a skill-complementary variety sells to all Northern
sectors $i>I^n$.  From Exercise 18.22, the quantity demanded in sector $i$ is
$x_H(i,\nu)=p^n(i)^{1/\beta}i\omega h^n(i)=P_H^{n\,1/\beta}\omega H^n/(1-I^n)$, independent of
$i$, so total sales are $\int_{I^n}^{1}x_H\dd i=P_H^{n\,1/\beta}\omega H^n$.  With price $1$ and
cost $\psi=1-\beta$, the markup is $\beta$ per unit and
\[
\pi_H=\beta\,P_H^{n\,1/\beta}\,\omega H^n ,
\qquad
\pi_L=\beta\,P_L^{n\,1/\beta}\,L^n .
\]
Only Northern sales appear: there are no IPR in the South, so the Southern copier collects the
Southern profits.

\emph{Relative prices.}  Along a BGP with $\eta_L=\eta_H=\eta$ and the lab-equipment
specification \bk{(18.24)}, both sectors must be researched, so
$V_H=\pi_H/r=\pi_L/r=V_L$, that is $\pi_H=\pi_L$:
\[
\Bigl(\frac{P^n_H}{P^n_L}\Bigr)^{1/\beta}=\frac{L^n}{\omega H^n}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\boxed{\ \frac{P^n_H}{P^n_L}=\Bigl(\frac{\omega H^n}{L^n}\Bigr)^{-\beta} . \ }
\]

\emph{Relative technologies.}  Equating this with \bk{(18.20)} evaluated in the North,
\[
\Bigl(\frac{\omega H^n}{L^n}\Bigr)^{-\beta}
=\Bigl(\frac{N_H}{N_L}\frac{\omega H^n}{L^n}\Bigr)^{-\beta/2}
\ \Longrightarrow\
\Bigl(\frac{\omega H^n}{L^n}\Bigr)^{2}=\frac{N_H}{N_L}\frac{\omega H^n}{L^n}
\ \Longrightarrow\
\boxed{\ \Bigl(\frac{N_H}{N_L}\Bigr)^{*}=\frac{\omega H^n}{L^n} , \ }
\]
which is \bk{(18.25)}.  Substituting into \bk{(18.21)} gives
$I^{n*}/(1-I^{n*})=(\omega H^n/L^n)^{-1}$, that is $(1-I^{n*})/I^{n*}=\omega H^n/L^n$, and into
\bk{(18.23)} gives
\[
\Bigl(\frac{w^n_H}{w^n_L}\Bigr)^{*}
=\omega\Bigl(\frac{\omega H^n}{L^n}\Bigr)^{1/2}\Bigl(\frac{\omega H^n}{L^n}\Bigr)^{-1/2}=\omega .
\]
The skill premium equals the pure absolute-advantage parameter: at the BGP, directed
technological change has exactly undone the effect of relative supplies on relative wages.

\emph{Stability.}  Off the BGP the relative profitability of the two kinds of research is
\[
\frac{\pi_H}{\pi_L}=\Bigl(\frac{P^n_H}{P^n_L}\Bigr)^{1/\beta}\frac{\omega H^n}{L^n}
=\Bigl(\frac{N_H}{N_L}\Bigr)^{-1/2}\Bigl(\frac{\omega H^n}{L^n}\Bigr)^{1/2} ,
\]
using \bk{(18.20)} again.  This is \emph{strictly decreasing} in $N_H/N_L$: if the skill-biased
technology is ahead of its BGP level, the price effect outweighs the market size effect and all
research goes to the unskilled-complementary sector, pushing the ratio back.  Hence the BGP is
globally stable, and since the level of $N_H+N_L$ is a jump-free state with the value of research
pinned by $V=\pi/r$, the equilibrium is saddle-path stable in the usual sense. $\blacksquare$

\rmk{The exponent $-1/2$ is $1-\sigma/2$ with $\sigma=2$, the elasticity of substitution
implied by \bk{(18.22)}.  The knife-edge of Chapter 15 --- the strong equilibrium bias that
appears when $\sigma$ exceeds a threshold --- is therefore not reached here, and the comparative
statics are the ``weak bias'' ones: a higher $H^n/L^n$ raises $N_H/N_L$ one for one, which is
just enough to leave the skill premium at $\omega$.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{18.24}{}
\emph{Preliminary: net output is proportional to gross output.}  Since revenue is equal across
sectors, summing the two expressions in Exercise 18.22 gives
$P_L^{1/\beta}N_LL=(1-\beta)YI$ and $P_H^{1/\beta}N_H\omega H=(1-\beta)Y(1-I)$, so the total
number of machines used is $(1-\beta)Y$ and total machine expenditure at cost $\psi=1-\beta$ is
$X_j=(1-\beta)^2Y_j$.  Hence
\[
Y^N_j=Y_j-X_j=\bigl[1-(1-\beta)^2\bigr]Y_j ,
\]
a fixed multiple of $Y_j$.  Maximizing net output is therefore the same as maximizing $Y_j$.

\emph{Part 1.}  Fix $N_L+N_H=\ol N$ and maximize \bk{(18.22)} for the North.  Since $Y_n$ is a
strictly increasing transformation of $\sqrt{N_LL^n}+\sqrt{N_H\omega H^n}$, differentiate that
sum:
\[
\frac{\dd}{\dd N_H}\Bigl[\sqrt{(\ol N-N_H)L^n}+\sqrt{N_H\omega H^n}\Bigr]
=-\frac12\sqrt{\frac{L^n}{N_L}}+\frac12\sqrt{\frac{\omega H^n}{N_H}}=0
\ \Longleftrightarrow\
\frac{N_H}{N_L}=\frac{\omega H^n}{L^n} ,
\]
which is exactly the BGP ratio \bk{(18.25)}.  The objective is strictly concave in $N_H$ on
$(0,\ol N)$ --- it is a sum of two strictly concave square roots --- so this stationary point is
the unique maximum.  The BGP technology is therefore the one that maximizes Northern net output
given the total stock of technology.

\emph{Part 2.}  Write $E_j\equiv L_j+\omega H_j$ and $\lambda_j\equiv\omega H_j/E_j$, the share of
effective labour that is skilled.  Then $N_LL_j=N_L(1-\lambda_j)E_j$ and
$N_H\omega H_j=N_H\lambda_jE_j$, so
\[
y^{\mathrm{eff}}_j=\frac{Y_j}{E_j}
=\frac{1}{e(1-\beta)}\Bigl[\sqrt{N_L(1-\lambda_j)}+\sqrt{N_H\lambda_j}\Bigr]^{2}
\equiv\frac{1}{e(1-\beta)}\Theta(\lambda_j) .
\]
By the same computation as in Part 1, $\Theta$ is strictly concave on $(0,1)$ with its unique
maximum at
\[
\lambda^{\dagger}=\frac{N_H}{N_L+N_H} ,
\]
and at the BGP ratio $N_H/N_L=\omega H^n/L^n=\lambda_n/(1-\lambda_n)$, so
$\lambda^{\dagger}=\lambda_n$ \emph{exactly}.  Since $H^n/L^n>H^s/L^s$ implies
$\lambda_s<\lambda_n=\lambda^{\dagger}$, strict concavity gives
\[
\boxed{\ y^{\mathrm{eff}}_s=\tfrac{1}{e(1-\beta)}\Theta(\lambda_s)
<\tfrac{1}{e(1-\beta)}\Theta(\lambda_n)=y^{\mathrm{eff}}_n . \ }
\]
Finally, for income per capita,
\[
\frac{y_j}{y^{\mathrm{eff}}_j}=\frac{L_j+\omega H_j}{L_j+H_j}=\frac{1+\omega H_j/L_j}{1+H_j/L_j} ,
\]
which is strictly increasing in $H_j/L_j$ whenever $\omega>1$.  Both factors are larger in the
North, so $y_n>y_s$ as well. $\blacksquare$

\emph{What drives it.}  The whole result rests on the coincidence
$\lambda^{\dagger}=\lambda_n$: research firms sell only to the North, so they choose the
technology mix that is optimal for the Northern factor proportions, and the South is then
necessarily off the peak of a strictly concave function.  The size of the loss is governed by the
curvature of $\Theta$ near its maximum --- that is, by the elasticity of substitution of $2$
implied by \bk{(18.22)} --- and by the distance $\lambda_n-\lambda_s$.  In
\texttt{code/ch18.py}, with $\omega=2$, $H^n/L^n=2/3$ and $H^s/L^s=1/9$, the ratio
$y^{\mathrm{eff}}_n/y^{\mathrm{eff}}_s$ is $1.196$ at unchanged $N_H+N_L$: directed technological
change alone opens a $20\%$ productivity gap between two economies with identical access to
identical blueprints.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{18.25}{}
\textbf{(a)}  Let there be $J^n$ Northern and $J^s$ Southern countries, and write
$H^w=J^nH^n+J^sH^s$, $L^w=J^nL^n+J^sL^s$.  With IPR everywhere, a technology monopolist collects
the markup on every sale in the world, so the profits of Exercise 18.23 become
\[
\pi_H=\beta\,P_H^{1/\beta}\,\omega H^w ,
\qquad
\pi_L=\beta\,P_L^{1/\beta}\,L^w ,
\]
taking the hint's assumption that final good prices are common across countries.  The BGP
requires $\pi_H=\pi_L$, so $(P_H/P_L)^{1/\beta}=L^w/(\omega H^w)$, and combining with
\bk{(18.20)} evaluated at world factor proportions exactly as in Exercise 18.23,
\[
\boxed{\ \Bigl(\frac{N_H}{N_L}\Bigr)^{\mathrm{IPR}}=\frac{\omega H^w}{L^w}
\qquad\text{in place of}\qquad
\Bigl(\frac{N_H}{N_L}\Bigr)^{*}=\frac{\omega H^n}{L^n} . \ }
\]
The thresholds and skill premia follow from \bk{(18.21)} and \bk{(18.23)} country by country, now
with this common $N_H/N_L$.

\textbf{(b)}  Since $H^n/L^n>H^s/L^s$, the world ratio lies strictly between the two, so
\[
\Bigl(\frac{N_H}{N_L}\Bigr)^{\mathrm{IPR}}<\Bigl(\frac{N_H}{N_L}\Bigr)^{*} .
\]
IPR enforcement in the South makes world technology \emph{less skill-biased}.  By Exercise 18.24,
$y^{\mathrm{eff}}_j$ peaks at $\lambda^{\dagger}=N_H/(N_L+N_H)$, so lowering $N_H/N_L$ moves
$\lambda^{\dagger}$ from $\lambda_n$ down toward the world average, which lies between $\lambda_s$
and $\lambda_n$: the South moves \emph{closer} to the peak and the North moves \emph{away} from
it.  In the calibration of \texttt{code/ch18.py} ($\omega=2$, one Northern and one Southern
country, $H^n/L^n=2/3$, $H^s/L^s=1/9$, $N_L+N_H$ held fixed), $N_H/N_L$ falls from $1.333$ to
$0.667$, $y^{\mathrm{eff}}_n$ falls from $2.000$ to $1.941$, $y^{\mathrm{eff}}_s$ rises from
$1.673$ to $1.883$, and the North--South ratio narrows from $1.196$ to $1.031$.  Enforcing IPR in
the South is thus, in this model, a redirection of world technology toward Southern needs, and it
compresses the income distribution.  There is also a \emph{scale} effect: profits per variety are
higher, so $V$ is higher, free entry supports more research, and the BGP growth rate of
$N_L+N_H$ rises.

\textbf{(c)}  Yes --- and the answer turns on whether the South acts alone.  Enforcing IPR has two
effects on a Southern country.  First, a \emph{pure transfer}: without enforcement, the Southern
copier charges the same markup to Southern producers, so machine prices and hence the static
allocation are unchanged, but the monopoly rents accrue to a Southern firm instead of a Northern
inventor.  Enforcement hands those rents abroad.  Second, a \emph{redirection}: the country's
factor proportions now enter the world market size, which pulls $N_H/N_L$ toward the South.
\begin{itemize}
\item With a \emph{single small} Southern country, the redirection is of order $J^s/J^w\to0$ while
      the transfer is first order.  The country strictly prefers no enforcement.
\item With \emph{many} Southern countries enforcing together, the world ratio moves
      substantially, and the appropriateness gain can dominate the transfer.
\end{itemize}
Southern IPR is therefore a coordination problem with a free-rider structure: each Southern
country would like the \emph{others} to enforce so that technology is redirected, while itself
keeping the rents.  This is a precise reason why individually rational Southern policy resists
enforcement even when a collective Southern commitment --- of the kind a multilateral agreement
attempts --- could raise Southern welfare.  The growth effect noted in part (b) works in favour of
enforcement and matters more the more patient the South is.

\textbf{(d)}  For the North the two effects have opposite signs.  Northern \emph{innovators}
unambiguously gain, since they now collect the Southern rents.  Northern \emph{output at a given
technology stock} falls, because by Exercise 18.24 the North was sitting exactly at the maximum of
$\Theta$ and any redirection moves it off ($y^{\mathrm{eff}}_n$ falls by $2.9\%$ in the
calibration above).  Against this, the larger market raises the growth rate of $N_L+N_H$, which
benefits the North forever.  A level loss against a growth gain is settled by discounting, so for
$\rho$ small enough the North gains and for $\rho$ large enough it does not --- but the
distributional point inside the North is unambiguous: the gains accrue to the owners of
technology and the losses to Northern workers through the composition of technology.
\end{aex}

\solsection{18.5}{Contracting Institutions and Technology Adoption}

\begin{aex}{18.27}{}
With complete contracts the firm dictates every $x(i,j)$ and pays each supplier her outside
option, so \bk{(18.34)} binds and the problem reduces to the unconstrained \bk{(18.35)},
\[
\max_{N,x}\ A^{1-\beta}N^{\beta(\kappa+1)}x^{\beta}-\psi Nx-\Gamma(N)-w_0N .
\]
The objective is strictly concave in $x$ for each $N$, so the first-order conditions are
necessary and sufficient given the restrictions on $\Gamma$.

\emph{The condition in $x$.}
$\beta A^{1-\beta}N^{\beta(\kappa+1)}x^{\beta-1}=\psi N$, that is
\begin{equation}
A^{1-\beta}N^{\beta(\kappa+1)-1}x^{\beta-1}=\frac{\psi}{\beta} .
\label{eq:cx}
\end{equation}

\emph{The condition in $N$.}
$\beta(\kappa+1)A^{1-\beta}N^{\beta(\kappa+1)-1}x^{\beta}-\psi x=\Gamma'(N)+w_0$.  Multiplying
\eqref{eq:cx} by $x$ turns the first term into $(\kappa+1)\psi x$, so the left-hand side collapses
to $\kappa\psi x$ and
\[
\boxed{\ x^*=\frac{\Gamma'(N^*)+w_0}{\kappa\psi} \ }
\]
which is \bk{(18.37)}.  Substituting back into \eqref{eq:cx} and solving for $\Gamma'+w_0$,
\[
\bigl(N^*\bigr)^{\frac{\beta(\kappa+1)-1}{1-\beta}}A\kappa\beta^{1/(1-\beta)}
\psi^{-\beta/(1-\beta)}=\Gamma'(N^*)+w_0 ,
\]
which is \bk{(18.36)}.

\emph{Uniqueness.}  Write $\theta_0\equiv[\beta(\kappa+1)-1]/(1-\beta)$ and
$\Xi(N)\equiv\log\bigl(\Gamma'(N)+w_0\bigr)-\theta_0\log N$.  Then \bk{(18.36)} is
$\Xi(N^*)=\log\bigl(A\kappa\beta^{1/(1-\beta)}\psi^{-\beta/(1-\beta)}\bigr)$, and
\[
\Xi'(N)=\frac{\Gamma''(N)}{\Gamma'(N)+w_0}-\frac{\theta_0}{N}>0
\iff
\frac{N\Gamma''(N)}{\Gamma'(N)+w_0}>\theta_0 ,
\]
which is exactly restriction 2 on $\Gamma$.  So $\Xi$ is strictly increasing and \bk{(18.36)} has
at most one root; the boundary behaviour of $\Gamma$ gives existence, and $x^*>0$ follows.

\emph{Comparative statics.}  The right-hand side of $\Xi(N^*)=\log(A\kappa\beta^{1/(1-\beta)}
\psi^{-\beta/(1-\beta)})$ is strictly increasing in $A$ and $\Xi$ is strictly increasing, so
$\partial N^*/\partial A>0$; then
$\partial x^*/\partial A=\Gamma''(N^*)\,\partial N^*/\partial A\,/(\kappa\psi)\ge0$.  Finally
$\alpha$ appears nowhere in \bk{(18.35)}--\bk{(18.37)}, so
$\partial N^*/\partial\alpha=\partial x^*/\partial\alpha=0$. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Why $\alpha$ is irrelevant here.}  The elasticity of substitution between inputs,
$\varepsilon=1/(1-\alpha)$, enters the model only through the firm's ex post bargaining power
$\gamma=\alpha/(\alpha+\beta)$ of \bk{(18.43)}.  Under complete contracts there is no ex post
bargaining --- the firm is the residual claimant on every investment and pays each supplier her
outside option --- so $\alpha$ has nothing to act on.  The contrast with \bk{Proposition 18.11},
where $\partial\wt N/\partial\alpha>0$, is therefore the cleanest possible statement that the
effect of input substitutability on technology adoption is entirely a contracting phenomenon.

\rmk{Restriction 2 as printed, $N\Gamma''(N)/[\Gamma'(N)+w_0]>\theta_0$ ``for all $N>0$,'' cannot
hold at $N\to0$ when $w_0>0$ and $\theta_0>0$, since the left-hand side then tends to zero.  It
should be read as holding on the relevant range, or one should restrict attention to
$\beta(\kappa+1)<1$, in which case $\theta_0<0$ and the restriction holds automatically for any
convex $\Gamma$ and any $w_0\ge0$.  The numerical illustrations below use the latter.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{18.28}{}
Write $m\equiv1-\mu$ and $a\equiv\alpha(1-\gamma)=\alpha\beta/(\alpha+\beta)$, and note
$a<\beta$ and $a/\beta=\gamma$.  By \bk{(18.46)},
$\ol x_n(N,x_c)=\bigl[a\psi^{-1}x_c^{\beta\mu}A^{1-\beta}N^{\beta(\kappa+1)-1}\bigr]^{1/(1-\beta m)}$.

\emph{Step 1: a simplification of the objective.}  Write
$\Omega\equiv A^{1-\beta}x_c^{\beta\mu}N^{\beta(\kappa+1)-1}$, so
$\ol x_n^{1-\beta m}=a\Omega/\psi$, and the revenue term in \bk{(18.47)} is
$\Omega N\ol x_n^{\beta m}=\tfrac{\psi}{a}N\ol x_n$.  Hence \bk{(18.47)} equals
\[
\Pi(N,x_c)=\psi N\Bigl[\Bigl(\tfrac1a-m\Bigr)\ol x_n-\mu x_c\Bigr]-\Gamma(N)-w_0N .
\]

\emph{Step 2: the condition in $x_c$.}  Since $\ol x_n\propto x_c^{\beta\mu/(1-\beta m)}$,
$\partial\ol x_n/\partial x_c=\tfrac{\beta\mu}{1-\beta m}\ol x_n/x_c$, and
$\partial\Pi/\partial x_c=0$ gives
\[
\Bigl(\tfrac1a-m\Bigr)\frac{\beta}{1-\beta m}\frac{\ol x_n}{x_c}=1
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\boxed{\ \frac{\wt x_n}{\wt x_c}
=\frac{a\,(1-\beta m)}{\beta\,(1-a m)}
=\frac{\alpha(1-\gamma)\bigl[1-\beta(1-\mu)\bigr]}{\beta\bigl[1-\alpha(1-\gamma)(1-\mu)\bigr]} \ }
\]
which is \bk{(18.51)}, and is $<1$ because $a(1-\beta m)<\beta(1-am)\iff a<\beta$.

\emph{Step 3: the condition in $N$.}  Since $\ol x_n\propto N^{[\beta(\kappa+1)-1]/(1-\beta m)}$,
$\partial\Pi/\partial N=0$ gives, after substituting
$(\tfrac1a-m)\ol x_n=\tfrac{1-\beta m}{\beta}x_c$ from Step 2,
\[
\psi x_c\Bigl[\frac{1-\beta m}{\beta}-\mu\Bigr]
+\psi x_c\frac{1-\beta m}{\beta}\cdot\frac{\beta(\kappa+1)-1}{1-\beta m}
=\psi x_c\Bigl[\frac{1-\beta}{\beta}+\frac{\beta(\kappa+1)-1}{\beta}\Bigr]=\kappa\psi x_c ,
\]
so $\kappa\psi\wt x_c=\Gamma'(\wt N)+w_0$, which is \bk{(18.49)} --- \emph{identical} to
\bk{(18.37)}.

\emph{Step 4: the technology condition.}  Writing $\wt x_n=\rho_0\wt x_c$ with
$\rho_0=a(1-\beta m)/[\beta(1-am)]$ and substituting into \bk{(18.46)},
$\rho_0^{1-\beta m}x_c^{1-\beta m-\beta\mu}=a\psi^{-1}A^{1-\beta}N^{\beta(\kappa+1)-1}$, and
$1-\beta m-\beta\mu=1-\beta$, so
\[
x_c^{1-\beta}=\frac{a\,\rho_0^{-(1-\beta m)}}{\psi}A^{1-\beta}N^{\beta(\kappa+1)-1} .
\]
Comparing with \eqref{eq:cx} of Exercise 18.27, incomplete contracts replace $\beta$ by
$a\rho_0^{-(1-\beta m)}$.  Combining with Step 3,
\[
\boxed{\ \bigl(\wt N\bigr)^{\theta_0}A\kappa\beta^{1/(1-\beta)}\psi^{-\beta/(1-\beta)}\,\Lambda
=\Gamma'(\wt N)+w_0 ,
\qquad
\Lambda\equiv\Bigl[\frac{a}{\beta}\,\rho_0^{-(1-\beta m)}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}} , \ }
\]
so that $\Xi(\wt N)=\Xi(N^*)+\log\Lambda$, and \bk{(18.50)} follows from
$\wt x_n=\rho_0\wt x_c$.  Existence and uniqueness are as in Exercise 18.27, since $\Xi$ is
strictly increasing.

\emph{Step 5: the comparative statics.}  $\Xi$ is strictly increasing, so the sign of
$\partial\wt N/\partial z$ is the sign of $\partial\log(A\Lambda)/\partial z$.
\begin{itemize}
\item $\partial\wt N/\partial A>0$ and hence $\partial\wt x_c/\partial A\ge0$, exactly as before;
      and $\partial\wt x_n/\partial A\ge0$ since $\wt x_n=\rho_0\wt x_c$ with $\rho_0$
      independent of $A$.
\item $\Lambda=1$ at $\mu=1$ and $\Lambda<1$ for $\mu<1$, and $\Lambda$ is strictly increasing in
      $\mu$ and in $\alpha$.  The numerical table in \texttt{code/ch18.py} records, at
      $\beta=0.5$ and $\kappa=0.8$, $\Lambda=0.609,\,0.813,\,1$ at $\mu=0,\,0.5,\,1$ for
      $\alpha=0.3$, and $0.793,\,0.913,\,1$ for $\alpha=0.6$.  Hence
      $\partial\wt N/\partial\mu>0$, $\partial\wt N/\partial\alpha>0$, and
      $\partial\wt x_c/\partial\mu\ge0$, $\partial\wt x_c/\partial\alpha\ge0$ through
      \bk{(18.49)}.
\item $\partial(\wt x_n/\wt x_c)/\partial\mu>0$: from Step 2 the ratio is
      $\rho_0(m)=a(1-\beta m)/[\beta(1-am)]$ and
      $\dfrac{\dd\log\rho_0}{\dd m}=\dfrac{a}{1-am}-\dfrac{\beta}{1-\beta m}<0$
      because $z\mapsto z/(1-zm)$ is increasing and $a<\beta$; so $\rho_0$ is decreasing in $m$
      and increasing in $\mu$.
\item $\partial(\wt x_n/\wt x_c)/\partial\alpha>0$: $a=\alpha\beta/(\alpha+\beta)$ is increasing
      in $\alpha$, and $\rho_0$ is increasing in $a$ (numerator up, denominator down).
\end{itemize}
$\blacksquare$

\emph{Numerical verification.}  Maximizing \bk{(18.47)} directly over $(N,x_c)$ by
Nelder--Mead, with $\alpha=0.6$, $\beta=0.5$, $\kappa=0.8$, $A=3$, $\Gamma(N)=N^4/4$ and
$w_0=0.05$, reproduces the boxed closed form at every $\mu$ to six decimals, and confirms
$\kappa\psi\wt x_c=\Gamma'(\wt N)+w_0$ and the ratio \bk{(18.51)}:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrrr}
\toprule
$\mu$ & $\wt N$ (brute force) & $\wt N$ (boxed) & $\wt x_n/\wt x_c$ & \bk{(18.51)}\\
\midrule
$0.05$ & $0.973935$ & $0.973935$ & $0.38650$ & $0.38650$\\
$0.25$ & $0.993601$ & $0.993601$ & $0.42857$ & $0.42857$\\
$0.50$ & $1.013847$ & $1.013847$ & $0.47368$ & $0.47368$\\
$0.75$ & $1.030523$ & $1.030523$ & $0.51220$ & $0.51220$\\
$1.00$ & $1.044519$ & $1.044519$ & $0.54545$ & $0.54545$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\rmk{The exponent printed on the first bracketed term of \bk{(18.48)} is
$-\tfrac{1-\beta(1-\mu)}{1-\beta}$ applied to
$\tfrac{1-\alpha(1-\gamma)(1-\mu)}{1-\beta(1-\mu)}$, which is the reciprocal of what the algebra
of Step 4 gives.  With the printed exponent the implied $\wt N$ is $0.764$, $0.882$ and $0.959$ at
$\mu=0.05$, $0.5$ and $0.75$ against the true $0.974$, $1.014$ and $1.031$; the two agree only at
$\mu=1$.  The sign of the exponent does not affect any of the comparative statics in
\bk{Proposition 18.11}, nor \bk{Proposition 18.12}, since both forms give $\Lambda<1$ increasing
in $\mu$ and $\alpha$ --- it changes only the magnitude of the distortion.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{18.29}{}
Three claims, in the order in which they follow.

\emph{(i) $\wt N<N^*$.}  From Exercise 18.28, $\Xi(\wt N)=\Xi(N^*)+\log\Lambda$ with
\[
\Lambda=\Bigl[\gamma\,\rho_0^{-(1-\beta m)}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}},
\qquad
\rho_0=\frac{a(1-\beta m)}{\beta(1-am)},\qquad \gamma=\frac{a}{\beta}=\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+\beta} .
\]
At $\mu=1$ ($m=0$) we have $\rho_0=\gamma$ and $\Lambda=1$.  For $\mu<1$, $\Lambda<1$: the
supplier is not the residual claimant on her own noncontractible investment, so $\ol x_n$ falls
short of what the firm would choose, and since $x_c$ and $x_n$ are complements in \bk{(18.28)}
this lowers the marginal return to a higher $N$.  Since $\Xi$ is strictly increasing,
$\log\Lambda<0$ gives
\[
\boxed{\ \wt N<N^* . \ }
\]

\emph{(ii) $\wt x_c<x^*$.}  By \bk{(18.37)} and \bk{(18.49)} the two investment levels in
contractible activities are given by the \emph{same} function of the technology level,
$x=\bigl[\Gamma'(N)+w_0\bigr]/(\kappa\psi)$, evaluated at $N^*$ and $\wt N$ respectively.  Since
$\Gamma''>0$ and $\wt N<N^*$, it follows that $\wt x_c<x^*$.  Note what this says: for a
\emph{given} technology level the two environments would produce the same contractible
investment, so the entire shortfall in $\wt x_c$ is inherited from the shortfall in technology.

\emph{(iii) $\wt x_n<\wt x_c$.}  Immediate from \bk{(18.51)}, since
$\alpha(1-\gamma)=\alpha\beta/(\alpha+\beta)<\beta$, as shown in Exercise 18.28 Step 2.
$\blacksquare$

\emph{The chain of causation.}  Contractual incompleteness bites in exactly one place: suppliers
choose the noncontractible investments and capture only the fraction $1-\gamma$ of the revenue
they generate, so $\wt x_n<\wt x_c$.  Because inputs are produced by a Cobb--Douglas aggregate of
contractible and noncontractible activities \bk{(18.29)}, the shortfall in one lowers the
marginal product of the other, and hence the payoff to a wider range of inputs; so the firm
adopts a less advanced technology, $\wt N<N^*$.  The lower $N$ then feeds back into
\emph{contractible} investment through \bk{(18.49)}.  Measured productivity is
$\wt P=\wt N^{\kappa}<(N^*)^{\kappa}=P^*$, so weak contracting institutions show up in the data as
a total factor productivity gap even though nothing about the physical technology differs across
countries.

Figure~\ref{fig:ch18} plots $\wt N/N^*$ against $\mu$ for three values of $\alpha$: the loss is
largest when contracts are worst and when inputs are most complementary --- at $\mu=0$ and
$\alpha=0.3$ the firm adopts only $85\%$ of the first-best technology, while at $\alpha=0.9$ it
adopts $96\%$.  The interaction is the model's sharpest prediction: weak courts should depress
technology adoption most in exactly those industries whose inputs are least substitutable.
\end{aex}

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\includegraphics{figures/ch18_diffusion.pdf}
\caption{Left: the Basu--Weil steady-state income ratio against the inappropriateness parameter
$\gamma$ (Exercise 18.20), on a logarithmic scale; the exponent diverges as
$\gamma\uparrow\alpha$.  Right: technology adoption relative to the complete-contracts benchmark
