against the contractible share $\mu$, for three elasticities of substitution between inputs
(Exercises 18.28 and 18.29).}
\label{fig:ch18}
\end{figure}

\begin{aex}{18.30}{}
\textbf{(a)}  Labour is the only factor, and a worker is indifferent between the two occupations,
so $w_0=w$: the supplier's outside option \emph{is} the wage.  A product that adopts technology
$N$ employs $N$ suppliers and $\Gamma_0(N)$ adoption workers, so total labour demand is
$M[\Gamma_0(N)+N]$ and labour market clearing is
\[
\boxed{\ M\bigl[\Gamma_0(N^*)+N^*\bigr]=L . \ }
\]
The firm's condition is \bk{(18.48)} with $\Gamma'(N)=w\Gamma_0'(N)$ and $w_0=w$, that is
\[
\bigl(N^*\bigr)^{\theta_0}A\kappa\beta^{1/(1-\beta)}\psi^{-\beta/(1-\beta)}\Lambda(\mu,\alpha,\beta)
=w\bigl[\Gamma_0'(N^*)+1\bigr] .
\]
The equilibrium is the pair $(N^*,w)$ solving these two equations, together with
$\wt x_c=w[\Gamma_0'(N^*)+1]/(\kappa\psi)$ and \bk{(18.50)}.  Note the recursive structure: since
$\Gamma_0(N)+N$ is strictly increasing, market clearing determines $N^*$ \emph{by itself}, as the
unique solution of $\Gamma_0(N^*)+N^*=L/M$, and the firm's condition then determines $w$.

\textbf{(b)}  Therefore
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{\partial N^*}{\partial\mu}=0 . \ }
\]
Better contracting institutions raise the \emph{demand} for technology at any given wage, exactly
as in \bk{Proposition 18.11}, but with $M$ fixed the supply of labour to be divided between
adoption and supply is fixed too, so the technology level cannot move.  The entire effect is
absorbed by the wage: from the firm's condition,
$\partial\log w/\partial\log\Lambda=1$, so $w$ rises one for one with $\Lambda$.  The partial
equilibrium result of \bk{Proposition 18.11} is thus a statement about the \emph{returns} to
better institutions, not about the quantity of technology adopted, once the resource constraint
is imposed at fixed $M$.

\textbf{(c)}  If each product $z$ has its own $\alpha$, drawn from $G$, then each solves its own
version of \bk{(18.48)} facing the common wage $w$, giving $N^*(\alpha)$ with
$\partial N^*(\alpha)/\partial\alpha>0$ by \bk{Proposition 18.11}.  Labour demand aggregates
across products, so market clearing is
\[
\boxed{\ M\int_0^1\bigl[\Gamma_0\bigl(N^*(\alpha)\bigr)+N^*(\alpha)\bigr]\dd G(\alpha)=L . \ }
\]

\textbf{(d)}  Now a higher $\mu$ raises $\Lambda(\mu,\alpha)$ for \emph{every} $\alpha$, so at a
given $w$ every $N^*(\alpha)$ rises and aggregate labour demand exceeds $L$; the wage rises until
the integral is back to $L$.  The \emph{aggregate} amount of labour devoted to technology is
therefore again unchanged, but the \emph{composition} is not, because
$\partial\log\Lambda/\partial\mu$ is not the same for all $\alpha$: by the numbers in
Exercise 18.28, $\Lambda$ rises from $0.609$ to $1$ as $\mu$ goes from $0$ to $1$ when
$\alpha=0.3$, but only from $0.872$ to $1$ when $\alpha=0.9$.  Improving contracting institutions
therefore \emph{reallocates} technology adoption toward the complementarity-intensive products
--- those with low $\alpha$, for which the hold-up problem was worst --- at the expense of the
substitutable ones.  This is the general-equilibrium counterpart of Nunn's finding that
contract-intensive industries are disproportionately large in countries with good courts, and it
survives even when the aggregate scale of technology adoption is pinned down by resources.

\textbf{(e)}  Endogenize $M$ by free entry into product creation: let creating a product cost
$\chi$ units of labour, and require that the equilibrium profit of a producer, $\Pi(N^*,w)$ from
Exercise 18.28's Step 1, equal $\chi w$.  Market clearing becomes
$M[\Gamma_0(N^*)+N^*+\chi]=L$, and the two equations determine $(M,N^*)$ jointly with $w$
following.  The insight this generates is a \emph{variety--depth trade-off}: an improvement in
contracting institutions raises producer profits, which draws entry, which raises $M$, which
lowers $L/M$ and hence \emph{lowers} $N^*$ per product.  Better institutions may therefore show up
as a wider range of goods produced with shallower supply chains rather than as deeper supply
chains, and which margin dominates depends on $\chi$ relative to the curvature of $\Gamma_0$.
Since measured productivity in this model is $N^\kappa$ per product but total output also rises
with $M$, the two margins have opposite implications for firm-level and aggregate productivity ---
which is a useful caution when mapping \bk{Proposition 18.12} to plant-level data.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{18.31}{}
The model of Section 18.5 holds the organization of production fixed: arm's-length suppliers,
one-shot interaction, Shapley bargaining.  Each of those three assumptions can be relaxed, and
each relaxation is a substitute for formal contract enforcement.

\emph{1. Vertical integration.}  Integration transfers residual control rights over the
supplier's assets to the producer.  Model it as in Grossman--Hart--Moore: for an integrated input,
the producer can direct the activities that were noncontractible with an arm's-length supplier, so
the effective contractible share rises from $\mu$ to some $\mu^V>\mu$; but the supplier, now an
employee, loses her claim on the marginal return, so her bargaining share falls from $1-\gamma$
toward zero and the residual activities she still controls receive even less investment.  The
firm integrates input $j$ when the first effect dominates:
\[
\Pi\bigl(\mu^V,\ \gamma^V\bigr)>\Pi\bigl(\mu,\ \gamma\bigr) ,
\]
with $\Pi$ the maximized objective \bk{(18.40)}.  Two predictions follow.  Integration is more
attractive the lower is $\mu$, so weak-court economies should be more vertically integrated ---
which is what the data on firm boundaries across countries show.  And integration is more
attractive the higher is $\beta$ relative to $\alpha$, since $\gamma=\alpha/(\alpha+\beta)$ is
then low and the producer is capturing little of the surplus anyway.  Integration mitigates but
does not undo \bk{Proposition 18.12}, because the activities the producer cannot monitor even
inside the firm remain under-supplied.

\emph{2. Relational contracts.}  If the producer and its suppliers interact repeatedly with
discount factor $\delta$, the producer can promise a discretionary bonus $b$ conditional on an
observed but unverifiable investment, enforced by the threat of terminating the relationship.  The
self-enforcement constraint is
\[
\frac{\delta}{1-\delta}\Bigl[\Pi^{\mathrm{rel}}-\Pi^{\mathrm{spot}}\Bigr]\ \ge\ N b ,
\]
where $\Pi^{\mathrm{rel}}$ is the per-period profit under the relational contract and
$\Pi^{\mathrm{spot}}$ the one-shot profit of Exercise 18.28.  Whenever this binds slackly for
activities in $(\mu,\hat\mu]$, the effective contractible share rises from $\mu$ to
$\hat\mu(\delta)$, and everything in \bk{Propositions 18.11} and \bk{18.12} goes through with
$\hat\mu$ in place of $\mu$.  The comparative statics are the interesting part: since the
constraint's left-hand side is increasing in the value of the relationship and hence in $N$, while
the right-hand side is proportional to $N$, relational contracting is \emph{hardest to sustain
precisely where technology is most advanced} --- a large $N$ means many suppliers, each of whom is
individually small and easily replaced, so the rents at stake in any one relationship are thin.
This generates a complementarity between formal and relational enforcement rather than pure
substitution.

\emph{3. What this does to the cross-country predictions.}  Both margins compress the technology
gap that \bk{Proposition 18.12} attributes to contracting institutions, so the raw correlation
between $\mu$ and productivity overstates the causal effect of courts.  But both leave a residual,
and they predict \emph{where} it should be concentrated: in industries with many suppliers, high
supplier turnover, thin or spot markets, and inputs that are neither standardizable (which would
raise $\alpha$) nor producible in house (which would allow integration).  Other substitutes worth
modelling the same way are input standardization, which raises $\alpha$ and by
\bk{Proposition 18.11} raises $\wt N$ directly; third-party certification and reputation, which
raise $\mu$ at a cost; and business groups and family firms, which extend the relational
mechanism to a network by making deviations observable across relationships.
\end{aex}


%% ========================================================================
\solchapter{19}{Trade and Growth}

\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{0.86\textwidth}
\small
The twenty-two exercises of Chapter 19 left unsolved in the instructor's manual: 19.1, 19.5,
19.6, 19.8--19.10, 19.12, 19.14--19.23, 19.30--19.32, 19.35 and 19.36.  The manual solves 19.2,
19.3, 19.4, 19.7, 19.11, 19.13, 19.24--19.29, 19.33, 19.34 and 19.37.  The numerical parts are
computed by \texttt{code/ch19.py}; Figure~\ref{fig:ch19} is its output.
\end{minipage}
\end{center}

\bigskip
\hrule
\bigskip

\solsection{19.1}{Growth and Financial Capital Flows}

\begin{aex}{19.1}{}
\emph{\bk{Proposition 19.1}.}  Capital is rented by competitive firms in every country at the
world rental rate $r(t)+\delta$ --- free capital flows make the opportunity cost of a unit of
capital the same wherever it is installed.  Profit maximization by the representative firm in
country $j$,
\[
\max_{K_j}\ F\bigl(K_j,A_j(t)L_j(t)\bigr)-\bigl(r(t)+\delta\bigr)K_j ,
\]
has the first-order condition $F_K(K_j,A_jL_j)=r(t)+\delta$, and by constant returns
$F_K(K_j,A_jL_j)=f'(k_j(t))$ with $k_j\equiv K_j/(A_jL_j)$.  Hence
\[
\boxed{\ k_j(t)=f'^{-1}\bigl(r(t)+\delta\bigr)\equiv k(t)\quad\text{for every }j , \ }
\]
the inverse being well defined because $f'$ is strictly decreasing with range $(0,\infty)$ under
\bk{Assumptions 1--2}. $\blacksquare$

Note carefully what is \emph{not} equalized: $K_j/L_j=A_j(t)k(t)$ differs across countries in
exact proportion to $A_j$.  This is the observation that Section 19.2 turns into
\bk{Proposition 19.4}.

\emph{\bk{Proposition 19.2}.}  Each country solves
$\max\int_0^\infty e^{-(\rho-n)t}\frac{c_j^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}\dd t$ subject to \bk{(19.2)},
\bk{(19.4)} and \bk{(19.5)}.  The two constraints can be consolidated.  Multiplying
\bk{(19.4)} by the discount factor $\exp(-\int_0^t(r-g-n))$ and integrating, \bk{(19.5)} is
equivalent to the intertemporal budget constraint
\[
\int_0^\infty \exp\Bigl(-\int_0^t\bigl(r(s)-g-n\bigr)\dd s\Bigr)\,b_j(t)\dd t=a_j(0) ,
\]
so that the household may choose any path of $b_j$ with this present value.  Substituting into
\bk{(19.2)}, the country's problem is the standard one-sector problem with the \emph{additional}
intertemporal technology of borrowing at $r(t)$.

Now the Euler equation and the two first-order conditions give
\[
\frac{\dt c_j}{c_j}=\frac1\theta\bigl(r(t)-\rho\bigr),\qquad
r(t)=f'(k(t))-\delta ,
\]
where the second is \bk{Proposition 19.1}.  Both are the same for every $j$, so
$\dt c_j/c_j$ is common: the world behaves as a single closed neoclassical economy whose
aggregate effective capital-labor ratio is $k(t)$ and whose aggregate resource constraint is
obtained by summing \bk{(19.2)} and using $\sum_jA_jb_j=0$ from \bk{(19.6)}:
\[
\dt{\ol k}(t)=f\bigl(k(t)\bigr)-\ol{\wt c}(t)-(n+g+\delta)k(t) ,
\]
with $\ol{\wt c}$ the productivity-weighted average of $\wt c_j$.  Chapter 8 then gives a unique
steady state with $\dt c=0$, that is $r^*=\rho+\theta g$ and
\[
\boxed{\ k^*_j=k^*=f'^{-1}(\rho+\delta+\theta g)\quad\text{for every }j , \ }
\]
with all per capita quantities growing at $g$, and \bk{Assumption 4} ($\rho-n>(1-\theta)g$) makes
lifetime utility finite and the transversality condition hold.

\emph{Why $\dt a_j(t)\to0$.}  From \bk{(19.4)}, $\dt a_j=(r-g-n)a_j-b_j$.  In the steady state
$r^*-g-n=\rho+\theta g-g-n=\rho-n-(1-\theta)g>0$ by \bk{Assumption 4}.  Suppose
$\dt a_j(t)\to\alpha\ne0$.  Then $a_j(t)$ grows linearly in $t$ while the no-Ponzi condition
\bk{(19.5)} requires $a_j(t)e^{-(r^*-g-n)t}\to0$ --- which a linearly growing $a_j$ satisfies, so
\bk{(19.5)} alone is not enough.  Local nonsatiation makes \bk{(19.5)} hold with equality, which
combined with \bk{(19.4)} forces the present value of the $b_j$ path to equal $a_j(0)$ exactly.
If $\dt a_j\to\alpha>0$ the country is accumulating claims whose present value it never spends,
which is dominated by consuming them; if $\dt a_j\to\alpha<0$ it is running down claims at a
constant rate, which eventually violates \bk{(19.5)}.  Hence $\dt a_j(t)\to0$: in the steady state
each country's \emph{normalized} asset position is constant, and with equal $\rho$ there is no
systematic long-run lending. $\blacksquare$

\rmk{The last step is the one that fails when discount rates differ (\bk{Exercise 19.2}).  With
$\rho_j<\rho_{j'}$ the patient country's consumption grows faster than the world rate for ever, so
its asset position must keep rising relative to effective labour, and $\dt a_j(t)\not\to0$.  No
steady state with a nondegenerate world distribution then exists --- the same mechanism that
Exercise 19.9 exhibits in the Heckscher--Ohlin model of Section 19.3.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{19.5}{}
\emph{Why the interpretation fails.}  The neoclassical reading of a $2\%$ convergence rate is that
capital-poor regions have a high marginal product of capital, accumulate faster, and close the gap
slowly because of diminishing returns.  That story requires capital to be \emph{immobile}:
its entire content is that a region can raise its capital stock only out of its own saving.

\bk{Corollary 19.1} says that when capital is mobile the transitional dynamics of an individual
economy disappear.  The effective capital-labor ratios $k_j(t)$ are equalized at every instant by
\bk{Proposition 19.1}; if a fraction $\lambda$ of a region's capital is destroyed, capital flows in
immediately ($\dt a_j\to-\infty$) and $k_j$ is restored at once.  Between U.S.\ states the
conditions for mobility are as close to ideal as anywhere in the world: a common currency and
legal system, no capital controls, no exchange-rate or sovereign risk, an integrated banking and
equity market, and firms that operate across state lines.  Whatever is converging slowly, it is
not the capital stock.

There is also a quantitative embarrassment.  In the closed-economy model the convergence rate
near the steady state is approximately $(1-\alpha)(\rho+\delta)$ with $\alpha$ the capital share,
so matching $2\%$ per year requires $\alpha\approx0.75$ --- roughly twice the measured share.
This is the same puzzle that motivated broad capital in Chapter 3.

\emph{An alternative.}  What is slow across regions is \emph{technology}, not capital.  The
Chapter 18 model gives the natural formulation: let $A_j$ obey
$\dt A_j=\sigma_j(A-A_j)+\lambda_jA_j$ with $A$ the national frontier.  Capital-labor ratios adjust
instantly to the world (national) rental rate, while $a_j=A_j/A$ converges at the rate
$\sigma_j+g-\lambda_j$.  The observed $2\%$ is then an estimate of \emph{that} rate, which requires
no implausible capital share, and it is consistent with the microeconomic evidence in Section 18.1
that technologies diffuse slowly even within a country.  This reading also explains why
convergence is faster where the mechanisms of technology diffusion are stronger --- more
education, more mobility of managers, more entry --- rather than where saving rates are higher.

Two further candidates, not exclusive of this one: \emph{labour mobility}, which makes the
relevant adjustment migration rather than accumulation, so that the regression coefficient on
initial income partly measures the speed of population reallocation; and \emph{sectoral
reallocation} out of agriculture, which is a compositional convergence with its own time scale.
Distinguishing these is exactly the empirical programme the corollary points to.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{19.6}{}
\emph{The equilibrium.}  With $Y_j=AK_j$ in every country, the marginal product of capital is $A$
regardless of where or how much capital is installed, so the world interest rate is
\[
r=A-\delta\qquad\text{at every date and in every country.}
\]
Because $r$ is independent of the capital stock, \emph{there is no arbitrage incentive for capital
to move at all}: a dollar earns $A-\delta$ wherever it is placed.  Free capital mobility is
therefore vacuous here, and the no-flow allocation is an equilibrium (the location of the world
capital stock is indeterminate, and autarky is one of the indeterminate allocations).

Each country's wealth obeys $\dt W_j=rW_j-C_j$ with, from the CRRA Euler equation,
$C_j=\vartheta_jW_j$ and $\vartheta_j=\rho_j+\bigl(1-\tfrac1\theta\bigr)(r-\rho_j)$, which for
$\theta=1$ is simply $\rho_j$.  Hence
\[
\boxed{\ s_j=\frac{\dt W_j}{rW_j}=1-\frac{\vartheta_j}{r}
\qquad\text{and, in the no-flow allocation,}\qquad i_j=s_j . \ }
\]
Saving and investment rates are constant, differ across countries with $\rho_j$, and are
\emph{perfectly correlated in the cross section} --- with no financial friction whatsoever.  The
lesson is Obstfeld and Rogoff's: a saving--investment correlation is evidence of a common cause,
not of immobility.  Here the common cause is that the reason a country saves more --- patience ---
does not change the return, so nothing pushes its saving abroad.

\emph{Why this does not settle the puzzle.}  Feldstein and Horioka's regression, and the
literature after it, is not about the cross section of \emph{levels} but about co-movement in
\emph{differences}: within a country over time, changes in the saving rate are matched almost
one for one by changes in the investment rate.  In the model just written, $s_j$ and $i_j$ are
constants, so $\Delta s_j=\Delta i_j=0$ and the model has nothing to say about that regression.
It explains the level correlation and is silent on the one that is puzzling.

\emph{An extension that does.}  Let productivity be country-specific and time-varying,
$Y_j(t)=A_j(t)K_j(t)$ with $A_j$ following a persistent stochastic process.  Then
\begin{itemize}
\item a positive shock to $A_j$ raises the return $A_j-\delta$, so domestic investment rises ---
      and with a country-specific return, capital now genuinely wants to flow in;
\item the same shock raises current and permanent income; with $\theta>1$ the consumption response
      is less than one for one, so the saving rate rises too.
\end{itemize}
Both series move together in differences, with a coefficient that is closer to one the more
persistent the shocks and the larger $\theta$.  Nothing in this requires a barrier to capital
flows: the correlation in differences comes from the fact that investment opportunity shocks are
country-specific and are also income shocks.  The same construction with $\rho_j(t)$ stochastic
would generate a negative co-movement instead, which is how one could in principle separate the
two.  What the exercise establishes is that the Feldstein--Horioka coefficient is not, by itself,
a measure of capital market integration.
\end{aex}

\solsection{19.3}{Economic Growth in a Heckscher--Ohlin World}

\begin{aex}{19.8}{}
The rate of return to capital in country $j$ is \bk{(19.10)}:
\[
r_j(t)=p_K(t)-\delta\qquad\text{for every }j ,
\]
because capital is used only in the production of the capital-intensive intermediate and that
intermediate is freely traded at the world price $p_K(t)$.  Two consequences follow immediately.

\emph{No arbitrage exists to be exploited.}  Rates of return are already equalized at every date,
so an international bond paying $r(t)$ can be introduced only at $r(t)=p_K(t)-\delta$.  At that
rate no country has any incentive to take a nonzero position for \emph{arbitrage} reasons.

\emph{The bond is a redundant asset.}  The substantive question is whether the bond enlarges the
country's intertemporal opportunity set.  It does not.  The country's own capital already offers
exactly the same intertemporal technology: by \bk{(19.12)} and conditional factor price
equalization, one unit of the final good saved at $t$ returns $1+ (p_K(t)-\delta)\dd t$, and ---
crucially --- this return \emph{does not depend on the country's own capital stock}, since
$p_K$ is a world price and the country is small.  Each country therefore faces a linear (AK)
intertemporal technology at precisely the rate a bond would pay, so a bond spans nothing new.
Formally, replacing \bk{(19.11)}--\bk{(19.12)} by
\[
\dt K_j=F(X^K_j,X^L_j)-C_j-\delta K_j+B_j,\qquad \dt{\mathcal A}_j=r(t)\mathcal A_j-B_j
\]
and eliminating $B_j$ gives
$\dt{(K_j+\mathcal A_j)}=p_KK_j+p_LA_jL_j-C_j-\delta K_j+r\mathcal A_j$, which depends on
$K_j$ and $\mathcal A_j$ only through $K_j+\mathcal A_j$ when $r=p_K-\delta$.  Only total wealth
matters, and the split between domestic capital and foreign claims is indeterminate.  Hence
\[
\boxed{\ \text{the equilibrium allocation }\{c_j(t),\ x_j(t),\ p_K(t),\ p_L(t)\}
\text{ is unchanged.} \ }
\]
$\blacksquare$

The one caveat is the nonnegativity constraint $K_j\ge0$: a country that wished to hold negative
wealth could do so only with a bond.  On the equilibrium path wealth is strictly positive in every
country, so the constraint never binds.

\rmk{This is the sharpest statement of the theme running through Sections 19.1--19.3.  Trade in
goods and trade in assets are substitutes for the purpose of equalizing rates of return, and
either one suffices to make the world economy behave as a single integrated neoclassical economy.
Since Heckscher--Ohlin trade already does the job, observing little net capital flow in the data
is not evidence that capital markets are closed.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{19.9}{}
Suppose, for a contradiction, that a steady-state world equilibrium exists.  By definition all
prices are constant, say at $p_K^*$ and $p^*_L$, and each $k_j(t)$ and $c_j(t)$ is constant.
By \bk{(19.10)} every country faces the same rate of return $r^*=p^*_K-\delta$, so the Euler
equation of country $j$ gives
\[
\frac{\dt c_j(t)}{c_j(t)}=\frac{1}{\theta}\bigl(p^*_K-\delta-\rho_j\bigr) .
\]
Constancy of $c_j$ requires $p^*_K=\rho_j+\delta$ \emph{for every $j$}.  With $\rho_j$ differing
across countries this is impossible, so no steady state exists. $\blacksquare$

\emph{What happens instead.}  Order the countries so that $\rho_1<\rho_2\le\dots\le\rho_J$.  At any
constant $p_K$, country 1 grows at the highest rate and its share of the world capital stock rises
without bound.  Two things then happen.
\begin{enumerate}
\item By \bk{Proposition 19.7}, the world price satisfies $f'(x)=p_K$ with
      $x=\sum_jK_j/\sum_jA_jL_j$, so as country 1 accumulates, $x$ rises and $p_K$ falls toward
      the level $\rho_1+\delta$ at which country 1 itself stops growing.
\item At that price every other country has
      $\dt c_j/c_j=(\rho_1-\rho_j)/\theta<0$ and shrinks to zero.
\end{enumerate}
The limit is therefore \emph{degenerate}: the most patient country owns the entire world capital
stock and the world income distribution collapses.  Along the way the small-country assumption
fails --- which is the caveat the text attaches to \bk{Proposition 19.9}, and the reason the
``growth miracle'' there can only be a medium-run phenomenon.

\emph{Why the model is knife-edge.}  A country in this world faces an AK technology: its return
$p_K-\delta$ is independent of its own capital stock.  Nothing inside the country stops
accumulation, and the only restraining force is the world price, which responds to the world
capital stock and therefore treats all countries symmetrically.  With identical $\rho$ this is
enough; with different $\rho$ it is not, because a common world price cannot simultaneously
satisfy $J$ different Euler equations.  The model of Section 19.4 repairs exactly this by giving
each country its \emph{own} price --- its terms of trade --- which falls as it accumulates.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{19.10}{}
\emph{The closed economy.}  Without trade the country must use what it produces, so
\bk{(19.11)} is replaced by $X^K_j=Y^K_j=K_j$ and $X^L_j=Y^L_j=A_jL_j$.  Then
\[
x_j(t)=\frac{K_j(t)}{A_jL_j(t)}=\frac{k_j(t)}{A_j}
\quad\text{and}\quad
Y_j(t)=A_jL_j(t)f\Bigl(\frac{k_j(t)}{A_j}\Bigr) ,
\]
the standard neoclassical production function.  Factor prices are now domestic:
$R_j=f'(k_j/A_j)$ and $w_j=A_j[f-\frac{k_j}{A_j}f']$, both functions of the country's own factor
proportions.  The resource constraint and the Euler equation give the Chapter 8 system
\[
\dt k_j=A_jf\Bigl(\frac{k_j}{A_j}\Bigr)-\wt c_j-(n+\delta)k_j ,
\qquad
\frac{\dt c_j}{c_j}=\frac1\theta\Bigl(f'\Bigl(\frac{k_j}{A_j}\Bigr)-\delta-\rho\Bigr) ,
\]
with the unique saddle-path-stable steady state
\[
\boxed{\ f'\Bigl(\frac{k^*_j}{A_j}\Bigr)=\rho+\delta
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\frac{k^*_j}{A_j}=x^*\ \text{for every }j,\qquad y^*_j=A_jf(x^*) . \ }
\]

\emph{Comparison.}  Three differences matter and one similarity is worth stating.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The steady-state effective ratio is the same.}  \bk{(19.17)} gives
      $f'(x^*)=\rho+\delta$ under trade too.  So in the long run trade changes neither the
      world's effective capital-labor ratio nor the rate of return.
\item \emph{The transitional dynamics are completely different.}  In the closed economy the
      return $f'(k_j/A_j)-\delta$ is strictly decreasing in the country's own capital stock, so
      there is a within-country convergence force of the familiar strength and slow convergence
      to $x^*$ is the central prediction.  Under trade the return is $p_K(t)-\delta$, independent
      of $k_j$: there is \emph{no} within-country convergence force at all, only convergence of
      the world average (\bk{Proposition 19.8}).  This is the point Exercise 19.5 turns on.
\item \emph{The cross-country distribution is pinned down in one case and not the other.}  In the
      closed economy $y^*_j=A_jf(x^*)$: relative incomes are determined by technology alone and
      are independent of initial conditions.  Under trade, the steady-state conditions leave
      $(k^*_j,\wt c^*_j)$ satisfying the single equation
      $0=(\rho-n)k^*_j+p^*_LA_j-c^*_j$ per country, with only the world average pinned down by
      \bk{(19.18)}.  The distribution is therefore \emph{history dependent}: a country that starts
      rich stays permanently richer than its technology alone would warrant, and a transitory
      episode of high saving has permanent level effects (which is \bk{Proposition 19.9}).
\item \emph{A change in a country's discount rate.}  In the closed economy a fall in $\rho$ raises
      $k^*_j$ and produces a transition with a declining growth rate.  Under trade
      (\bk{Proposition 19.9}) it produces growth at the \emph{constant} rate
      $(\rho-\rho')/\theta$ for as long as the country stays small.
\end{enumerate}
Trade therefore does not change where the world ends up; it changes the speed with which
individual countries can get there, and it converts level differences from a technological
consequence into a historical one.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{19.12}{}
\textbf{(a)}  The only step in Section 19.3 that used $L_j(t)=L(t)$ is the last line of the proof
of \bk{Proposition 19.5}.  Redo it without that assumption.  Cost minimization in the final good
sector gives, exactly as before,
\[
\frac{f'(x_j(t))}{f(x_j(t))-x_j(t)f'(x_j(t))}=\frac{p_K(t)}{p_L(t)} ,
\]
whose left-hand side is strictly decreasing, so $x_j(t)$ is the same for all $j$.  World market
clearing \bk{(19.13)} then gives
\[
\boxed{\ x_j(t)=x(t)=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{J}K_j(t)}{\sum_{j=1}^{J}A_jL_j(t)}\quad\text{for every }j . \ }
\]
Define the world aggregates
\[
\ol A(t)\equiv\frac{\sum_jA_jL_j(t)}{\sum_jL_j(t)},\qquad
k(t)\equiv\frac{\sum_jK_j(t)}{\sum_jL_j(t)},\qquad
c(t)\equiv\frac{\sum_jC_j(t)}{\sum_jL_j(t)} ,
\]
so that $x(t)=k(t)/\ol A(t)$.  With a common $n$, $\ol A$ is constant and the proof of
\bk{Proposition 19.6} goes through verbatim with $A$ replaced by $\ol A$: summing the
country-level laws of motion and using the homogeneity of $F$,
\[
\frac{\dt c}{c}=\frac1\theta\Bigl(f'\Bigl(\frac{k}{\ol A}\Bigr)-\delta-\rho\Bigr),
\qquad
\dt k=\ol Af\Bigl(\frac{k}{\ol A}\Bigr)-c-(n+\delta)k .
\]
\bk{Propositions 19.7} and \bk{19.8} follow unchanged, with $f'(x^*)=\rho+\delta$ and
$p^*_K=\rho+\delta$.  Unequal populations change the weights in the world average and nothing
else --- large countries count for more in determining world prices, exactly as they should.

\textbf{(b)}  Now let $n_j$ differ, with $\rho>n_j$ for every $j$ so that each country's utility is
finite.  Two things change.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{The world average is no longer constant.}  Let $j^*=\arg\max_jn_j$ and suppose it is
      unique.  Then $L_{j^*}(t)/\sum_jL_j(t)\to1$, so $\ol A(t)\to A_{j^*}$: asymptotically the
      world's average productivity is that of the fastest-breeding country, whatever its level.
      A country with a high $A_j$ and a low $n_j$ becomes demographically negligible and stops
      influencing world prices.
\item \emph{A steady state in effective terms still exists.}  The Euler equations are still
      identical across countries (they involve $\rho$ and $p_K$ only), so $p^*_K=\rho+\delta$ and
      $f'(x^*)=\rho+\delta$ as before, and $x(t)\to x^*$.  What fails is stationarity of the
      \emph{composition}: $k(t)=\ol A(t)x^*\to A_{j^*}x^*$ and the world income distribution is
      dominated in the limit by country $j^*$.  Per capita consumption still grows at the common
      rate in every country, so the differences remain level differences.
\end{itemize}
The substantive point is that with a common $\rho$ the \emph{prices} are insensitive to
demographics, while the \emph{weights} are not; a model in which demography mattered for growth
would need $\rho_j$ or $A_j$ to respond to $n_j$.
\end{aex}

\solsection{19.4}{Trade, Specialization, and the World Income Distribution}

\begin{aex}{19.14}{}
Write $Z_j\equiv\bigl[\int_0^Nx_j(\nu)^{(\varepsilon-1)/\varepsilon}\dd\nu\bigr]^{\varepsilon/(\varepsilon-1)}$,
so that \bk{(19.24)} is $C_j=\chi(K^C_j)^{1-\tau}Z_j^{\tau}$, a Cobb--Douglas in two composites.

\emph{The inner nest.}  Minimizing $\int_0^Np(\nu)x(\nu)\dd\nu$ subject to $Z=1$ gives the
familiar CES demands $x(\nu)\propto p(\nu)^{-\varepsilon}$ and the ideal price index
\[
P(t)\equiv\Bigl[\int_0^Np(t,\nu)^{1-\varepsilon}\dd\nu\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}} .
\]

\emph{The outer nest.}  Minimize $r_jK^C+PZ$ subject to $\chi(K^C)^{1-\tau}Z^{\tau}=1$.  The
Cobb--Douglas first-order conditions make each factor's expenditure a fixed share of the unit
cost $c$: $r_jK^C=(1-\tau)c$ and $PZ=\tau c$.  Substituting into the constraint,
\[
\chi\Bigl(\frac{(1-\tau)c}{r_j}\Bigr)^{1-\tau}\Bigl(\frac{\tau c}{P}\Bigr)^{\tau}=1
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
c=\frac{1}{\chi}\,(1-\tau)^{-(1-\tau)}\tau^{-\tau}\,r_j^{1-\tau}P^{\tau} .
\]
So \bk{(19.27)} holds exactly, $B^C_j=r_j^{1-\tau}P^\tau$, if and only if
\[
\boxed{\ \chi=(1-\tau)^{-(1-\tau)}\,\tau^{-\tau} . \ }
\]
For the investment good, \bk{(19.25)} multiplies output by $\zeta_j^{-1}$, hence the unit cost by
$\zeta_j$, giving \bk{(19.28)}, $B^I_j=\zeta_jr_j^{1-\tau}P^\tau$. $\blacksquare$

\rmk{$\chi$ is not innocuous.  It is chosen so that the unit cost has \emph{no} $\tau$-dependent
constant in front of it, which means the model's normalization has removed the static level gain
that a higher $\tau$ would otherwise produce.  Since $p^I_j/p^C_j=\zeta_j$ regardless of $\tau$,
the consumption function \bk{(19.31)} gives $C_j(t)=\rho_j\zeta_jK_j(t)$, and \emph{every} effect
of openness on welfare in the AK version must therefore run through the growth rate $g^*$.  This
is what makes Exercise 19.23 as delicate as it is.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{19.15}{}
Write the household's problem as the maximization of \bk{(19.20)} subject to \bk{(19.21)}.  It is
convenient to use the value of the capital stock, $W_j(t)\equiv p^I_j(t)K_j(t)$, as the state.
The rate of return on a unit of that value is the rental yield plus the capital gain,
\[
R_j(t)\ \equiv\ \frac{r_j(t)}{p^I_j(t)}+\frac{\dt p^I_j(t)}{p^I_j(t)} ,
\]
and \bk{(19.21)} becomes $\dt W_j=R_jW_j-p^C_jC_j$.  The current-value Hamiltonian is
$\mathcal H=\log C_j+\lambda_j\bigl[R_jW_j-p^C_jC_j\bigr]$, with
\[
\frac{1}{C_j}=\lambda_jp^C_j,\qquad
\dt\lambda_j=\rho_j\lambda_j-\lambda_jR_j .
\]
Taking logarithms of the first, differentiating and substituting the second,
\[
-\frac{\dt C_j}{C_j}=\frac{\dt\lambda_j}{\lambda_j}+\frac{\dt p^C_j}{p^C_j}
=\rho_j-R_j+\frac{\dt p^C_j}{p^C_j} ,
\]
that is
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{r_j(t)}{p^I_j(t)}+\frac{\dt p^I_j(t)}{p^I_j(t)}-\frac{\dt p^C_j(t)}{p^C_j(t)}
=\rho_j+\frac{\dt C_j(t)}{C_j(t)} , \ }
\]
which is \bk{(19.29)}.  The transversality condition of the maximum principle is
\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-\rho_jt}\lambda_j(t)W_j(t)=0 ,
\]
and substituting $\lambda_j=1/(p^C_jC_j)$ and $W_j=p^I_jK_j$ gives
\[
\lim_{t\to\infty}\Bigl[\exp(-\rho_jt)\,\frac{p^I_j(t)K_j(t)}{p^C_j(t)C_j(t)}\Bigr]=0 ,
\]
which is \bk{(19.30)}. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Integrating to the consumption function.}  Solving $\dt\lambda_j=(\rho_j-R_j)\lambda_j$ and
using \bk{(19.30)} to rule out explosive paths, the intertemporal budget constraint
$\int_0^\infty e^{-\int_0^tR_j}p^C_jC_j\dd t=W_j(0)$ combines with
$p^C_jC_j=\lambda_j^{-1}=\lambda_j(0)^{-1}e^{(R_j-\rho_j)t}\!\cdot$ to give
$p^C_j(t)C_j(t)=\rho_jp^I_j(t)K_j(t)$, which is \bk{(19.31)}: with logarithmic utility the
household spends the fraction $\rho_j$ of its wealth at every instant, whatever the path of
returns.

\rmk{The symbol $r_j$ does double duty in this section.  In \bk{(19.23)} it is the rental
\emph{price} of capital --- and hence, by the one-for-one technology, the price of country $j$'s
intermediates.  In \bk{(19.29)}, \bk{(19.43)} and \bk{(19.47)} it must be read as the \emph{rate of
return} on the value of capital, $r_j/p^I_j=\zeta_j^{-1}r_j^{\tau}$, since $p^I_j=\zeta_jr_j^{1-\tau}$
by \bk{(19.33)}.  With the first reading, \bk{(19.29)} would give $\dt K_j/K_j=r_j-\rho_j$, which
contradicts \bk{(19.35)}.  Everything is consistent once the substitution is made, and
\bk{(19.35)} is stated in the correct form.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{19.16}{}
\textbf{(a)}  Cobb--Douglas cost shares give the capital used in each activity.  In the
consumption and investment sectors capital takes the share $1-\tau$ of expenditure,
\[
r_jK^C_j=(1-\tau)\,p^C_jC_j,\qquad r_jK^I_j=(1-\tau)\,p^I_jI_j ,
\]
and the capital used to produce intermediates is valued at their sales.  Each country spends the
share $\tau$ of its income on the world bundle of intermediates, so world spending on the $\mu_j$
intermediates produced by country $j$ --- which by the CES demand system is the fraction
$\mu_jp_j^{1-\varepsilon}$ of all intermediate spending, using the normalization \bk{(19.32)} --- is
\[
r_jK^{\mu}_j=\tau\,\mu_jr_j^{1-\varepsilon}\,Y(t) ,
\]
where $p_j=r_j$ by \bk{(19.23)}.  Adding the three and imposing \bk{(19.26)} with equality,
\[
r_jK_j=(1-\tau)\bigl(p^C_jC_j+p^I_jI_j\bigr)+\tau\mu_jr_j^{1-\varepsilon}Y
=(1-\tau)Y_j+\tau\mu_jr_j^{1-\varepsilon}Y ,
\]
using \bk{(19.21)}.  Since $Y_j=r_jK_j$ in the AK version, the left-hand side is $Y_j$, and
cancelling $(1-\tau)Y_j$ and dividing by $\tau$,
\[
\boxed{\ Y_j(t)=\mu_j\,r_j(t)^{1-\varepsilon}\,Y(t) , \ }
\]
which is \bk{(19.34)}.  Walras's Law is thus verified: capital market clearing and trade balance
are the same equation.

\textbf{(b)}  Country $j$ spends $\tau Y_j$ on intermediates, of which the fraction
$\mu_jr_j^{1-\varepsilon}=y^*_j$ is on its own goods.  So
\[
\text{imports}=\tau\bigl(1-y^*_j\bigr)Y_j,\qquad
\boxed{\ \frac{\text{imports}}{\text{GDP}}=\tau\bigl(1-y^*_j\bigr)\ \approx\ \tau\ }
\]
for a small country.  Exports are the rest of the world's spending on $j$'s goods,
$\tau y^*_j(Y-Y_j)$, and setting exports equal to imports reproduces $Y_j=y^*_jY$ --- balanced
trade again.  The parameter $\tau$ is therefore directly measurable as the share of imported
intermediates in GDP, which is what makes the calibration in Exercise 19.19 possible.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{19.17}{}
\emph{Reduction to the simplex.}  Let $y_j(t)\equiv Y_j(t)/Y(t)$ be country $j$'s share of world
income, so that $y\in\Delta^{J-1}$.  \bk{(19.36)} says $y_j=\mu_jr_j^{1-\varepsilon}$, which inverts
to
\[
r_j=\Bigl(\frac{\mu_j}{y_j}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon-1}} ,
\]
a strictly decreasing function of the country's own income share --- this is the terms-of-trade
effect, stated as an identity.  From $Y_j=r_jK_j$ and \bk{(19.35)},
\[
\frac{\dt Y_j}{Y_j}=\frac{\dt r_j}{r_j}+\frac{\dt K_j}{K_j}
=-\frac{1}{\varepsilon-1}\frac{\dt y_j}{y_j}+\frac{r_j^{\tau}}{\zeta_j}-\rho_j ,
\]
while by definition $\dt Y_j/Y_j=\dt y_j/y_j+g(t)$ with $g\equiv\dt Y/Y$.  Solving,
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\ \frac{\dt y_j}{y_j}=\frac{\varepsilon-1}{\varepsilon}\Bigl[h_j(y_j)-\bar h(y)\Bigr],
\qquad
h_j(y)\equiv\frac{1}{\zeta_j}\Bigl(\frac{\mu_j}{y}\Bigr)^{\frac{\tau}{\varepsilon-1}}-\rho_j ,\ }
\label{eq:rep}
\end{equation}
where $\bar h(y)=\sum_iy_ih_i(y_i)$ is forced by $\sum_j\dt y_j=0$, and $\bar h(y)=g(t)$.  This is
a \emph{replicator equation} on the simplex, autonomous, with each $h_j$ continuous and strictly
decreasing.  There is no jump variable: \bk{(19.31)} pins consumption to the predetermined capital
stock, so the whole equilibrium is the solution of \eqref{eq:rep} from the given $y(0)$.

\emph{The rest point.}  $\dt y=0$ on the interior requires $h_j(y_j)=\bar h$ for all $j$; calling
the common value $g^*$ and inverting $h_j$ gives
$y^*_j=\mu_j[\zeta_j(\rho_j+g^*)]^{(1-\varepsilon)/\tau}$, which is \bk{(19.40)}, and
$\sum_jy^*_j=1$ is \bk{(19.38)}.  Since each $h_j$ is strictly decreasing, $y^*_j$ is a strictly
decreasing function of $g^*$, so $\sum_jy^*_j(g)$ is strictly decreasing and the rest point is
unique.

\emph{A Lyapunov function.}  Take the Kullback--Leibler divergence of $y$ from $y^*$,
\[
V(y)\equiv\sum_{j=1}^{J}y^*_j\log\frac{y^*_j}{y_j}\ \ge\ 0,
\qquad V(y)=0\iff y=y^* ,
\]
which is continuous on the interior of the simplex and tends to $+\infty$ at its boundary.  Along
\eqref{eq:rep}, and using $\sum_jy^*_j=1$ and $\sum_j(y^*_j-y_j)=0$,
\[
\dt V=-\sum_jy^*_j\frac{\dt y_j}{y_j}
=-\frac{\varepsilon-1}{\varepsilon}\Bigl[\sum_jy^*_jh_j(y_j)-\bar h(y)\Bigr]
=-\frac{\varepsilon-1}{\varepsilon}\sum_j\bigl(y^*_j-y_j\bigr)\bigl[h_j(y_j)-g^*\bigr] .
\]
Each summand is strictly positive unless $y_j=y^*_j$: if $y_j<y^*_j$ then, $h_j$ being strictly
decreasing and $h_j(y^*_j)=g^*$, we have $h_j(y_j)>g^*$, so both factors are positive; if
$y_j>y^*_j$ both are negative.  Hence
\[
\boxed{\ \dt V<0\ \text{ for every } y\ne y^*\ \text{ in the interior of }\Delta^{J-1} . \ }
\]
$V$ is a strict Lyapunov function with compact sublevel sets, so $y^*$ is globally asymptotically
stable on the interior, and $g(t)=\bar h(y(t))\to g^*$. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Verification.}  \texttt{code/ch19.py} integrates \eqref{eq:rep} for $J=6$ with
$\varepsilon=4$, $\tau=0.3$ and drawn $(\mu_j,\zeta_j,\rho_j)$, from the uniform distribution and
from random Dirichlet draws.  Every trajectory reaches \bk{(19.40)} to within $3\times10^{-14}$,
$V$ falls monotonically along each of them, and the realized growth rate converges to
$g^*=0.024275$.  The left panel of Figure~\ref{fig:ch19} shows the shares.

\rmk{The reduction to a replicator equation explains the economics in one line.  A country's
``fitness'' $h_j(y_j)$ is its own growth rate $\zeta_j^{-1}r_j^{\tau}-\rho_j$, and it is
\emph{decreasing in the country's own share} because a larger share means worse terms of trade.
Shares grow when fitness is above the average and shrink when it is below, and a strictly
decreasing fitness is exactly frequency-dependent selection with a stable polymorphic rest point.
It is the terms-of-trade effect, and nothing else, that supplies the monotonicity.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{19.18}{}
\emph{Autarky.}  Country $j$ now uses only the $\mu_j$ intermediates it can produce, all priced at
$r_j$, so the ideal price index of the bundle it buys is
\[
P_j=\Bigl[\int_0^{\mu_j}r_j^{1-\varepsilon}\dd\nu\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}}
=\mu_j^{-\frac{1}{\varepsilon-1}}r_j ,
\]
rather than the world index $1$.  The unit cost functions \bk{(19.27)}--\bk{(19.28)} become
\[
p^C_j=r_j^{1-\tau}P_j^{\tau}=\mu_j^{-\frac{\tau}{\varepsilon-1}}r_j ,
\qquad
p^I_j=\zeta_j\mu_j^{-\frac{\tau}{\varepsilon-1}}r_j ,
\]
so the rate of return on the value of capital is
\[
\frac{r_j}{p^I_j}=\frac{\mu_j^{\tau/(\varepsilon-1)}}{\zeta_j} ,
\]
\emph{independent of $r_j$}: a pure AK economy, exactly as the text says each country would be
without the terms-of-trade effect.  From \bk{(19.35)} with $p^I_j$ replaced by its autarky value,
\[
\boxed{\ g^A_j=\frac{\mu_j^{\tau/(\varepsilon-1)}}{\zeta_j}-\rho_j , \ }
\]
which differs across countries whenever $(\mu_j,\zeta_j,\rho_j)$ do.  There is no world income
distribution: relative incomes diverge at the rate $g^A_j-g^A_{j'}$, and the most advanced,
least distorted, most patient country eventually has all of world output.

\emph{Comparison.}  Solve the boxed equation for $\zeta_j$ and substitute into \bk{(19.38)}.  Since
$\zeta_j(\rho_j+g^*)=\mu_j^{\tau/(\varepsilon-1)}\frac{\rho_j+g^*}{\rho_j+g^A_j}$, the term
$\mu_j[\zeta_j(\rho_j+g^*)]^{(1-\varepsilon)/\tau}$ collapses to
$\bigl[\frac{\rho_j+g^A_j}{\rho_j+g^*}\bigr]^{(\varepsilon-1)/\tau}$ and \bk{(19.38)} becomes
\[
\boxed{\ \sum_{j=1}^{J}\Bigl[\frac{\rho_j+g^A_j}{\rho_j+g^*}\Bigr]^{\frac{\varepsilon-1}{\tau}}=1 . \ }
\]
With $J\ge2$ these are $J$ strictly positive numbers summing to one, so \emph{every} one of them
is strictly below one, and therefore
\[
g^*>g^A_j\qquad\text{for every }j .
\]
The world growth rate under trade exceeds the autarky growth rate of \emph{every} country,
including the fastest-growing one.  The intuition is love of variety: in autarky the effective
productivity of capital is $\mu_j^{\tau/(\varepsilon-1)}/\zeta_j$ and under trade it is governed by
the whole world stock $N=\sum_j\mu_j$; the terms-of-trade effect redistributes the gain but does
not destroy it.

\emph{Welfare.}  Since $p^I_j/p^C_j=\zeta_j$ in \emph{both} regimes, \bk{(19.31)} gives
$C_j(t)=\rho_j\zeta_jK_j(t)$ in both, and with $K_j(0)$ given, steady-state welfare is
\[
U_j=\frac{\log\bigl(\rho_j\zeta_jK_j(0)\bigr)}{\rho_j}+\frac{g}{\rho_j^{2}} ,
\qquad g\in\{g^A_j,\ g^*\} .
\]
It is strictly increasing in $g$, so
\[
\boxed{\ \text{no: every country, including the fastest-growing one, strictly prefers trade.} \ }
\]
The calibration in \texttt{code/ch19.py} (with $\varepsilon=4$, $\tau=0.3$ and six drawn
countries) gives $g^*=0.024275$ against autarky rates ranging from $-0.014$ to $0.018$, and the
displayed identity holds to twelve digits.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{19.19}{}
\textbf{(a)}  \bk{(19.40)} is immediate: \bk{(19.34)} gives $Y_j/Y=\mu_jr_j^{1-\varepsilon}$, and
substituting the steady-state terms of trade \bk{(19.39)}, $r^*_j=[\zeta_j(\rho_j+g^*)]^{1/\tau}$,
\[
\boxed{\ y^*_j=\mu_j\bigl[\zeta_j(\rho_j+g^*)\bigr]^{\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\tau}} . \ }
\]
For \bk{(19.41)}, take the Solow model with $Y_j=K_j^{\alpha}(A_jL_j)^{1-\alpha}$, saving rate
$s_j$, population growth $n$, depreciation $\delta$ and common technology growth $g^*$.  The
steady-state effective capital-labor ratio solves $s_j\kappa^{\alpha-1}=n+g^*+\delta$, so
$\kappa^*_j=[s_j/(n+g^*+\delta)]^{1/(1-\alpha)}$ and
\[
y^*_j=A_j(\kappa^*_j)^{\alpha}=A_j\Bigl(\frac{s_j}{n+g^*+\delta}\Bigr)^{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}} ,
\]
which is \bk{(19.41)} once $n+\delta$ is absorbed into $g^*$, as the text does.

\textbf{(b)}  Taking logarithms of \bk{(19.40)},
\[
\frac{\partial\log y^*_j}{\partial\log\mu_j}=1 ,
\qquad
\frac{\partial\log y^*_j}{\partial\log\zeta_j}
=\frac{\partial\log y^*_j}{\partial\log(\rho_j+g^*)}=-\frac{\varepsilon-1}{\tau} ,
\]
against $\partial\log y^*_j/\partial\log A_j=1$ and
$\partial\log y^*_j/\partial\log s_j=\alpha/(1-\alpha)$ in the Solow model.  The correspondence
the text draws is exact: $\mu_j$ plays the role of $A_j$ with the same unit elasticity, $\zeta_j$
and $\rho_j$ play the role of the saving rate, and $(\varepsilon-1)/\tau$ replaces
$\alpha/(1-\alpha)$ as the amplification factor.

The difference is one of magnitude.  With $\alpha=1/3$ the Solow amplification is $1/2$, so a
fourfold difference in saving rates produces only a twofold difference in income --- the familiar
failure of the neoclassical model.  Here the amplification is $(\varepsilon-1)/\tau$, and
plausible values make it an order of magnitude larger:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrrr}
\toprule
$\varepsilon$ & $\tau$ & $(\varepsilon-1)/\tau$ & $2\times\zeta$ & $4\times\zeta$\\
\midrule
$3$ & $0.30$ & $6.67$ & $102$ & $1.0\times10^{4}$\\
$4$ & $0.30$ & $10.0$ & $1024$ & $1.0\times10^{6}$\\
$4$ & $0.15$ & $20.0$ & $1.0\times10^{6}$ & $1.1\times10^{12}$\\
$8$ & $0.30$ & $23.3$ & $1.1\times10^{7}$ & $1.1\times10^{14}$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
$\tau$ is directly measurable as the import share of intermediates in GDP (Exercise 19.16), which
puts it in the range $0.15$--$0.30$ for most countries, and the trade literature estimates
$\varepsilon$ between $3$ and $8$.  So the model can generate \emph{any} observed income
difference from tiny differences in $\zeta_j$ or $\rho_j$ --- which is its strength relative to
the neoclassical model and, equally, its weakness.  A theory that can explain a factor of $30$
with a $10\%$ difference in distortions is not disciplined by the income data at all; what would
discipline it is independent measurement of $\zeta_j$, of the terms of trade $r^*_j$ (which the
model says are observable and satisfy $y^*_j=\mu_j(r^*_j)^{1-\varepsilon}$), and of $\mu_j$
through the range of goods a country exports.  That is the sense in which \bk{(19.40)} is more
useful as a set of cross-equation restrictions than as a calibration.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{19.20}{}
In the general model the household owns capital and supplies one unit of labour.  Write, as in
Exercise 19.15, $R_j(t)\equiv r_j(t)/p^I_j(t)+\dt p^I_j(t)/p^I_j(t)$ for the rate of return on the
value of capital.  The flow constraint \bk{(19.21)} is
\[
\frac{\dd}{\dd t}\bigl[p^I_jK_j\bigr]=R_j\,p^I_jK_j+w_j-p^C_jC_j .
\]
Multiply by $\exp(-\int_0^tR_j)$, integrate from $t$ to $\infty$, and impose the transversality
condition \bk{(19.30)}, which kills the boundary term.  This gives the intertemporal budget
constraint
\[
\int_t^\infty\exp\Bigl(-\int_t^zR_j(s)\dd s\Bigr)p^C_j(z)C_j(z)\dd z
\ =\ \underbrace{p^I_j(t)K_j(t)+\int_t^\infty\exp\Bigl(-\int_t^zR_j(s)\dd s\Bigr)w_j(z)\dd z}
_{\textstyle \equiv\ \mathcal W_j(t)} ,
\]
so that total wealth is the value of the capital stock plus the present value of labour income,
discounted at the return on capital.

From the first-order conditions of Exercise 19.15, $p^C_jC_j=1/\lambda_j$ and
$\dt\lambda_j/\lambda_j=\rho_j-R_j$, so
$p^C_j(z)C_j(z)=p^C_j(t)C_j(t)\exp\bigl(\int_t^z(R_j-\rho_j)\bigr)$.  Substituting into the
left-hand side, the discount factors cancel and
\[
p^C_j(t)C_j(t)\int_t^\infty e^{-\rho_j(z-t)}\dd z=\frac{p^C_j(t)C_j(t)}{\rho_j}=\mathcal W_j(t) ,
\]
that is
\[
\boxed{\ p^C_j(t)C_j(t)=\rho_j\Bigl[p^I_j(t)K_j(t)
+\int_t^\infty\exp\Bigl(-\int_t^zR_j(s)\dd s\Bigr)w_j(z)\dd z\Bigr] , \ }
\]
which is \bk{(19.43)}. $\blacksquare$

\emph{The simplification \bk{(19.45)}.}  Labour market clearing \bk{(19.44)} makes
$w_j(t)=(1-\gamma)(1-\tau)p^C_j(t)C_j(t)$ at every date.  Substituting this into the human wealth
term and using the same discounting identity backwards,
\[
\int_t^\infty e^{-\int_t^zR_j}w_j(z)\dd z
=(1-\gamma)(1-\tau)\int_t^\infty e^{-\int_t^zR_j}p^C_jC_j\dd z
=(1-\gamma)(1-\tau)\,\mathcal W_j(t) ,
\]
so $\mathcal W_j=p^I_jK_j+(1-\gamma)(1-\tau)\mathcal W_j$ and hence
$\mathcal W_j=\frac{p^I_jK_j}{1-(1-\gamma)(1-\tau)}$, giving \bk{(19.45)}.  Human wealth is a fixed
multiple of physical wealth, which is what keeps the model as tractable as the AK version.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{19.21}{}
\emph{The trade balance \bk{(19.46)}.}  Repeat Exercise 19.16(a) with labour added.  Cost shares
now give
\[
r_jK^C_j=(1-\tau)\gamma\,p^C_jC_j,\quad
w_j=(1-\tau)(1-\gamma)\,p^C_jC_j,\quad
r_jK^I_j=(1-\tau)\,p^I_jI_j,\quad
r_jK^{\mu}_j=\tau\mu_jr_j^{1-\varepsilon}Y .
\]
Adding the capital uses and imposing \bk{(19.26)} with equality,
\[
r_jK_j=(1-\tau)\gamma p^C_jC_j+(1-\tau)p^I_jI_j+\tau\mu_jr_j^{1-\varepsilon}Y .
\]
Adding $w_j=(1-\tau)(1-\gamma)p^C_jC_j$ to both sides and using
$Y_j=r_jK_j+w_j=p^C_jC_j+p^I_jI_j$,
\[
Y_j=(1-\tau)\bigl(p^C_jC_j+p^I_jI_j\bigr)+\tau\mu_jr_j^{1-\varepsilon}Y
=(1-\tau)Y_j+\tau\mu_jr_j^{1-\varepsilon}Y ,
\]
and cancelling,
\[
\boxed{\ r_jK_j+w_j=\mu_jr_j^{1-\varepsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{J}\bigl[r_iK_i+w_i\bigr] , \ }
\]
which is \bk{(19.46)}.

\emph{The labour share \bk{(19.47)}.}  Combine \bk{(19.44)} and \bk{(19.45)}:
\[
w_j=(1-\gamma)(1-\tau)\,p^C_jC_j
=\frac{(1-\gamma)(1-\tau)\rho_j}{1-(1-\gamma)(1-\tau)}\,p^I_jK_j .
\]
Write $a\equiv\gamma+(1-\gamma)\tau=1-(1-\gamma)(1-\tau)$ and divide by $r_jK_j$, using
$p^I_j=\zeta_jr_j^{1-\tau}$ so that $p^I_j/r_j=\zeta_jr_j^{-\tau}$:
\[
\frac{w_j}{r_jK_j}=\frac{(1-\gamma)(1-\tau)\rho_j}{a\,\zeta_j^{-1}r_j^{\tau}} ,
\]
and therefore
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{w_j}{r_jK_j+w_j}
=\frac{(1-\gamma)(1-\tau)\rho_j}
{\bigl[\gamma+(1-\gamma)\tau\bigr]\zeta_j^{-1}r_j^{\tau}+(1-\gamma)(1-\tau)\rho_j} , \ }
\]
which is \bk{(19.47)} once $r_j$ inside it is read --- as in Exercise 19.15 --- as the rate of
return $\zeta_j^{-1}r_j^{\tau}$ rather than the rental price.  Equation \bk{(19.35)} is unchanged,
since it follows from \bk{(19.21)} and \bk{(19.45)} exactly as before. $\blacksquare$

\emph{A check.}  At $g^*=0$ the boxed expression reduces to
$w_j/Y_j=(1-\gamma)(1-\tau)$, since $\zeta_j^{-1}(r^*_j)^{\tau}=\rho_j+g^*=\rho_j$ then.  That is
right: with no growth all income is consumed, and labour's share of consumption-good expenditure
is exactly $(1-\gamma)(1-\tau)$.  It also confirms that the correct exponent on $r_j$ is $\tau$:
with $r_j$ in place of $r_j^{\tau}$ the check fails.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{19.22}{}
\emph{A common growth rate.}  In a steady state all prices, hence all $r_j$, are constant.  By
\bk{(19.47)} the labour share $s^w_j\equiv w_j/Y_j$ is then constant too, so
$Y_j=r_jK_j/(1-s^w_j)$ is proportional to $K_j$ and grows at $\dt K_j/K_j$.  Taking the ratio of
\bk{(19.46)} for two countries $j$ and $j'$,
\[
\frac{Y_j}{Y_{j'}}=\frac{\mu_jr_j^{1-\varepsilon}}{\mu_{j'}r_{j'}^{1-\varepsilon}}=\text{constant},
\]
so $Y_j$ and $Y_{j'}$ grow at the same rate; call it $g^*$.  Then \bk{(19.35)} gives
$\zeta_j^{-1}(r^*_j)^{\tau}=\rho_j+g^*$, that is \bk{(19.39)}, and substituting into
\bk{(19.46)} summed over $j$ gives \bk{(19.38)}.  Uniqueness is as in \bk{Proposition 19.10}: the
left-hand side of \bk{(19.38)} is strictly decreasing in $g^*$.

\emph{Global stability.}  Run the argument of Exercise 19.17.  With $y_j=Y_j/Y=\mu_jr_j^{1-\varepsilon}$
we still have $\dt r_j/r_j=-\frac{1}{\varepsilon-1}\dt y_j/y_j$, and now
$Y_j=r_jK_j/(1-s^w_j(r_j))$ with, from \bk{(19.47)},
\[
\frac{1}{1-s^w_j(r_j)}=1+\frac{(1-\gamma)(1-\tau)\rho_j\zeta_j}{a\,r_j^{\tau}},
\qquad
\Upsilon_j\equiv\frac{\dd\log\bigl[1/(1-s^w_j)\bigr]}{\dd\log r_j}\in(-\tau,0) .
\]
Hence
\[
\frac{\dt y_j}{y_j}\Bigl[1+\frac{1+\Upsilon_j}{\varepsilon-1}\Bigr]=h_j(y_j)-g(t),
\qquad h_j\ \text{as in \eqref{eq:rep}},
\]
and since $\tau<1<\varepsilon$ the bracket is strictly positive.  The system is therefore again a
replicator equation with a strictly decreasing fitness $h_j$ --- only the (positive,
state-dependent) speed factor differs --- and the same Lyapunov function
$V(y)=\sum_jy^*_j\log(y^*_j/y_j)$ has
\[
\dt V=-\sum_j\frac{y^*_j-y_j}{1+(1+\Upsilon_j)/(\varepsilon-1)}\bigl[h_j(y_j)-g^*\bigr]<0
\qquad\text{for }y\ne y^* .
\]
So $y^*$ is globally asymptotically stable and $g(t)\to g^*$. $\blacksquare$

\emph{The relative price of investment goods.}  The substantive gain from the general model is
\[
\frac{p^I_j}{p^C_j}=\zeta_j\Bigl(\frac{r_j}{w_j}\Bigr)^{(1-\tau)(1-\gamma)} ,
\]
so investment goods are expensive not only where distortions $\zeta_j$ are large but also where
wages are low --- and by \bk{(19.47)} wages are low where $\mu_j$ is small and $\rho_j$ is large.
The model therefore reproduces the observed cross-section of investment-good prices without
attributing all of it to investment-sector distortions, which is a warning about the one-sector
closed-economy calibrations that read the relative price directly as a measure of $\zeta_j$.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{19.23}{}
\textbf{(a)}  Write $\phi\equiv(\varepsilon-1)/\tau$ and $z_j\equiv\zeta_j(\rho_j+g^*)=(r^*_j)^{\tau}$,
so that \bk{(19.38)} reads $\Theta(g,\phi)\equiv\sum_j\mu_jz_j^{-\phi}=1$.  The crucial observation
is that the terms of this sum \emph{are} the relative incomes: by \bk{(19.40)},
$y^*_j=\mu_jz_j^{-\phi}$ and $\sum_jy^*_j=1$.  Differentiating,
\[
\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial\phi}=-\sum_jy^*_j\log z_j,
\qquad
\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial g}=-\phi\sum_j\frac{y^*_j}{\rho_j+g^*} ,
\]
and since $\dd\phi/\dd\tau=-\phi/\tau$, the implicit function theorem gives
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{\dd g^*}{\dd\tau}
=\frac{\sum_jy^*_j\log z_j}{\tau\sum_jy^*_j/(\rho_j+g^*)}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
\operatorname{sgn}\frac{\dd g^*}{\dd\tau}=\operatorname{sgn}\sum_jy^*_j\log r^*_j . \ }
\]
So $g^*$ rises with openness exactly when the income-weighted geometric mean of the terms of trade
exceeds one.  It need not.  Using $y^*_j=\mu_jz_j^{-\phi}$ to eliminate $z_j$,
\[
\sum_jy^*_j\log z_j=-\frac{1}{\phi}\sum_jy^*_j\log\frac{y^*_j}{\mu_j}
=\frac{1}{\phi}\Bigl[\log N-D\bigl(y^*\,\big\|\,\mu/N\bigr)\Bigr] ,
\]
where $N=\sum_j\mu_j$ and $D(\cdot\|\cdot)$ is the Kullback--Leibler divergence.  Hence
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{\dd g^*}{\dd\tau}>0
\iff
D\bigl(y^*\,\big\|\,\mu/N\bigr)<\log N . \ }
\]
\emph{Intuition.}  A higher $\tau$ shifts weight in production from domestic capital toward the
world bundle of intermediates.  That has two effects.  The \emph{variety} effect is a gain of
$\log N$: the world bundle contains $N$ goods and, with love of variety, is more productive per
unit of expenditure than any single country's own output --- this is the only effect when
countries are identical.  The \emph{dispersion} effect works the other way: relying more heavily
on traded goods amplifies the differences in terms of trade $r^*_j$ across countries, and since
those differences are precisely what makes the income distribution unequal, they subtract
$D(y^*\|\mu/N)$.  When the world income distribution is very unequal relative to the distribution
of technological capabilities, openness lowers world growth.  \texttt{code/ch19.py} confirms the
sign rule against numerical derivatives in four configurations, including one with $N>1$ and
$D=1.05>\log N=0.18$ where $\dd g^*/\dd\tau<0$.

\textbf{(b)}  In the AK version this cannot happen, and it is worth saying why.  By Exercise
19.14, $p^I_j/p^C_j=\zeta_j$ independently of $\tau$, so
\bk{(19.31)} gives $C_j(t)=\rho_j\zeta_jK_j(t)$ and steady-state welfare is
\[
U_j=\frac{\log\bigl(\rho_j\zeta_jK_j(0)\bigr)}{\rho_j}+\frac{g^*}{\rho_j^{2}} ,
\]
which depends on $\tau$ through $g^*$ alone.  The normalization $\chi=(1-\tau)^{-(1-\tau)}\tau^{-\tau}$
has removed every static level gain from openness, so welfare and growth must move together.

The general model of Section 19.4.3 supplies the missing channel.  There
$p^C_j=w_j^{(1-\tau)(1-\gamma)}r_j^{(1-\tau)\gamma}$ with $w_j$ proportional to $K_j$, so
$p^C_j\propto K_j^{b}$ with $b\equiv(1-\tau)(1-\gamma)$, while $p^C_jC_j\propto K_j$.  Hence
\emph{physical} consumption grows more slowly than capital,
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{\dt C_j}{C_j}=\bigl[\gamma+(1-\gamma)\tau\bigr]\,g^*=(1-b)\,g^* , \ }
\]
because the consumption good uses a fixed factor whose relative price rises.  Openness raises
$1-b=\gamma+(1-\gamma)\tau$, so
\[
\frac{\dd}{\dd\tau}\bigl[(1-b)g^*\bigr]=(1-\gamma)g^*+(1-b)\frac{\dd g^*}{\dd\tau} ,
\]
which is positive whenever $\dd g^*/\dd\tau>-(1-\gamma)g^*/[\gamma+(1-\gamma)\tau]$ --- a strictly
weaker requirement than $\dd g^*/\dd\tau>0$.  Trade raises welfare over a strictly larger range of
parameters than it raises the growth rate of GDP, because part of its benefit is to reduce the
economy's reliance on the fixed factor.

\textbf{(c)}  Three readings for the empirical debate.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The sign of the growth effect is not a theoretical matter.}  It is the sign of
      $\log N-D(y^*\|\mu/N)$, which mixes a parameter of the world technology with the
      \emph{endogenous} dispersion of world income.  A cross-country regression of growth on
      openness is therefore estimating something that is not a structural parameter and that
      can change sign across samples --- which is precisely the fragility Rodriguez and Rodrik
      document.
\item \emph{The growth effect is the wrong statistic.}  Part (b) shows that welfare and measured
      GDP growth can move in opposite directions.  Studies that ask whether trade raises growth
      are not asking whether trade is good.
\item \emph{Level effects are not second order here.}  In an endogenous growth world the static
      gains from trade are converted into permanently faster accumulation, so the distinction
      between ``level'' and ``growth'' effects that organizes much of the empirical literature is
      not one the model respects.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{(d)}  A sufficient condition is
\[
D\bigl(y^*\,\big\|\,\mu/N\bigr)<\log N ,
\]
of which the cleanest special cases are: all countries identical, so that $y^*=\mu/N$, $D=0$ and
the condition reduces to $N>1$; and, more generally, any configuration in which the world income
distribution is close to the distribution of technological capabilities.  Read economically: the
gains from variety must outweigh the inequality that trade prices in.  Since $D$ grows with the
dispersion of $\zeta_j$ and $\rho_j$ amplified by $(\varepsilon-1)/\tau$, the condition is easiest
to satisfy in a world of many varieties and similar policies, and hardest in a world of few
varieties and very unequal distortions.
\end{aex}

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\includegraphics{figures/ch19_trade.pdf}
\caption{Left: world income shares converging to \bk{(19.40)} under the replicator dynamics
\eqref{eq:rep}, from three initial conditions, with the steady-state shares marked
(Exercise 19.17).  Right: the allocation of labour to the learning sector under autarky and under
trade, $\varepsilon=2$ (Exercises 19.35 and 19.36).}
\label{fig:ch19}
\end{figure}

\solsection{19.5}{Trade, Technology Diffusion, and the Product Cycle}

\begin{aex}{19.30}{}
\textbf{(a)}  Without trade each country consumes only what it produces.  One worker produces one
unit of any good the country can make, and by the symmetry of \bk{(19.48)} labour is spread evenly
over the available goods.  In the North, $L^n$ workers spread over $N(t)$ goods give
$c_n(\nu)=L^n/N(t)$ for each, so
\[
C_n=\Bigl[N\bigl(L^n/N\bigr)^{\frac{\varepsilon-1}{\varepsilon}}\Bigr]^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon-1}}
=N^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon-1}}L^n ,
\]
and in the South, with only the $N^o(t)$ old goods available,
$C_s=(N^o)^{1/(\varepsilon-1)}L^s$.  Since all income is labour income and each country has its own
price level, the real wage is $C_j/L^j$:
\[
\boxed{\ w^n(t)=N(t)^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon-1}},\qquad w^s(t)=N^o(t)^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon-1}} . \ }
\]
Labour supplies drop out entirely: without trade the \emph{only} thing that makes the North rich
is that it consumes more varieties.

\textbf{(b)}  Using the steady-state ratio \bk{(19.53)}, $N^n/N^o=\eta/\iota$, the autarky gap is
\[
\Omega^A\equiv\frac{w^n}{w^s}=\Bigl(\frac{N}{N^o}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon-1}}
=\Bigl(1+\frac{\eta}{\iota}\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon-1}} ,
\]
while with trade \bk{(19.54)} gives
$\Omega^T=\max\bigl\{\bigl(\tfrac{\eta}{\iota}\tfrac{L^s}{L^n}\bigr)^{1/\varepsilon},1\bigr\}$.
Since $\Omega^A>1$ always, $\Omega^T>\Omega^A$ requires the first branch, and the condition is
\[
\boxed{\ \Bigl(1+\frac{\eta}{\iota}\Bigr)^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon-1}}
\ <\ \frac{\eta}{\iota}\cdot\frac{L^s}{L^n} . \ }
\]
It needs a \emph{large South} and \emph{substitutable goods}: at $\varepsilon=2$ it requires
$L^s/L^n>(1+\eta/\iota)^2\iota/\eta\ge4$, and as $\varepsilon\to\infty$ it relaxes to
$L^s/L^n>1+\iota/\eta$.

\emph{Why.}  Without trade the South's disadvantage is a pure love-of-variety loss, and it depends
on the technology gap alone --- the size of the South is irrelevant, because Southern workers
produce for Southern consumers.  With trade the South gains access to every variety, but its wage
is now set in a world market for the old goods in which it specializes.  If the South is large, it
floods that market, the relative price of old goods collapses, and the terms-of-trade loss can
exceed the variety gain.  Trade therefore \emph{converts a variety disadvantage into a
terms-of-trade disadvantage}, and the latter is the one that scales with the South's size.  This
is exactly the concern raised by the entry of India and China as large low-cost producers that the
text mentions.

\textbf{(c)}  No.  Relative income is not welfare, and here the two move in opposite directions.
With trade the South consumes all $N$ goods at prices $p_n=w^n$ and $p_o=w^s$, so its ideal price
index is $P=w^s\bigl[N^n\Omega^{1-\varepsilon}+N^o\bigr]^{1/(1-\varepsilon)}$ with $\Omega=w^n/w^s$,
and real consumption per worker is
\[
c_s^T=\frac{w^s}{P}=\bigl[N^n\Omega^{1-\varepsilon}+N^o\bigr]^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon-1}} .
\]
Against the autarky level $c_s^A=(N^o)^{1/(\varepsilon-1)}$,
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{c^T_s}{c^A_s}=\Bigl[1+\frac{N^n}{N^o}\Omega^{1-\varepsilon}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{\varepsilon-1}}
\ >\ 1\quad\text{always.} \ }
\]
The South gains from trade unconditionally, because the bracket exceeds one for any finite
$\Omega$.  The same computation for the North gives
$c^T_n/c^A_n=\bigl[(N^n+N^o\Omega^{\varepsilon-1})/N\bigr]^{1/(\varepsilon-1)}>1$ whenever
$\Omega>1$, which holds in the specialization equilibrium.  \texttt{code/ch19.py} tabulates both:
at $\varepsilon=3$, $\eta/\iota=2$ and $L^s/L^n=12$, trade raises the relative gap from $1.73$ to
$2.88$ while raising Southern real consumption by $11\%$ and Northern by $85\%$.

The moral is the standard one about gains from trade, stated where it is easiest to lose sight of:
a policy that widens the \emph{ratio} of incomes can raise \emph{both} levels.  A Southern
government reading only the relative-income statistic would draw the wrong conclusion.
\end{aex}

\solsection{19.6}{Trade and Endogenous Technological Change}

\begin{aex}{19.31}{}
Each economy is the lab-equipment model of Section 13.1 with $L=1$.  Three steps.

\emph{Profits.}  A monopolist owning one machine variety charges the constant markup over the
marginal cost $\psi=1-\beta$ and earns the flow profit $\pi=\beta L=\beta$, independent of $N$ and
of time.

\emph{The value of a variety.}  The no-arbitrage condition is $rV=\pi+\dt V$.  Free entry into
research, with the innovation possibilities frontier $\dt N=\eta Z$ and a unit cost of $1$ per unit
of $Z$, requires $\eta V\le1$ with equality when $Z>0$.  Hence $V=1/\eta$ is constant, $\dt V=0$,
and
\[
r=\eta\pi=\eta\beta .
\]

\emph{Growth.}  The Euler equation gives
\[
\boxed{\ g^A=\frac{r-\rho}{\theta}=\frac{1}{\theta}\bigl(\eta\beta-\rho\bigr) , \ }
\]
which is \bk{(19.56)}, and positive by the first part of \bk{(19.55)}.  Both countries are
identical and closed, so both grow at $g^A$ from any initial $N$ --- with free entry and a linear
innovation technology there are no transitional dynamics.  Finally, finite utility and the
transversality condition require $(1-\theta)g^A<\rho$, that is $(1-\theta)\eta\beta<\rho$.
$\blacksquare$

\rmk{The second condition in \bk{(19.55)}, $2(1-\theta)\eta\beta<\rho$, is stronger than the
autarky economy needs: it is exactly the transversality condition for the \emph{trade}
equilibrium, $(1-\theta)g^T<\rho$ with $g^T=(2\eta\beta-\rho)/\theta$.  Stating both conditions at
once lets \bk{Propositions 19.13} and \bk{19.14} share a hypothesis, at the cost of restricting the
autarky case more than necessary.  For $\theta\ge1$ both conditions are vacuous.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{19.32}{}
Nothing in Exercise 19.31 changes except the market size.  With costless trade in inputs, a
monopolist that invents a variety sells it to final good producers in \emph{both} countries, so
the relevant labour force is $L^1+L^2=2$ and the flow profit becomes
\[
\pi^T=\beta\bigl(L^1+L^2\bigr)=2\beta .
\]
Free entry still gives $V=1/\eta$ and $\dt V=0$, so the interest rate is
\[
r^T=\eta\pi^T=2\eta\beta ,
\]
and the Euler equation gives
\[
\boxed{\ g^T=\frac{1}{\theta}\bigl(2\eta\beta-\rho\bigr)=g^A+\frac{\eta\beta}{\theta}>g^A . \ }
\]
Since the two economies are identical and all inputs are traded, the integrated world economy has
a single interest rate and a single growth rate, so both countries grow at $g^T$.  Finite utility
requires $(1-\theta)g^T<\rho$, which is the second condition in \bk{(19.55)}. $\blacksquare$

\emph{What is and is not robust.}  The mechanism is the market size effect: doubling the market
doubles the flow profit from an innovation, which doubles the return to research.  It is the
cleanest statement of why trade should promote growth, and it is present in any model of
endogenous technological change.  Three caveats limit it, and each is worth stating with the
result.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{It is a pure scale effect.}  The gain $\eta\beta/\theta$ is exactly proportional to the
      increase in market size, which is the feature that the semi-endogenous growth models of
      Section 13.3 remove.  There, as \bk{Exercise 19.34} shows, trade opening raises innovation
      temporarily and leaves the long-run growth rate untouched.
\item \emph{It relies on the lab-equipment specification.}  If R\&D used labour rather than final
      goods, trade opening would also raise the demand for production workers and bid up the wage
      that research must pay.  \bk{Exercise 19.33} constructs a case in which this offset is
      complete.
\item \emph{It assumes no knowledge spillovers.}  With spillovers the answer depends on an
      assumption about how far knowledge travelled \emph{before} trade opening, which is exactly
      the thing the lab-equipment specification was chosen to avoid having to assume.
\end{enumerate}
Taken together with Section 19.7, the honest conclusion is the one the text draws: theory delivers
both signs, so the question is empirical.
\end{aex}

\solsection{19.7}{Learning-by-Doing, Trade, and Growth}

\begin{aex}{19.35}{}
\emph{The allocation of labour.}  With both sectors active, wages must be equal, which is
\bk{(19.59)}: $p^1_jA_j=p^2_j$.  Profit maximization by the final good producer with $\gamma=1/2$
gives $p^1_j/p^2_j=(X^1_j/X^2_j)^{-1/\varepsilon}$.  Substituting $X^1_j=A_jL^1_j$ and
$X^2_j=1-L^1_j$ and eliminating the price ratio,
\[
\frac{1}{A_j}=\Bigl(\frac{A_jL^1_j}{1-L^1_j}\Bigr)^{-1/\varepsilon}
\ \Longrightarrow\
\frac{L^1_j}{1-L^1_j}=A_j^{\varepsilon-1}
\ \Longrightarrow\
\boxed{\ L^1_j(t)=\frac{A_j(t)^{\varepsilon-1}}{1+A_j(t)^{\varepsilon-1}} \ }
\]
which is \bk{(19.60)}.

\emph{The case $\varepsilon>1$, $\delta\to0$.}  At $t=0$, $A_j(0)\to1$ so $L^1_j(0)=1/2$.  Then
$\dt A_j/A_j=\eta L^1_j>0$ by \bk{(19.57)}, so $A_j$ increases; since $\varepsilon>1$,
$A_j^{\varepsilon-1}$ increases, and $L^1_j$ is a strictly increasing function of it.  So $L^1_j$
rises monotonically, and since $A_j\to\infty$ (its growth rate is bounded below by $\eta/2$),
$L^1_j(t)\uparrow1$ and $\dt A_j/A_j\to\eta$.  As $L^1_j\to1$, $X^2_j\to0$ and
$Y_j\to 2^{-\varepsilon/(\varepsilon-1)}A_j$, so output also grows at
\[
\boxed{\ g^*=\eta . \ }
\]
The feedback is the engine: more labour in sector 1 raises $A_j$, a higher $A_j$ raises the
marginal product of sector-1 labour relative to sector 2, which draws in more labour.  The
process is self-reinforcing precisely because $\varepsilon>1$, so that the price of good 1 falls
by less, proportionally, than its quantity rises.

\emph{The case $\varepsilon=1$.}  With $Y_j=(X^1_j)^{1/2}(X^2_j)^{1/2}$ the expenditure shares are
fixed at one half, so $p^1_jX^1_j=p^2_jX^2_j$.  Combined with $p^1_jA_j=p^2_j$ this gives
$A_jL^1_j\cdot\frac{1}{A_j}=1-L^1_j$, whence $L^1_j=1/2$ for all $t$ --- which is also
\bk{(19.60)} evaluated at $\varepsilon=1$.  Therefore
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{\dt A_j}{A_j}=\frac{\eta}{2}\equiv g^{**}\ \text{for all }t . \ }
\]
Here the price effect exactly cancels the quantity effect at every date, so nothing draws labour
toward the learning sector and the allocation is frozen.  Note that output then grows at
$\eta/4$, not $\eta/2$: with $Y_j=\tfrac12A_j^{1/2}$ the productivity gain is halved again by the
Cobb--Douglas aggregation, so $g^{**}$ in the proposition is the growth rate of \emph{productivity}
rather than of output. $\blacksquare$
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{19.36}{}
\textbf{(a)}  With free trade there is one world price pair, and the wage in country $j$ is
$\max\{p^1A_j,\,p^2\}$.  Conjecture $L^{1}_n=1$ and $L^1_s=0$.  Then $X^1=A_n$ and $X^2=1$ per
North--South pair, so
\[
\frac{p^2}{p^1}=\Bigl(\frac{X^1}{X^2}\Bigr)^{1/\varepsilon}=A_n^{1/\varepsilon} ,
\]
and the conjecture is an equilibrium precisely when
\[
A_s\ <\ A_n^{1/\varepsilon}\ <\ A_n .
\]
At $t=0$, $A_n=1$ and $A_s=1-\delta<1=A_n^{1/\varepsilon}$, so the left inequality holds, and the
right holds weakly.  Thereafter $L^1_n=1$ gives $\dt A_n/A_n=\eta$ while $L^1_s=0$ gives
$\dt A_s/A_s=0$, so $A_n>1$ strictly and both inequalities hold strictly for all $t>0$: the
conjecture is self-confirming.  World output is
$\propto[\tfrac12A_n^{(\varepsilon-1)/\varepsilon}+\tfrac12]^{\varepsilon/(\varepsilon-1)}
\to2^{-\varepsilon/(\varepsilon-1)}A_n$, so the world grows at $\eta$.  Finally, with all income
being labour income and one worker per country,
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{Y_n(t)}{Y_s(t)}=\frac{w^n}{w^s}=\frac{p^1A_n}{p^2}
=\frac{A_n}{A_n^{1/\varepsilon}}=A_n(t)^{\frac{\varepsilon-1}{\varepsilon}} , \ }
\]
which diverges if $\varepsilon>1$ and is constant if $\varepsilon=1$. $\blacksquare$

\textbf{(b)}  Decompose the North's real income gain from a rise in $A_n$.  A one percent increase
in $A_n$ raises $X^1$ by one percent and lowers $p^1/p^2$ by $1/\varepsilon$ percent, so the
North's real income rises by $(1-1/\varepsilon)$ percent.  At $\varepsilon=1$ this is zero: the
deterioration of the North's terms of trade exactly offsets its productivity growth.  Equivalently,
with unit elasticity the expenditure share on each good is fixed at one half, so the South ---
which produces all of good 2 --- captures a constant half of world income no matter how much
better the North becomes at producing good 1.  It is the same mechanism as the stable income
distribution of Section 19.4, and it shows that the case against trade in this model is not about
specialization as such but about the elasticity of substitution.

\textbf{(c)}  With $\varepsilon<1$ the required inequality $A_n^{1/\varepsilon}<A_n$ \emph{fails}
for $A_n>1$: the price of good 2 rises so fast relative to good 1 that Northern workers prefer
sector 2.  Complete specialization is not sustainable.  Instead the North diversifies, with
$p^1A_n=p^2$ pinning
\[
L^1_n=\frac{2A_n^{\varepsilon-1}}{1+A_n^{\varepsilon-1}}<1 ,
\]
which \emph{falls} as $A_n$ rises, so $\dt A_n/A_n=\eta L^1_n\to0$ and Northern learning dies out.
Meanwhile the South stays in sector 2 and, since the North is now the marginal producer of good 2,
$w^n=p^1A_n=p^2=w^s$: incomes are \emph{equalized}.  So with complements rather than substitutes,
trade does not impoverish the South at all --- it equalizes incomes and kills growth in the North.
The numerical run at $\varepsilon=0.5$ reproduces this: $L^1_n$ falls to $0.249$ after $160$
periods and the wage ratio is exactly one.

\textbf{(d)}  Suppose all economies are closed until $T_0$.  By \bk{Proposition 19.15} both blocks
then have $L^1_j\to1$ and $A_j$ growing toward $\eta$, with $A_n(T_0)>A_s(T_0)$ preserved.  Opening
at $T_0$, complete specialization requires $A_s(T_0)<A_n(T_0)^{1/\varepsilon}$, and after a period
of protection this typically fails: both productivities are large and close together.  The
equilibrium then has the North fully in sector 1 and the \emph{South diversified}, with
$p^1A_s=p^2$ and
\[
L^1_s=\frac{A_s^{\varepsilon}-A_n}{A_s^{\varepsilon}+A_s}>0 ,
\qquad \frac{w^n}{w^s}=\frac{A_n}{A_s} .
\]
Writing $x\equiv A_s^{\varepsilon}/A_n$ and $u\equiv A_s/A_n$, we have
$L^1_s=(x-1)/(x+u)$ and
\[
\frac{\dd\log x}{\dd t}=\eta\bigl[\varepsilon L^1_s-1\bigr] ,
\]
so the South's foothold expands if and only if $L^1_s>1/\varepsilon$, that is
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{A_s(T_0)^{\varepsilon}}{A_n(T_0)}\ >\ \frac{\varepsilon+A_s(T_0)/A_n(T_0)}{\varepsilon-1} . \ }
\]
The condition is self-reinforcing --- $x$ rises and $u$ falls, which lowers the threshold --- so it
is a genuine dichotomy.  \texttt{code/ch19.py} evaluates it at $\varepsilon=2$, $\eta=0.05$,
$\delta=0.02$:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrrrl}
\toprule
$T_0$ & $A_n(T_0)$ & $A_s(T_0)$ & $x$ & threshold & outcome\\
\midrule
$0$ & $1.000$ & $0.980$ & $0.96$ & $2.98$ & locked out, $w^n/w^s$ grows at $\eta/2$\\
$20$ & $1.763$ & $1.718$ & $1.67$ & $2.97$ & locked out\\
$30$ & $2.470$ & $2.400$ & $2.33$ & $2.97$ & locked out\\
$40$ & $3.589$ & $3.478$ & $3.37$ & $2.97$ & keeps a foothold, $L^1_s\to1$\\
$120$ & $149.3$ & $143.4$ & $137.7$ & $2.96$ & keeps a foothold\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\rmk{This is a correction to the remark that follows \bk{Proposition 19.16} in the text, that
``even if international trade is prevented in the South for a period of duration $T>0$ \dots\ the
ultimate outcome is the same.''  That is true only for \emph{short} protection.  Because both
blocks learn under autarky and $A_s^{\varepsilon}$ grows faster than $A_n$ when $\varepsilon>1$, a
long enough closure carries the South past the threshold above, after which it retains a share of
the learning sector, $L^1_s\to1$, both blocks grow at $\eta$, and the wage ratio settles at a
constant instead of diverging.  In the calibration the critical duration is between $30$ and $40$
periods.  Infant industry protection therefore does have a permanent effect in this model,
provided it lasts long enough --- which, read the other way, is not much comfort for the policy:
the required duration is the time it takes for $A_s^{\varepsilon-1}$ to grow by a factor of about
$(\varepsilon+1)/(\varepsilon-1)$, and the analysis says nothing about the cost of forgoing the
static gains from trade for that long.}
\end{aex}


%% ========================================================================
\solchapter{20}{Structural Change and Economic Growth}

\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{0.86\textwidth}
\small
The ten exercises of Chapter 20 left unsolved in the instructor's manual: 20.1, 20.2, 20.4,
20.10--20.15 and 20.20.  The manual solves 20.3, 20.5--20.9, 20.16--20.19 and 20.21.  The
numerical parts are computed by \texttt{code/ch20.py}; Figure~\ref{fig:ch20} is its output.
\end{minipage}
\end{center}

\bigskip
\hrule
\bigskip

\solsection{20.1}{Nonbalanced Growth: The Demand Side}

\begin{aex}{20.1}{}
\emph{The demands.}  The aggregator
$C=(c^A-\gamma^A)^{\eta^A}(c^M)^{\eta^M}(c^S+\gamma^S)^{\eta^S}$ is Cobb--Douglas in the
translated variables, so at prices $(p^A,1,p^S)$ and total expenditure $e$ the demands are the
Stone--Geary ones,
\[
p^A\bigl(c^A-\gamma^A\bigr)=\eta^A\wt e,\qquad
c^M=\eta^M\wt e,\qquad
p^S\bigl(c^S+\gamma^S\bigr)=\eta^S\wt e ,
\]
where $\wt e\equiv e-p^A\gamma^A+p^S\gamma^S$ is \emph{supernumerary} expenditure --- what is
left after the subsistence food bill has been paid and the notional services endowment has been
sold.  Note that $c^S>0$ requires $\eta^S\wt e>p^S\gamma^S$, so services are demanded only above
the threshold $e>\bigl[(1-\eta^S)p^S\gamma^S+\eta^Sp^A\gamma^A\bigr]/\eta^S$, which is the
feature the text mentions.

\emph{Engel's Law.}  Dividing by $e$, the budget shares are
\begin{align*}
\boxed{\ s^A=\eta^A+\frac{(1-\eta^A)p^A\gamma^A+\eta^Ap^S\gamma^S}{e}\ } \qquad
&\boxed{\ s^S=\eta^S-\frac{\eta^Sp^A\gamma^A+(1-\eta^S)p^S\gamma^S}{e}\ }\\[4pt]
\boxed{\ s^M=\eta^M\Bigl[1-\frac{p^A\gamma^A-p^S\gamma^S}{e}\Bigr]\ } &
\end{align*}
Since $\gamma^A,\gamma^S>0$, the numerator in $s^A$ is strictly positive and
$\partial s^A/\partial e<0$: \emph{the budget share of food falls as income rises}, which is
Engel's Law.  Equivalently the income elasticity is
$\partial\log c^A/\partial\log e=\eta^A/s^A<1$, since $s^A>\eta^A$.  Symmetrically
$\partial s^S/\partial e>0$ and the income elasticity of services is $\eta^S/s^S>1$: services are
a luxury.

\emph{Why \bk{(20.18)} is the condition for a constant manufacturing share.}  Manufacturing is
the residual, and its share is constant \emph{if and only if} $p^A\gamma^A=p^S\gamma^S$.  By
\bk{Proposition 20.1}, $p^A=B^M/B^A$ and $p^S=B^M/B^S$, so this is
\[
\frac{B^M\gamma^A}{B^A}=\frac{B^M\gamma^S}{B^S}
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\frac{\gamma^A}{B^A}=\frac{\gamma^S}{B^S} ,
\]
which is exactly \bk{(20.18)}.  So the knife-edge condition for a CGP has a direct
preference-side reading: it is the condition under which the value of the food subsistence
requirement equals the value of the notional services endowment, so that the two nonhomotheticities
cancel in the manufacturing budget.  \texttt{code/ch20.py} exhibits this: with
$\eta=(0.3,0.4,0.3)$, $B=(1,1.2,0.8)$ and $\gamma^A=0.4$, imposing \bk{(20.18)} gives
$\gamma^S=0.32$ and $s^M=0.40000$ at \emph{every} income, while $s^A$ falls from $0.540$ to
$0.304$ and $s^S$ rises from $0.060$ to $0.296$ as $e$ goes from $2$ to $128$; with $\gamma^S=0$,
$s^M$ rises from $0.304$ to $0.399$ instead.  The left panel of Figure~\ref{fig:ch20} plots both
cases.

\emph{Alternatives.}  Stone--Geary is the simplest, but it has two defects: the income
elasticities tend to one as income grows, so the structural change it generates dies out, and the
subsistence terms make the demand system undefined at low incomes.  Four alternatives avoid one or
both.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Quasi-homothetic (Gorman polar form) preferences} in general, with expenditure
      function $e(p,u)=a(p)+b(p)u$.  Stone--Geary is the case in which $b$ is Cobb--Douglas.  Any
      $a(p)\ne0$ gives non-unit income elasticities while preserving exact aggregation across
      heterogeneous households --- which is what makes the representative household assumption
      legitimate.
\item \emph{Non-homothetic CES} (Hanoch; Comin, Lashkari and Mestieri), defined implicitly by
      $\sum_i\Omega_i^{1/\varepsilon}C^{\epsilon_i(\varepsilon-1)/\varepsilon}
      c_i^{(\varepsilon-1)/\varepsilon}=1$.  The income elasticities are the constants
      $\epsilon_i$, so structural change does \emph{not} die out as the economy grows, and there
      are no subsistence terms.  This is the specification most of the recent quantitative
      literature uses.
\item \emph{PIGL preferences} (Boppart), which give Engel curves linear in the logarithm of
      income and are the only class consistent both with exact aggregation and with a balanced
      growth path in which relative prices trend.
\item \emph{Hierarchic preferences} with indivisible goods (Foellmi and Zweim\"uller; Matsuyama):
      a continuum of goods consumed in zero-one amounts in a fixed hierarchy.  Structural change
      then operates on the extensive margin --- richer households consume \emph{more kinds} of
      goods --- and the income elasticity of any good not yet consumed exceeds one.
\item Simplest of all, additively separable utility with different curvatures,
      $\sum_i\vartheta_ic_i^{1-\sigma_i}/(1-\sigma_i)$, whose income elasticities are proportional
      to $1/\sigma_i$.  It does not aggregate exactly, which is why the literature usually prefers
      the first three.
\end{enumerate}
