\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{20.2}{}
\emph{Equal effective capital-labor ratios.}  All three sectors use the same $F$ and the same
labour-augmenting term $X(t)$, and factors are freely mobile, so competitive pricing equates the
\emph{values} of the marginal products:
\[
p^jB^jF_K\bigl(K_j,XL_j\bigr)=r,
\qquad
p^jB^jX\,F_L\bigl(K_j,XL_j\bigr)=w
\qquad\text{for }j\in\{A,M,S\} ,
\]
with $p^M=1$.  Dividing the two and using that $F$ has constant returns, so that
$F_K=f'(k_j)$ and $F_L=f(k_j)-k_jf'(k_j)$ with $k_j\equiv K_j/(XL_j)$,
\[
\frac{f'(k_j)}{f(k_j)-k_jf'(k_j)}=\frac{r}{Xw}\qquad\text{for every }j .
\]
The left-hand side is the marginal rate of technical substitution, and it is strictly decreasing
in $k_j$: its derivative has the sign of
$f''(f-kf')-f'(-kf'')=f''f<0$ by strict concavity.  A strictly monotone function takes the common
value $r/(Xw)$ at only one point, so
\[
k_A(t)=k_M(t)=k_S(t) .
\]
Market clearing \bk{(20.5)}--\bk{(20.6)} then gives
$K=\sum_jK_j=\sum_jk_jXL_j=k\,XL$, so the common value is
\[
\boxed{\ k_A=k_M=k_S=\frac{K(t)}{X(t)L(t)}\equiv k(t) , \ }
\]
which is \bk{(20.14)}.

\emph{Prices.}  With $k_j=k$ common, the rental rate read off each sector is
$r=p^AB^Af'(k)=B^Mf'(k)=p^SB^Sf'(k)$, using $p^M=1$.  Cancelling $f'(k)>0$,
\[
\boxed{\ p^A(t)=\frac{B^M}{B^A},\qquad p^S(t)=\frac{B^M}{B^S} \ }
\]
for all $t$, which is \bk{(20.15)}.  Condition \bk{(20.13)} guarantees that the economy can
produce more than the subsistence food requirement at $t=0$, hence that all three sectors are
active and the interior first-order conditions used above hold. $\blacksquare$

\rmk{This proposition is doing more work than it looks.  Because $p^A$ and $p^S$ are pinned at
constants \emph{for ever}, no relative price in this economy can ever move --- the only
Hicks-neutral differences $B^A,B^M,B^S$ are constants, and the single labour-augmenting term
$X(t)$ is common.  Every source of structural change in Section 20.1 is therefore a
\emph{quantity} phenomenon driven by Engel's Law, with no relative-price channel at all.  That is
the exact complement of Section 20.2, where preferences are homothetic and everything runs through
relative prices, and it is also the reason the answer to Exercise 20.10 is the one given below.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{20.4}{}
\emph{\bk{Proposition 20.3}.}  Suppose all three sectors grew at a common rate.  By \bk{(20.8)}
and $Y^M=c^ML+\dt K$, per capita consumptions $c^A,c^M,c^S$ would then grow at a common rate
$g_c$.  \bk{(20.17)} requires
\[
\frac{B^M\bigl(c^A(t)-\gamma^A\bigr)}{B^A\eta^A}=\frac{c^M(t)}{\eta^M}
\qquad\text{for all }t .
\]
Differentiating logarithmically, the left-hand side grows at
$\dfrac{\dt c^A}{c^A-\gamma^A}=g_c\dfrac{c^A}{c^A-\gamma^A}$ and the right-hand side at $g_c$.
Equality at every date forces
\[
g_c\,\frac{c^A}{c^A-\gamma^A}=g_c
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
g_c\,\gamma^A=0 ,
\]
so either $\gamma^A=0$ or $g_c=0$.  The second equation of \bk{(20.17)} gives $g_c\gamma^S=0$ in
the same way.  Finally $g_c=0$ is impossible when $g>0$: a constant $c^M$ requires, by
\bk{(20.16)}, $r=\rho$, hence a constant $k$ by \bk{Proposition 20.1} and \bk{(20.12)}; but then
$K=kX L$ grows at $g+n$ while $Y^M$ and $c^ML$ grow at $n$, contradicting \bk{(20.7)}.  Hence with
$\gamma^A>0$ or $\gamma^S>0$ there is no equilibrium with equal sectoral growth rates.
$\blacksquare$

\emph{Existence.}  Reduce the equilibrium to two differential equations.  Since
$Y^j=B^jXL_jf(k)$ by \bk{Proposition 20.1}, dividing by $B^j$ and summing over $j$ with
\bk{(20.6)} gives the resource identity
\[
\frac{Y^A}{B^A}+\frac{Y^M}{B^M}+\frac{Y^S}{B^S}=X(t)L(t)f\bigl(k(t)\bigr) .
\]
Substituting $Y^A=c^AL$, $Y^S=c^SL$, $Y^M=c^ML+\dt K$ from \bk{(20.7)}--\bk{(20.8)}, dividing by
$XL$, and writing $\wt c^j\equiv c^j/X$,
\[
\frac{\wt c^A}{B^A}+\frac{\wt c^M}{B^M}+\frac{\wt c^S}{B^S}
+\frac{\dt K}{B^MXL}=f(k) .
\]
Now eliminate $\wt c^A$ and $\wt c^S$ with \bk{(20.17)}.  Since
$\wt c^A=\gamma^A/X+\frac{B^A\eta^A}{B^M\eta^M}\wt c^M$ and
$\wt c^S=-\gamma^S/X+\frac{B^S\eta^S}{B^M\eta^M}\wt c^M$, and $\eta^A+\eta^M+\eta^S=1$,
\[
\frac{\wt c^A}{B^A}+\frac{\wt c^M}{B^M}+\frac{\wt c^S}{B^S}
=\frac{1}{X(t)}\Bigl(\frac{\gamma^A}{B^A}-\frac{\gamma^S}{B^S}\Bigr)+\frac{\wt c^M}{B^M\eta^M} .
\]
Using $\dt K/(XL)=\dt k+(n+g)k$, the equilibrium is therefore the two-dimensional system
\begin{equation}
\boxed{\
\dt k=B^Mf(k)-\frac{\wt c^M}{\eta^M}
-\frac{B^M}{X(t)}\Delta-(n+g)k ,
\qquad
\frac{\dt{\wt c}^M}{\wt c^M}=\frac{B^Mf'(k)-\rho}{\theta}-g ,\ }
\label{eq:ch20sys}
\end{equation}
with $\Delta\equiv\dfrac{\gamma^A}{B^A}-\dfrac{\gamma^S}{B^S}$.

Existence is now the standard argument of Chapter 8.  Given $k(0)>0$ the system
\eqref{eq:ch20sys} is a non-autonomous planar system whose non-autonomy enters only through the
term $B^M\Delta/X(t)$, which decays to zero at the rate $g$.  Its limiting autonomous system has a
unique saddle-point-stable rest point, and for each $k(0)$ there is exactly one $\wt c^M(0)$ whose
trajectory satisfies the transversality condition (by \bk{Assumption 4} and the sandwich argument
between the autonomous systems with $\Delta/X$ replaced by $\pm\varepsilon$).  Feasibility holds
along it: \bk{(20.13)} makes $c^A>\gamma^A$ at $t=0$, and $c^A$ is increasing along the path;
$c^S\ge0$ holds once expenditure clears the threshold identified in Exercise 20.1.  The sectoral
labour allocations are then read off \bk{(20.8)}, and they move over time --- which is
\bk{Proposition 20.3} --- but nothing prevents the path from existing. $\blacksquare$

\rmk{System \eqref{eq:ch20sys} makes \bk{Proposition 20.4} transparent as well.  It is autonomous
--- and hence can have a rest point with $k$ and $\wt c^M$ constant --- \emph{if and only if}
$\Delta=0$, that is if and only if $\gamma^A/B^A=\gamma^S/B^S$, which is \bk{(20.18)}.  The rest
point is then $B^Mf'(k^*)=\rho+\theta g$, and \bk{(20.19)} follows by differentiating
\bk{(20.17)} and \bk{(20.8)}.  Conversely, if $\Delta\ne0$ the term $B^M\Delta/X(t)$ is a moving
target, so no pair $(k,\wt c^M)$ can be a rest point at two different dates.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{20.10}{}
Throughout, let $X^A,X^M,X^S$ denote the \emph{inputs} into the final good sector, $p^Y$ the price
of the final good and $p^M=1$ the numeraire.

\emph{The demand system is unchanged.}  Profit maximization by the final good sector gives
\[
p^Y\eta^A\frac{Y}{X^A-\gamma^AL}=p^A,\qquad
p^Y\eta^M\frac{Y}{X^M}=1,\qquad
p^Y\eta^S\frac{Y}{X^S+\gamma^SL}=p^S ,
\]
which are \bk{(20.9)}--\bk{(20.10)} with $X^j$ in place of $c^jL$ and $p^YY$ in place of the
consumption aggregate.  \bk{Proposition 20.1} is untouched, since it uses only the production side:
$k_A=k_M=k_S=k$ and $p^A=B^M/B^A$, $p^S=B^M/B^S$.  Two consequences follow immediately.  First,
the unit cost of the final good,
$p^Y=\bigl(p^A/\eta^A\bigr)^{\eta^A}\bigl(1/\eta^M\bigr)^{\eta^M}\bigl(p^S/\eta^S\bigr)^{\eta^S}$,
is a \emph{constant}.  Second, the final good sector's profit is
\[
p^YY-p^AX^A-X^M-p^SX^S=-B^M\Delta L,
\qquad \Delta\equiv\frac{\gamma^A}{B^A}-\frac{\gamma^S}{B^S} ,
\]
so free entry into final good production requires $\Delta=0$, that is \bk{(20.18)}, in either
variant.  The knife-edge condition has acquired a second interpretation: it is the condition under
which the final good technology is genuinely constant returns.

\textbf{(a)}  With investment out of $Y^M$, market clearing is $Y^A=X^A$, $Y^S=X^S$,
$Y^M=X^M+\dt K$, and the household consumes $C=Y$.  The resource identity
$\sum_jY^j/B^j=XLf(k)$ and the demands above give
$\sum_jX^j/B^j=\Delta L+p^YY/B^M$, whence
\[
\dt k=B^Mf(k)-p^Y\wt C-\frac{B^M\Delta}{X(t)}-(n+g)k ,
\qquad \wt C\equiv\frac{C}{XL} .
\]
This is \eqref{eq:ch20sys} with $p^Y\wt C$ in the role of $\wt c^M/\eta^M$.  The Euler equation is
unchanged, because the investment good is $Y^M$ with price $1$ and the consumption good is $Y$
with the constant price $p^Y$, so no relative price moves:
$\dt C/C=(B^Mf'(k)-\rho)/\theta$.  Hence every result of Section 20.1 --- \bk{Propositions
20.1}--\bk{20.4} and the sectoral growth rates \bk{(20.19)} --- holds verbatim. $\blacksquare$

\textbf{(b)}  Now $Y^j=X^j$ for all $j$ and $\dt K+cL=Y$.  The same resource identity gives
\[
Y=\frac{B^M}{p^Y}\Bigl[X(t)L(t)f(k)-\Delta L(t)\Bigr] ,
\]
so
\[
\dt k=\frac{B^M}{p^Y}\Bigl[f(k)-\frac{\Delta}{X(t)}\Bigr]-\wt c-(n+g)k ,
\qquad
\frac{\dt c}{c}=\frac{1}{\theta}\Bigl(\frac{B^Mf'(k)}{p^Y}-\rho\Bigr) ,
\]
the interest rate now being the marginal product of capital divided by the price of the investment
good.  This is again \eqref{eq:ch20sys} up to the constant $p^Y$, and a CGP exists under exactly
the same condition $\Delta=0$, with $B^Mf'(k^*)=p^Y(\rho+\theta g)$ and a constant capital share
$k^*f'(k^*)/f(k^*)$.

\rmk{The exercise asserts that in case (b) a CGP does not exist, and the text draws from it the
conclusion that the Kuznets and the Kaldor facts can no longer be reconciled.  The computation
above says otherwise, and it is worth being precise about why.  Any difference between (a) and (b)
must operate through the \emph{relative price of the investment good in terms of the consumption
good}, since that is the only thing the identity of the investment good can change --- it is the
term $\dt p^I/p^I-\dt p^C/p^C$ in the two-sector Euler equation of Section 11.3.  But in this
model that relative price cannot move: by \bk{Proposition 20.1} the three sectors share a
production function and a technology term, so $p^A$ and $p^S$ are frozen at $B^M/B^A$ and
$B^M/B^S$ for ever, and $p^Y$ --- a constant-elasticity aggregate of frozen prices --- is frozen
too.  The composition of the investment good is therefore irrelevant here, and both variants
deliver a CGP under \bk{(20.18)}.

The exercise's conclusion \emph{is} correct in the model it is presumably reaching for, namely
Rebelo's, where the investment sector has a different production function from the consumption
sector.  There $p^I/p^C$ moves with the capital-labor ratio, an extra term enters the Euler
equation, and a CGP requires a further restriction.  What Exercise 20.10 shows in the Kongsamut,
Rebelo and Xie environment is the weaker but still useful point made above: \bk{(20.18)} is needed
not only for a CGP but simply for the final good sector to break even, since its profit is
$-B^M\Delta L$.}
\end{aex}

\solsection{20.2}{Nonbalanced Growth: The Supply Side}

\begin{aex}{20.11}{}
Suppose for a contradiction that growth is balanced, $\dt Y_l/Y_l=\varsigma$ for every
$l=1,\dots,N$.  Since $F$ has constant returns, $p_l=\partial F/\partial Y_l$ is homogeneous of
degree zero in $(Y_1,\dots,Y_N)$, so with all $Y_l$ growing at the same rate every $p_l$ is
constant:
\begin{equation}
\frac{\dt p_l}{p_l}=0\qquad\text{for every }l .
\label{eq:pconst}
\end{equation}

\emph{Two conditions from factor pricing.}  With $g_l(k_l)\equiv G_l(K_l,L_l)/L_l$ and
$k_l\equiv K_l/L_l$, competitive pricing gives $r=p_lA_lg_l'(k_l)$ and
$w=p_lA_l[g_l(k_l)-k_lg_l'(k_l)]$.  Differentiating logarithmically and using
\eqref{eq:pconst},
\[
\frac{\dt A_l}{A_l}+\varepsilon_{g_l'}\frac{\dt k_l}{k_l}
=\frac{\dt r}{r},
\qquad
\frac{\dt A_l}{A_l}-\frac{\sigma_l}{1-\sigma_l}\varepsilon_{g_l'}\frac{\dt k_l}{k_l}
=\frac{\dt w}{w} ,
\]
where $\varepsilon_{g_l'}\equiv g_l''(k_l)k_l/g_l'(k_l)<0$; the second uses
$\sigma_l=k_lg_l'(k_l)/g_l(k_l)$ and hence $1-\sigma_l=[g_l-k_lg_l']/g_l$.  Applying both to the
pair $i,j$, whose technological progress is balanced, and subtracting,
\begin{equation}
\varepsilon_{g_i'}\frac{\dt k_i}{k_i}=\varepsilon_{g_j'}\frac{\dt k_j}{k_j},
\qquad
\frac{\sigma_i}{1-\sigma_i}\varepsilon_{g_i'}\frac{\dt k_i}{k_i}
=\frac{\sigma_j}{1-\sigma_j}\varepsilon_{g_j'}\frac{\dt k_j}{k_j} .
\label{eq:twoconds}
\end{equation}

\emph{Case 1: the common value in the first equation is nonzero.}  Dividing the second equation
by the first,
\[
\frac{\sigma_i}{1-\sigma_i}=\frac{\sigma_j}{1-\sigma_j}
\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \sigma_i=\sigma_j ,
\]
contradicting the factor proportion differences.

\emph{Case 2: the common value is zero.}  Since $\varepsilon_{g_i'}<0$ strictly, this means
$\dt k_i=0$.  But the wage-rental ratio can be read off \emph{any} sector,
\[
\frac{w}{r}=\frac{g_l(k_l)}{g_l'(k_l)}-k_l ,
\]
whose derivative in $k_l$ is $-g_lg_l''/(g_l')^2>0$.  So each $k_l$ is a strictly increasing
function of the \emph{common} $w/r$, and $\dt k_i=0$ forces $\dt k_l=0$ for \emph{every} $l$.
Then every $\sigma_l=k_lg_l'(k_l)/g_l(k_l)$ is constant; balanced growth and \eqref{eq:pconst}
make every revenue share $p_lY_l/Y$ constant; hence every
$rK_l/Y=\sigma_l\,p_lY_l/Y$ and every $K_l/K$ is constant.  Therefore each $K_l$ grows at
$\dt K/K$, and since $k_l$ is constant each $L_l$ does too, giving $\dt L/L=\dt K/K$ ---
contradicting capital deepening.

Both cases are impossible, so growth cannot be balanced, and in particular
$\dt Y_i/Y_i\ne\dt Y_j/Y_j$. $\blacksquare$

\emph{The economics} is exactly \bk{Proposition 20.5}'s.  If capital and labour were allocated in
fixed proportions, the more capital-intensive sector would grow faster under capital deepening.
Relative prices then move against it, reallocating factors toward the less intensive sector --- but
that reallocation cannot be complete, because if it were, the price change that caused it would not
have happened.  The multi-sector version adds one observation: it is the \emph{common} wage-rental
ratio that ties the sectors together, which is why a single sector cannot be exempted from the
capital deepening that the aggregate experiences.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{20.12}{}
Profit maximization in the final good sector gives \bk{(20.38)},
\[
p_s(t)=\frac{\partial Y}{\partial Y_s}
=\gamma_s\Bigl(\frac{Y_s(t)}{Y(t)}\Bigr)^{-1/\varepsilon},
\qquad \gamma_1\equiv\gamma,\ \gamma_2\equiv1-\gamma ,
\]
directly from \bk{(20.31)}.  The intermediate producers are competitive and Cobb--Douglas, so
\[
w=p_1\frac{\partial Y_1}{\partial L_1}=p_1(1-\alpha_1)\frac{Y_1}{L_1},
\qquad
r=p_1\frac{\partial Y_1}{\partial K_1}=p_1\alpha_1\frac{Y_1}{K_1} ,
\]
and substituting $p_1$ gives \bk{(20.41)} and \bk{(20.42)},
\[
\boxed{\ w(t)=\gamma(1-\alpha_1)\Bigl(\frac{Y}{Y_1}\Bigr)^{1/\varepsilon}\frac{Y_1}{L_1},
\qquad
r(t)=\gamma\alpha_1\Bigl(\frac{Y}{Y_1}\Bigr)^{1/\varepsilon}\frac{Y_1}{K_1} . \ }
\]
Writing the same two expressions for sector 2 and equating --- factors are mobile, so $w$ and $r$
are common --- gives \bk{(20.39)} and \bk{(20.40)} respectively. $\blacksquare$

\emph{The allocations \bk{(20.43)}--\bk{(20.44)}.}  Dividing \bk{(20.40)} for the two sectors,
\[
\frac{K_1}{K_2}=\frac{\gamma\alpha_1}{(1-\gamma)\alpha_2}
\Bigl(\frac{Y_1}{Y_2}\Bigr)^{\frac{\varepsilon-1}{\varepsilon}} ,
\]
and with $\kappa\equiv K_1/K$ this inverts to \bk{(20.43)}.  Dividing \bk{(20.39)} for the two
sectors gives the same expression with $(1-\alpha_s)$ in place of $\alpha_s$, so taking the ratio
of the two,
\[
\frac{L_1/L_2}{K_1/K_2}=\frac{(1-\alpha_1)/\alpha_1}{(1-\alpha_2)/\alpha_2} ,
\]
which with $\lambda\equiv L_1/L$ rearranges to \bk{(20.44)}.  Since $\alpha_1<\alpha_2$ the factor
$\alpha_1(1-\alpha_2)/[\alpha_2(1-\alpha_1)]$ is below one, so $\lambda>\kappa$: the less
capital-intensive sector takes a larger share of labour than of capital, as it must.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{20.13}{}
Write $\Lambda\equiv\dfrac{\alpha_2}{\alpha_1}\dfrac{1-\gamma}{\gamma}
\bigl(\tfrac{Y_1}{Y_2}\bigr)^{(1-\varepsilon)/\varepsilon}$, so that \bk{(20.43)} is
$\kappa=(1+\Lambda)^{-1}$ and $1-\kappa=\Lambda/(1+\Lambda)$.  Then
\begin{equation}
\dd\log\kappa=-(1-\kappa)\,\dd\log\Lambda
=-(1-\kappa)\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}\,D,
\qquad D\equiv\dd\log Y_1-\dd\log Y_2 .
\label{eq:dlogk}
\end{equation}

\emph{Expressing $D$.}  From \bk{(20.33)} with $K_1=\kappa K$, $K_2=(1-\kappa)K$,
$L_1=\lambda L$, $L_2=(1-\lambda)L$,
\[
D=\dd\log\frac{A_1}{A_2}+(\alpha_1-\alpha_2)\bigl(\dd\log K-\dd\log L\bigr)
+\Bigl[\alpha_1+\frac{\alpha_2\kappa}{1-\kappa}\Bigr]\dd\log\kappa
+\Bigl[(1-\alpha_1)+\frac{(1-\alpha_2)\lambda}{1-\lambda}\Bigr]\dd\log\lambda .
\]
\bk{(20.44)} says $\frac{1-\lambda}{\lambda}=\Theta\frac{1-\kappa}{\kappa}$ with $\Theta$
constant; differentiating its logarithm gives
$\frac{\dd\lambda}{\lambda(1-\lambda)}=\frac{\dd\kappa}{\kappa(1-\kappa)}$, that is
\[
\dd\log\lambda=\frac{1-\lambda}{1-\kappa}\,\dd\log\kappa .
\]
Substituting, the two bracketed terms combine into
\[
\frac{\alpha_1(1-\kappa)+\alpha_2\kappa+(1-\alpha_1)(1-\lambda)+(1-\alpha_2)\lambda}{1-\kappa}
\,\dd\log\kappa
=\frac{1+(\alpha_2-\alpha_1)(\kappa-\lambda)}{1-\kappa}\,\dd\log\kappa ,
\]
since the numerator expands to $1+(\alpha_2-\alpha_1)(\kappa-\lambda)$.

\emph{Solving.}  Write $S\equiv1+(\alpha_2-\alpha_1)(\kappa-\lambda)$.  Substituting
\eqref{eq:dlogk} for $\dd\log\kappa$,
\[
D=\dd\log\frac{A_1}{A_2}-(\alpha_2-\alpha_1)\bigl(\dd\log K-\dd\log L\bigr)
-S\frac{1-\varepsilon}{\varepsilon}D ,
\]
so $D\bigl[\varepsilon+(1-\varepsilon)S\bigr]/\varepsilon$ equals the first two terms, and
putting this back into \eqref{eq:dlogk},
\[
\dd\log\kappa=\frac{-(1-\kappa)(1-\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon+(1-\varepsilon)S}
\Bigl[\dd\log\frac{A_1}{A_2}-(\alpha_2-\alpha_1)\bigl(\dd\log K-\dd\log L\bigr)\Bigr] .
\]
Finally $\varepsilon+(1-\varepsilon)S=1+(1-\varepsilon)(\alpha_2-\alpha_1)(\kappa-\lambda)$, so
\begin{align*}
\boxed{\ \frac{\dd\log\kappa}{\dd\log K}=-\frac{\dd\log\kappa}{\dd\log L}
=\frac{(1-\varepsilon)(\alpha_2-\alpha_1)(1-\kappa)}
{1+(1-\varepsilon)(\alpha_2-\alpha_1)(\kappa-\lambda)}\ }\\[4pt]
\boxed{\ \frac{\dd\log\kappa}{\dd\log A_2}=-\frac{\dd\log\kappa}{\dd\log A_1}
=\frac{(1-\varepsilon)(1-\kappa)}
{1+(1-\varepsilon)(\alpha_2-\alpha_1)(\kappa-\lambda)}\ }
\end{align*}
which are \bk{(20.45)} and \bk{(20.46)}. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Signs.}  The denominator is always strictly positive.  By Exercise 20.12, $\kappa<\lambda$
whenever $\alpha_1<\alpha_2$, so $(\alpha_2-\alpha_1)(\kappa-\lambda)<0$; for $\varepsilon>1$ the
factor $(1-\varepsilon)$ is also negative and the denominator exceeds one, while for
$\varepsilon<1$ the product has magnitude below $(\alpha_2-\alpha_1)(\lambda-\kappa)<1$.  Hence
\bk{(20.45)} has the sign of $(1-\varepsilon)(\alpha_2-\alpha_1)$ and \bk{(20.46)} the sign of
$1-\varepsilon$, as the proposition claims.

\emph{Verification.}  \texttt{code/ch20.py} solves the static equilibrium for
$\alpha_1=0.25$, $\alpha_2=0.55$, $\gamma=\tfrac12$, $K=3$, $L=A_1=A_2=1$, and compares the boxed
formulas with two-sided numerical derivatives:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrrrrr}
\toprule
$\varepsilon$ & $\kappa$ & $\lambda$ & $\dd\log\kappa/\dd\log K$ & \bk{(20.45)} &
$\dd\log\kappa/\dd\log A_2$ & \bk{(20.46)}\\
\midrule
$0.3$ & $0.3671$ & $0.6802$ & $0.14225877$ & $0.14225877$ & $0.47419590$ & $0.47419590$\\
$0.7$ & $0.3345$ & $0.6482$ & $0.06163747$ & $0.06163747$ & $0.20545824$ & $0.20545824$\\
$1.5$ & $0.2801$ & $0.5879$ & $-0.10321815$ & $-0.10321815$ & $-0.34406050$ & $-0.34406050$\\
$3.0$ & $0.2075$ & $0.4899$ & $-0.40660645$ & $-0.40660645$ & $-1.35535482$ & $-1.35535482$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{20.14}{}
\textbf{(a)}  \emph{The limiting allocation.}  With $\varepsilon>1$ and $g^*_1>g^*_2$ we have
$Y_1/Y_2\to\infty$ and $(1-\varepsilon)/\varepsilon<0$, so
$(Y_1/Y_2)^{(1-\varepsilon)/\varepsilon}\to0$ and \bk{(20.43)} gives $\kappa^*=1$; \bk{(20.44)}
then gives $\lambda^*=1$.  By \bk{Proposition 20.9} with $\varepsilon>1$,
$g^*=\max\{g^*_1,g^*_2\}=g^*_1$.  So sector 1 is asymptotically dominant for the opposite reason
to the case in the text --- it grows \emph{faster}, and with substitutable goods the economy moves
toward the expanding sector.

\emph{The rates.}  With $\kappa^*=\lambda^*=1$, sector 1 asymptotically absorbs all factors, so
$n^*_1=n$ and $z^*_1=\dt K/K$.  The resource constraint \bk{(20.32)} with consumption growing at
$g^*$ forces $K$ to grow at $g^*$ as well (if it grew more slowly the consumption--output ratio
would rise above one; if faster, the transversality condition \bk{(20.56)} would fail), so
$z^*_1=g^*$.  The sector-1 production function then gives
$g^*=a_1+\alpha_1g^*+(1-\alpha_1)n$, that is
\[
\boxed{\ g^*=g^*_C=g^*_1=z^*_1=n+\frac{a_1}{1-\alpha_1} , \ }
\]
which is \bk{(20.62)}.  Now write $x\equiv g^*-g^*_2$ and
$d\equiv\frac{\varepsilon-1}{\varepsilon}x$.  \bk{(20.57)} and \bk{(20.58)} give
$n^*_2=n-d$ and $z^*_2=g^*-d$, and the sector-2 production function gives
$g^*_2=a_2+\alpha_2(g^*-d)+(1-\alpha_2)(n-d)$.  Substituting and solving the resulting linear
equation in $x$,
\[
\boxed{\ x=\varepsilon\Bigl[(1-\alpha_2)\frac{a_1}{1-\alpha_1}-a_2\Bigr] , \ }
\]
whence
\[
g^*_2=n+\varepsilon a_2+\bigl[1-\varepsilon(1-\alpha_2)\bigr]\frac{a_1}{1-\alpha_1},
\quad
z^*_2=n+(\varepsilon-1)a_2+\bigl[1-(\varepsilon-1)(1-\alpha_2)\bigr]\frac{a_1}{1-\alpha_1},
\]
\[
n^*_2=n-(\varepsilon-1)(1-\alpha_2)\Bigl[\frac{a_1}{1-\alpha_1}-\frac{a_2}{1-\alpha_2}\Bigr] ,
\]
which are \bk{(20.63)}, \bk{(20.64)} and \bk{(20.65)} \emph{verbatim}.  So the closed forms are
the same in both branches of \bk{(20.61)}; only the signs need care.  Under \bk{(20.61)} with
$\varepsilon>1$ we have $a_1/(1-\alpha_1)>a_2/(1-\alpha_2)$, hence $x>0$ and $d>0$, so
\[
z^*_2<g^*\ \text{and}\ n^*_2<n^*_1\quad\text{(as in \bk{(20.63)} and \bk{(20.65)}), but }
g^*_2<g^*
\]
--- the inequality in \bk{(20.64)} \emph{reverses}.  That is as it must be: in this branch sector 1
is dominant because it is the fast one. $\blacksquare$

\textbf{(b)}  Under the converse of \bk{(20.61)} --- either $\varepsilon<1$ with
$a_1/(1-\alpha_1)>a_2/(1-\alpha_2)$, or $\varepsilon>1$ with
$a_1/(1-\alpha_1)<a_2/(1-\alpha_2)$ --- the same reasoning applied to \bk{(20.43)} gives
$\kappa^*=\lambda^*=0$ and $g^*=g^*_2$.  The model is symmetric in the two sectors apart from the
labelling, so the statement is the mirror image.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Proposition 20.10$'$.}  \emph{Suppose \bk{(20.34)}, \bk{(20.60)} and the converse
of \bk{(20.61)} hold.  Then there exists a unique CGP with}
\[
g^*=g^*_C=g^*_2=z^*_2=n+\frac{a_2}{1-\alpha_2},\qquad n^*_2=n ,
\]
\[
g^*_1=n+\varepsilon a_1+\bigl[1-\varepsilon(1-\alpha_1)\bigr]\frac{a_2}{1-\alpha_2},
\qquad
z^*_1=n+(\varepsilon-1)a_1+\bigl[1-(\varepsilon-1)(1-\alpha_1)\bigr]\frac{a_2}{1-\alpha_2},
\]
\[
n^*_1=n-(\varepsilon-1)(1-\alpha_1)\Bigl[\frac{a_2}{1-\alpha_2}-\frac{a_1}{1-\alpha_1}\Bigr] ,
\]
\emph{and $z^*_1<g^*$, $n^*_1<n^*_2$; $g^*_1>g^*$ if $\varepsilon<1$ and $g^*_1<g^*$ if
$\varepsilon>1$.}

\emph{Proof.}  Interchange the indices $1$ and $2$ throughout part (a).  The only asymmetry in the
model is \bk{(20.34)}, $\alpha_1<\alpha_2$, and it enters nowhere in the derivation above ---
\bk{(20.62)}--\bk{(20.65)} use only which sector is asymptotically dominant. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Verification.}  \texttt{code/ch20.py} evaluates all four sign configurations with
$\alpha_1=0.25$, $\alpha_2=0.55$, $n=0.01$, and checks five conditions in each: the two production
functions, \bk{(20.57)}, \bk{(20.58)} and \bk{Proposition 20.9}.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrlrrrrr}
\toprule
$\varepsilon$ & $a_1$ & $a_2$ & branch & dom. & $g^*$ & $g^*$ other & $z^*$ other &
$n^*$ other\\
\midrule
$0.4$ & $0.010$ & $0.030$ & \bk{(20.61)} & 1 & $0.023333$ & $0.032933$ & $0.008933$ & $-0.004400$\\
$0.4$ & $0.030$ & $0.010$ & converse & 2 & $0.032222$ & $0.037556$ & $0.024222$ & $0.002000$\\
$2.5$ & $0.030$ & $0.010$ & \bk{(20.61)} & 1 & $0.050000$ & $0.030000$ & $0.038000$ & $-0.002000$\\
$2.5$ & $0.010$ & $0.030$ & converse & 2 & $0.076667$ & $-0.023333$ & $0.016667$ & $-0.050000$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
All five checks pass in every row.  Simulating the static equilibrium along
$K(t)=K(0)e^{g^*t}$ in the first configuration reproduces the predicted rates: at $t=1000$, with
$1-\kappa=5.8\times10^{-7}$, the measured $(g_1,g_2,z_2,n_2)$ are
$(0.023333,\,0.032933,\,0.008933,\,-0.004400)$ against predictions
$(0.023333,\,0.032933,\,0.008933,\,-0.004400)$.

\rmk{Note the negative $n^*_2$ in three of the four rows: employment in the non-dominant sector
shrinks \emph{absolutely}, not merely as a share.  That is the strongest form of the Baumol
prediction, and it is what \bk{(20.65)} delivers whenever
$(\varepsilon-1)(1-\alpha_2)\bigl|\frac{a_1}{1-\alpha_1}-\frac{a_2}{1-\alpha_2}\bigr|$ exceeds
the population growth rate.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{20.15}{}
\emph{Constancy, directly.}  From \bk{(20.42)},
$r(t)=\gamma\alpha_1\bigl(\tfrac{Y}{Y_1}\bigr)^{1/\varepsilon}\dfrac{Y_1}{\kappa(t)K(t)}$, so
\[
\frac{\dt r}{r}=\frac1\varepsilon\bigl(g(t)-g_1(t)\bigr)+g_1(t)
-\frac{\dt\kappa}{\kappa}-\frac{\dt K}{K} .
\]
Along the CGP, $g(t)\to g^*$ and $g_1(t)\to g^*_1=g^*$, so the first term vanishes;
$\kappa(t)\to1$, so $\dt\kappa/\kappa\to0$; and $\dt K/K\to z^*_1=g^*$.  Hence
$\dt r(t)\to0$: the interest rate is asymptotically constant.  Equivalently,
\[
\frac{Y}{Y_1}=\Bigl[\gamma+(1-\gamma)\Bigl(\frac{Y_2}{Y_1}\Bigr)^{\frac{\varepsilon-1}{\varepsilon}}\Bigr]^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon-1}}
\longrightarrow\gamma^{\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon-1}} ,
\]
because under \bk{(20.61)} the exponent and the limit of $Y_2/Y_1$ have opposite characters in
both branches, and $Y_1/K_1$ is asymptotically constant since both grow at $g^*$.

\emph{The closed form.}  Along a CGP per capita consumption grows at the constant rate
$g^*_c=g^*-n$, so the Euler equation \bk{(20.55)} gives $r^*=\rho+\theta g^*_c$, and substituting
\bk{(20.62)},
\[
\boxed{\ r^*=\rho+\frac{\theta\,a_1}{1-\alpha_1}\ }
\]
in the case where sector 1 is asymptotically dominant, and $r^*=\rho+\theta a_2/(1-\alpha_2)$ in
the converse case of Exercise 20.14(b).  In either case,
\[
r^*=\rho+\theta\max\Bigl\{\frac{a_1}{1-\alpha_1},\frac{a_2}{1-\alpha_2}\Bigr\}
\ \text{ if }\varepsilon>1,
\qquad
r^*=\rho+\theta\min\Bigl\{\frac{a_1}{1-\alpha_1},\frac{a_2}{1-\alpha_2}\Bigr\}
\ \text{ if }\varepsilon<1 .
\]
$\blacksquare$

\emph{What this says.}  The interest rate is governed entirely by the \emph{augmented} rate of
technological progress $a_s/(1-\alpha_s)$ of the asymptotically dominant sector --- Hicks-neutral
progress amplified by the capital deepening it induces.  Condition \bk{(20.60)} is exactly
$\rho-n\ge(1-\theta)\max_s a_s/(1-\alpha_s)$, which guarantees $r^*>g^*$ and hence that the
transversality condition holds.  Equating this with the direct expression also yields a by-product:
the asymptotic output--capital ratio of the dominant sector is
$\lim Y_1/K_1=\bigl[\rho+\theta a_1/(1-\alpha_1)\bigr]\big/\bigl[\alpha_1
\gamma^{\varepsilon/(\varepsilon-1)}\bigr]$.

\rmk{The Kaldor facts survive here for a reason worth stating.  The interest rate and, by
\bk{(20.48)} evaluated at $\kappa^*=1$, the aggregate capital share are asymptotically constant
even though the two sectors grow at permanently different rates and one of them may be losing
employment absolutely.  The aggregate looks balanced because one sector comes to dominate the
aggregate; the structural change is happening entirely in a sector whose share of output is
vanishing.  That is the sense in which Section 20.2 reconciles Kuznets with Kaldor, and it is a
different reconciliation from Section 20.1's knife-edge \bk{(20.18)}.}
\end{aex}

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\includegraphics{figures/ch20_structural_change.pdf}
\caption{Left: Engel curves from the Stone--Geary aggregator \bk{(20.2)}; solid curves impose
\bk{(20.18)}, which makes $s^M$ exactly constant, dashed curves set $\gamma^S=0$
(Exercise 20.1).  Right: manufacturing employment in the open-economy Matsuyama model, with the
home economy's autarky share marked in grey (Exercise 20.20).}
\label{fig:ch20}
\end{figure}

\solsection{20.3}{Agricultural Productivity and Industrialization}

\begin{aex}{20.20}{}
\textbf{(a)}  In either country the allocation of labour equates the value of the marginal product
across sectors, which is \bk{(20.71)}: $B^AG'(1-n)=pXF'(n)$, so the autarky relative price of
manufactures is
\[
p^{\mathrm{aut}}(n)=\frac{B^A}{X}\cdot\frac{G'(1-n)}{F'(n)} .
\]
The second factor is common to the two countries --- $F$ and $G$ are the same everywhere --- so at
any common allocation the ratio of the two autarky prices is
\[
\frac{p^{\mathrm{aut}}}{p^{F,\mathrm{aut}}}=\frac{B^A/X}{B^F/X^F}
=\frac{X^F(0)/B^F}{X(0)/B^A} .
\]
Comparative advantage in manufacturing goes to the country with the \emph{lower} autarky price of
manufactures, that is
\[
\boxed{\ \text{home has a comparative advantage in manufacturing}\iff
\frac{X(0)}{B^A}>\frac{X^F(0)}{B^F} . \ }
\]

\emph{Interpretation.}  Comparative advantage is Ricardian and depends on the \emph{ratio} of
manufacturing to agricultural productivity, not on absolute levels: a country can be better at
both and still specialize in agriculture.  The sting is that $B^A$ enters with the wrong sign
relative to the closed economy.  In autarky, \bk{(20.75)} says a higher $B^A$ \emph{raises}
manufacturing employment, because a smaller workforce suffices to feed the population; in the open
economy a higher $B^A$ is precisely what gives the country a comparative advantage in agriculture
and so \emph{lowers} it.  Agricultural productivity is a spur to industrialization behind closed
borders and a brake on it in an open economy.

\textbf{(b)}  With free trade there is a single world price $p(t)$, and \bk{(20.71)} holds in each
country at that price:
\[
p(t)=\frac{B^AG'(1-n^*(t))}{X(t)F'(n^*(t))}=\frac{B^FG'(1-n^{F*}(t))}{X^F(t)F'(n^{F*}(t))} ,
\]
which rearranged at $t=0$ is exactly \bk{(20.78)}.

For the inequality, define
$\Psi(n)\equiv\dfrac{B^AG'(1-n)}{X(0)F'(n)}$.  Since $G'$ is decreasing, $G'(1-n)$ is increasing
in $n$; since $F'$ is decreasing, $1/F'(n)$ is increasing in $n$.  So $\Psi$ is strictly
increasing, and $n=\Psi^{-1}(p)$ is strictly increasing in the price of manufactures.  Free trade
places the world price strictly between the two autarky prices, and by part (a) the home autarky
price of manufactures is the higher one; hence $p(0)<\Psi(n^*)=p^{\mathrm{aut}}$ and
\[
\boxed{\ n^*(0)=\Psi^{-1}\bigl(p(0)\bigr)<\Psi^{-1}\bigl(p^{\mathrm{aut}}\bigr)=n^* . \ }
\]
Opening to trade immediately shifts labour out of home manufacturing.

\textbf{(c)}  Take logarithms of \bk{(20.78)} written for a general $t$ and define
$H(n)\equiv\log F'(n)-\log G'(1-n)$, which is strictly decreasing.  Then
\[
\log X(t)+H\bigl(n(t)\bigr)-\log B^A=\log X^F(t)+H\bigl(n^F(t)\bigr)-\log B^F ,
\]
and differentiating with respect to time, using \bk{(20.70)} and its foreign counterpart
$\dt X^F=\kappa Y^{M,F}$,
\[
\boxed{\ \kappa F\bigl(n(t)\bigr)+H'\bigl(n(t)\bigr)\dt n(t)
=\kappa F\bigl(n^F(t)\bigr)+H'\bigl(n^F(t)\bigr)\dt n^F(t) . \ }
\]
By part (b), $n(0)<n^F(0)$, so $F(n(0))<F(n^F(0))$ and the foreign manufacturing technology grows
strictly faster from the first instant.  The gap $X^F/X$ therefore widens, which by part (a)
strengthens the foreign comparative advantage, which by part (b) lowers $n$ further.  The process
is self-reinforcing and $n(t)$ declines monotonically to zero, while $n^F(t)$ rises --- not to one,
because the world must still be fed, but to the constant at which the foreign country alone
supplies the residual food demand.

\emph{Numerically}, with $F(x)=G(x)=x^{0.6}$, $\eta=0.4$, $\gamma^A=0.25$, $\kappa=0.02$,
$B^A=1.6$, $B^F=1$ and $X(0)=X^F(0)=1$, \texttt{code/ch20.py} gives the home autarky share
$n^*=0.5307$ and
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrrr}
\toprule
$t$ & $n(t)$ & $n^F(t)$ & $X/B^A$ & $X^F/B^F$\\
\midrule
$0$ & $0.3879$ & $0.6723$ & $0.625$ & $1.00$\\
$50$ & $0.3065$ & $0.7417$ & $1.065$ & $2.25$\\
$150$ & $0.0660$ & $0.8619$ & $2.182$ & $13.1$\\
$300$ & $0.0001$ & $0.8813$ & $2.591$ & $209$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The right panel of Figure~\ref{fig:ch20} plots the paths.  Note that the home economy is the
\emph{more productive farmer} and it is exactly that which de-industrializes it.

\rmk{This is Section 19.7's mechanism with a different source of the initial asymmetry.  There a
small exogenous productivity gap $\delta$ in the learning sector was amplified by
learning-by-doing until the South was locked out; here the asymmetry is agricultural productivity,
which is the very thing the closed-economy model of Section 20.3 identifies as the \emph{cause} of
industrialization.  The juxtaposition is Matsuyama's point, and it is a caution about reading
\bk{Proposition 20.11} as a historical account of English industrialization: eighteenth-century
England had both high agricultural productivity and substantial trade, and which force dominates
depends on whether one thinks the relevant market for its manufactures was domestic or foreign.
By the same token, the model gives an infant-industry argument with the same structure --- and the
same qualification --- as Exercise 19.36: a temporary closure changes the asymptotic outcome only
if it lasts long enough to reverse the comparative advantage, that is until $X(T_0)/B^A$ exceeds
$X^F(T_0)/B^F$, which it never does here because the foreign economy is learning faster
throughout.}
\end{aex}


%% ========================================================================
\solchapter{21}{Structural Transformations and Market Failures in Development}

\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{0.86\textwidth}
\small
The six exercises of Chapter 21 left unsolved in the instructor's manual: 21.3, 21.5, 21.7,
21.8, 21.13 and 21.14.  The manual solves 21.1, 21.2, 21.4, 21.6, 21.9--21.12.  The numerical
parts are computed by \texttt{code/ch21.py}; Figure~\ref{fig:ch21} is its output.
\end{minipage}
\end{center}

\bigskip
\hrule
\bigskip

\solsection{21.2}{Fertility, Mortality, and the Demographic Transition}

\begin{aex}{21.3}{}
\emph{The law of motion.}  Individual maximization of \bk{(21.6)} is unaffected by the shock,
so $n(t+1)=(1-\alpha)\eta_0^{-1}L(t+1)^{-\alpha}$ as in the text.  Substituting,
\[
L(t+1)=\epsilon(t)\,\frac{1-\alpha}{\eta_0}\,L(t+1)^{-\alpha}L(t)
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\boxed{\ L(t+1)=\Bigl[\epsilon(t)\frac{1-\alpha}{\eta_0}\Bigr]^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}
L(t)^{\frac{1}{1+\alpha}} , \ }
\]
which is \bk{(21.10)} multiplied by $\epsilon(t)^{1/(1+\alpha)}$.  Taking logarithms with
$\ell\equiv\log L$ turns this into a \emph{linear} first-order autoregression,
\begin{equation}
\ell(t+1)=\varphi\,\ell(t)+\mu+\frac{\log\epsilon(t)}{1+\alpha},
\qquad
\varphi\equiv\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\in(0,1),
\qquad \mu\equiv\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\log\frac{1-\alpha}{\eta_0} .
\label{eq:malar1}
\end{equation}

\emph{The stochastic correspondence.}  The dynamics are described by \emph{two} concave curves
through the origin, one for each realization of $\epsilon$, differing by the constant factor
$[(1+\epsilon)/(1-\epsilon)]^{1/(1+\alpha)}$.  Each crosses the $45^\circ$ line once, at
\[
L^{\pm}=\Bigl[(1\pm\epsilon)\frac{1-\alpha}{\eta_0}\Bigr]^{1/\alpha}
=(1\pm\epsilon)^{1/\alpha}L^* ,
\]
with $L^*$ from \bk{(21.11)}.  Below $L^-$ both branches lie above the $45^\circ$ line, so
population rises whatever the shock; above $L^+$ both lie below it, so population falls.  Hence
$[L^-,L^+]$ is forward invariant and absorbing, and this is the Malthusian counterpart of
Figure 17.1.  The left panel of Figure~\ref{fig:ch21} plots it.

\emph{The invariant distribution.}  Iterating \eqref{eq:malar1},
\[
\log L_\infty=\log L^*+\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\varphi^{\,s}\,\frac{\log\epsilon(t-s)}{1+\alpha} ,
\]
using $\mu/(1-\varphi)=\alpha^{-1}\log[(1-\alpha)/\eta_0]=\log L^*$.  With i.i.d.\ shocks,
\[
\boxed{\
\E[\log L_\infty]=\log L^*+\frac{\E[\log\epsilon]}{\alpha},
\qquad
\Var(\log L_\infty)=\frac{\Var(\log\epsilon)}{(1+\alpha)^2-1}
=\frac{\Var(\log\epsilon)}{\alpha(2+\alpha)} , \ }
\]
and $\Corr\bigl(\log L(t),\log L(t+1)\bigr)=\varphi$.  Since
$\E[\log\epsilon]=\tfrac12\log(1-\epsilon^2)<0$, \emph{shocks lower average population}: the law
of motion is concave, so by Jensen's inequality the mean of $\log L$ falls by
$|\E\log\epsilon|/\alpha$ --- the effect being $1/\alpha$ times the per-period effect, because
the deviations are persistent.

The support is the whole interval $[\ell^-,\ell^+]$, where $\ell^{\pm}=\log L^{\pm}$.  Each
branch maps that interval onto a subinterval of length $\Lambda/(1+\alpha)$, where
$\Lambda=\alpha^{-1}\log\frac{1+\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}$ is its length, and the two images cover it
whenever $2/(1+\alpha)\ge1$, that is whenever $\alpha\le1$.  So the invariant distribution has no
gaps; for $\alpha>1$ it would be supported on a Cantor set.

\emph{Income.}  From \bk{(21.9)}, $\log y(t)=\log(1-\alpha)-\alpha\log L(t)$, so income is an
affine decreasing function of log population and inherits the same autoregression with the
opposite sign:
\[
\mathrm{sd}\bigl(\log y\bigr)=\alpha\,\mathrm{sd}(\log L)
=\frac{\mathrm{sd}(\log\epsilon)}{\sqrt{(2+\alpha)/\alpha}} ,
\qquad
\Corr\bigl(\log y(t),\log y(t+1)\bigr)=\frac{1}{1+\alpha} .
\]
A favourable shock raises population, depresses the real wage, and the depressed wage then
lowers fertility, bringing population back --- the classical Malthusian cycle.  The half-life of a
shock is $\log2/\log(1+\alpha)$ generations, and a one-time shock $u$ moves $\log L$ by $u$ and
$\log y$ by $-\alpha u$, both decaying geometrically at rate $\varphi$.  The narrative fits the
standard account of the Black Death: a large negative population shock raises real wages, and the
wage gain erodes over a few generations as population recovers.

\emph{Verification.}  Simulating \eqref{eq:malar1} for $400{,}000$ generations with $\alpha=0.3$
and $\epsilon=0.15$ gives
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\toprule
& analytic & simulated\\
\midrule
$\E[\log L]$ & $-1.226845$ & $-1.227546$\\
$\mathrm{sd}(\log L)$ & $0.181952$ & $0.182488$\\
$\min\log L$ & $-1.730646$ & $-1.725713$\\
$\max\log L$ & $-0.723043$ & $-0.727835$\\
autocorrelation & $0.769231$ & $0.770787$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
--- the simulated minimum and maximum confirming that the invariant support is exactly
$[\ell^-,\ell^+]$.

\rmk{The exercise as printed writes $L(t+1)=\epsilon(t)\bigl(n(t+1)-1\bigr)L(t)$.  Taken
literally, population would shrink whenever $n<2$, so that even the deterministic version of the
model has no positive steady state unless $\eta_0$ is small enough; the $-1$ is presumably a
misprint for the $L(t+1)=\epsilon(t)n(t+1)L(t)$ used above.  Nothing qualitative changes if one
instead reads it as net-of-mortality growth $L(t+1)=L(t)+\epsilon(t)(n(t+1)-1)L(t)$: the law of
motion is still increasing and concave, and the two-branch correspondence and the invariant
interval survive, though the log-linearity of \eqref{eq:malar1} does not.}
\end{aex}

\solsection{21.3}{Migration, Urbanization, and the Dual Economy}

\begin{aex}{21.5}{}
\emph{The problem.}  Let $\upsilon(\xi)$ be the consumption value of the enforcement services a
villager enjoys and a city dweller forgoes.  Population is normalized to one, the migration
margin is $X(t)F_L(L^U(t),Z)=B^A+\xi$ as in the text, so $L^U(t)=\phi\bigl(X(t)/(B^A+\xi)\bigr)$,
and aggregate consumption is
\[
C(t;\xi)=\bigl[B^A+\upsilon(\xi)\bigr]\bigl(1-L^U(t)\bigr)+X(t)F\bigl(L^U(t),Z\bigr),
\qquad
\dt X=\eta\,L^U(t)\,X(t)^{\zeta} .
\]
Date-$0$ utility is $U(\xi)=\int_0^\infty e^{-\rho t}\frac{C(t;\xi)^{1-\theta}-1}{1-\theta}\dd t$.

\emph{The first-order condition.}  The equilibrium $L^U$ maximizes $C$ given $(X,\xi)$ --- that
is what $XF_L=B^A+\xi$ says --- so the envelope theorem kills the indirect effect through
$L^U$ and
\[
\boxed{\
U'(\xi)=\int_0^\infty e^{-\rho t}\,C(t)^{-\theta}
\Bigl[\upsilon'(\xi)\bigl(1-L^U(t)\bigr)+F\bigl(L^U(t),Z\bigr)\,v(t)\Bigr]\dd t=0 , \ }
\]
where $v(t)\equiv\partial X(t)/\partial\xi<0$ solves the variational equation
\[
\dt v=\eta\Bigl[\phi'\Bigl(\frac{X}{B^A+\xi}\Bigr)\frac{X^{\zeta}}{B^A+\xi}
+\zeta\,\phi\Bigl(\frac{X}{B^A+\xi}\Bigr)X^{\zeta-1}\Bigr]v
-\eta\,\phi'\Bigl(\frac{X}{B^A+\xi}\Bigr)\frac{X^{\zeta+1}}{(B^A+\xi)^2},
\qquad v(0)=0 .
\]
The bracket in the boxed expression is exactly the trade-off the text describes: a \emph{static
gain}, the extra enforcement services enjoyed by the $1-L^U$ villagers, against a \emph{dynamic
loss}, the reduction in modern-sector productivity caused by slower urbanization.

\emph{But $U$ is not concave, and the maximum is at a corner.}  Two structural facts make the
two terms fail to balance at an interior point.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The dynamic cost is a bounded delay, not a permanent loss.}  Since $L^U$ is bounded
      above by one and reaches it, integrating the law of motion gives
      \[
      X(t)^{1-\zeta}-X(0)^{1-\zeta}=(1-\zeta)\,\eta\int_0^tL^U(s)\dd s ,
      \]
      and two paths with different $\xi$ differ only by the bounded integral of the $L^U$ gap.
      A higher $\xi$ therefore \emph{delays} the takeoff by a finite time and does not lower $X$
      permanently --- a delay that is expensive only if it straddles the takeoff.
\item \emph{The static gain accrues at every date before the takeoff}, when $1-L^U$ is close to
      one.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently $U(\xi)$ first \emph{falls} --- a small $\xi$ buys little extra village consumption
but already delays the takeoff --- and then \emph{rises} --- a large $\xi$ buys a lot and the
economy simply never urbanizes within the horizon that matters.  \texttt{code/ch21.py} tabulates
$U$ with $\upsilon(\xi)=3\xi/(1+\xi)$, $\eta=1$, $\zeta=\tfrac12$, $\theta=1$:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrrrrrr}
\toprule
$\rho$ & $\xi=0$ & $1$ & $2$ & $3$ & $4$ & $6$ & argmax\\
\midrule
$0.03$ & $70.46$ & $34.75$ & $36.74$ & $39.35$ & $40.85$ & $42.48$ & $\xi=0$\\
$0.05$ & $20.18$ & $18.58$ & $22.02$ & $23.62$ & $24.52$ & $25.50$ & $\xi=6$\\
$0.07$ & $7.31$ & $13.15$ & $15.74$ & $16.88$ & $17.52$ & $18.23$ & $\xi=6$\\
$0.09$ & $3.01$ & $10.22$ & $12.25$ & $13.14$ & $13.64$ & $14.18$ & $\xi=6$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The right panel of Figure~\ref{fig:ch21} plots the curves.  So the welfare-maximizing $\xi$ is at
one end of the admissible range or the other, and which one wins switches with the discount
rate: $\xi=0$ for a patient economy, the largest admissible $\xi$ for an impatient one.  (Under
the alternative reading in which $\xi$ is a pure deadweight urban cost, so that
$C=B^A(1-L^U)+XF-\xi L^U$, the envelope theorem gives $\dd C/\dd\xi=-L^U<0$ at every date, both
effects point the same way, and $\xi^*=0$ trivially.)

\emph{If the actual $\xi$ is greater.}  The answer is sharper than ``welfare falls.''  The
economy is in a \emph{development trap}: it stays rural, learning-by-doing never gets going, and
--- because $U$ is falling on that branch --- a \emph{small} reduction in $\xi$ makes matters
\emph{worse}.  Only a reduction past the interior minimum helps.  This is the big-push logic of
Section 21.5 applied to the dual economy: gradual institutional reform is counterproductive and
the intervention has to be large.

\rmk{The first-best remedy is different again, and better.  The planner would choose $L^U$ to
internalize the learning externality, setting
$XF_L=B^A+\xi-\lambda(t)\eta X^{\zeta}$ with $\lambda$ the shadow value of $X$, so the
decentralized economy has too few urban workers for \emph{every} $\xi>0$.  A Pigouvian subsidy to
urban employment equal to $\lambda(t)\eta X(t)^{\zeta}$ restores efficiency at any $\xi$ and
forfeits none of the static gain --- which is why dismantling community enforcement is the wrong
policy even when $\xi$ is above its second-best level.}
\end{aex}

\solsection{21.4}{Distance to the Frontier and the Organization of Production}

\begin{aex}{21.7}{}
\textbf{(a)}  Flow profits in this class of models are proportional to the firm's own
productivity, $\pi(\nu,t)=\pi_0A(\nu,t)$.  Comparing the two organizational forms,
\[
\pi^{V}=\pi_0\bigl[\eta\ol A(t-1)+\gamma A(t-1)\bigr],
\qquad
\pi^{O}=(1-\beta)\,\pi_0\bigl[\eta\ol A(t-1)+(\gamma+\theta)A(t-1)\bigr] .
\]
Outsourcing is preferred iff $\pi^O>\pi^V$, that is
\[
(1-\beta)\theta\,A(t-1)>\beta\bigl[\eta\ol A(t-1)+\gamma A(t-1)\bigr] .
\]
Dividing by $\ol A(t-1)$ and writing $a(t-1)=A(t-1)/\ol A(t-1)$,
\[
\boxed{\ \text{outsource}\iff a(t-1)>\ol a\equiv
\frac{\beta\eta}{(1-\beta)\theta-\beta\gamma} , \ }
\]
provided $(1-\beta)\theta>\beta\gamma$; otherwise outsourcing is never profitable.  So there is
vertical integration for all $a(t)\le\ol a$ and outsourcing for all $a(t)>\ol a$, as claimed.

\emph{Why.}  The \emph{benefit} of outsourcing, $\theta A(t-1)$, is proportional to the economy's
own knowledge stock, because innovative effort builds on what the country already knows.  The
\emph{cost}, a fraction $\beta$ of profits, falls on the entire profit stream including the
imitation component $\eta\ol A(t-1)$, for which the entrepreneur's effort is worthless.  Far from
the frontier most productivity comes from imitation, so paying $\beta$ to buy innovative effort
is a bad bargain; close to the frontier innovation is what matters and outsourcing pays.  This is
the same appropriate-organization logic as the $R=1$ to $R=0$ switch in the text, now applied to
firm boundaries.

Note also that $\ol a<1$, so that the switch ever occurs, requires
$\beta\eta<(1-\beta)\theta-\beta\gamma$, that is
\[
\beta<\frac{\theta}{\theta+\eta+\gamma} .
\]

\textbf{(b)}  The growth-maximizing organization is the one that maximizes $a(t)$ given
$a(t-1)$.  Aggregating and dividing by $\ol A(t)$ as in \bk{(21.39)},
\[
a(t)=\frac{1}{1+g}\bigl[\eta+\gamma a(t-1)\bigr]\ \text{under integration},
\qquad
a(t)=\frac{1}{1+g}\bigl[\eta+(\gamma+\theta)a(t-1)\bigr]\ \text{under outsourcing},
\]
and since $\theta>0$ the second is strictly larger for \emph{every} $a(t-1)>0$.  Hence
\[
\boxed{\ \text{the growth-maximizing threshold is }0:\ \text{outsource always.} \ }
\]
The private threshold $\ol a$ is strictly positive, so the economy is \emph{vertically integrated
too often}, for every $a\in(0,\ol a]$.

\emph{The wedge} is a pure externality, and it is worth locating precisely.  The entrepreneur
does internalize the effect of $\theta$ on his \emph{own} $A(\nu,t)$ --- that is why his profit
rises with $\theta$.  What he does not internalize is that his higher $A(\nu,t)$ raises the
\emph{aggregate} $A(t)$, which enters every firm's law of motion at $t+1$ through the term
$\gamma A(t)$.  The social benefit of outsourcing therefore exceeds the private one by the
discounted value of that spillover, and a subsidy to outsourcing (or to innovation) of at least
$\beta$ restores the growth-maximizing organization.

\emph{The consequence for convergence} is not merely transitional.  Under permanent integration
the economy converges to $a^*=\eta/(1+g-\gamma)$, while under permanent outsourcing it converges
to $\min\{\eta/(1+g-\gamma-\theta),1\}$.  If $\ol a$ exceeds the first of these, the economy never
switches and is stuck permanently at $a^*$ --- an organizational nonconvergence trap of exactly
the kind Figure 21.7 exhibits for $R$.  \texttt{code/ch21.py} tabulates this with $\eta=0.1$,
$\gamma=0.6$, $g=0.03$:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrlrr}
\toprule
$\beta$ & $\theta$ & $\ol a$ & switches? & $a^*$ integrated & limit\\
\midrule
$0.10$ & $0.30$ & $0.0476$ & yes & $0.2326$ & $0.7692$\\
$0.20$ & $0.30$ & $0.1667$ & yes & $0.2326$ & $0.7692$\\
$0.30$ & $0.30$ & $1.0000$ & no & $0.2326$ & $0.2326$\\
$0.10$ & $0.15$ & $0.1333$ & yes & $0.2326$ & $0.3571$\\
$0.35$ & $0.15$ & $\infty$ & no & $0.2326$ & $0.2326$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
A country with weak contracting institutions --- a high $\beta$, in the sense of Section 18.5 ---
therefore ends up not merely with a slower transition but with a permanently larger distance to
the frontier, because it is locked into the wrong firm boundaries.
\end{aex}

\solsection{21.5}{Aggregate Demand Externalities and the Big Push}

\begin{aex}{21.8}{}
All households are identical and own all the firms, so Pareto dominance is simply higher
utility.  From the text, the two candidate allocations are
\[
\text{no investment: } C(1)=C(2)=L,
\qquad
\text{investment: } C(1)=L-F,\ C(2)=\alpha L ,
\]
with lifetime utilities $U^N=(1+\beta)u(L)$ and $U^I=u(L-F)+\beta u(\alpha L)$, where
$u(c)=(c^{1-\theta}-1)/(1-\theta)$.

\emph{The argument.}  Write the difference as
\[
U^I-U^N=-\bigl[u(L)-u(L-F)\bigr]+\beta\bigl[u(\alpha L)-u(L)\bigr] ,
\]
and bound each bracket by concavity:
\[
u(L)-u(L-F)\le u'(L-F)\,F=(L-F)^{-\theta}F,
\qquad
u(\alpha L)-u(L)\ge u'(\alpha L)\,(\alpha-1)L=(\alpha L)^{-\theta}(\alpha-1)L .
\]
Hence
\[
U^I-U^N\ \ge\ \beta(\alpha L)^{-\theta}(\alpha-1)L-(L-F)^{-\theta}F ,
\]
and the right-hand side is positive if and only if
\[
\boxed{\ \beta\Bigl(\frac{\alpha L}{L-F}\Bigr)^{-\theta}(\alpha-1)L\ >\ F , \ }
\]
which is \emph{exactly} the left half of \bk{(21.51)} --- the condition $\Delta\pi^I>0$ that makes
investment profitable when all other firms invest.  Since the existence of multiple equilibria
requires \bk{(21.50)}, and \bk{(21.50)} contains $\Delta\pi^I>0$, the investment equilibrium
Pareto dominates whenever both equilibria exist. $\blacksquare$

\emph{The same statement without the inequalities.}  The equilibrium interest rate in the
investment allocation is $\tilde r$, and by the consumption Euler equation
\[
\frac{1}{1+\tilde r}=\beta\Bigl(\frac{\alpha L}{L-F}\Bigr)^{-\theta}
=\beta\,\frac{u'\bigl(C(2)\bigr)}{u'\bigl(C(1)\bigr)} .
\]
So $\Delta\pi^I=-F+\frac{(\alpha-1)L}{1+\tilde r}$ is positive precisely when the household's own
marginal rate of substitution values the extra date-2 consumption above the date-1 sacrifice.
Because households are identical and own the firms, \emph{a profit gain evaluated at the
equilibrium interest rate is a utility gain} --- which is the whole content of the result, and
the reason the concavity bound above is not merely sufficient but tight.

\emph{Verification.}  A random scan of $200{,}000$ parameter draws over
$\alpha\in(1.01,3)$, $\beta\in(0.5,0.99)$, $\theta\in(0,4)$, $F/L\in(0.001,0.8)$ in
\texttt{code/ch21.py} finds $4{,}262$ draws satisfying \bk{(21.50)} --- so that both equilibria
exist --- and $43{,}790$ satisfying $\Delta\pi^I>0$; in none of them does the investment
allocation fail to dominate.

\rmk{The economics is Rosenstein-Rodan's, and the model makes the policy statement unusually
clean.  The failure here is \emph{pure coordination}: the two allocations are both equilibria at
the same parameters, they differ only in beliefs about what other firms will do, and one makes
every household strictly better off.  A ``big push'' that does nothing but coordinate
expectations therefore achieves a Pareto improvement with no subsidies, no transfers and no
distortions --- unlike almost every other market failure in this chapter, where the remedy
requires a Pigouvian instrument.  The market cannot do it on its own only because each firm is
infinitesimal and takes $Y(2)$ and $\tilde r$ as given, so the aggregate demand externality in
\bk{(21.49)} is external to every one of them.}
\end{aex}

\solsection{21.6}{Inequality, Credit Market Imperfections, and Occupational Choice}

\begin{aex}{21.13}{}
\textbf{(a)}  With $u=(1-\delta)^{-(1-\delta)}\delta^{-\delta}c^{1-\delta}b^{\delta}-z$ and
end-of-period resources $y$ to be split between $c$ and $b$, the Cobb--Douglas optimum is
$c=(1-\delta)y$, $b=\delta y$, and substituting,
\[
\boxed{\ V=(1-\delta)^{-(1-\delta)}\delta^{-\delta}
\bigl[(1-\delta)y\bigr]^{1-\delta}\bigl[\delta y\bigr]^{\delta}-z=y-z . \ }
\]
The normalizing constant is chosen precisely so that indirect utility is \emph{linear} in
resources --- there is no risk aversion and no wealth effect in occupational choice, only the
credit constraints.  Comparing occupation 2 with occupation 1 gives $\hat rw+v-1$ against
$\hat rw$, so nobody supplies labour at a wage below $1$.

\textbf{(b)}  Evaluate the three active occupations against subsistence at the reservation wage
$v=1$:
\[
\underbrace{I(\ol r-\hat r)-1}_{\text{self-employment}},
\qquad
\underbrace{\mu\bigl[I(\ol r-\hat r)-1\bigr]-1}_{\text{entrepreneurship}} .
\]
The right inequality says self-employment beats subsistence, and the left says entrepreneurship
beats self-employment --- both at the \emph{lowest possible wage}.  So at $v=1$ the ranking is
entrepreneurship $\succ$ self-employment $\succ$ subsistence, and it can only be reversed by a
higher wage.  This is what makes the two wage regimes of part (g) possible.

\textbf{(c)}  With limited enforcement a lender will advance funds only against collateral, so
the borrowing capacity is increasing in own wealth.  Self-employment requires $I$ and
entrepreneurship $\mu I>I$, so the wealth thresholds satisfy $w^{**}>w^*$.  The consequence is
the defining feature of this literature: occupations are allocated \emph{by wealth} rather than
by ability or comparative advantage, so the wealth distribution is an input into the allocation
of talent and hence into aggregate output.

\textbf{(d)}  Expected indirect utilities at wealth $w$ and wage $v$ are
\[
\hat rw;\qquad \hat rw+v-1;\qquad \hat rw+I(\ol r-\hat r)-1;
\qquad \hat rw+\mu I(\ol r-\hat r)-\mu v-1 .
\]
Self-employment beats entrepreneurship iff
$I(\ol r-\hat r)>\mu I(\ol r-\hat r)-\mu v$, that is
\[
\boxed{\ v>\ol v\equiv\frac{(\mu-1)(\ol r-\hat r)I}{\mu} . \ }
\]

\textbf{(e)}  Only agents with $w\ge w^{**}$ can be entrepreneurs, and each hires $\mu$ workers.
They prefer entrepreneurship iff $v<\ol v$ and are indifferent at $v=\ol v$, so
\[
x=0\ \text{ if }v>\ol v;\qquad
x\in\bigl[0,\mu(1-G_t(w^{**}))\bigr]\ \text{ if }v=\ol v;\qquad
x=\mu(1-G_t(w^{**}))\ \text{ if }v<\ol v .
\]

\textbf{(f)}  An agent supplies labour iff wage work beats his best alternative.  Against
subsistence the condition is $v\ge1$; against self-employment, available only to those with
$w\ge w^*$, it is $v-1\ge I(\ol r-\hat r)-1$, that is $v\ge\tilde v\equiv(\ol r-\hat r)I$.  So
agents with $w<w^*$ work whenever $v\ge1$, and those with $w\ge w^*$ only when $v\ge\tilde v$,
giving exactly the correspondence in the exercise.  Note $\tilde v=(\ol r-\hat r)I>\ol v$, since
$(\mu-1)/\mu<1$.

\textbf{(g)}  Compare the two correspondences on $(1,\tilde v)$, where supply is $G_t(w^*)$.
\begin{itemize}
\item If $G_t(w^*)>\mu(1-G_t(w^{**}))$, supply exceeds the maximum demand at every $v>1$, so
      there is excess supply and the wage falls to the reservation level $v=1$, at which supply
      is the correspondence $[0,G_t(w^*)]$ and can meet demand.
\item If $G_t(w^*)<\mu(1-G_t(w^{**}))$, demand exceeds supply just below $\ol v$ and collapses to
      zero just above it, so the market clears at $v=\ol v$, where demand is the correspondence
      $[0,\mu(1-G_t(w^{**}))]$.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{(h)}  By part (a) each agent bequeaths the fraction $\delta$ of end-of-period income, and
the effort cost is borne in utility, not in income.  Hence $b=\delta\hat rw$ for subsistence,
$\delta(\hat rw+v)$ for a worker, $\delta[\ol rI+\hat r(w-I)]$ for the self-employed --- the
amount $I$ earns $\ol r$ and the remainder $\hat r$ --- and
$\delta[\ol r\mu I+\hat r(w-\mu I)-\mu v]$ for an entrepreneur, whose wage bill is a genuine
cost.

\textbf{(i)}  Each of these is an affine map $w\mapsto\delta\hat rw+\delta\cdot(\text{extra})$
with slope $\delta\hat r<1$ (this is where $\hat r<1/\delta$ is used), so it converges to
\[
\boxed{\ w_w(v)=\frac{\delta v}{1-\delta\hat r},\qquad
w_{se}=\frac{\delta(\ol r-\hat r)I}{1-\delta\hat r},\qquad
w_e(v)=\frac{\delta\bigl[(\ol r-\hat r)\mu I-\mu v\bigr]}{1-\delta\hat r} . \ }
\]
If $w_w(1)<w^*$, a worker paid the reservation wage never accumulates enough to become
self-employed; if $w_e(\ol v)>w^{**}$, an entrepreneur paid the high wage stays rich enough to
remain one.  Both are statements about the \emph{wage}, which is itself determined by the
occupational composition --- circular causation, and hence multiplicity.

\textbf{(j)}  Starting from a distribution with $\mu(1-G(w^{**}))<G(w^*)$, part (g) gives $v=1$;
by (i) the workers stay below $w^*$, so $G(w^*)$ remains high and the configuration reproduces
itself: a \emph{low-wage steady state with no self-employment}.  Starting from
$\mu(1-G(w^{**}))>G(w^*)$, part (g) gives $v=\ol v>1$; workers accumulate toward
$w_w(\ol v)$, which may exceed $w^*$, so a self-employed class emerges and the configuration
again reproduces itself: a \emph{high-wage steady state with self-employment}.  Output is
$\hat rW+(\ol r-\hat r)\bigl[I\cdot\#\text{self-employed}+\mu I\cdot\#\text{entrepreneurs}\bigr]$
with $W$ aggregate wealth, so the second regime, which puts more wealth into the high-return
technology, has \emph{higher} output.

\textbf{(k)}  The mechanism is attractive and the comparison is not.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{In its favour.}  The multiplicity requires no nonconvexity in technology --- only the
      minimum scale $I$ and the collateral constraint --- and it delivers the striking and
      genuinely useful prediction that \emph{cheap labour is a poverty trap}: the prosperous
      economy is the high-wage one, because high wages are what let workers accumulate past the
      collateral threshold.  That is the opposite of the standard development prescription and it
      is testable.
\item \emph{Against.}  The prosperous steady state has \emph{more} self-employment and fewer wage
      employees.  The data run the other way: the self-employment share falls sharply with
      development, and poor countries are characterized by a mass of tiny own-account firms, not
      by large employers.  The model also has no productivity growth at all, so the output gap
      between the two steady states is bounded by $(\ol r-\hat r)$ times the wealth reallocated,
      which is far too small to speak to observed cross-country differences.
\item \emph{Against Galor--Zeira.}  There the nonconvexity is in human capital, the multiplicity
      is in the \emph{long-run distribution} rather than in the aggregate occupational structure,
      and the prediction --- that initial inequality affects long-run output through education ---
      maps directly onto schooling data.  But Galor--Zeira takes the wage structure as exogenous
      and has no feedback from occupational composition to prices, which is exactly what
      Banerjee--Newman supplies.
\end{itemize}
The two are best read as complements: the dynastic human-capital channel and the
general-equilibrium labour-market channel are different market failures, and a satisfactory
account of persistent development differences needs both.
\end{aex}

\solsection{21.7}{Toward a Unified Theory of Development and Growth}

\begin{aex}{21.14}{}
\textbf{1.}  The locus is $sf(k,x)=\delta k$.  Implicit differentiation gives
\[
\frac{\dd x}{\dd k}\Big|_{\dt k=0}
=-\frac{\partial\bigl[sf(k,x)-\delta k\bigr]/\partial k}
{\partial\bigl[sf(k,x)-\delta k\bigr]/\partial x}
=\frac{\delta-sf_k(k,x)}{sf_x(k,x)}
=\boxed{\ \frac{f(k,x)-kf_k(k,x)}{k\,f_x(k,x)}\ >\ 0 , \ }
\]
the last equality using $sf=\delta k$ on the locus.  The numerator is strictly positive because
$f(\cdot,x)$ is strictly concave with $f(0,x)=0$, so $f(k,x)>kf_k(k,x)$; the denominator is
positive because $f_x>0$.  When $f_x\equiv0$ the derivative is infinite and the locus is the
vertical line of Figure 21.10. $\blacksquare$

\textbf{2.}  The Jacobian of $\bigl(\dt k,\dt x\bigr)=\bigl(sf(k,x)-\delta k,\ g(k,x)\bigr)$ at
$(k^*,x^*)$ is
\[
J=\begin{pmatrix} sf_k-\delta & sf_x\\ g_k & g_x\end{pmatrix} .
\]
Its entries have known signs.  At the steady state $sf=\delta k$, so
\[
sf_k-\delta=sf_k-s\frac{f}{k}=\frac{s}{k}\bigl(kf_k-f\bigr)<0
\]
by strict concavity, and $g_x<0$ by assumption.  Hence
\[
\tr J=\bigl(sf_k-\delta\bigr)+g_x<0
\qquad\text{always.}
\]
Local asymptotic stability therefore requires only $\det J>0$, and
\[
\det J=\bigl(sf_k-\delta\bigr)g_x-sf_xg_k
=\underbrace{\bigl|sf_k-\delta\bigr|\,\bigl|g_x\bigr|}_{>0}-\underbrace{sf_xg_k}_{>0} ,
\]
so
\[
\boxed{\ \det J>0
\iff
f_x(k^*,x^*)<\frac{\bigl[f(k^*,x^*)/k^*-f_k(k^*,x^*)\bigr]\,|g_x|}{g_k} , \ }
\]
which is ``$f_x$ sufficiently small.'' $\blacksquare$

\emph{The geometric reading.}  The condition is exactly that the $\dt k=0$ locus be
\emph{steeper} than the $\dt x=0$ locus at the intersection.  The slope of the second is
$-g_k/g_x=g_k/|g_x|$, so
\[
\frac{f-kf_k}{kf_x}>\frac{g_k}{|g_x|}
\iff (f-kf_k)|g_x|>kf_xg_k
\iff \bigl(\delta-sf_k\bigr)|g_x|>sf_xg_k ,
\]
multiplying through by $s/k>0$ --- the same inequality.  This explains the figures.  In
Figure 21.12 the unique steady state has the $\dt k=0$ locus steeper and is globally stable; in
Figure 21.11 the outer two intersections have that ordering and are stable, while the middle one
has it reversed, so $\det J<0$ there and it is a \emph{saddle} whose stable manifold is the
boundary between the two basins of attraction.

\emph{Verification.}  \texttt{code/ch21.py} takes
$f(k,x)=k^{0.35}\bigl[1+\psi/\bigl(1+e^{-(x-x_0)/\varsigma}\bigr)\bigr]$ and
$g(k,x)=0.05k-x$, with $s=0.25$, $\delta=0.06$, $\psi=3$, $x_0=1.5$.  Shrinking $\varsigma$ makes
$f_x$ large over a narrow range of $x$ without changing $f$ much --- the text's ``$f_x$ large over
some range'' --- and produces the three intersections:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrrrrl}
\toprule
$\varsigma$ & $k^*$ & $x^*$ & $\tr J$ & $\det J$ & slope $\dt k=0$ & slope $\dt x=0$\\
\midrule
$1.00$ & $61.128$ & $3.056$ & $-1.039$ & $\phantom{-}0.016246$ & $0.0857$ & $0.05$\\
$0.30$ & $10.607$ & $0.530$ & $-1.039$ & $\phantom{-}0.028563$ & $0.1868$ & $0.05$\\
$0.30$ & $25.627$ & $1.281$ & $-1.039$ & $-0.046399$ & $0.0228$ & $0.05$\\
$0.30$ & $75.775$ & $3.789$ & $-1.039$ & $\phantom{-}0.038724$ & $7.0630$ & $0.05$\\
$0.10$ & $8.986$ & $0.449$ & $-1.039$ & $\phantom{-}0.038978$ & $88.18$ & $0.05$\\
$0.10$ & $29.030$ & $1.452$ & $-1.039$ & $-0.248522$ & $0.0068$ & $0.05$\\
$0.10$ & $75.817$ & $3.791$ & $-1.039$ & $\phantom{-}0.039000$ & $1.0\times10^{7}$ & $0.05$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The sign of $\det J$ and the ordering of the slopes agree in every row, as they must, and the
middle steady state is a saddle in both multiple-steady-state cases.

\rmk{The result is the formal content of the chapter's closing argument.  Multiple development
traps require the social variable to feed back strongly enough into productivity --- $f_x$ large
--- and then history selects among them; a weak feedback leaves a unique globally stable steady
state in which structural change is a by-product of capital deepening rather than a cause of it.
Since $\tr J<0$ unconditionally, the only thing that can ever go wrong in this model is
$\det J\le0$, which is to say the only source of complicated dynamics is the interaction between
$k$ and $x$, never either equation on its own.}
\end{aex}

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\includegraphics{figures/ch21_development.pdf}
\caption{Left: the stochastic correspondence of the Malthusian model, with the invariant
interval $[L^-,L^+]$ marked (Exercise 21.3).  Right: date-$0$ utility against the
community-enforcement advantage $\xi$, normalized by its maximum, for four discount rates; the
curve falls and then rises, so the optimum is at a corner (Exercise 21.5).}
\label{fig:ch21}
\end{figure}
