\solchapter{22}{Institutions, Political Economy, and Growth}

\begin{center}
\begin{minipage}{0.86\textwidth}
\small
The seventeen exercises of Chapter 22 left unsolved in the instructor's manual: 22.1, 22.4--22.7,
22.10--22.15, 22.23, 22.24, 22.28, 22.29, 22.31 and 22.32.  The manual solves 22.2, 22.3, 22.8,
22.9, 22.16--22.22, 22.25--22.27 and 22.30.  The numerical parts are computed by
\texttt{code/ch22.py}; Figure~\ref{fig:ch22} is its output.
\end{minipage}
\end{center}

\bigskip
\hrule
\bigskip

\solsection{22.2}{Distributional Conflict in a Simple Society}

\begin{aex}{22.1}{}
By \bk{(22.14)} the elite's per-period transfer is proportional to tax revenue, so their
problem is
\[
\max_{\tau\in[0,1]}R(\tau),\qquad R(\tau)\equiv\tau f\bigl(\hat k(\tau)\bigr),
\qquad
\hat k(\tau)=(f')^{-1}\Bigl(\frac{\beta^{-1}+\delta-1}{1-\tau}\Bigr) .
\]

\emph{The two endpoints give zero.}  At $\tau=0$, $R(0)=0$ trivially.  As $\tau\uparrow1$ the
argument of $(f')^{-1}$ diverges, and \bk{Assumption 2} (the Inada conditions) makes
$f'(k)\to\infty$ only as $k\to0$, so $\hat k(\tau)\to0$ and hence $f(\hat k(\tau))\to f(0)=0$.
Therefore $R(\tau)\to0$ as $\tau\uparrow1$, and at $\tau=1$ output and revenue are both zero.

\emph{The interior is strictly positive.}  For $\tau\in(0,1)$ the same Inada conditions give
$\hat k(\tau)\in(0,\infty)$, so $f(\hat k(\tau))>0$ and $R(\tau)=\tau f(\hat k(\tau))>0$.

\emph{Conclusion.}  $R$ is continuous on the compact interval $[0,1]$, so it attains a maximum;
the maximum is strictly positive, hence attained in the interior, hence
\[
\boxed{\ \hat\tau\in(0,1) . \ }
\]
Any interior maximizer satisfies $R'(\hat\tau)=0$, that is
$f(\hat k(\hat\tau))+\hat\tau f'(\hat k(\hat\tau))\hat k'(\hat\tau)=0$; substituting
$\hat k'(\tau)=f'/[(1-\tau)f'']$ from \bk{(22.12)} gives \bk{(22.16)}. $\blacksquare$

The argument also shows why the elite never impose $100\%$ taxation, which is what the text
needs in order to dispense with the complementary slackness conditions after \bk{(22.10)}: at
$\tau=1$ the middle class produce nothing and the elite collect nothing, so $\tau=1$ is
dominated by every interior tax.  Note that $R$ need not be concave --- \bk{(22.16)} may have
several roots --- but this does not matter, since the argument above locates a global maximizer
without using concavity.

\emph{The Cobb--Douglas case.}  With $f(k)=A^{1-\alpha}k^{\alpha}/\alpha$ and $\delta=1$,
\bk{(22.18)} gives $\hat k(\tau)=[\beta(1-\tau)]^{1/(1-\alpha)}A$ and
\[
R(\tau)=\frac{A\beta^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}}{\alpha}\,\tau(1-\tau)^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)} ,
\]
whose first-order condition is $1-\tau=\tau\alpha/(1-\alpha)$, that is $\hat\tau=1-\alpha$.
\texttt{code/ch22.py} confirms this numerically to ten digits for
$\alpha\in\{0.2,\tfrac13,0.5,0.7\}$.  The comparative static is the one the text emphasizes: a
higher $\alpha$ makes the demand for capital more elastic and pushes the peak of the Laffer curve
down.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{22.4}{}
\emph{The strategy profile} is the one in the hint.  On the equilibrium path each entrepreneur
contributes $\wt T/N$ at every date and the elite announce $\tau=0$; if total contributions ever
fall below $\wt T$, or if the elite ever announce a positive tax, both sides revert for ever to
the MPE of \bk{Proposition 22.2}: $\tau=1-\alpha$ and zero contributions.  The punishment is an
equilibrium of the continuation game, so the profile is subgame perfect provided the two
one-shot-deviation conditions hold.

\emph{The elite's constraint.}  Under the timing of Section 22.2 the tax for date $t+1$ is
announced at $t$, so a deviation yields the elite \emph{nothing extra today}: they still receive
$\wt T$ at $t$ and the higher tax only bites from $t+1$, by which time the punishment has
started.  Comparing continuation values from $t+1$,
\[
\boxed{\ \frac{\wt T}{1-\beta}\ \ge\ \frac{R^{RE}}{1-\beta}
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\wt T\ \ge\ R^{RE}\equiv\hat\tau f\bigl(\hat k(\hat\tau)\bigr)\times(\text{total labour}) , \ }
\]
the elite's per-period revenue in the MPE.  No condition on $\beta$ is needed here.

\emph{An entrepreneur's constraint.}  Let $S(\tau)$ be a single entrepreneur's per-period
surplus at tax rate $\tau$.  Contributing for ever gives $[S(0)-\wt T/N]/(1-\beta)$; free-riding
saves $\wt T/N$ once --- today's tax is already set at zero --- and yields $S(\hat\tau)$ from
$t+1$ on.  Hence
\[
\frac{S(0)-\wt T/N}{1-\beta}\ \ge\ S(0)+\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}S(\hat\tau)
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\boxed{\ \beta\ \ge\ \frac{\wt T/N}{S(0)-S(\hat\tau)} . \ }
\]
Taking the smallest admissible transfer $\wt T=R^{RE}$, the profile is an SPE for all $\beta$
close enough to one \emph{provided}
\begin{equation}
N\bigl[S(0)-S(\hat\tau)\bigr]>R^{RE} ,
\label{eq:contribfeas}
\end{equation}
that is, provided the entrepreneurs' collective gain from abolishing the tax exceeds the revenue
the elite give up. $\blacksquare$

\emph{When \eqref{eq:contribfeas} holds.}  This is where the answer needs care, because it
depends on who bears the tax.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{With wages at zero} (Condition 22.1 failing, as in Section 22.4) the entrepreneur
      keeps $(1-\tau)f(\hat k(\tau))-\hat k(\tau)$ per worker, and abolishing the tax transfers
      the whole distorted surplus to him.  \texttt{code/ch22.py} gives, at $\alpha=\tfrac13$ and
      $\beta=0.9$, $S(0)-S(\hat\tau)=1.609$ against $R^{RE}=1.095$ per worker, so
      \eqref{eq:contribfeas} holds and the threshold is $\beta\ge0.681$.
\item \emph{With positive wages} (the full-employment case of Section 22.2, where \bk{(22.6)}
      holds) it fails.  The entrepreneur's surplus is
      $\hat k(\tau)(1-\beta)/\beta$ per worker, because \bk{(22.10)} with $\delta=1$ gives
      $(1-\tau)f'(\hat k)=1/\beta$; the tax mostly reduces the \emph{wage},
      $\hat w(\tau)=(1-\tau)(1-\alpha)f(\hat k(\tau))$.  Numerically, per worker,
      \begin{center}
      \small
      \begin{tabular}{lrr}
      \toprule
       & $\tau=0$ & $\tau=1-\alpha$\\
      \midrule
      output $f(\hat k)$ & $2.846$ & $1.643$\\
      wage $\hat w$ & $1.897$ & $0.365$\\
      entrepreneur surplus $S$ & $0.0949$ & $0.0183$\\
      elite revenue & $0$ & $1.095$\\
      \bottomrule
      \end{tabular}
      \end{center}
      so $S(0)-S(\hat\tau)=0.077$ against $R^{RE}=1.095$: the entrepreneurs gain only a
      fourteenth of what the elite would forgo, and cannot buy them off.  The scheme then needs
      the \emph{workers} to contribute too --- which is natural, since they are the ones the tax
      actually falls on.
\end{itemize}

\rmk{The exercise's insistence on $N<\infty$ is the substantive point, and it is footnote 9 of
the chapter made precise.  With a continuum of entrepreneurs each individual contribution has
measure zero, a shortfall is undetectable, no trigger can ever fire, and the scheme unravels ---
which is exactly why with a continuum the SPE and MPE coincide (\bk{Proposition 22.7}).  So the
Pareto efficiency of the MPE in Section 22.2 is not a property of elite-dominated politics but
an artifact of the continuum: with finitely many producers a Pareto improvement is available and
implementable, and the MPE is Pareto \emph{inefficient}.  This is the cleanest illustration in
the chapter of how much the choice of equilibrium concept, and of market structure, is doing.}
\end{aex}

\solsection{22.4}{Distributional Conflict and Competition}

\begin{aex}{22.5}{}
Suppose Condition 22.1 fails, so by \bk{Proposition 22.3} $w(t)=0$ at every date, and $\phi>0$.

\emph{No taxes on themselves, no transfers to others.}  The elite's payoff is their own
production surplus plus the per capita transfer $T^e$ determined residually by \bk{(22.19)}.
Raising $\tau^e$ above zero reduces their own capital-labor ratio through \bk{(22.20)} and
returns at most the fraction $\phi\le1$ of the tax collected on their own output; the net effect
is a strict loss even at $\phi=1$, because the distortion is first order in the tax at any
$\tau^e>0$ once the capital response is taken into account.  Hence $\tau^e(t)=0$.  Transfers
$T^m$ and $T^w$ subtract one for one from $T^e$, so they are zero. $\blacksquare$

\emph{The tax on the middle class.}  With $w=0$, the elite's employment and their own surplus do
not depend on $\tau^m$, so their problem reduces to maximizing revenue
\[
\frac{\phi}{\alpha}\,\tau^m\bigl(\beta(1-\tau^m)\bigr)^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}A^m\theta^m\bar L ,
\]
which is the Laffer problem of Exercise 22.1.  Its unique maximizer is
\[
\boxed{\ \tau^m(t)=\tau^{RE}\equiv1-\alpha\quad\text{for all }t , \ }
\]
and the objective is strictly single-peaked, so the maximizer is unique.  Since the per-period
objective depends only on the current tax --- linear preferences again --- the same tax is
optimal at every date and the MPE is stationary.  $T^e(t)$ follows from \bk{(22.19)} holding as
equality. $\blacksquare$

The role of the hypothesis is worth stating: Condition 22.1 \emph{fails}, so wages are zero and
the elite have neither the ability nor the incentive to manipulate factor prices.  The only
motive left is revenue extraction, which puts them exactly at the peak of the Laffer curve.
Everything that follows in Section 22.4 is a departure from this benchmark in one direction ---
taxes \emph{beyond} the peak.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{22.6}{}
Now Condition 22.1 holds --- so that by \bk{Proposition 22.3} the wage is strictly positive ---
and $\phi=0$, so taxation raises no usable revenue at all.

With $\phi=0$ the elite's income is their own production surplus plus $R^N/\theta^e$:
\[
\Bigl[\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\beta^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}\bigl(1-\tau^e\bigr)^{1/(1-\alpha)}A^e
-w(t)\Bigr]L^e(t)+\frac{R^N}{\theta^e} .
\]
Taxing themselves only lowers the first bracket, with no offsetting revenue, so $\tau^e(t)=0$;
transfers to others are likewise zero.  Given $\tau^e=0$, \bk{(22.22)} gives
\[
w(t)=\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\beta^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}
\min\Bigl\{A^e,\ \bigl(1-\tau^m(t)\bigr)^{1/(1-\alpha)}A^m\Bigr\} ,
\]
so the elite's payoff is strictly decreasing in $w$ and $w$ is nonincreasing in $\tau^m$,
strictly so whenever the middle class are the marginal employer.  The elite therefore drive
$\tau^m$ as high as possible:
\[
\boxed{\ \tau^m(t)=\tau^{FPM}\equiv1,\qquad \tau^e(t)=T^m(t)=T^w(t)=0 , \ }
\]
at which $w(t)=0$ and the elite employ $\bar L$ workers each at zero cost. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Why this exceeds the revenue-maximizing rate.}  Under revenue extraction the elite
\emph{want} the middle class to produce, because middle-class output is their tax base; that is
what puts them at the peak of the Laffer curve.  Under factor price manipulation they want the
opposite --- to destroy middle-class profitability so as to kill their labour demand and so the
wage.  The two motives point in opposite directions, and $\tau^{FPM}=1>\tau^{RE}=1-\alpha$.

Note also who really pays.  With $\phi=0$ the tax transfers nothing to the elite directly; the
elite gain entirely at the expense of the \emph{workers}, whose wage falls from
$\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\beta^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}\min\{A^e,A^m\}$ to zero.  An observer who
looked only at the tax code would see a tax on the middle class; its incidence is on labour.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{22.7}{}
Condition 22.1 holds, $\phi>0$, and \bk{(22.27)} holds so that \bk{(22.26)} fails at the
solution --- the elite produce themselves and the middle class employ the residual labour.  Then
$L^e(t)=\bar L$ and, by \bk{(22.25)}, $\theta^mL^m(t)=1-\theta^e\bar L$, while by \bk{(22.22)}
the wage is set by the middle class,
$w=\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\beta^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}(1-\tau^m)^{1/(1-\alpha)}A^m$.

Write $B\equiv\beta^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}A^m/\alpha$ and
$\Psi\equiv\phi\bigl(1-\theta^e\bar L\bigr)/\theta^e$.  Dropping terms independent of $\tau^m$,
the objective \bk{(22.24)} is
\[
-(1-\alpha)B\bar L\,(1-\tau)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}
+\Psi B\,\tau(1-\tau)^{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}} .
\]
Differentiating, dividing by $B(1-\tau)^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)-1}>0$ and using
$\tfrac{1}{1-\alpha}-1=\tfrac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}$,
\[
\bar L(1-\tau)+\Psi\Bigl[(1-\tau)-\frac{\alpha\tau}{1-\alpha}\Bigr]=0
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\bigl(\bar L+\Psi\bigr)(1-\tau)=\frac{\alpha\Psi}{1-\alpha}\,\tau ,
\]
so that
\[
\boxed{\ \tau^{COM}=\frac{(1-\alpha)\bigl(\bar L+\Psi\bigr)}
{(1-\alpha)\bigl(\bar L+\Psi\bigr)+\alpha\Psi} . \ }
\]
This is \bk{(22.28)}.  Indeed $\bar L/\Psi=\theta^e\bar L/[(1-\theta^e\bar L)\phi]$, so
\bk{(22.29)} reads $\kappa=\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\frac{\Psi+\bar L}{\Psi}$, and
$\kappa/(1+\kappa)$ is exactly the boxed expression. $\blacksquare$

\emph{The two bounds the proposition asserts} follow by inspection of $\kappa$.  Since
$\Psi<\infty$, $\kappa<\infty$ and $\tau^{COM}<1=\tau^{FPM}$: the prospect of revenue pulls the
elite back from the pure-manipulation corner.  Since $\bar L>0$, $\kappa>\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}$
and therefore $\tau^{COM}>\frac{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}{1+(1-\alpha)/\alpha}=1-\alpha=\tau^{RE}$: the
wage motive always pushes them past the peak of the Laffer curve.  The comparative statics read
off $\kappa$ directly --- $\tau^{COM}$ rises with $\bar L$ and $\theta^e$ (a larger elite
employing more workers cares more about the wage) and falls with $\phi$ (a more capable state
cares more about revenue).

\emph{Verification.}  \texttt{code/ch22.py} maximizes \bk{(22.24)} numerically for five
parameter configurations and matches the boxed formula to seven decimals in every case, with
$\tau^{COM}$ ranging from $0.725$ to $0.766$ against $\tau^{RE}=0.667$.

\emph{On condition \bk{(22.27)}.}  It is what makes the interior case the relevant one.  If
$A^e$ were small relative to $\phi\alpha^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}A^m\theta^m/\theta^e$, the elite
would rather withdraw from production altogether, let the middle class hire the entire labour
force, and simply maximize revenue --- which returns them to \bk{Proposition 22.4} and
$\tau^{RE}$.  Competition in the marketplace requires the elite actually to be in the
marketplace.
\end{aex}

\solsection{22.5}{Subgame Perfect versus Markov Perfect Equilibria}

\begin{aex}{22.10}{}
The point is that in these models there is nothing to punish \emph{with}.

\emph{Step 1: the private sector has a unique best response to each tax, independent of
history.}  Every entrepreneur is infinitesimal and takes prices as given, so his capital choice
solves \bk{(22.10)}, whose solution \bk{(22.20)} is unique and depends only on the current
announced tax; and his labour demand \bk{(22.21)} depends only on the current wage.  No
individual can alter any aggregate, so no individual has any incentive to deviate from that best
response after any history --- there is no history-dependent behaviour available to the private
sector that is consistent with individual optimization.

\emph{Step 2: the elite's continuation payoff is therefore history-independent.}  By Step 1 the
aggregate capital-labor ratios, the wage and hence the elite's per-period payoff $\Pi(\tau(t))$
are functions of the current tax alone.  So for any strategy profile the elite's continuation
value from date $t$ is $\sum_{s\ge t}\beta^{s-t}\Pi(\tau(s))$, and the sequence
$\{\tau(s)\}_{s\ge t}$ can be chosen freely, period by period, with no consequence for any other
player's future behaviour.

\emph{Step 3: conclude.}  Maximizing a discounted sum of terms each depending on a separate,
freely chosen variable means maximizing each term, so in every SPE
$\tau(t)\in\arg\max\Pi(\tau)$ at every date and after every history.  That is precisely the MPE
of \bk{Propositions 22.4}--\bk{22.6}, and it is unique because each of those maximizers is
unique. $\blacksquare$

\rmk{The contrast with Exercise 22.4 is exact and instructive.  There the middle class are
\emph{finitely many}, so a contribution can be withheld and detected, Step 1 fails, and
history-dependent strategies become available.  Here the continuum assumption is doing the
work: it is not that punishment is unprofitable, it is that no punishment exists.  This is also
why \bk{Proposition 22.7} does not extend to Section 22.5.2, where the timing of taxes creates a
genuine holdup and the middle class's investment can respond to the \emph{expected} tax --- which
is something an implicit agreement can move.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{22.11}{}
Two features of that model must be checked, since it has an extra state variable --- who holds
power.

\emph{The private sector, as before.}  Middle-class entrepreneurs are infinitesimal and price
taking, so Step 1 of Exercise 22.10 applies verbatim: their capital choice is the unique
function \bk{(22.20)} of the current tax, whatever the history.  In particular, the replacement
probability $\eta(\theta^mC^m(t))$ is a function of the current tax alone.

\emph{The political state.}  The transition is \emph{permanent and one-directional}: power
shifts from the elite to the middle class and never back.  Hence
\begin{itemize}
\item after the switch, the middle class hold power for ever and face a stationary problem whose
      per-period payoff again depends only on the current tax, so their behaviour is pinned down
      exactly as in Exercise 22.10, giving the constant $V^e(M)$;
\item before the switch, the elite's continuation value is
      $V^e(E)=\max_{\tau}\{\Pi^e(\tau)+\beta[(1-\eta[\tau])V^e(E)+\eta[\tau]V^e(M)]\}$, in which
      every object is a function of the current tax and of the two scalars $V^e(E)$, $V^e(M)$.
\end{itemize}
There is therefore no player and no date at which behaviour can be conditioned on history in a
payoff-relevant way, and the argument of Exercise 22.10 gives a unique SPE, coinciding with the
MPE and featuring the constant tax $\tau^{PC}>\tau^{RE}$. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Where this would break.}  Suppose instead that power could change hands repeatedly.  Then
the group currently out of power has a future in which it will set policy, and the two groups
can sustain an implicit agreement --- ``moderate your taxes now and we shall moderate ours when
our turn comes'' --- enforced by the threat of reverting to the MPE.  Such strategies are
history dependent and are not Markovian, so the SPE set strictly contains the MPE, exactly as
the text notes.  The one-switch assumption is thus not innocuous: it is what makes the sharp
prediction $\tau^{PC}>\tau^{RE}$ robust to the choice of equilibrium concept.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{22.12}{}
\textbf{(a)}  Condition 22.1 fails, so $w=0$; with holdup the tax at $t$ is chosen after the
capital for $t$ is sunk.  Consider the trigger profile of the text: the elite set
$\tau^m(t)=1-\alpha$ and the middle class invest according to \bk{(22.20)} at that rate, so long
as the history has been exactly that; after any other action, the elite set $\tau^m=1$ and the
middle class invest zero for ever, which is the MPE of \bk{Proposition 22.8} and yields the elite
a continuation value of zero.

The middle class have no profitable deviation: at each date they are best responding to the
announced tax.  For the elite, the on-path value is \bk{(22.31)},
\[
\frac{\phi}{1-\beta}(1-\alpha)\,\alpha^{-(1-2\alpha)/(1-\alpha)}\beta^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}
A^m\theta^m\bar L ,
\]
while the most profitable deviation is to set $\tau^m=1$ on the sunk capital
$\hat k(1-\alpha)=(\alpha\beta)^{1/(1-\alpha)}A^m$, which yields \bk{(22.32)},
\[
\phi\,\alpha^{-(1-2\alpha)/(1-\alpha)}\beta^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}A^m\theta^m\bar L ,
\]
and zero thereafter.  Comparing, all the common factors cancel and the condition is simply
\[
\frac{1-\alpha}{1-\beta}\ \ge\ 1
\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad
\boxed{\ \beta\ \ge\ \alpha . \ }
\]
$\blacksquare$

\textbf{(b)}  With a ceiling $\bar\tau<1$ two things change at once, and they pull in opposite
directions.  The punishment is \emph{weaker}, since the MPE now involves $\tau^m=\bar\tau$ rather
than $1$ and leaves the elite a strictly positive continuation revenue; but the deviation is also
\emph{smaller}, since the elite can only raise the tax to $\bar\tau$.

Write $r(\tau)\equiv\tau(1-\tau)^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}$, so that per-period revenue is proportional
to $r$.  Along a stationary SPE with announced tax $\tau^*$, a deviation raises the tax on the
sunk capital $\hat k(\tau^*)$ to $\bar\tau$ and yields $\frac{\bar\tau}{\tau^*}r(\tau^*)$ this
period, followed by $r(\bar\tau)$ for ever.  The incentive constraint is therefore
\[
\frac{r(\tau^*)}{1-\beta}\ \ge\ \bar\tau(1-\tau^*)^{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}}
+\frac{\beta}{1-\beta}r(\bar\tau) ,
\]
that is
\[
\boxed{\ (1-\tau^*)^{\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}}
\bigl[\tau^*-(1-\beta)\bar\tau\bigr]\ \ge\ \beta\,r(\bar\tau) . \ }
\]
At $\bar\tau=1$ the right-hand side is zero and the condition reduces to
$\alpha^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}(\beta-\alpha)\ge0$, recovering part (a).

The best stationary SPE then has
\[
\tau^*=\begin{cases}
\bar\tau, & \bar\tau\le1-\alpha,\\[2pt]
1-\alpha, & \bar\tau>1-\alpha\ \text{and the constraint is slack at }1-\alpha,\\[2pt]
\tau^{\dagger}, & \text{otherwise,}
\end{cases}
\]
where $\tau^{\dagger}$ is the smallest root of the boxed constraint in $(1-\alpha,\bar\tau]$.
The first line deserves emphasis: if the ceiling already binds \emph{below} the elite's own
unconstrained optimum $1-\alpha$, then the MPE tax $\bar\tau$ is the best the elite can do and
an implicit agreement has nothing to offer.  Implicit agreements are useful only where the
holdup problem actually bites, that is where $\bar\tau>1-\alpha$.  \texttt{code/ch22.py}
tabulates this at $\alpha=\tfrac13$, $\beta=0.9$:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{rrr}
\toprule
$\bar\tau$ & MPE tax & best stationary SPE\\
\midrule
$0.30$ & $0.30$ & $0.30$\\
$0.50$ & $0.50$ & $0.50$\\
$0.6\overline{6}$ & $0.6\overline{6}$ & $0.6\overline{6}$\\
$0.80$ & $0.80$ & $0.6\overline{6}$\\
$0.95$ & $0.95$ & $0.6\overline{6}$\\
$1.00$ & $1.00$ & $0.6\overline{6}$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
At $\beta=0.9>\alpha$ the constraint is slack throughout, so wherever the SPE helps it delivers
the full-commitment optimum $1-\alpha$ exactly.
\end{aex}

\solsection{22.6}{Inefficient Economic Institutions}

\begin{aex}{22.13}{}
\emph{The MPE given $\bar\tau$.}  With holdup, the capital stock for date $t$ is already
installed when $\tau^m(t)$ is chosen, so the elite's date-$t$ objective is increasing in
$\tau^m(t)$ on two counts: a higher tax raises revenue on a fixed base, and --- since Condition
22.1 holds --- it lowers middle-class labour demand and hence the wage they pay.  Both effects
point the same way, so the objective is strictly increasing on $[0,\bar\tau]$ and
\[
\boxed{\ \tau^m(t)=\bar\tau\quad\text{for all }t . \ }
\]
An MPE conditions only on payoff-relevant state, and the sunk capital makes the static objective
monotone, so this is the unique MPE.

\emph{The choice of $\bar\tau$ at $t=0$.}  Anticipating $\tau^m(t)=\bar\tau$ for ever, the middle
class invest $\hat k(\bar\tau)$ by \bk{(22.20)}, since they know the tax they will face.  But
that is exactly the investment response the elite faced in \bk{Proposition 22.6}, where the tax
could be committed to one period ahead.  The elite's date-$0$ problem in $\bar\tau$ is therefore
\emph{identical} to the problem solved in Exercise 22.7, and its solution is
\[
\boxed{\ \bar\tau=\tau^{COM}<1 . \ }
\]
$\blacksquare$

\emph{What this says.}  A constitutional ceiling is worth nothing to the elite in the world of
\bk{Proposition 22.11}, where they can already commit one period ahead; under holdup it is worth
a great deal, because it restores exactly the commitment they have lost.  The elite are not
being generous: the ceiling they choose is the tax rate that maximizes \emph{their own} utility,
and it happens also to raise the utility of the middle class and of workers because the holdup
outcome $\tau^m=1$ was Pareto inefficient.  Secure property rights emerge here as a by-product
of the elite's own commitment problem, not of any concern for producers.

\rmk{The restriction to MPE matters, and the text says so.  If the parties can coordinate on the
trigger-strategy SPE of Exercise 22.12, they can already sustain $\tau^{RE}$ --- indeed
$\tau^{COM}$ --- without any constitution, so the demand for the institution disappears.  Formal
institutions and informal agreements are substitutes here, and which one a society uses depends
on whether it can coordinate.  Exercise 22.14 shows a case where the substitution fails.}
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{22.14}{}
\emph{After date $0$.}  Once $A^m$ has been chosen at $t=0$ it is sunk, and the game from $t=1$
onward is exactly the game of Section 22.4.1 with the original timing --- taxes committed one
period ahead.  Condition 22.1 fails and $\phi>0$, so \bk{Proposition 22.4} gives the unique MPE
$\tau^m(t)=\tau^{RE}=1-\alpha$, and \bk{Proposition 22.7} --- Exercise 22.10 --- says this is
also the unique SPE.  The middle class are a continuum of price takers with unique best
responses, so there is nothing to punish with. $\blacksquare$

\emph{The date-$0$ choice of $\bar\tau$.}  Technology is chosen once, at $t=0$, anticipating the
tax.  From \bk{(22.33)} the first-order condition for $A^m$ at a constant tax $\tau^m$ is
\[
\Gamma'(A^m)=\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha(1-\beta)}\beta^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}
(1-\tau^m)^{1/(1-\alpha)}\bar L ,
\]
and differentiating, $\dd A^m/\dd\tau^m<0$: a higher expected tax buys less technology.  The
elite's date-$0$ problem is to maximize
$\frac{\phi}{\alpha}\tau^m(\beta(1-\tau^m))^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}A^m(\tau^m)\theta^m\bar L$,
whose first-order condition is \bk{(22.34)},
\[
A^m-\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}\frac{\tau^m}{1-\tau^m}A^m+\tau^m\frac{\dd A^m}{\dd\tau^m}=0 .
\]
Evaluate the left-hand side at $\tau^m=1-\alpha$.  The first two terms cancel exactly --- that
is the definition of $\tau^{RE}$ --- leaving $\tau^m\,\dd A^m/\dd\tau^m<0$.  The derivative of
the objective is therefore strictly negative at $1-\alpha$, so the maximum lies strictly below:
\[
\boxed{\ \bar\tau=\tau^{TA}<\tau^{RE}=1-\alpha . \ }
\]
$\blacksquare$

\emph{Why an implicit agreement cannot substitute here.}  In Exercise 22.12 the middle class
invested at \emph{every} date, so a deviation by the elite could be met by a change in
\emph{future} investment --- the threat that supports the trigger strategy.  Here the only
long-horizon decision is made once, at $t=0$, and thereafter the middle class have unique
period-by-period best responses.  There is no continuation action with which to punish, so the
SPE cannot improve on the MPE and only an explicit, credible institution can.  This is the
sharpest statement in the chapter of when formal economic institutions are \emph{necessary}
rather than merely convenient: when the investments that policy distorts are infrequent and
long-lived, reputation has nothing to work with.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{22.15}{}
Condition 22.1 holds, $\phi=0$, $\bar\tau<1$, and
$(1-\bar\tau)^{1/(1-\alpha)}A^m<A^e$.

\emph{The elite's objective.}  With $\phi=0$ taxation raises no usable revenue, so --- exactly as
in Exercise 22.6 --- the elite set $\tau^e=0$, make no transfers, and their payoff is
\[
\Bigl[\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\beta^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}A^e-w\Bigr]\bar L+\frac{R^N}{\theta^e} ,
\]
which they maximize by minimizing $w$.  They also set the middle-class tax as high as the
constitution permits, $\tau^m=\bar\tau$.

\emph{The wage.}  By \bk{(22.22)},
\[
w=\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\beta^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}
\min\Bigl\{A^e,\ (1-\bar\tau)^{1/(1-\alpha)}A^m(g)\Bigr\} ,
\]
and the hypothesis $(1-\bar\tau)^{1/(1-\alpha)}A^m<A^e$ says the minimum is attained at the
second term: \emph{even at the maximum permitted tax the middle class remain the marginal
employer and set the wage.}  Hence $w$ is strictly increasing in $A^m$, and since
$A^m(1)>A^m(0)$,
\[
\boxed{\ \text{the elite choose } g=0 \text{ in any MPE or SPE.} \ }
\]
That it holds in any SPE as well follows from Exercise 22.10: with a continuum of price-taking
producers there is no history-dependent behaviour to sustain anything else. $\blacksquare$

\emph{Why $\bar\tau<1$ is necessary.}  If $\bar\tau=1$ the elite can set $\tau^m=1$, which drives
middle-class labour demand and hence the wage to zero regardless of $A^m$.  The wage is then
already at its floor, blocking buys nothing, and the elite are indifferent --- weakly preferring
$g=1$ as soon as any revenue motive is reintroduced.  The lesson is exact and worth stating: the
elite resort to the \emph{inefficient} instrument of blocking technology only because the
\emph{efficient} one --- an unconstrained tax --- has been taken away from them.  Constitutional
limits on taxation, which Exercise 22.13 showed the elite may themselves want, here have the
perverse consequence of redirecting their rent extraction into a more destructive channel.

\emph{Why $(1-\bar\tau)^{1/(1-\alpha)}<A^e/A^m$ is necessary.}  If the inequality were reversed,
the minimum in \bk{(22.22)} would be $A^e$: the \emph{elite} would be the marginal employer and
the wage would be set by their own net marginal product, independent of $A^m$.  Blocking would
then have no effect on the wage at all, and the factor price manipulation channel would be dead.
The condition is precisely the statement that the middle class are close enough to the elite in
productivity, even after being taxed at $\bar\tau$, to matter in the labour market --- which is
when they are a competitive threat worth suppressing.

\rmk{Condition 22.1 is needed for the wage to be positive in the first place, and $\phi=0$ to
switch off the countervailing revenue motive.  With $\phi>0$ the elite value middle-class output
as a tax base, and $g=1$ can win --- which is \bk{Proposition 22.14}.  Putting
\bk{Propositions 22.14} and \bk{22.15} together gives the chapter's answer to its motivating
question: whether those in power block development depends on whether they extract rents
\emph{from} the productive sector or \emph{in competition with} it.}
\end{aex}

\solsection{22.7}{Heterogeneous Preferences and the Median Voter}

\begin{aex}{22.23}{}
By \bk{Theorem 22.4}, under A1, A2 and the single-crossing property a Condorcet winner exists
and equals $p^M$, the bliss point of the median voter: for every $p\ne p^M$, a majority of
voters strictly prefers $p^M$ to $p$.  Two office-motivated parties $A$ and $B$ simultaneously
announce platforms, the majority-preferred platform wins, and a tie is broken by a fair coin;
A4 says each party maximizes its probability of winning.

\emph{$(p^M,p^M)$ is an equilibrium.}  At that profile each party wins with probability
$\tfrac12$.  If $A$ deviates to any $p\ne p^M$, then since $p^M$ is a Condorcet winner a
majority prefers $B$'s platform and $A$ loses with probability one.  The deviation is strictly
unprofitable, and symmetrically for $B$.

\emph{No other equilibrium.}  Suppose $p^A\ne p^M$ in some equilibrium, and consider $A$'s
deviation to $p^M$.
\begin{itemize}
\item If $p^B=p^M$, then $A$ currently loses with probability one and after the deviation ties,
      winning with probability $\tfrac12$ --- a strict improvement.
\item If $p^B\ne p^M$, then after the deviation $A$ offers a Condorcet winner against $p^B$ and
      wins with probability one, which is at least as good and strictly better unless $A$ was
      already winning for sure --- impossible, since $p^M$ beats $p^A$ too, so $A$ cannot have
      been the majority choice against a $B$ that could itself deviate to $p^M$.
\end{itemize}
In either case the deviation is profitable, so no equilibrium has $p^A\ne p^M$; by symmetry the
same holds for $B$.  Hence
\[
\boxed{\ p^A=p^B=p^M\ \text{is the unique Nash equilibrium.} \ }
\]
$\blacksquare$

The proof used nothing about single peakedness --- only that a Condorcet winner exists.  That is
the general shape of every Downsian convergence result: electoral competition between two
office-motivated parties implements the Condorcet winner whenever one exists, and the work is
all in establishing existence, which is what \bk{Theorems 22.1} and \bk{22.4} do under two
different restrictions on preferences.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{22.24}{}
\emph{The key observation.}  For any two policies $p,p'\in R$, write
$\Delta_1\equiv G_1(p)-G_1(p')$ and $\Delta_2\equiv G_2(p)-G_2(p')$.  Then
\[
U(p;\alpha_i)-U(p';\alpha_i)=\Delta_1+B(\alpha_i)\Delta_2 ,
\]
which is \emph{affine} in $B(\alpha_i)$ and hence, $B$ being monotone, monotone in $\alpha_i$.
Therefore the set of voters preferring $p$ to $p'$ is
\[
\{i:\ B(\alpha_i)>-\Delta_1/\Delta_2\}\ \text{ if }\Delta_2>0,
\qquad
\{i:\ B(\alpha_i)<-\Delta_1/\Delta_2\}\ \text{ if }\Delta_2<0,
\]
and is either everybody or nobody if $\Delta_2=0$.  In every case it is an \emph{upper or lower
set in $\alpha_i$}.  This is single crossing (\bk{Definition 22.3}) stated in the type rather
than in the policy, and --- crucially --- it makes no use of any ordering of $R$, so it is
available in $\R^K$.

\emph{The theorem.}  Let $p^M\equiv p(\alpha^M)$ be the bliss point of the median type and take
any $p'\ne p^M$.  By definition of a bliss point, $U(p^M;\alpha^M)>U(p';\alpha^M)$, so the median
voter belongs to the set preferring $p^M$ to $p'$.  By the observation above, that set is an
upper or lower set in $\alpha_i$ containing $\alpha^M$; since $\alpha^M$ is the median, such a
set contains at least half the voters.  Hence $p^M$ defeats $p'$ in a pairwise majority vote,
for every $p'$:
\[
\boxed{\ p^M=p(\alpha^M)\ \text{is a Condorcet winner.} \ }
\]
$\blacksquare$

\emph{What has been gained.}  Single peakedness is a property of preferences \emph{over an
ordered policy space}, and it is exactly the requirement that breaks down in more than one
dimension --- which is why \bk{Theorem 22.1} is confined to $R\subset\R$.  Intermediate
preferences instead order the \emph{voters}, and require that policy disagreements be resolved
the same way at every pair of alternatives.  The structure $G_1+B(\alpha)G_2$ is restrictive ---
it says every voter's indirect utility is the same function of policy up to one scalar loading
--- but it is satisfied in a great many applied models, including every setting in which voters
differ by a single scalar that enters the budget constraint linearly.  Exercise 22.29 shows that
the model of Section 22.8 is exactly of this form, with $B(\alpha_i)=A_i$, which is how
\bk{Proposition 22.17} is proved.
\end{aex}

\solsection{22.8}{Heterogeneity and the Median Voter}

\begin{aex}{22.28}{}
The value function \bk{(22.42)} is
\[
V_i\bigl(k_i(t)\mid p^t\bigr)=\max_{k_i(t+1)\ge0}
\Bigl\{\bigl(1-\tau(t)\bigr)A_if\bigl(k_i(t)\bigr)-A_ik_i(t+1)+T(t)
+\beta V_i\bigl(k_i(t+1)\mid p^{t+1}\bigr)\Bigr\} .
\]
The first-order condition in $k_i(t+1)$ is
\[
-A_i+\beta\,V_i'\bigl(k_i(t+1)\bigr)=0 ,
\]
and the envelope condition, differentiating the maximand with respect to the state, is
\[
V_i'\bigl(k_i(t)\bigr)=\bigl(1-\tau(t)\bigr)A_if'\bigl(k_i(t)\bigr) .
\]
Advancing the envelope condition one period and substituting into the first-order condition,
\[
A_i=\beta\bigl(1-\tau(t+1)\bigr)A_if'\bigl(k_i(t+1)\bigr) ,
\]
and cancelling $A_i>0$,
\[
\boxed{\ \beta\bigl(1-\tau(t+1)\bigr)f'\bigl(k_i(t+1)\bigr)=1\qquad\text{for all }i,t . \ }
\]
$\blacksquare$

\emph{Why $A_i$ cancels.}  The productivity term is labour augmenting, so total capital is
$K_i=A_ik_i$ and total output is $A_if(k_i)$: a more productive entrepreneur scales up both the
cost of an extra unit of effective capital and the return to it by the same factor $A_i$.  The
\emph{effective} capital-labor ratio is therefore common to all entrepreneurs, which is
\bk{Proposition 22.16} and is what makes total revenue \bk{(22.45)} depend on the distribution of
productivities only through its mean $\ol A$.  That in turn is the source of the distributional
conflict in this section: the benefit of taxation is proportional to the \emph{mean}
productivity and its cost to the voter's \emph{own} productivity, so the tax is positive exactly
when the median productivity lies below the mean.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{22.29}{}
\emph{Single crossing.}  Group \bk{(22.46)} in $A_i$.  Since the continuation value
$\wt V_i(p^{t+2})$ does not depend on $\tau'$,
\[
\boxed{\ \begin{aligned}
\wt V_i(\tau')&=G_1(\tau')+A_i\,G_2(\tau')+\text{const},\\
G_1(\tau')&=\beta\tau'\ol Af\bigl(\hat k(\tau')\bigr),\\
G_2(\tau')&=\beta(1-\tau')f\bigl(\hat k(\tau')\bigr)-\hat k(\tau') .
\end{aligned}\ }
\]
This is exactly the intermediate-preferences form of Exercise 22.24 with $B(\alpha_i)=A_i$.
Hence for any two tax rates
\[
\wt V_i(\tau)-\wt V_i(\tau')=\bigl[G_1(\tau)-G_1(\tau')\bigr]+A_i\bigl[G_2(\tau)-G_2(\tau')\bigr]
\]
is affine, hence monotone, in $A_i$, so the set of entrepreneurs preferring $\tau$ to $\tau'$ is
an interval in $A_i$.  That is \bk{Definition 22.3}.  (The direction: $G_2$ is decreasing, so for
$\tau>\tau'$ the difference is decreasing in $A_i$, and if a high-productivity entrepreneur
prefers the higher tax then so does every lower-productivity one --- the reversed order, which
the definition allows.) $\blacksquare$

\emph{An exact criterion for quasi-concavity.}  The envelope theorem applied to $G_2$ --- the
entrepreneur's own continuation value, in which $\hat k$ is chosen optimally --- gives
$G_2'(\tau)=-\beta f(\hat k(\tau))$, the $\hat k'$ terms cancelling by \bk{(22.44)}.  Hence
\[
\wt V_i'(\tau)=\beta f\bigl(\hat k(\tau)\bigr)\Bigl[\bigl(\ol A-A_i\bigr)+\ol A\,\Phi(\tau)\Bigr],
\qquad
\Phi(\tau)\equiv\frac{\tau}{1-\tau}\cdot\frac{\bigl(f'(\hat k)\bigr)^2}{f''(\hat k)f(\hat k)} ,
\]
which is \bk{(22.47)}.  Writing $\varepsilon(k)\equiv kf'(k)/f(k)$ for the capital share and
$\eta(k)\equiv-kf''(k)/f'(k)$ for the curvature,
$\frac{(f')^2}{f''f}=-\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}$, so
\[
\boxed{\ \wt V_i\ \text{is quasi-concave for \emph{every} }A_i
\iff
\frac{\tau}{1-\tau}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon\bigl(\hat k(\tau)\bigr)}{\eta\bigl(\hat k(\tau)\bigr)}
\ \text{is monotone in }\tau . \ }
\]
Indeed the sign of $\wt V_i'$ is the sign of $(\ol A-A_i)-\ol A\frac{\tau}{1-\tau}
\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}$, which changes at most once for every $A_i$ precisely when that
expression is monotone.

\emph{Cobb--Douglas is the special case that works.}  There $\varepsilon=\alpha$ and
$\eta=1-\alpha$ are constants, the bracket is $\frac{\tau}{1-\tau}\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}$,
strictly increasing, and quasi-concavity holds for every $A_i$.  A counterexample therefore
needs a production function whose curvature varies.

\emph{A counterexample.}  Take $f$ with log-curvature
$\eta(k)=\eta_0+\eta_1\exp\bigl[-(\log k-m)^2/(2\varsigma^2)\bigr]$, so that $f$ is nearly linear
over a narrow band of $k$.  With $\eta_0=0.9$, $\eta_1=-0.88$ --- so $\eta$ dips to $0.02$ ---
$m=-2$ and $\varsigma=0.1$, \texttt{code/ch22.py} finds that
$\frac{\tau}{1-\tau}\frac{\varepsilon}{\eta}$ changes direction twice and that $\wt V_i$ has
\emph{two} local maxima for $A_i=0$ and $A_i=0.3$, while the Cobb--Douglas benchmark has one at
every $A_i$.  The left panel of Figure~\ref{fig:ch22} plots the three curves.

\rmk{The two properties are logically independent, and the example shows it.  Single crossing is
an algebraic consequence of the $A_i$-affine structure and survives any $f$ whatsoever;
quasi-concavity is a statement about the shape of one voter's objective and depends on the
technology.  This is exactly why \bk{Proposition 22.17} appeals to \bk{Theorem 22.4} rather than
to the ordinary Median Voter Theorem: the equilibrium tax is still the median entrepreneur's
bliss point even though nobody's preferences are single peaked.}
\end{aex}

\solsection{22.9}{Capital Taxation, Public Goods, and the Strength of the State}

\begin{aex}{22.31}{}
\textbf{(a)}  Competitive firms rent capital and labour from $Y_j=AK_j^{1-\alpha}G^{\alpha}L_j^{\alpha}$.
With $L=1$ in aggregate, $G=\tau\ol K$ and all firms symmetric,
\[
\boxed{\ r=(1-\alpha)A\ol K^{-\alpha}(\tau\ol K)^{\alpha}=(1-\alpha)A\tau^{\alpha},
\qquad
w=\alpha A\tau^{\alpha}\ol K . \ }
\]
The rental rate is a constant, independent of $\ol K$: the Romer-type externality embodied in
\bk{(22.64)} makes the private return to capital constant, so the economy is an $AK$ economy.
The net return faced by a household is $R(\tau)\equiv r-\tau=(1-\alpha)A\tau^{\alpha}-\tau$, and
with logarithmic utility consumption and capital grow at
\[
1+g=\beta\bigl(1+R(\tau)\bigr) ,
\]
which is positive for $A$ large enough and, crucially, depends on $\tau$ alone --- not on how
$\ol K(0)$ is distributed.

\textbf{(b)}  Since $w(s)=\alpha A\tau^{\alpha}\ol K(s)$ and $\ol K$ grows at $1+g$, human
wealth is
\[
H(t)=\sum_{s\ge t}\frac{w(s)}{(1+R)^{s-t}}=\frac{\alpha A\tau^{\alpha}}{1-\beta}\,\ol K(t)
\equiv h\,\ol K(t) ,
\]
using $1+g=\beta(1+R)$.  Total wealth $W_i(t)=(1+R)K_i(t)+H(t)$, and logarithmic utility gives
$C_i=(1-\beta)W_i$, hence $W_i(t+1)=\beta(1+R)W_i(t)$: \emph{all} households' wealth grows at the
same rate, so wealth shares are constant.  Since $H$ is common and proportional to $\ol K$,
\[
\frac{K_i(t)}{\ol K(t)}
=\frac{\omega^W_i\bigl[(1+R)+h\bigr]-h}{1+R}
\]
is independent of $t$, so
\[
\boxed{\ K_i(t)=\omega_i\,\ol K(t)\quad\text{for all }t , \ }
\]
with $\omega_i$ fixed by $K_i(0)$. $\blacksquare$

\textbf{(c)}  Lifetime utility is
$U_i=\frac{\log C_i(0)}{1-\beta}+\frac{\beta\log(1+g)}{(1-\beta)^2}$ with
$C_i(0)=(1-\beta)\bigl[(1+R)\omega_i+h\bigr]\ol K(0)$, so up to constants
\[
\boxed{\
U_i(\tau)=\frac{1}{1-\beta}
\log\Bigl[\bigl(1+R(\tau)\bigr)\omega_i+\frac{\alpha A\tau^{\alpha}}{1-\beta}\Bigr]
+\frac{\beta}{(1-\beta)^2}\log\bigl(\beta(1+R(\tau))\bigr) . \ }
\]
\emph{Single peakedness.}  $R$ is strictly concave, so $1+R$ and
$(1+R)\omega_i+\alpha A\tau^{\alpha}/(1-\beta)$ are concave and positive on the relevant range;
the logarithm of a positive concave function is concave, and a sum of concave functions is
concave.  So $U_i$ is strictly concave, hence single peaked, and \bk{Theorem 22.1} applies.

\emph{The preferred rate.}  Let $\tau^{gr}\equiv[\alpha(1-\alpha)A]^{1/(1-\alpha)}$ be the
growth-maximizing rate, at which $R'=0$.  Then
\[
U_i'(\tau^{gr})=\frac{1}{1-\beta}\cdot
\frac{\alpha^2A(\tau^{gr})^{\alpha-1}/(1-\beta)}
{(1+R)\omega_i+\alpha A(\tau^{gr})^{\alpha}/(1-\beta)}\ >\ 0 ,
\]
so \emph{every} voter prefers a tax above the growth-maximizing rate: at $\tau^{gr}$ the growth
term is flat while the wage term $\alpha A\tau^{\alpha}/(1-\beta)$ is still rising.  The
magnitude of that wedge falls with $\omega_i$, since a capital-rich voter weights the return on
his own capital more heavily.  \texttt{code/ch22.py} confirms both, at $\alpha=0.4$, $\beta=0.95$
and $A=2$, where $\tau^{gr}=0.2943$:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{rrr}
\toprule
$\omega_M$ & preferred $\tau$ & growth $1+g$\\
\midrule
$0$ & $0.34326$ & $1.36719$\\
$0.5$ & $0.34002$ & $1.36745$\\
$1$ & $0.33717$ & $1.36767$\\
$5$ & $0.32271$ & $1.36858$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
By the Median Voter Theorem the implemented rate is $\tau^M=\tau^*(\omega^M)$, and a fall in
$\omega^M$ --- a more unequal distribution of capital --- \emph{raises} the tax.  Since the tax
already exceeds $\tau^{gr}$, this \emph{lowers} growth.  That is the Alesina--Rodrik and
Persson--Tabellini mechanism: inequality is bad for growth because the median voter owns little
capital and therefore internalizes little of the cost of taxing it.

\textbf{(d)}  The equilibrium is not an MPE because the tax is legislated once at $t=0$ and
committed to for ever, whereas in an MPE the policy at date $t$ may depend only on the date-$t$
payoff-relevant state.  Once the capital stock is installed the median voter would want a
\emph{higher} tax than he wanted ex ante --- exactly the holdup of Section 22.5.2 --- so the
date-$0$ choice is not sequentially rational.

To set the problem up as an MPE: by part (b) the shares $\omega_i$ are constant, so the state
reduces to the aggregate $\ol K$.  Posit a policy rule $\tau=\Phi(\ol K)$, solve each household's
Bellman equation
$V_i(K_i,\ol K)=\max_{K_i'}\{\log C_i+\beta V_i(K_i',\ol K')\}$ taking $\Phi$ and the implied law
of motion of $\ol K$ as given, compute the median voter's most preferred \emph{current} tax given
$\Phi$ for the future, and require that this coincide with $\Phi(\ol K)$ --- a fixed point in the
policy function rather than in a number.  Because the economy is $AK$ and homogeneous of degree
one in $\ol K$, one looks for a $\Phi$ that is constant in $\ol K$; the resulting constant is
higher than the $\tau^M$ of part (c), by the holdup logic.
\end{aex}

\begin{aex}{22.32}{}
\textbf{(a)}  Three steps, each of which uses the timing: taxes at $t$ are set after the
capital for $t$ is sunk.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The tax.}  With capital sunk, revenue $\tau Y$ is strictly increasing in $\tau$ up
      to the informality ceiling, so the elite set $\tau(t)=\bar\tau$ for all $t$, which is
      \bk{(22.51)}.
\item \emph{Private investment.}  Anticipating $\bar\tau$, each citizen solves the Euler
      equation of Section 22.3, giving \bk{(22.52)},
      $k_i(t)=(\beta(1-\bar\tau))^{1/(1-\alpha)}A(t)$, and hence revenue \bk{(22.53)},
      $T(A)=(\beta(1-\bar\tau))^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}\bar\tau A/\alpha$.
\item \emph{Public goods.}  The elite's problem \bk{(22.54)} is a concave dynamic programme, so
      by \bk{Theorems 6.3}, \bk{6.4} and \bk{6.6} the value function is concave and
      differentiable and the policy is unique.  The first-order condition \bk{(22.55)} and the
      envelope condition \bk{(22.56)}, $(V^e)'=T'(A)=(\beta(1-\bar\tau))^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}
      \bar\tau/\alpha$, combine to
      \[
      \frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}A(t+1)^{\zeta-1}
      =\frac{\beta^{1/(1-\alpha)}}{\alpha}(1-\bar\tau)^{\alpha/(1-\alpha)}\bar\tau ,
      \]
      that is $A(t+1)=A[\bar\tau]$ as in \bk{(22.57)}, for every $t$.
\end{enumerate}
Substituting into the production function gives \bk{(22.59)}, and uniqueness at each step gives
uniqueness of the MPE. $\blacksquare$

\textbf{(b)}  Write $\vartheta\equiv\alpha/(1-\alpha)$.  From \bk{(22.57)} and \bk{(22.59)},
\[
\log Y[\bar\tau]=\text{const}+\vartheta\log(1-\bar\tau)
+\frac{1}{\zeta-1}\bigl[\vartheta\log(1-\bar\tau)+\log\bar\tau\bigr]
=\text{const}+\frac{\vartheta\zeta}{\zeta-1}\log(1-\bar\tau)+\frac{\log\bar\tau}{\zeta-1} .
\]
The first-order condition is $\frac{\vartheta\zeta}{1-\bar\tau}=\frac{1}{\bar\tau}$, that is
$(1-\alpha)(1-\bar\tau)=\alpha\zeta\bar\tau$, so
\[
\boxed{\ \bar\tau^*=\frac{1-\alpha}{1-\alpha+\alpha\zeta} , \ }
\]
which is \bk{(22.60)}. $\blacksquare$

\textbf{(c)}  The same logarithmic bookkeeping gives the other two in closed form.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{The elite.}  Their steady-state flow is proportional to
      $(1-\bar\tau)^{\vartheta}\bar\tau\,A[\bar\tau]$, whose logarithm is
      $\frac{\zeta}{\zeta-1}\bigl[\vartheta\log(1-\bar\tau)+\log\bar\tau\bigr]$; maximizing gives
      $1-\bar\tau=\vartheta\bar\tau$ and
      \[
      \boxed{\ \bar\tau^e=\frac{1}{1+\vartheta}=1-\alpha . \ }
      \]
\item \emph{The citizens.}  Consumption is $(1-\bar\tau)Y-k$ and, using
      $k=\alpha\beta(1-\bar\tau)Y$, it is proportional to
      $(1-\bar\tau)^{1+\vartheta}A[\bar\tau]$.  Its logarithm is
      $(1+\vartheta)\log(1-\bar\tau)+\frac{1}{\zeta-1}[\vartheta\log(1-\bar\tau)+\log\bar\tau]$,
      and the first-order condition collapses to $1=\bar\tau(1+\vartheta)\zeta$, so
      \[
      \boxed{\ \bar\tau^c=\frac{1}{(1+\vartheta)\zeta}=\frac{1-\alpha}{\zeta} . \ }
      \]
\end{itemize}
Now compare.  $\bar\tau^c<\bar\tau^*$ reads $\frac{1}{(1+\vartheta)\zeta}<\frac{1}{1+\vartheta\zeta}$,
that is $1+\vartheta\zeta<\zeta+\vartheta\zeta$, that is $\zeta>1$ --- true by assumption.
$\bar\tau^*<\bar\tau^e$ reads $\frac{1}{1+\vartheta\zeta}<\frac{1}{1+\vartheta}$, again $\zeta>1$.
And both are strictly inside $(0,1)$.  Hence
\[
\boxed{\ 0<\bar\tau^c<\bar\tau^*<\bar\tau^e<1 . \ }
\]
For social welfare, total consumption is
$W[\bar\tau]=Y[\bar\tau]\bigl[1-\beta\bar\tau/\zeta-\alpha\beta(1-\bar\tau)\bigr]$, which has no
equally clean closed form; \texttt{code/ch22.py} maximizes all four objectives numerically at
$\alpha=0.4$, $\beta=0.9$, $\zeta=2$:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\toprule
objective & numerical & closed form\\
\midrule
citizens & $0.30000000$ & $(1-\alpha)/\zeta=0.30000000$\\
welfare & $0.41305895$ & ---\\
output & $0.42857142$ & $(1-\alpha)/(1-\alpha+\alpha\zeta)=0.42857143$\\
elite & $0.60000000$ & $1-\alpha=0.60000000$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
confirming both chains, $0<\bar\tau^c<\bar\tau^*<\bar\tau^e<1$ and
$0<\bar\tau^c<\bar\tau^{wm}<\bar\tau^e<1$.  Note that the exercise does not assert an ordering
between $\bar\tau^{wm}$ and $\bar\tau^*$, and rightly so: the welfare bracket is affine in
$\bar\tau$ with slope $\beta(\alpha-1/\zeta)$, whose sign depends on whether $\alpha\zeta$
exceeds one.  The right panel of Figure~\ref{fig:ch22} plots the four objectives.

\emph{The economics of the ordering.}  The citizens want the lowest tax because they bear it one
for one on their own output and value the public good only through their own production.  Output
counts the elite's spending as well, so $\bar\tau^*$ is higher.  The elite want the highest tax
because, with investment already sunk each period, they sit at the peak of \emph{their} Laffer
curve and ignore the investment the anticipated tax discourages.  A state whose strength
$\bar\tau$ falls below $\bar\tau^c$ is too \emph{weak} --- the elite expect too few future rents
to be worth investing in public goods --- and one above $\bar\tau^e$ is too strong.  This is the
chapter's balance-of-powers conclusion, and the parallel with the theory of the firm is exact:
ex post bargaining power should go to whichever party's investment matters more, which here
means a higher $\bar\tau$ when $\alpha$ is low and the state's investment is the binding one.
\end{aex}

\begin{figure}[ht]
\centering
\includegraphics{figures/ch22_political_economy.pdf}
\caption{Left: the median entrepreneur's objective \bk{(22.46)} for three productivities, with a
production function whose curvature dips; the curves cross once in $A_i$ --- single crossing ---
but each has two local maxima, so none is quasi-concave (Exercise 22.29).  Right: the four
objectives of Exercise 22.32 against the strength of the state $\bar\tau$, each normalized by its
own maximum, with the maximizers marked.}
\label{fig:ch22}
\end{figure}
